<meta>This page serves as the formal title page for one of the most influential works of the Northern Renaissance, signaling the beginning of Reuchlin's public engagement with Hebrew mysticism.</meta>

# JOHANN REUCHLIN OF <term>PFORZHEIM<note>Latin: *Phorcensis*. Scholars of this period often Latinized their names and hometowns; Pforzheim was Reuchlin's birthplace in the Black Forest region of Germany.</note></term>
## <term>DOCTOR OF LAWS<note>Latin: *LL. Doctoris*. This title indicates Reuchlin was a "Doctor of both Laws"—meaning Civil and Canon (Church) law—reflecting his high status as a professional jurist.</note></term>, THE BOOK CONCERNING
## THE <term>WONDER-WORKING WORD<note>Latin: *De Verbo Mirifico*. This refers to the mystical power Reuchlin attributed to the sacred names of God, particularly the Hebrew name of Jesus, which he believed could perform miracles.</note></term>

<summary>The title page of Johann Reuchlin's 1494 treatise, "On the Wonder-Working Word," a foundational text that introduced Jewish Kabbalistic thought to the Christian scholarly world.</summary>

<meta>This letter follows the title page of Johann Reuchlin's "On the Wonder-Working Word." It serves as a commendatory preface, written by the humanist Conrad Leontorius to the famous educator Jacob Wimpfeling, urging that Reuchlin be recognized among the great scholars of their age.</meta>

### A commendatory letter from <term>Conrad Leontorius of Maulbronn<note>Conrad Leontorius (c. 1460–1511) was a Cistercian monk and humanist scholar based at the monastery of Maulbronn.</note></term> in praise of the most eloquent man and expert in the three principal languages, Johann Reuchlin of Pforzheim, and the books he recently published concerning the *Wonder-Working Word*.

->Conrad Leontorius sends many greetings to <term>Jacob Wimpfeling<note>Jacob Wimpfeling (1450–1528) was a leading German humanist and educator often called the "Teacher of Germany."</note></term>.<-

As soon as I departed from you at <term>Mainz<note>Latin: *Moguntiaco*.</note></term>, I immediately experienced in myself that proverb common among our French neighbors: a friend is never enough, whether present or absent. For when I was present with you, I did not enjoy your company enough to satisfy our friendship—as usually happens to all who care for one another, who realize upon departing that they omitted many things they had intended to do or say. But what I omitted, letters will repair; I wish that through their exchange I could be with you more often. 

We were speaking of the "Book of Illustrious Men" by the <term>Lord of Sponheim<note>Leontorius refers to Johannes Trithemius, the Abbot of Sponheim, who was compiling a catalog of famous ecclesiastical writers.</note></term>, which you said had been sent to the press at Basel. I asked about Doctor Johann Reuchlin, and whether he had been included. When you seemed to doubt it and said no, I was astonished. For whom could you consider more illustrious in every kind of learning in this age? If he were intimately known to you, you would find no one greater. Therefore, you and your Abbot would pass him over only by doing great injury and loss to the German name. 

For he is a most skilled interpreter of the three languages: Greek, Hebrew, and Latin—add also, if you wish, French, which he learned thoroughly at Orleans while he was devoting himself to the study of law. As a young man, he translated many speeches, some of which—unless I am mistaken—are those of Xenophon composed in defense of Socrates. He has composed many delightful poems and written many epistolary disputes, which he filled with the insights of the Greeks and even the Hebrews. 

However, to penetrate the secret dogmas of the Hebrews, he labored most strenuously for many years and studied their entire library so thoroughly that you will not easily find any Hebrew <note>i.e., Jewish scholar</note> to oppose him. Led by this abundance of talent and learning, he applied himself to writing a dialogue which he titled *<term>Capnion<note>Latinized from the Greek *Kapnion*, meaning "Little Smoke." This was Reuchlin’s humanist nickname, a direct translation of his German name (Räuchlein).</note>*</term> or *Concerning the Wonder-Working Word*, than which I have never seen anything more eloquent or more marvelous. 

It is divided into three books. The first explains all the secrets of philosophy under the name of Sidonius. The second creates the character of Baruchias the Jew, who unravels all those secret and portentous names and many other Hebrew mysteries. The third introduces Capnion <note>Reuchlin's own persona</note> demonstrating the Christian faith from the previous two parts. There, he applies all the "wonder-working words" to the name of Jesus in such a way that he demonstrates that the <term>ineffable Tetragrammaton<note>The sacred, four-letter Hebrew name of God (YHWH), which was traditionally considered too holy to be spoken aloud.</note></term> has now been made pronounceable. 

If you read this dialogue, you will affirm that no one—neither philosopher, nor Jew, nor Christian—can rightly be preferred to Johann Reuchlin. It would be a lasting shame for us not to present such a man to all the Italians. Therefore, my Jacob, since I am about to head to Augsburg and I do not know when I shall reach Basel, I would like you to write with the greatest care and diligence to <term>Johann Amerbach<note>One of the most important printers in Basel, known for his scholarly editions.</note></term> the printer, so that he may assign Johann Reuchlin of Pforzheim his proper place in the Catalog. Farewell, my most pleasant Jacob, happy and fortunate. 

Speyer, the 21st of April, 1494. <note>Latin: *xi. Kal. Maias. M. CCCC. XCIIII.*</note>

<summary>This letter from Conrad Leontorius to Jacob Wimpfeling serves as a glowing recommendation of Johann Reuchlin, highlighting his mastery of Greek, Latin, and Hebrew and his groundbreaking work on the mystical power of the name of Jesus.</summary>

<meta>This page is the formal dedication of Johann Reuchlin’s seminal work, "De Verbo Mirifico" (On the Wonder-Working Word). It follows the previous commendatory letter by Conrad Leontorius. Reuchlin addresses Johann von Dalberg, the Bishop of Worms, explaining his motivation for writing this dialogue on mystical names and the "Wonder-Working Word."</meta>

# THE *CAPNION* OR THE WONDER-WORKING WORD
## BY JOHANN REUCHLIN OF PFORZHEIM, DOCTOR OF LAWS

To the most sacred <term>Bishop of Worms<note>Latin: *antistes Vangionum*. The Vangiones were an ancient tribe located in the area of Worms.</note></term>, **Johann von Dalberg**, <term>Chancellor<note>Latin: *Camerarie* (Camerarius), a high-ranking official title held by the Dalberg family.</note></term>:

There are in our time certain curious explorers of secret things (if I see correctly) who are troubled by the hidden powers of words, the abstruse energy of voices, and the divine characters of secret names. These men are found to have departed significantly from the ancient footsteps of the foremost philosophers. They often stray far regarding the operations of mysteries full of wonderful effects, primarily for this reason: because the symbols of that sacred philosophy and the venerable signs of supernatural virtues can neither be understood nor even read. This is due either to the fading of the figures into obscurity or the perverse and faulty corruption of the copyists. Therefore, many people, affected by weariness and exhausted by fruitless effort, either flee from such a laudable kind of contemplation in dread or abandon what they have already begun through neglect.

For this reason, I was led by an incredible desire to be of service and by a singular love for that outstanding pillar of all good arts, **Johann Lapidanus**, <term>Doctor of Theology<note>Johann Heynlin (c. 1425–1496), known as Lapidanus, was a highly influential scholar and Reuchlin's teacher.</note></term>—distinguished both for his monastic life among the Carthusians and for his publication of books, and the first under whose tutelage I submitted my hands to be instructed in the liberal arts. To him, I add **Sebastian Brant** of Strasbourg, a man most learned in both laws and highly adorned in polite letters, bound to me by long-standing friendship. And likewise **Johann Amerbach**, a man of excellent talent and noble skill in the crafting of type <note>Amerbach was a famous printer in Basel; Reuchlin highlights his "artificio characterum" or skill with fonts/characters.</note>, learned in various disciplines. Both are most celebrated in the literary republic of learned men; one hardly knows whether they are more famous for their deeds or for their illustrious city, for they are held in no small honor among the highest citizens of **Basel**.

Moved therefore by the great bond of friendship with such great men, I have dared to enter into such darkness and the obscured hiding places of sacred—indeed, secret—words. From the very shrines of the oracles and the inner chambers of the most ancient philosophy, I have set out to explain to our age, as much as my memory allows, nearly all the names that wise men endowed with miraculous powers used in sacred rites in times past. These include the sacraments of the **Pythagoreans** and older philosophers, the foreign <term>incantations<note>Latin: *memoracula*, referring to words or tokens used for memory or ritual power.</note></term> of the **Hebrews** and **Chaldeans**, and the devout prayers of the Christians. These can be seen brought forth from their books and languages in this work.

I have dedicated this work to your name because sacred things are due to sacred men, and especially priestly matters to the princes of priests. You stand above others in dignity, not only for the magnitude of your domain and power—in which you are most abundantly wealthy—but also for your unspeakable expertise in various literatures. Of this, your library is witness, filled with **Latin, Greek, and Hebrew** volumes. It is the one treasure of our **Germany**, which I have always been accustomed to use according to the desires of my soul.

Receive, therefore, this dispute among three philosophers concerning the *Wonder-Working Word*. I have <insert>presented<note>original: *finxi*, literally "I have fashioned" or "invented." Reuchlin is noting that the dialogue is a literary invention.</note></insert> them debating among themselves (as the controversy between their schools required), so that the hidden properties of the sacred names might shine forth more clearly. Concerning these, and so many and such great...

<summary>Johann Reuchlin dedicates his work on the mystical power of names to Bishop Johann von Dalberg. He explains that he wrote the book to recover lost ancient wisdom and names used by Pythagoreans, Hebrews, and Christians, which had become corrupted over time.</summary>

<keywords>Johann Reuchlin, Johann von Dalberg, Johann Lapidanus, Sebastian Brant, Johann Amerbach, Wonder-Working Word, sacred names, Kabbalah, Pythagoreanism, Basel, linguistics</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the formal dedication to the narrative structure of the dialogue. It introduces the three main characters: Sidonius (representing pagan philosophy), Baruchia (representing Jewish tradition), and Capnion (representing Christian Kabbalah, and the pseudonym of Reuchlin himself). It sets the scene in Reuchlin's hometown of Pforzheim.</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

...an easier occasion might finally be provided for us to choose that one thing which is truly supreme, wonder-working, and beatific. And so that you may know the whole matter in a brief summary: Sidonius—who was first thought to be of the <term>Epicurean school<note>A school of philosophy that emphasized pleasure as the highest good and a world made of atoms, often viewed as atheistic or materialistic by Renaissance thinkers.</note></term>, but was later found to have sworn his word to no single master—is a philosopher of every kind. Driven by a desire for learning, he travels abroad. After many journeys, he enters <term>Swabia<note>Latin: *Suevia*, a region in southwestern Germany.</note></term> and meets two philosophers in the town of <term>Pforzheim<note>Latin: *Phorce*, the author Johann Reuchlin's birthplace.</note></term>: **Baruchia** the Hebrew, and **Capnion** the Christian. With them, he discusses various disciplines, and soon moves to the very knowledge of divine and human things. They debate opinion, faith, miracles, the power of words and figures, and the secret operations and mysteries of seals. In this way, they conduct an enumeration of symbols from all nations that possess some excellent philosophy or noble ceremonies, examining sacred names and consecrated characters. Each character is diligently examined according to the rites of their own sect, until at last, in the third book, **Capnion** gathers from all the sacred things one single name: <term>ihsuh<note>Original: "ihsuh". This is the "Pentagrammaton," Reuchlin's mystical rendering of the name of Jesus (YHSVH) by inserting the Hebrew letter "Shin" into the center of the four-letter name of God (YHVH).</note></term>. Into this name, the power and authority of all sacred things are referred, which is blessed forever and above all. But now, let us hear Sidonius himself.

->**SIDONIUS**<-

If there is anything anywhere in human nature, my friend Baruchia and you, Capnion—both of you being the very <term>Pillars of Hercules<note>Latin: *Gades*, referring to the limits of the known world, implying they are the ultimate destination of his search.</note></term> and the goal of my incredible desire, and the refuge for my long-pursuing wonder (for I am pleased to speak fearlessly to you alone, as you command and listen)—which might produce perpetual pleasure for us, it consists, I believe, in this one thing and everything: almost nothing else than perceiving daily new things, strange to our senses, or things perceived at another time or in another way than they were sensed long ago. This usually entices our mind, which is greedy for beauty, to a desire for experience by some most recent and wonderful marvel, and propels it into a task, however arduous. Because of this, I could easily believe that not only my ancestral line (which was clearly famous for many centuries and is now celebrated for its skill in all things) but also the entirety of <term>Sidon<note>An ancient Phoenician port city in modern-day Lebanon.</note></term>—from which I take both my name and origin—and even my whole fatherland, was incited toward these labors (sweet yet hard) either by pleasant novelty or by stupendous antiquity. For since ancient times, the **Phoenicians** have been accustomed to indulge, more than can be said, in the exploration of various regions and the study of new things. By their own diligence, they were the first to invent letters, the benefit of which they bestowed upon the Greeks—who were perhaps not ungrateful men. After a refined calculation of numbers, they measured the lands and the heavens. They were likewise most expert in the natural and martial arts. Finally, that nation always saw both the customs and the cities of many people, a fact which resulted in the greatest glory for them in the largest part of the world and a great proclamation above others. By your leave, I perhaps praise my ancestors too much, but love of country prevails, and nonetheless, truth itself reveals it. For even that ancient doctrine of atoms was first the work of a man who was once a fellow citizen of mine, a Sidonian named **Mochus**. And this was before the Trojan War, so that you may know how much the Phoenicians excelled in industry, in talent, and in the investigation of many affairs. Indeed, this was not even hidden from <term>Lucan<note>Marcus Annaeus Lucanus (39–65 AD), a Roman poet.</note></term>, who was no less a historian than a poet, when he sang: "The Phoenicians were the first, if fame is to be believed, who dared to mark the enduring voice with unrefined figures. Not yet had **Memphis** learned to weave the river-side...

<meta>catchword: bi (from "biblo" or "biblis" - referring to papyrus/reeds)</meta>

Sidonius begins by praising his Phoenician heritage and their invention of writing and science.</summary>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general introduction of the work to the beginning of the dialogue itself. Sidonius, the traveler and narrator, is speaking. He addresses Capnion (Reuchlin) and Baruchia, explaining his long journey from the East and across Europe to reach the famous scholars of Swabia.</meta>

# BOOK ONE

...[he] knew, and only the birds and wild beasts and animals carved in stone preserved the magical languages. Eager to follow the monuments of their praise and their footsteps—to which (as is natural) the flow of my paternal blood finally led me—I have now spent many years traveling through many lands and seas, seeking out the finest masters of speech and of things, so that I might hear what was said and see what ought to be done. For I was not content only with my own homeland or the whole neighboring "Three-City" region—rightly the mistress of diverse and even all arts, though it has now begun to be twisted by barbaric ritual—but I went away, I departed, I broke out further. I traveled beyond Lebanon and Syria, wandering more than that famous Menelaus who informed his guests Telemachus and Pisistratus of his travels with these verses:

->I have suffered not a few things, and arriving with my ships after many dangers in the eighth year, I came to Cyprus, then Phoenicia and the Egyptian fields; then through the Ethiopians and Sidonians and Erembi, and Libya, where the horned lambs graze.<- <note>Sidonius is quoting a Latin translation of Homer’s *Odyssey* (4.81–85) to emphasize the scale of his own travels.</note>

Thus, I did not lack labor in the long dangers of those same paths, until at last, having left Britain, then Gaul, and then with the Rhine as my guide, my journey was granted into Germany. My goal was to see and test both of you wise men—not so much with my eyes as with my ears. For I would adapt that saying of **Plautus** <note>Titus Maccius Plautus was a Roman playwright; Sidonius is referencing a line from his play *Truculentus*.</note> in this way: *One ear-witness is worth more than ten eye-witnesses*, especially when it comes to getting to know philosopher-men, as I have heard from every direction that you are most celebrated by fame. 

And let me speak first of you, **Capnion** <note>original: "Capnion". This is the Greek-style pseudonym of the author, Johannes Reuchlin; "Capnion" means "little smoke," just as "Reuchlin" is derived from the German "Rauch" (smoke).</note>, whom I had learned was a native of **Pforzheim** from the various inscriptions on your books and records. I was amazed—more than by almost anything else—that the "wooded **Swabia**" <note>original: "syluestris Sueuia". A reference to the Black Forest region.</note> could produce philosophy. At that time, I had certain companions on my journey who were fellow students of the hidden arts; they replied that it should not be hard to believe that such a rugged land could produce an excellent natural talent. They reminded me that the former Bishop of **Regensburg** <note>original: "Ratisponantū".</note>—called "Polyphemus" in the Greek tongue but known by his family name as **Albertus Magnus** <note>original: "Albrechtū cognomento magnū". Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280) was a monumental scholar and teacher of Thomas Aquinas.</note>—seemed to surpass not only **Aristotle** but also the other primary followers of the <term>Peripatetic philosophy<note>The school of philosophy founded by Aristotle, so named because he famously walked while teaching.</note></term>, and yet he was both born and raised in **Swabia**. I myself knew this to be true. For I had already read several notable books written by that man in Latin, which, because of their singular excellence, had been translated into Greek (a rare occurrence). 

Finally, they brought forward no vain or fragile reasons for this belief: that your people easily migrate elsewhere, partly because of the scarcity of food, the difficulty of farming the fields, and the lack of money. I will add myself that the region cannot be accustomed to an abundance of trade goods. Therefore, I believe that among the migrating Swabians there will always be a bounty of the best teachers, since those who flee sterile places must migrate to the very best locations. Thus, the fact that you excel in doctrine, eloquence, and counsel is not because you were born in a forest (which they once called the **Bacenian** or **Hercynian** <note>Names for the vast ancient forest of Central Germany.</note>, and now call the **Black Forest**). Rather, as I hear from your own people, it is because you have traversed not only ancient **Paris** <note>original: "Parrhisiorum Lutetiam".</note>, but almost all the universities of Gaul and Germany, several in Italy, and **Rome**, the capital of studies. Although, I do seem to see that the suitability of the Pforzheim countryside contributes not a little to the genius of the natives, and the huge number of literate men...

<sig>a iii</sig>

<summary>Sidonius describes his extensive travels from the East to Germany, specifically seeking out Reuchlin (Capnion) in Swabia. He defends the intellectual reputation of the region by citing the example of Albertus Magnus and Reuchlin’s own international education.</summary>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the speech of Sidonius, a traveler who has arrived in Germany to consult with the scholars Capnion (Johann Reuchlin) and Baruchia. Having previously described his travels through the East, he now attempts to bridge the gap between his Mediterranean heritage and the local history of his hosts.</meta>

### ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

...the number of ancestors thereafter creates. For a fatherland has no less power over the condition of one's birth than the father himself, if **Porphyry** <note>Porphyry of Tyre (c. 234–305 AD) was a Neoplatonic philosopher; he wrote extensively on the influence of place and birth on the soul.</note> is to be believed. You have here a quite long speech from a newcomer and a traveler who is attempting to discuss your own region. But so that you do not think me a sluggish investigator of that place—which is a home to one and a place of exile to another—it would certainly be fitting for me not to omit even this fact: what I discovered in Phoenicia from the most learned authors of ancient monuments.

For it is well known that once in the Trojan War—or rather, the Trojan battle—**Phorcys** the younger was a leader of the Phrygian army, by the testimony of **Homer**, who in the "Catalog of Ships" speaks thus: *That Phorcys was the leader of the Phrygians, and Ascanius too.* <note>original: "Phorcys ille Phrygum ductor fuit Ascaniuſque"; a reference to the Iliad, 2.862.</note> And so, with fortune turned against them while Ilium <note>Troy</note> fell, those few survivors from the countless many who had saved their lives were forced to seek other seats for themselves and to hope for other kingdoms.

Therefore, **Aeneas** <note>The legendary Trojan hero who fled the fall of Troy and eventually founded the Roman lineage.</note> with **Ascanius** and their companion in danger, Phorcys, along with many others, sought Italy. But while Ascanius was founding a town beneath the Alban Mount <note>Alba Longa, the precursor to Rome</note>, and each man was marking out a place for himself to stay here or there, this Phorcys the younger—the grandson of the elder Phorcys (who was King of Sardinia and Corsica, said to have been defeated once by Atlas and submerged in the sea) by Euryale—entered through Italy and the land of the **Insubres** <note>A Celtic people living in what is now Lombardy, Italy.</note> into Germany. 

Having crossed a massive lake between the sources of the **Danube** <note>original: "Istri"</note> and the **Rhine**, he traveled through easier mountain passes and upland plains toward the setting sun, where he came upon a most clear and fish-filled river, and on its bank, a humble hut. Dragging his tired steps toward it, an old man caught sight of him—burdened with years and leaning entirely upon a staff. When the old man saw Phorcys marching through such a deserted forest with such a great company of followers, he was terrified, thinking he was nearly about to give up the ghost. But when Phorcys asked what this flow of water was called, the old man himself replied that its name was **Aeneas**. <note>The river Enz, which flows through Reuchlin’s hometown of Pforzheim, was known in Latin as the *Enus* or *Anisus*; Reuchlin is playfully connecting it to the name of the Trojan hero Aeneas.</note>

Then Phorcys, astonished by that name and turning his face toward the river, immediately added: *Are you that Aeneas whom nourishing Venus bore to Dardanian Anchises by the wave of the Phrygian Simois?* <note>original: "Tune ille Aeneas quem Dardanio Anchisae Alma Venus Phrygii genuit Simoentis ad undam?"; Reuchlin is quoting Virgil’s *Aeneid* (1.617–618).</note> **Virgil Maro** later transferred these verses into his *Aeneid*, having been delighted by their ancient, foreign character. Here, therefore, Phorcys took an omen that as a traveler he would likely have to stay, and he began to exhort his multitude not to despise the divine oracle which he had discovered in the name of the Trojan priest. For to those whom the hero Aeneas had long ago left behind at no small distance, another Aeneas <note>the river</note> now clearly showed itself. 

The Phrygian leader, therefore, with a royal mind and manner, commanded all of them to strive as much as they could to uproot the strength of the trees on every side and create a level field out of the wilderness. In that place, he built a small town and a citadel, which he called **Phorcen** <note>Pforzheim</note> after his own name and his grandfather's. To such an extent does that most grave, holy, and worthy man Aeneas remain beloved, that at the mere sound of the voice saying the river was called Aeneas, Phorcys chose to settle there as if at a perpetual monument to the Trojan Aeneas. 

And so, with the passing of time, as a modest household became a populous people, and the narrowness of their first habitation forced them to cut down the neighboring woods, cultivate the fields, build villages, and lead out cities, they say that he founded many other towns there besides the rural and urban ones. Each of these he marked with individual names of both his mother and his aunts for the eternal memory of his lineage; some of these, devouring antiquity has abolished, and some the ignorance of barbarians has changed...

<summary>Sidonius recounts a foundational myth for the city of Pforzheim (Phorce), claiming it was settled by the Trojan leader Phorcys, who chose the site after discovering a river named Aeneas (the Enz).</summary>
<keywords>Pforzheim, Phorcys, Aeneas, Trojan War, Enz River, Virgil, Homer, Germany, genealogy, etymology</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues Sidonius’s address to Capnion (Johann Reuchlin). Sidonius is weaving a mythological and etymological tapestry that connects the ancient world of Troy and Rome to the German lands of Swabia, specifically to validate the intellectual heritage of his host.</meta>

<header>BOOK ONE</header>

...strives for modesty. For what was once **Euryale** is now **Aurelium**, what was **Stheno** before is now **Sthein**, what is called **Scylla** is **Scell**, and what was once **Medusa**, was in a previous age changed into a new citadel. <note>The speaker is using creative etymology to link the three Gorgons (Euryale, Stheno, and Medusa) and the sea-monster Scylla to German place names like Öhringen (Aurelium), Stein, and Schelklingen.</note> Thus the Gorgon daughters of **Phorcys** (whose fame is widespread and whose memory is most ancient) are preserved in the eternal memory of men, lasting through every age, by this fertile soil and the pleasant situation of this town, O **Capnion** <note>original: "Capnion"; the Greek name for Reuchlin, meaning "little smoke."</note>—that is, in the furrow of your own origin. 

And lest there be lacking for posterity a lasting monument to **Ascanius’s** <note>The son of the Trojan hero Aeneas.</note> returned companion and colleague, he also began, with the thought of that city which he himself had furrowed in a circle beneath the **Alban Mount**, to build something similar, which he called by the same name, **Alba Longa**. <note>Alba Longa was the ancient mother-city of Rome. The author is suggesting a German equivalent was founded by the same lineage.</note> This name remains today, starting from **Phorce** within the second milestone. 

Thus you have seen me paint a picture of your origin in the manner of **Eudoxus** <note>Eudoxus of Cnidus (c. 390–337 BC), a Greek astronomer and geographer known for his maps of the world.</note>, like some sailors' chart, insofar as it leads us to that knowledge: that it can be believed that such great men, most accomplished in every branch of learning—though of different sects, yet born from the same turf—are to be found in this very **Swabia**, whose conversation I have sought with singular desire for a long time in these thickets and glades, finding them more precious than gold, silver, pearls, and all costly things. 

Now, pay attention to why I have come here. After the arts of speaking, oratory, and perceiving, in which I was initiated under the schoolmaster’s rod; then after the disciplines of numbering and measuring, which I drank in with a freer spirit, I was led by an admiration of things both on earth and in the heavens toward natural proofs, with which I had decided to complete the whole course of my life. 

Because concerning **ethics** (which some philosophers consider a branch of philosophy), I thought little effort should be given. I do not judge it worthy of too much mental occupation, because moral practice consists in the observation of custom and the common social life of the world. It is something that threats and charms teach to boys, and punishment to adults; also, innate diligence grants something to the virtues themselves, which cannot be taught by study but grow strong as the institution of each region demands. 

Hence we see that what is called **virtue** here is considered a **vice** elsewhere, according to the opinions of the inhabitants. Thus I say that **law** <note>original: "ius"</note> is nothing other than what men want. It is my opinion, therefore, that civility and jurisprudence are contained solely in the persuasion of the common people or the judges, whether you achieve it through oratory, gifts, or flatteries, as long as the one presiding over the law is soothed. 

In the works of **Ulpian** <note>Domitius Ulpianus (c. 170–223 AD), one of the great authorities of Roman Law.</note>, an indisputably learned man, we read of a certain **Barbarius Philippus**, who, though a fugitive slave, sought the praetorship <note>An elected magistracy in ancient Rome.</note> in Rome; then, having been designated praetor and finally having performed the duties of the office, it was accepted that none of those things he had done while under the cloak of such great dignity should be rejected. The same weighty author left this written for posterity concerning the status of men: that a person must be accepted as freeborn <note>original: "Ingenuum"</note> even if a sentence has been passed regarding them, even if in reality they were a freedman, because *a matter judged is accepted as truth*. <note>original: "res iudicata pro ueritate accipitur"; a fundamental legal maxim.</note> 

And **Paulus** <note>Julius Paulus, another influential Roman jurist of the 3rd century AD.</note>, most skilled in Roman law, says: Today, because of the practice of the emperors, if a candelabrum made of silver is recorded as being of silver, it is indeed of silver, even if it is not furniture, because **error makes the law**. <note>original: "error ius faciat"; the principle that a general mistake can create a legal right or status.</note> Therefore, whatever has pleased the people, the prince, the king, or the judge, has the force of law, whether through custom or through legislative constitutions. For as **Julian** <note>Salvius Julianus, a jurist who lived during the reign of Hadrian.</note> says, since the laws themselves bind us for no other reason than that they have been received by the judgment of the people, and the people...

<sig>a iiii</sig>

<summary>Sidonius uses mythological etymologies to link Swabian geography to Troy and Rome, before shifting to a skeptical argument that ethics and law are merely matters of local custom and social consensus rather than universal truths.</summary>
<keywords>Gorgons, Alba Longa, Swabia, Ulpian, Barbarius Philippus, Roman Law, legal error, ethics, Johann Reuchlin, Eudoxus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue of Sidonius as he addresses Capnion (Johann Reuchlin). Having previously discussed the mythological and geographical origins of Capnion’s homeland, Sidonius now shifts his focus to a critique of the legal profession, contrasting the "low" art of human jurisprudence with higher intellectual pursuits.</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

Since the royal law <note>original: "regia [lex]"; likely referring to the *Lex Regia*, the Roman law that transferred the power of the people to the Emperor.</note> conferred all command and power upon the king, it appears clearly that all jurisprudence depends solely on the will of men. If this will supports what is good and subjugates what is evil, it is called a "good thing," both in public and private life. But if it is otherwise, it is the opposite. Indeed, because of the injustices committed with the people’s approval or tolerance, **Hesiod** <note>An 8th-century BC Greek poet.</note>, in his book *Works and Days*, after writing much concerning kings, judges, and justice, finally concluded that the common people must often pay for the sins of their kings—that is, their judges. Indeed, not only he, but also **Homer** and all of antiquity, called all judges "kings," for whom the scepter is the distinguished sign of their jurisdiction. To this, that noble oath of **Achilles** against **Agamemnon**, explained in six or seven lines of the *Iliad*, serves as witness.

I am not speaking before the ignorant, but rather, as I believe, in the presence of most learned and refined men—men far more numerous than those with me—whom it does not escape that this so celebrated and common practice of law is often sold at a great price by those seeking profit. Though held as almost more than divine by those who consult on controversies, it should more rightly be esteemed as of no or even moderate importance, like some sedentary craft <term>sellulariam</term> <note>A "sitting craft" or manual trade, often used disparagingly for work done by artisans in shops rather than liberal arts.</note> or a cobbler’s trade. This is especially true for a man who would gladly direct himself toward high and most worthy matters with the greatest praise, rather than admiring any of the fragile, falling, fleeting, and empty mockeries of glory, beauty, riches, or age.

For this discourse, a grave and holy witness and a very good authority will be cited (if you wish): **Cicero**. He dared to defend my opinion on this matter before the tribunals, even while **Servius** <note>Servius Sulpicius Rufus, a famous Roman jurist and contemporary of Cicero.</note> was listening and unwilling—Servius, who then considered himself a man learned in civil law. I ask you, **Capnion**, you who are said to have even sweated in the workshop of that same profession until you earned the crown and the laurel: what ornament, what dignity, what grace, what admiration resides in that study? It hangs upon such a thin opinion of men, occupied with every single letter and punctuation of words. It is shouted by everyone—not only by the commoners, but even by the nobles—to be the authority of all factions, the strength of injustices, the fire of quarrels, and the fuel of iniquity. It sets as its goal only the empty names of magistrates, honors full of daily fear, mercenary gains, and riches that no wise man should crave; therefore, it openly brings upon its practitioners the enmities of many. To these practitioners, so many **Antonys** and so many **Catilines** <note>Reference to Mark Antony and Catiline, famous rivals or enemies of Cicero, representing political and civil strife.</note> appear, and they do not allow a mind seeking rest to be at peace. 

Furthermore, it is not difficult to know that which everyone observes; indeed, they claim that whatever is not observed in every village is not law. Why then should I marvel so greatly at something that any butcher, cook, tailor, fisherman, fowler, or hunter might know as well as I? Does it seem like a great thing to you to know the name of every law and to apply it to every event? Why will you not esteem more highly the perfumer—whom they call an apothecary because he stores things away—who knows every little jar, box, pitcher, bowl, cup, vial, spice, pigment, and drug in his shop? Or the cobbler, who remembers how to add any shape to any foot, and does not forget the awls, needles, shears, and thimbles? All these things are taught by use, practice, necessity, and the pressing want of things; and sometimes a brute hunger forces these similar things upon us. Who smoothed out the "Greetings" <note>original: "χαι̑ρε" (chaire), a Greek greeting.</note> for the parrot, and taught magpies to attempt our words? As the Satirist <note>The Roman poet Persius, in the prologue to his Satires.</note> says: "The belly, master of art and giver of wit, is the craftsman that follows voices denied to it." Therefore, that which one does not learn for its own sake, but—

iiii B

<meta>This page continues the speech of Sidonius as he pivots from a critique of human law to a defense of natural philosophy. Having just dismissed the "sitting craft" of jurisprudence as a matter of human will and commerce, he now defines the boundaries of true "science" against the abstractions of metaphysics.</meta>

BOOK ONE
<meta>catchword: for indeed</meta>

...because of the daily commerce of human livelihood, and because of earthly desires foreign to the human mind, in what way could I join it [the law] as a limb to most sacred philosophy? Without it, philosophy would seem crippled, just as one lacking a foot is called lame, or one without a tongue is called mute. I leave these things aside, for I know not what storm or what wave has diverted me to this point. I return to where I turned aside. For I have determined to spend the entire course of my life in the contemplation of natural things, from which alone arises that which we can truly call science <term>scientia</term> <note>original: "scientia"; here referring to certain, demonstrable knowledge derived from the physical world.</note>. For other things are either instruments of science, or disciplines of calculation <note>original: "supputationū disciplinę"; likely referring to mathematics or accounting.</note>, or the customs of common estimation, or fictions of thought concerning things that men believe subsist without body or matter—and therefore they call them "simple" [beings]. Since these exist nowhere, there is nonetheless a type of man who is not satisfied with drawing science from things that offer themselves to our senses—knowledge which can neither be deceived nor deceive—but instead they burden the mind with the thought of certain most thin spirits <note>original: "tenuissimorū flatuum"; literally "thinnest breaths" or "vapors."</note> and separated forms. Because these are not in nature, but are thought to be above nature, they therefore call them **metaphysics**. 

I do not argue these things with the intention of refuting learned men, although they seem worthy of rebuke if we were to weigh with a balanced scale the rite of knowing, the fountain from which it is drawn, and the path by which it proceeds. Regarding this, best **Capion** <note>Johann Reuchlin, whom the speaker addresses by his humanist name.</note>, permit your **Aristotle** to speak—in whose wrestling school <note>original: "palæstra"; a metaphor for the university or school of thought.</note> they say you have exercised yourself most greatly, and from which, once your time in the gymnastic contest was complete, you carried away the distinguished headband <note>original: "tiaram"</note> and gold rings. For this **Baruchias** <note>A Jewish scholar participating in the dialogue.</note> is said (as I hear) to follow **Pythagoras** more closely, and whatever other more ancient **Magus** <note>A practitioner of the "magical" or ancient wisdom tradition.</note> might have lived. Therefore, that **Stagirite** <note>Aristotle, born in Stagira.</note>, in the second book of the *Posterior Analytics* <note>original: "resolutoriorū posteriorū"</note>, near the very end, set forth this opinion, which I believe fits here quite well. Listen, both of you, if by chance I can explain it well enough in Latin. For, as you know, Aristotle is excessively thorny <note>original: "senticosus"</note> in Greek, as well as obscure and even rough, and he did not wish to be understood except by his own followers. 

His words are of this sort: "From sense sprouts that which we call memory; from memory, indeed, when it is frequently obtained from the same thing, is born experience <term>peritia</term> <note>original: "peritia"; practical skill or expertise derived from trial.</note>. For many memories in number make up one experience. From experience, however, or from the universal entirely resting in the soul—the one thing assumed from the many, which indeed shall be one within those individual things—there arises the beginning of art and science." 

You have heard Aristotle, whom the poet **Afranius** once resembled both charmingly and wisely, and no less truly in the book titled *The Chairs* <note>original: "Sellæ"; a lost Roman comedy.</note>, regarding the fountain-head of the sciences, produced in this verse: "Practice begat me, and Memory gave birth to me" <note>original: "Vsus me genuit, mater peperit memoria."</note>. Therefore, since those about to attain science must use limbs and the instruments of the senses—without which one happens to be ignorant of all things (for the blind man knows not colors, nor the deaf man the measures of verses or the mode of the lyre)—it will now be necessary that we can comprehend only bodily things. This is because that which is most thin flows from the body in a certain perpetual stream to the openings of our senses; then, by constant motion and with no leisure removed, imagination itself introduces that very thing into its inner chamber <term>thalamū</term> <note>original: "thalamū"; the "inner room" or "bridal chamber" of the mind.</note>, in which the forms of things shine back into the intelligence of men, just as a ray bent from a standing wave enters the pupil. All these things are bodies and the flows of bodies. Whence **Empedocles** seemed to me to have spoken more correctly, saying that "we know like by like." Let no delay or rest be given to this flowing. Since we perceive perpetually, as **Lucretius** says, for indeed...

<summary>Sidonius argues that true knowledge is derived exclusively from sensory experience and the natural world, dismissing metaphysics as a collection of "separated forms" that do not exist in nature. He uses the Aristotelian progression from sense to memory to experience as his primary evidence for this empiricist worldview.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, metaphysics, empiricism, natural philosophy, Johann Reuchlin, memory, sensory perception, Empedocles, Lucretius</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>Continuing the discourse of Sidonius from the previous page, where he argues that true knowledge is rooted only in the physical, sensible world, dismissing the abstractions of metaphysics as mere poetic fictions or "dreams."</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

Living beings possess various senses, each of which perceives its own proper object within itself. Why "properly"? Because everything else either deceives or is deceived—at least those things which a person thinks he knows without the aid of any senses. But by Hercules, that shrewd interpreter of **Epicurus** <note>likely referring to the Roman poet Lucretius, who translated Epicurus's atomist philosophy into Latin</note> tries to pull me back, fearing I might suggest things too foreign to you. He says, "Indeed, the greatest part of these things deceive us because of the opinions of the mind which we ourselves add to the things seen, so that things not perceived by the senses are treated as if they were." For nothing is more excellent than to discern clear facts. 

This is not considered by those who attempt to climb above nature; they leap into a narrow place from which they cannot retreat. Despite so many things born in the vastness of this universe, they still exhaust themselves over fictions—over what they call "bare beings," and the "species of things," over "immovable divine entities," over "subsistences," over the "first axioms of reason," over "principles," and over the "simplest, incorporeal substances of the gods" dwelling outside the home of heaven and there leading some "best life." All of these things—on the authority of the poets—were discovered as delights for the ears and as solaces for our greed, always most eager for various affairs, serving as a singular and beautiful art of invention. Therefore, let all such gods and those meditating on such divine things be gone; let them go to bed and sleep the whole night through. 

In **Homer's** judgment, sleep does not touch Jupiter alone <note>original: "Iouem"; Jupiter/Zeus</note>, as if the other celestial beings were nothing. He alone can be, yet we clearly know nothing of him; we only seem to dream of him. The same poet, quite prudently in my view, suggests this when he describes the dream sent to **Agamemnon** as being a messenger from Jove. For whatever we know about Jove is most similar to a dream. 

And yet, what should we think of "axioms" and "principles"? The **Peripatetics** <note>followers of Aristotle, known for walking while teaching</note>—whom now all **Germany** especially follows to the exclusion of all other most noble authors—competently argue that there is no "science" of these things. They claim this because these principles are better known than any science or demonstration; indeed, all science depends upon them, and there is no "principle of a principle," since the principle itself exists before all things. Yet it is not right for that to remain unknown by which all things become clear, appear, and are known. From where, then, shall we know these principles? From the senses. 

Listen again to your **Aristotle**, **Capnion** <note>original: "Capnion"; the Greek translation of Johann Reuchlin's name, meaning "Smoky"</note>, in the first volume of his *Topics*, when he says: "If anyone should doubt whether all snow is white or not, he has need of the senses." Therefore, the certainty that we have from principles consists entirely in things subject to nature and the senses; for this reason, nothing that is not connected to body and matter is rightly enclosed within the limits of science. 

If this is so, a single knowledge of things will suffice, which the Greeks call <term>physics</term> <note>original: "phyſicam"; here meaning natural philosophy or the study of the physical world</note> and you are accustomed to call **natural philosophy**. It investigates and searches out the conditions of those things which are true and perceived by us without a veil, as well as their causes, effects, times, places, modes, outcomes, entireties, and parts. 

->*From whence comes the tremor of the earth; by what force the deep seas swell,*<-
->*bursting their barriers, and sink back again into themselves;*<-
->*the sun's various eclipses and the labors of the moon;*<-
->*whence came the race of men and beasts, of rain and fire;*<-
->*Arcturus, the rainy Hyades, and the twin Bears;*<-
->*why the winter suns hasten so to dip themselves in the Ocean,*<-
->*or what delay stands in the way of the slow nights.*<-
<note>Sidonius is quoting or paraphrasing Virgil’s *Georgics* (Book II) and *Aeneid* (Book I), describing the scope of natural science.</note>

Physics, the observer of natures, embraces all these things, in which the most learned men may delight themselves in a wonderful way for their entire lives. For the sake of this study—as you have heard from me before—I have traveled through many places and the perils of places...

<summary>Sidonius argues that true science (scientia) is strictly limited to the physical world and sensory perception, dismissing metaphysics and abstract "principles" as poetic fictions or unknowable dreams. He identifies "Physics" (natural philosophy) as the only valid field of human knowledge, citing Aristotle and Virgil to support his focus on the material universe.</summary>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Sidonius—a representative of pagan, empirical philosophy—is speaking. Having dismissed metaphysical abstractions as mere "dreams," he now recounts his physical travels to find truth in the tangible wonders of the natural world.</meta>

->### BOOK ONE<-

I have undergone dangers so that I might see the wonders of nature, or at least grasp them by report. I even traveled to India itself, to see if I could catch sight of those things which, though rare, are said to be seen there occasionally with one's own eyes: monsters and things resembling prodigies, such as the manticore <note>original: "martichoras"—a mythical creature with a human head, lion's body, and scorpion's tail</note>, golden water, the lodestone, the pantarba stone <note>a legendary magnetic gemstone</note>, pygmies, sciapodes <note>original: "sciapodas"—mythical beings with a single giant foot used as a sunshade</note>, the tiger, the phoenix bird, pearls, and likewise the fish-eaters <note>original: "ichthyophagos"—peoples described by ancient geographers as living solely on fish</note>. Then I traveled through Africa to look upon the nation that keeps a dog as its king, by whose movements they divine the commands of their empire. I pass over other things I was able to hope for but not witness: one-eyed princes, man-eaters <note>original: "Anthropophagos"</note>, and dog-headed men <note>original: "Cynocephalos"</note>, the greater part of all of which is fabulous. 

Regarding springs and rivers, some of what is said is true, while part is adorned with fiction. In the Lake of Asphalt <note>The Dead Sea</note>, bitumen is truly produced, but as for the claim that nothing can sink in that Judean lake, the opposite is actually found at times. In Dodona, the spring of Jupiter both lights extinguished torches and puts out lit ones. The spring of Hammon <note>The Siwa Oasis in Egypt</note> is cold by day and boils by night. The island located opposite the Timavus flows with hot springs in the sea itself, which rise and fall along with the tide; I experienced this myself with the people of Trieste. The water of Lyncestis makes people drunk just like wine; moreover, *among your own Swabian people, by the river Fils at the town of Göppingen, a spring flows with a taste like wine.* <note>The author, Reuchlin, likely includes this local German "miracle" to flatter his audience and ground the dialogue in his own geography.</note> 

A year, not just a day, would fail me if I wished to recount every single miracle of nature, which nonetheless are not unknown to you. Indeed, I have heard from most learned men that you abound so much in diverse experiences of both letters and things that nothing is so small or so great as to escape you—whether seen in person or known through books. From this, I easily conclude that I speak of many wonders in vain; for what I marvel at for its novelty, you, through your constant study, find common and daily. 

But I, without dispute, seek you yourselves out as the one miracle in Germany. By what virtue and by what learning you are such grave and holy men! The judgments of all people regarding you are excellent beyond what can be said. I have explored this sufficiently at the very entrance, at the doors themselves and the start of our greeting: what a divine cast of your countenances, what charm in your words, what humanity in receiving me! Your conversations, often interspersed with exact wisdom, show you to be men of the most perfect character. It is incredible how much I rejoice to enjoy your company, your speech adorned with learned words and polished oratory, and your sentiments so worthy of being known and heard. Through such liberal and generous conversation with you, I am able to soothe and calm—or rather, entirely lift—the many great labors I have undertaken on so many journeys.

Then **BARUCHIAS**, moved by the abundance of these words, said: "Listen, Capnion <note>The Greek-style name Reuchlin used for himself, meaning "little smoke" (from the German 'Rauch')</note>, to what offspring Sidon has brought forth! For I would easily believe the great things he proclaims about the Phoenicians, if only those people could see him now—those who condemn almost the entire East to ignorance and barbarism. They say that some Scythians or Sarmatians <note>barbaric tribes from the Eurasian steppes in ancient geography</note>, enemies of humanity and learning, have invaded and hold that region, so that no one who cultivates the arts will survive. But Sidonius, into what wonders do you force me with your eloquence?"

But he replied: "There is little in me, Baruchias, that you should value so highly; for you strive in vain to extol things that I myself recognize as very minute within me. And beware lest you unjustly bestow the name of 'eloquence' on my narrow words. For what have we finally expressed with this little speech of ours other than Punic..."

<summary>Sidonius recounts his travels to find natural wonders across the world before praising the profound learning and hospitality of his German hosts. Baruchias responds by noting how Sidonius's eloquence contradicts the contemporary view of the East as a place of terminal barbarism.</summary>

<keywords>natural wonders, India, Africa, Germany, Göppingen, Baruchias, Capnion, Sidonius, Phoenicia, eloquence, manticore, sciapodes</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Sidonius—having traveled the world looking for empirical truths—recounts his impressions of various lands before pivoting to a debate on the nature of knowledge itself. He is addressing Capnion (Reuchlin) and Baruchias.</meta>

->### ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD<-

...of the nation’s character is most suitable, as all histories indeed record and the reality clearly shows. I also touched upon the praise of your Swabia <note>Suevia: the region in southwestern Germany where Reuchlin was born</note>, of which you both are abundant witnesses to the world, and no other spectacle besides you will be necessary. Afterward, as is the habit of a talkative tongue, a certain madness escaped my mouth—namely, the idea that unless a nature is perceptible to the senses, nothing is worthy of art or discipline. And if not about "no things" (as Socrates used to judge), then at least about those very things which lie beneath nature and are open to the senses, there will be that which we most especially call science <note>In this context, "science" (scientia) refers to certain, provable knowledge rather than modern laboratory science</note>. For regarding divine things, I judge that no true cognition has been granted to man.

When I had said this, Baruchias interrupted the conversation: "Most learned Sidonius," he said, "and as I think, the best of all men—which is far more excellent—forgive me. For a certain innate boldness of speech makes me get ahead of this Capnion <note>Capnion: The Greek translation of Reuchlin's name, meaning "little smoke"</note>, a man of venerable integrity, a veritable ocean of letters, and a divine specimen of our age. Because of this, he should rightly be preferred over me in this disputation; but it is an old custom that the more one stutters, the more one yearns to speak, and no field of speaking lies more open than to the ignorant. This is what has happened to me: while you are discoursing, I break the silence in the presence of this man of renowned wisdom. But bear it with an even mind, Capnion."

Then he replied, "Indeed, go ahead freely and weave together what you have begun."

"And so," Baruchias said, "you have spoken many things very industriously and eloquently on many subjects, Sidonius—more than our own philosophers are accustomed to do. They, like certain jackdaws <note>original: "graculi"—a common Humanist insult for Scholastic theologians, implying they chatter without understanding</note>, do not cease to chatter all day about every business with a single barbaric noise. They employ no ornaments, no flower of eloquence, nor any duty of the orator. You would think they were not dealing with most sacred philosophy, but with the profane commerce of the common folk, or the bidding for spoils at an auction, or the tumult of revelers, or the insults of the most frivolous men, or the abuses of the baths. From my association with them, I have contracted a similar contagion and a like stain, so that you might not unfairly hurl at me that verse of Tyndareus written in Euripides' *Orestes*: 'Live as a barbarian, since you are an old man among barbarians' <note>original: "Vivas barbare cum sis vetulus inter barbaros." Baruchias is lamenting that his own style has been corrupted by the "barbaric" Latin of the Scholastic philosophers.</note>. Nevertheless, however things stand, I will gladly and perhaps deliberately let pass what you have discussed concerning our country, yourself, and ourselves—since our own honor forbids me from refuting the former, your own fame the second, and the reputation of the whole world the third. To increase with speech those things which you have raised with praises would not be within my power—lest I carry 'owls to Athens,' as the Greek proverb goes <note>original: "noctuam Athenas"—a classic idiom meaning to perform a redundant or useless task</note>."

"But truly, I wonder greatly that you found so much light in so many and such varied bodies—which arise at any moment and are subject to the sea's gale and whirlwind, clinging to matter itself (the mother of corruption) and to daily motion and inconstancy—and that you found so much firmness and so much truth that you believe science arises from nothing except what is perceptible to the senses. I, on the contrary, consider nothing so opposed to sincere and perfect science as this very thing you call 'sensible.' I think that what appears to the body—which, as you were saying, flows into the openings of our senses like a certain fluid, not without the thinnest fleeces migrating through the air until they reach our bodies—is unstable. For how could the ray of the mind stand tranquilly or reflect unshaken upon such a shifting surface, as are all the things of nature which men perceive from the outside? How could there be in us so illustrious a [knowledge] of divine humanity, or human di[vinity]..."

<summary>Baruchias interrupts Sidonius's empirical argument, critiquing the "barbaric" style of contemporary Scholastic philosophers and arguing that true, stable knowledge cannot be derived from the ever-changing material world.</summary>
<keywords>Capnion, Baruchias, Sidonius, Scholasticism vs Humanism, Epistemology, Sense Perception, Swabia, Euripides, Socratic Ignorance</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues Baruchias’s argument against the possibility of certain knowledge regarding the natural, sensory world. He contrasts the changing nature of physical things with the unchanging "science" sought by philosophers, citing the lineage of Pythagoras, Plato, and Aristotle against the atomism of Epicurus.</meta>

<header>BOOK ONE</header>

...so that a work of divinity <note>original: "uinitatis," likely a fragment of "divinitatis"</note> might be fashioned from there. I do not deny that there is a certain doctrine, opinion, or judgment concerning such matters. We distinguish ice from water, and we judge the latter to be fluid and the former to be more lasting, even though the former arose from the latter just the other day, and the latter is ready to be dissolved back into the former the next day. 

But that a constant and perpetual science <note>In the medieval and Renaissance sense, "science" (scientia) refers to certain, demonstrated knowledge of things that cannot be otherwise</note> can subsist concerning them, that I indeed deny—and not only with Pythagoras as my guide, whose follower you wished me to be (a role I gladly accept as an honor), since I understand from Plato, that supreme man and almost a god among philosophers, that the disciples of Pythagoras were so highly esteemed that even though Plato was poor, he did not hesitate to buy the books of Philolaus the Pythagorean alone for ten thousand denarii. 

But Aristotle also agrees with us in this matter, whose patronage we fortunately employ. For in the first volume of the *Posterior Analytics* <note>original: "posterioris resolutionis" — Aristotle's work on the requirements for scientific demonstration</note>, when he had discussed at length what kind of cause we ought to choose in knowing, and who truly knows and who merely appears to be a "little scholar" <note>docticulus: a mocking term for one who has only a surface-level education</note>, he added: "Therefore, that of which there is science in the simple sense, it is impossible for it to be otherwise." This is not foreign even to Plato, who, after dividing the world into the first, "intelligible" world <note>the realm of ideas/forms</note> and the second, "sensible" world <note>the physical world we perceive</note>, added that the former is judged by the mind with reason, while the latter is judged by opinion joined with the senses. 

See which Epicurus <note>Epicurus was often associated with materialism and the belief that the senses are the only source of truth</note> you follow, who builds everything upon dust out of a shadowy discourse. With such great patrons on my side, I shall easily win. And if it should not seem shameful for me to be defeated, refuted, or proved wrong by a man as great as you—surpassing others in talent, learning, and dignity—who am often forbidden by the decree of our ancestors from touching philosophical matters (even "at the tips of the lips," as they say). And for that reason, Germany nourishes Hebrews <note>Baruchias refers here to the Jewish community in German lands</note> who, although religious, are nevertheless not very learned, because they persuade the studious that they should rather shrink from philosophers than fall into errors through neglect or rather curiosity—errors of which they believe the philosophers are the primary authors. 

But I have done as the greedy usually do: the more I was deterred, the more fervently I drank, according to the opportunity of circumstances, the time, and my own capacity. Thus I confess that I have sometimes read Aristotle and looked into Plato, from whom that which I cited to you before emerged. And even without them, the great diversity among all the greatest men writing about natural things—even while they earn immortal praise—would have made me believe, and does make me believe, the same thing: that truth cannot consist in such variety. 

First of all, to go from the streams to the source, I think that neither you nor our friend Capnion <note>Johannes Reuchlin</note> here will deny that a whole thing is known in the way that it is established to be. For if it were otherwise, opinion would deceive, bringing before the mind's eye something different from the reality. But the universe has its own principles so that it may exist, even according to your Epicurus and Lucretius, who says: "It is necessary that all things be separated from their principles." <note>Lucretius, *De Rerum Natura*</note> And that the world arose from principles of every kind: "Their discord threw into confusion the intervals, paths, connections, weights, blows, concurrences, and motions, mingling their battles." <note>A poetic description of the chaotic atomic motion required to form the universe</note> 

Therefore, we likewise know all things from the principles out of which they are made; upon their scale and rule <note>amussi: a mason's level or rule</note> depends the demonstration of the sciences, just as the inner chambers depend on the doors. But when one wanders at the doors, how can I believe that one will rightly enter the inner chambers? Yet they wander who, ignorant of the way, nevertheless proceed. They are ignorant who rush in crowds here and there around the crossroads, not in one...

<b>

<summary>Baruchias argues that certain knowledge (scientia) cannot be found in the shifting physical world, citing the authority of Plato and Aristotle over the materialism of Epicurus. He also reflects on the cultural restrictions placed on Jewish scholars in Germany regarding the study of secular philosophy.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Plato, Epicurus, Philolaus, Pythagoras, Jewish education in Germany, nature of knowledge, Posterior Analytics, Lucretius, atomism</keywords>
