<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Baruchias (a Jewish scholar), Sidonius (an Epicurean philosopher), and Capnion (a Christian scholar). Baruchias is currently critiquing the inconsistencies of the Greek natural philosophers to demonstrate that their "science" of the physical world is unstable, eventually leading to his argument for the superior certainty of divine revelation and the mystical power of names.</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

...traveling on a journey, seeing how much they differ from one another and how much they differ from the truth. Shall I begin with those Phoenicians of yours? You rightly thought they were the first and greatest to be endowed with the investigation of all nature. I speak even of Thales himself, who, although a citizen of Miletus, was nevertheless descended from the most noble Phoenicians, Cadmus and Agenor. It is said that he, before all others, disputed concerning the nature of things. And if Plato is to be believed, he was the first to be called a "wise man." <note>Thales of Miletus (c. 624–548 BCE) is traditionally regarded as the first philosopher in the Greek tradition.</note> What, then, did he find after his innumerable efforts and tireless studies—for which he cast aside both the republic and the highest magistracies? What, I ask, did he find by his constant searching that was a worthy foundation for this illustrious machine <note>The "machine of the world" was a common Renaissance metaphor for the universe.</note> that we behold? Listen to his famous conclusion: "Water is the principle of all things." For from water all things have come forth, and into water all things shall be resolved.

No one doubts that if we were to entrust the construction of this great mass of the world to Thales's judgment, we would always have to fear floods and surging stagnations. For by nature (according to his opinion), he would be shown to have placed this liquid floor beneath his buildings, which would surely collapse at any movement of the water. I will say nothing of how much those who labor in the architectonic art would object to one who chooses a marshy region for the structure of a home. But perhaps Thales intended some kind of "flow," or (as I judge) an influence from elsewhere which could have been the beginning of secondary causes—which Homer seems to me to name "Ocean" when he sang: "Ocean, the father of gods, and Tethys the mother." <note>original: "Oceanumq; deum patrem tethynq; parentem." This quote from the Iliad was often used by ancient philosophers to suggest that even the poets believed water was the source of life.</note> 

Yet his countryman Anaximander, first the auditor of his teaching and then his successor in the school, deliberately moved far away from that view. For he established that all things sprout from the "Infinite." <note>term: Infinite <gloss>Apeiron</gloss>. Anaximander's concept of an indefinite, boundless source of all things.</note> I shall add a third Milesian and another countryman of yours, a certain Anaximenes, the disciple of that Anaximander. He abandoned his master's precepts and dreamed that all things arise from the air, and into the air they also return. 

Heraclitus of Ephesus, a despiser of philosophers and a mocker of Homer, belched forth from his exhaling lungs and hot vitals an unheard-of thing. He left it written in obscure and uncommon letters: that a certain sterile element which we call Fire begets the universe, and all things are again referred back to it. In vain, therefore, did the Romans place Vesta <note>Vesta was the Roman goddess of the hearth, whose temple fire was kept burning by celibate priestesses.</note> in charge of celibacy. 

Anaximenes was succeeded by the renowned Anaxagoras, for a time a citizen of Clazomenae. Afterward, having spurned the republic and all popular dignity for the sake of philosophy, he became a most distinguished philosopher. He attributed much to a certain "similar participation," which he thought was both the cause and the generation of things. <note>term: similar participation <gloss>homoeomery</gloss>. The theory that things are composed of tiny particles that are of the same nature as the whole.</note> Then there was Archelaus of Athens, the son of Apollodorus (for there was also another certain Milesian mentioned by Homer). He thought that infinite, dense air provided the cause for all things to exist, and for this reason, he is called "the Physicist." 

Then followed a certain sun of all secret philosophy, from whom that craft first received its name: Pythagoras of Samos. <note>Pythagoras is credited with coining the term "philosopher" (lover of wisdom) rather than "wise man."</note> I would truly say that he did not err, but rather disagreed with the others. For he himself fashioned the universe out of numbers. He learned that discipline from our people—that most ancient wisdom of both Syria and the Chaldeans. <note>Baruchias is claiming that Pythagoras's famous mathematical philosophy was actually derived from Jewish and Near Eastern sources.</note> Upon returning from there, he illustrated it for the Greeks with words and sentiments, providing such great admiration of himself to his people that they hesitated as to what name they should call him. I cease to praise this prince of philosophers, who saw no predecessor and has no second, and for whom no equal praise can be found...

<summary>Baruchias reviews the conflicting theories of the Pre-Socratic philosophers regarding the primary element of the universe (water, air, fire, etc.) before introducing Pythagoras, whom he claims derived his superior numerical philosophy from ancient Jewish/Eastern wisdom.</summary>
<keywords>Thales, Anaximander, Anaximenes, Heraclitus, Anaxagoras, Pythagoras, natural philosophy, elements, Miletus, Phoenicia, Jewish wisdom</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue of Baruchias, who is systematically dismantling the conflicting theories of ancient Greek philosophers to show that human reason alone cannot find certain truth about the physical world.</meta>

# BOOK ONE

...could proceed from me. And at the same time, lest I seem to flatter myself too much if I were to extol those arts and the man whom you yourself pointed out that I follow, I leave the praise of **Epicurus** <note>Epicurus (341–270 BCE) was a Greek philosopher who taught that the world is composed of atoms and void, and that the goal of life is tranquility.</note> to you alone—though I wonder that he was born in Athens and not rather in the countryside. This man affirmed that the **principles of things** <note>original: "principia rerum"</note> were indivisible little bodies <note>atoms</note> and empty space. 

But the famous **Empedocles of Agrigentum** enumerates four **elements** <note>original: "elementa"</note> from which everything is born: fire, air, water, and earth. He also identifies two primary powers, **Strife and Friendship**, the latter of which brings things together while the former scatters them. 

**Plato**, the disciple of **Socrates**, decided there were three beginnings of things: God, the forms of things, and matter. He believed that from these, the first thing we perceive to result is "figure," and finally all the rest. 

Most recently, **Aristotle**—more famous for the nobility of his art than for being the tutor to Alexander, King of the Macedonians—maintained that all things have grown together from matter, which is stirred toward a "desire" for form through "privation." <note>Aristotle’s theory of change involved Matter, Form, and Privation (the absence of a form that ought to be there).</note> 

Whoever attempted to discover anything following these men established some kind of motion in place of privation, as a more effective way to drive the two together. 

Do you presume, **Sidonius**, that in such a great diversity and contention among so many men—the most distinguished of their age and (to use the names common then) so many "wise men" and "lovers of wisdom"—that a certain trust in the truth can be established? This is especially true when they fight with such a marvelous conflict regarding the very beginnings of the world, that you are forced to deny to whom among them you should justly and holistically award the palm of victory or the "harvest-grass" of surrender. <note>In ancient Rome, "giving the grass" (herbam dare) was a sign of admitting defeat.</note> For as **Firmianus** <note>Lactantius (c. 250 – c. 325), an early Christian author often called the "Christian Cicero."</note> says, "No one has yet descended from heaven to pass judgment on the opinions of each individual." 

Certainly, you might find some who approve of the mind of Epicurus along with you; I might find those who do not reject the dogmas of **Pythagoras** <note>Pythagoras (c. 570–495 BCE) emphasized the mathematical and mystical nature of the universe.</note> along with me. But the choice of our profession leads us away, so that suspicion removes us as judges, and we are not admitted as irreproachable arbitrators. 

What then? What shall we say are the principles of things? Certainly, there has always been a huge struggle among the most famous philosophers, and "the lawsuit is still before the judge." <note>A quote from Horace, meaning the matter is undecided.</note> Therefore, since the beginnings are not yet certain, I ask you: what kind of true knowledge of things are you going to have, since things are said to arise from beginnings of this sort? 

For Aristotle thinks we "know" a thing only when we recognize its individual principles, causes, and elements. And not only that, but also why the cause of that thing is as it is, and not otherwise. 

Stop, therefore, seeking stability in decaying things. Commit acorns to the pigs, hay to the oxen, and the sensory prediction of storms to the frogs and crows. Let the brutes perceive mortal things and those subject to destruction; let us strive for higher things and embrace with an eternal mind not those things which are fluid and fragile beneath the heavens, but those which are everlasting above the heavens. 

Nor shall I attempt to press upon the as-yet-unpracticed ears of our people those things which even the most highly trained can scarcely understand. For they would say that I am separating knowledge from all universal things—and indeed, they would speak the truth regarding those things subject to the volatility of nature. For we are looking at the perpetuity of knowledge and, at the same time, at that torrent of nature and that unstable river, of which we touch nothing at any time except a single point. 

Was our "Preacher" <note>Referring to the author of Ecclesiastes (Kohelet).</note> so foolish, whoever he was who wrote **Ecclesiastes**—whether it was **Solomon**, or as many rumor, **Isaiah** himself? "What is it," he asks, "that was? That which shall be." And a little before: "A generation passes away, and a generation arrives." Indeed, how will you be washed unless the wood is changed? How will you be nourished, unless the receptacle [of the stomach] transforms the vital juices through things cooked and changed...

<column-break/>

<summary>Baruchias critiques the conflicting theories of ancient philosophers like Epicurus, Plato, and Aristotle regarding the "principles of things," arguing that physical science is unstable and that one should instead seek eternal truths above the heavens.</summary>
<keywords>Epicurus, Empedocles, Plato, Aristotle, elements, strife and friendship, privation, Firmianus, Ecclesiastes, instability of nature, principles of things</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the speech of Baruchias, who is demonstrating the limits of human reason to Sidonius. He has just finished listing the various physical theories of the elements and is now moving toward the mystery of life, death, and the soul.</meta>

->**ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD**<-

...and distributes food and drink into each of the limbs? Cattle produce dung, dung becomes manure, manure grows grass, grass makes milk, and milk in turn makes the cattle. The world <note>original: "seculum"</note> rotates all things; it is said to be named from "following" <note>A traditional etymology: *saeculum* from *sequendo* (following), implying one generation follows another.</note> because it propels things born into it just as a wave drives a storm, a day drives the hour, and an hour drives the moment. It absorbs one thing so that it may bring forth another. Nature is always fashioning so as not to be idle—like a craftsman working in wax, she makes now a horse, now a lion, and once that is destroyed, a man. One survives the other, and the second always buries the first. **Lucilla** follows **Lucilla**. **Secunda** buried **Maximus**, then followed after him. **Antoninus** buried **Faustina**, then followed her himself. 

Everything goes this way. **Celer** buried **Hadrian**, then came **Celer’s** turn. Indeed, the entire court of **Augustus**—his wife, daughter, descendants, ancestors, sister, **Agrippa**, his relatives, household, friends, **Arius**, **Maecenas**, the priests, and the doctors—all are gone. Death is the mistress of the entire court. Here, you might establish a "perpetual knowledge" for me, so that I may know if whoever wove the history of these people wrote the truth. **Hippocrates**, though he cured innumerable diseases, died. **Heraclitus**, who filled the world with his theories of fire, is said to have been extinguished by "water under the skin" <note>dropsy (edema)</note>. A swarm of lice—the smallest of creatures—killed **Democritus**. 

It is no different in things that lack a soul. For among the types of trees, **Ebony** is sometimes wood and sometimes stone. And in the book called the **Quran** <note>original: "Alkorran"</note> among your people, it is asked: "What was first wood, then spirit?" They say it was the **Rod of Moses**, which was now wood, now a serpent. 

I will deal with you briefly, for you are not the kind of person whom I must feed like a nurse putting chewed food into the mouths of wailing boys. There is nothing written, painted, or fashioned that is hidden from you. Why then should I hesitate? For when I consider that our own wits lack the strength to scrutinize nature with absolute certainty, a doubt <note>original: "scrupulus," literally a small sharp stone in one's shoe</note> is always cast upon our thinking. A thorn fixed in the heel is not easily pulled out by the roots. Shall we boast that we can judge without error regarding every kind of form that shapes matter, when we do not yet perfectly know what or how our own **soul** <note>original: "anima"</note>—by which we live, breathe, feel, and understand—actually is? 

Regarding this matter, there will be no need for many words with you. You yourself know how many different opinions exist about it, and how many "labyrinths" of thought are carried around. **Epicurus**, whom you seem to imitate, says the soul is a form mixed from fire and air; from this comes heat, motion, and the "blind power of the winds," and from there all things are made mobile. **Xenophanes** says it is made from earth and water. **Parmenides** says earth and fire. **Empedocles** and **Critias** say it is blood. **Anaximenes** says it is air. **Hipparchus** says fire. **Critolaus** the Peripatetic says it consists of a "fifth essence." **Democritus** calls it a mobile spirit stuffed with atoms. **Zeno** says it is a spirit grown together with the body. **Heraclitus** the Physicist says it is a spark of stellar substance. **Heraclitus of Pontus** says it is light. **Hippocrates** says it is a thin breath diffused through the whole body. **Poseidonius** says it is an "Idea." **Pythagoras** and **Philolaus** say it is "Harmony." **Xenocrates** says it is a "self-moving number." **Plato** says it is an essence that moves itself. 

**Aristotle** boastfully invented a new name, <term>entelechy</term> <note>original: "entelechia"; Aristotle's term for the "actualization" or perfection of a body that has the potential for life.</note>, which is the "perfection of form," as if it were the final goal of a craftsman. That his discourses on the soul ought to be refuted as "mortal" <note>i.e., treating the soul as something that dies with the body</note> is attested by **Gregory Nazianzen**—a man of the greatest integrity and famous for the highest dignity among the **Christians** <note>original: "Galilaeos" (Galileans), a term sometimes used for Christians in late antiquity and by humanist writers.</note>—in his book against the **Eunomians**. 

Therefore, if we are ignorant of our own selves, how can we hope to obtain knowledge of other things? There has been a vast multitude of outstanding writers, and yet until the times of **Jerome** and **Augustine**, there was nothing certain regarding the soul upon which one could plant a firm foot...

<summary>Baruchias argues that human reason is insufficient to understand the world or the human soul, citing the inevitable cycle of death and a long list of conflicting ancient philosophical definitions of the soul.</summary>
<keywords>soul, anima, death, transience, Aristotle, entelechy, Plato, Epicurus, Gregory Nazianzen, Quran, Moses, human reason, skepticism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the speech of Baruchias. Having previously discussed the mortality of famous figures and the transience of the physical body, he now turns to the impossibility of achieving certain knowledge about the material world, contrasting human "opinion" with divine "wisdom." He addresses Capnion (the Greek name for Johannes Reuchlin) and Sidonius.</meta>

### BOOK ONE

->b iii<-

...it had been left behind, which (as you are, like me, a curious explorer of every sect) you were perhaps able to learn from our exchanged letters. For concerning you, **Capnion** <note>The Greek nickname for Johannes Reuchlin, meaning "Little Smoke."</note>, there is no reason for me to doubt, for you follow the teachings of the **Nazarene** <note>Jesus Christ</note>, of whose doctrine these two stood out as most zealous heralds, like two lights of the world. You have my opinion concerning bodily things—or those things adhering to the body—which can in some way fall under the senses. This opinion is that human knowledge cannot be quickly acquired, sit firmly, or remain faithfully, both because of the speed of the moment in which they persist (which the swift torrent of nature immediately seizes and alters), and because of our inexperience with first principles, the hidden recesses of causes, our "eyes of bats" <note>A common medieval and Renaissance metaphor, derived from Aristotle, suggesting that human intellect is as blinded by the "light" of truth as a bat is by the sun.</note>, and the very difficult discovery of the immediate conditions which they call "passions" <note>In this context, *passiones* refers to the properties or accidental qualities of an object.</note>. 

For, as is the opinion of **Heraclitus**, a thing changes before you can even apply your thought to its circumstances. I see **Empedocles** make a leap into the flames of **Etna**, and I think I know that "Empedocles is jumping into Etna," but while this voice is still held between my teeth, there is no **Empedocles**; it will be told as a fable about one who no longer exists, and more credit is given to the one speaking than is demonstrated to the one listening. Even if you wished to pronounce what you desire to know in a single moment—like **Lycophron**, the son of **Periander** and **Melissa**, who, lest he should say in a long-winded way that "the man is white," took away the verb of substance <note>The verb "to be" (is).</note> and called him "white-man" in a momentary utterance—surely not even in this way have you escaped the flux of the world and the rapid torrent of nature. 

For in every point of time we cast away the present, and like a swiftly rolling sphere, whatever is enclosed in this massive world never remains in one state. Indeed, the "adornment" <note>original: "ornatus." Baruchias is punning on the Greek word *Kosmos*, which means both "the world" and "ornament" or "order."</note> from which the world takes its name in Greek is itself nothing but variety. Therefore, since it is always in motion, a thing is always otherwise, and one property drives out another. Thus, neither can the ray of our knowledge be reflected steadily because of the whirlwind, nor can the light of our mind be directed when it is so often intercepted by the force of alteration. 

For this reason, we shall rightly approve the sentiment of **Ecclesiastes** when he says: *I understood that of all the works of God, man can find no explanation for those things that happen under the sun. And the more he labors to seek it out, the less he finds; even if the wise man says he knows it, he will not be able to find it.* <note>A paraphrase of Ecclesiastes 8:17.</note> This is what you said **Socrates** believed—although he did not wish for there to be *no* knowledge of things (he who disputes so many matters in the works of **Plato**), but rather no *natural* knowledge, while allowing for a great deal of *divine* knowledge. 

He held that there are certainly very famous arts in human life by which mortals are kept busy so they do not grow sluggish, which at the same time contribute something to public and private affairs: smithing, copper-working, domestic management, civil service, military arts, farming, and whatever similarly depends on the prudent will of man. For indeed, those things which are done by our own arrangement will be valid in their established place; in these matters, the transgressor who does not obey the rules we have devised will be in error—rules which we perhaps commanded because we were compelled by many experiments, or sometimes by our own will alone. **Socrates** thought all these things were "seen" <note>matters of opinion or appearance</note> rather than "known." However, he judged that whatever things were necessary should be sought from the divinity alone. And so, although it is commonly handed down that he said he knew nothing, it seems to me that he knew all things—not by nature, but by divine gift. Wherefore he was judged the wisest by **Apollo** (who is not a man but a god, as you, **Sidonius**, poetically persuade us), and he deserved it.

The histories of the ancients would be available to us, unless you are already tired, and the... of our ancestors...

<summary>Baruchias argues that certain knowledge of the physical world is impossible due to the constant flux of nature (Heraclitus) and human intellectual limitations, concluding that true wisdom is a divine gift rather than a human science.</summary>
<keywords>epistemology, flux, Heraclitus, Empedocles, Lycophron, Ecclesiastes, Socrates, divine wisdom vs. human opinion, Johannes Reuchlin</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the speech of Baruchias, who is arguing that true knowledge and skill are divine gifts rather than the result of human effort alone. He transitions from discussing the instability of the physical world to providing historical and mythological examples of divine inspiration, moving from Greek "pagan" examples to the Hebrew tradition.</meta>

### ON THE MIRACLE-WORKING WORD

...the annals of our ancestors, written concerning the most memorable deeds and matters most worthy of reading, would support this same opinion in a marvelous way. These annals (if I remember correctly) inform us about a certain **Hesiod** <note>An early Greek poet, often grouped with Homer</note>. When he was a shepherd of flocks in **Boeotia** <note>A region in central Greece</note>, and had driven his herd near a certain mountain, **Helicon**, at sunrise, he heard the harmony of the **Muses**—with a sweet melody, as was fitting for the Muses. Drawing closer, when he beheld those divine faces and the most beautiful dance of the goddesses, he began to imitate the song to which they were dancing in rhythm. Then the benevolent Muses, not scorning the simplicity of a rustic man, offered the **Ascraean** <note>Meaning "from Ascra," Hesiod’s birthplace</note> shepherd some small branches of laurel and several reeds. Once he accepted these, he suddenly acquired the poetic art—<note>original: "non natura sed deo"</note> not by nature, but by God. 

It was no different with that **Demodocus** in **Homer**, and among the Cretans, **Minos**, whom they say learned everything from **Jove** <note>Jupiter/Zeus</note>, since he had frequent conversation with the god on **Mount Ida** and indeed in the very cave of that mountain. The Athenians are accustomed to say that **Melisagora of Eleusis** was taught by nymphs, and **Epimenides the Cretan** by a certain very long, divinely-inspired sleep. There was also in **Proconnesus** a philosopher of especially marvelous knowledge named **Aristeas**, of whom there was a rumor that his soul would occasionally leave his body and, in turn, return to the body after seeing distant spaces. Although he knew innumerable things, he was believed to have had no teacher other than the divinity. 

So too our **Socrates**, a man uniquely perfect and (as we mentioned before) exceedingly wise by the testimony of **Apollo**, although he shrank from the study, care, and anxiety of natural things, yet nothing in nature had escaped him. The **Stoics** <note>A school of Hellenistic philosophy</note> affirm that this happened to him not through human investigation, but by an assisting god, whom he himself called his *daemonium* <note>A "divine sign" or inner voice that Socrates claimed warned him against mistakes</note>—whether it was a voice that he heard, or a vision that he saw, or something else by which he sensed the divine in a human way. 

But truly, let us proceed from your stories to ours, which in my judgment are more faithful. Antiquity itself, whose authority was always held in the highest honor, has handed down to memory through both fame and letters that the **Hebrew people** and my ancestors, with venerable worship, followed a certain God whose name they generally concealed (lest it become cheapened by common use). They followed Him on a long journey for more than forty years through deserted places, through rough and thorny paths, through mountains, valleys, and waters; He accompanied them for all that time with fatherly affection and looked upon them with merciful eyes, as long as they loved and observed Him holily. 

Therefore, that God—by whatever religious name He may finally wish to be called—descended in a cloud to one man out of so great a multitude and such a vast crowd, one man, I say, namely **Moses**, in whom a frequent devotion of mind was pleasing. This was so that He might join seventy men to him, who, with diverse wisdom, might act as magistrates of the republic and explain by various arts what needed to be done according to the state of the cases. But because this could not be done by natural industry—for the reason we contended above, that the human condition is not capable of all aspects of things in this world that are so subject to change—therefore, from the spirit which He had previously poured into Moses, He also distributed to the seventy elders. And when the spirit rested upon them, they prophesied and did not cease thereafter. Sacred history records the same concerning **sedentary craftsmen** <note>Manual laborers or artisans who work sitting down</note>. For God sent His spirit to the most skillful men, **Bezalel** and **Aholiab**, so that they might perform all works wisely, intelligently, and with knowledge.

<summary>Baruchias provides historical and mythological examples of "divine inspiration," arguing that true wisdom in poetry, philosophy, and leadership comes from God rather than human study, citing figures from Hesiod and Socrates to Moses and the biblical craftsmen.</summary>
<keywords>divine inspiration, Hesiod, Muses, Socrates, daemonium, Moses, seventy elders, Bezalel, Aholiab, Holy Spirit, Hebrew history, Greek mythology</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>The dialogue continues between Sidonius, a proponent of classical philosophy, and Baruchias, who represents the Hebrew mystical tradition. In the preceding text, Baruchias argued that true wisdom is a divine gift rather than a human achievement, citing Greek examples like Hesiod and Minos. Here, he moves specifically into the Jewish tradition of Cabala, which prompts a skeptical response from Sidonius based on Epicurean and Lucretian physics.</meta>

# BOOK ONE

<meta>archival pencil mark 91 in top right margin</meta>

...they found everything new that was necessary for the holy sanctuary. I am carried back again to your own writers. For what is read concerning **Tynnichus of Chalcis** <note>A poet mentioned in Plato's dialogue *Ion* as someone who, despite lacking talent, wrote one perfect hymn by divine inspiration</note> that is worthy of memory in Plato's *Ion*? From this, it seems to me that this poem of **Naso** <note>The Roman poet Ovid</note> arose when he says:

> There is a god within us, and we have dealings with heaven; 
> That spirit comes from ethereal seats.
> <note>original: "Est deus in nobis, sunt & commercia cæli, / Sedibus æthereis spiritus ille uenit." from Ovid's *Fasti*</note>

Therefore, Sidonius, and you too, **Capnion** <note>The Greek-style pen name of Johannes Reuchlin, the author</note>, I will grant to talented men caution, circumspection, and foresight in their actions; sagacity and skill in their studies; and teachability, art, intelligence, and opinion in the consideration of all things. But truly, I deny to man a constant, pure, and ineffable knowledge of any perceptible things, unless it has been received continually from one and then from another—not through human discipline, but by divine tradition—which we Hebrews call **Cabala** <note>original: "Cabalam," literally meaning "reception"</note>, that is, "reception." In this, our ancestors, to whom fame added the name of wisdom, exercised themselves with prepared minds: such as **Abraham** (whoever he may have been), **Shimon bar Yochai** <note>A 2nd-century rabbi to whom the authorship of the *Zohar*, the foundational work of Kabbalah, is traditionally attributed</note>, the second Abraham, surnamed **Alaphias** <note>Abraham Abulafia, a 13th-century Spanish mystic known for "prophetic" Kabbalah</note>, that **Ramban** <note>Moses ben Nachman, or Nachmanides, a major medieval Jewish scholar and mystic</note>, **Recanati** <note>Menahem Recanati, an Italian kabbalist who wrote an influential commentary on the Torah</note>, and the rest who were studious for many cycles of years concerning divine religion, institutes, solemnities, rites, shrines, observations, and sacred mysteries.

Then Sidonius said: "Hey there, Baruchias! How many rocks—no, massive boulders—do you roll along in this great river of your speech? Let those ancestors of yours perish, who (as you say) once tried to prohibit you from philosophical matters by an edict. For what would have become of you if it had been permitted for you to penetrate the books of the philosophers? Since I have never heard anything more copious, or even more combative, than this speech of yours; I am at a loss as to what battering ram I might use to break through that wall you have opposed to my empty words. Nor should I fight back, since you have blunted my sword with such heavy blows. I will fall silent, then, if I may just point this out: it would be a serious matter for men to be ignorant of what they see, hear, and touch. Like that **Sosia** in Plautus <note>A character from the comedy *Amphitryon* who is famously gaslit by the god Mercury into doubting his own identity</note>, who moves the laughter of the crowd only because, by touching, seeing, and hearing himself, he doubts his own existence. For who, looking at snow, would ever call it black? Who has not judged by the senses that fire is hot? Therefore, if you listen to **Epicurus** <note>A Greek philosopher who taught that all knowledge originates in sense perception</note>, you will find that the knowledge of truth was first created from the senses. 

But as for your claim that divine things can be known by men, I will not easily grant you that. For in that way, while a thing is grasped by the knower, you would admit that the divine is joined with the human—two things that are as opposite as can be. For Divinity is said to lack both a beginning and an end, while all human things are closed within boundaries. Moreover, between that which is finite and the infinite (as the **Peripatetics** <note>The followers of Aristotle, whose school was at the Lyceum</note> attest), there is no proportion. Therefore our **Lucretius** <note>A Roman poet and Epicurean philosopher whose work *On the Nature of Things* sought to explain the world through physics rather than divine intervention</note> skillfully says:

> Indeed, to think that the mortal can be joined to the eternal, 
> Or that they can exist together and function mutually, 
> Is madness.
> <note>original: "Quippe etenim mortalem æterno iungere & una / Cōstare, putare, & fungi mutua posse / Desipere est." from *De Rerum Natura*</note>

For what must be thought more diverse, or more disjointed and discrepant among themselves, than that which is mortal joined to the immortal and perennial? For my part, even if it were possible, I would not call down such a burden upon the gods as to have them assist us mortals as teachers at every moment; I would hardly dare ask that of a companion, let alone the gods."

But Baruchias said: "I suspect this Latin **Sardanapalus** <note>An Assyrian king legendary for decadence and luxury; Baruchias uses this as a derogatory slur for the materialist Lucretius</note> of yours, whom others call Lucretius, has served both philosophy and the whole choir of divinity poorly; and if you won't take offense..."

<meta>The dialogue continues between Baruchias and Sidonius. In the previous section, Baruchias introduced the concept of Cabala as a divine tradition. Here, he pivots to critique the Roman poet Lucretius and the Epicurean school of philosophy, arguing that even their "atheistic" materialist philosophy accidentally admits to divine influence when it praises wisdom.</meta>

### ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

...so here as in many other places he is at odds with himself. But let us touch upon the matter at hand. When he <note>Referring to the Roman poet Lucretius</note> was describing the sacred rites of the **Great Mother Cybele** <note>An ancient Phrygian goddess whose cult involved ecstatic music and armed dancers</note>, and asserting that armed men follow her chariot for this reason—because they thought the goddess foretold with what protection they should defend their fatherland—he nevertheless added in these words that such things are vain to believe:

> Which things, however well and excellently they are set forth and told, are yet far removed from true reason. For it is necessary that the whole nature of the gods, of itself, enjoy immortal life with the greatest peace, far removed and separated from our affairs. For free from all pain, free from dangers, powerful in its own resources, needing nothing from us, it neither takes favor for good deeds nor is touched by anger. 
> <note>original: "Omnis enim per se diuum natura necesse est..." from Lucretius, *De Rerum Natura*, Book 2</note>

By this, it appears the gods grant us nothing out of their mercy, lest they be affected by the annoyance of business. But afterwards, in the third book <note>Reuchlin cites the third, but the quote is from the beginning of Book 5</note>, he says thus:

> It must be said he was a god, O famous **Memmius**, a god indeed, who first found that rule of life which is now called **Wisdom** <note>original: "Sapiētia"</note>; and who by his art placed life out of such great waves and such great darkness into so calm and so clear a light.

This speech can signify nothing else than that philosophy is the breath of God and divine illumination. He finally confesses this, forced by the truth itself—a truth we have barely wrung from his hands, and which we have expressed without the threat of fig-wood switches <note>original: "ficularū," a metaphor for a mild or useless punishment; he means they extracted the truth without even trying hard</note>. I pass over how he asserts at one moment that the soul is mortal, and at another that it returns to its heavenly origin. And when I proceed to say the soul is mortal, for the sake of the word, you might believe I also mean the mind. In another poem he says:

> Finally, we are all sprung from heavenly seed; that same one is the father to all.

And after a few things he concludes:

> Likewise, that which was from the earth before returns back into the earth; but what was sent from the borders of the ether, the shining temples of heaven receive that back again.

Thus he does not fear to be refuted as thoughtless and shameless, but hoped to deceive us with flowery words, or at least to make us doubtful, and as he himself betrayed, as if:

> to touch [the poem] with the sweet honey of the Muses.

But I pray you, let him sing among the poets; let him not preside over the pulpit of philosophers. For if I thought that you or anyone else were moved by his decrees, his writings, or even by the latest and thus most impure faction of **Epicurus** <note>The Greek founder of Epicureanism, which focused on pleasure and atomism</note>, I would cry out and contend as much as I possibly could that this sect is the foulest nurse of all vices, planted by the fanatical dream of a certain **Leucippus**, and afterwards watered by the authority of **Democritus** <note>Greek philosophers who proposed that the world is made of atoms and void</note>. I would rouse philosophers of every kind—although there is no need for so many to uproot this sacrilegious race, nor for a particularly sharp or brilliant intellect.

Then **CAPNION** <note>Johannes Reuchlin's persona in the dialogue</note> said: "Put away the insults; let us consider the matter itself. Indeed, I seem to see both of you as explorers of the highest things, endowed with supreme talent and a singular abundance and faculty of speech, having contributed many things splendidly and magnificently, and many more wisely to the matter under discussion, offering as it were a noble tasting of your various wares. But when I think to myself what I can bring to your forum, worthy of such great litigators, I truly pity and weary of myself, who would not hesitate to press a too rustic and inelegant speech upon the ears of most learned men—unless this one thing frees me from fault: that I have joined your company deliberately and with effort, so that by comparison with my rudeness and ignorance, the dignity and glory of most lettered men might shine much more brightly, as white usually does among many black..."

# BOOK ONE

...r shine forth more eminently, and that anything appears more distinct by the presence of its opposite. Therefore, "Come now," said **Sidonius**, "I beseech you—and I know this **Baruchias** is with me—spare this air even in an inopportune matter. For what need is there for the renunciation of praise for a work of which we judge you most worthy? We have known one another for a long time. Therefore, proceed, **Capnion**, and explain your opinion to us. For this very convenient place for philosophy contains solitary spots, and we will not be hindered from hearing, nor you from speaking, by any noise." **CAPNION** said, "I will do so easily, since you command it, provided you bear my follies with a patient mind. And if my memory does not fail me, by Hercules, having left the whole number of enemies with their very camps behind, I think the war is being waged between you for two reasons. 

First, because no knowledge of things so high as the heavens or so low as the earth can be acquired by human means, an occasion provided both by human weakness regarding divine matters, and by the temporary mutability and continuous alteration of natural things, which is thought to pertain to the foundation of perfect science. A second reason also stands in the way: that mortals know nothing even by divine gift, lest the gods be affected by intolerable annoyance through the daily cravings of men eager to learn. Furthermore, and most importantly, because joining divine operation with human operation—which would necessarily happen in knowledge of this kind—would be no different than if you strove to connect most diverse things with a harmonious bond.

To explain all this as briefly as possible for our understanding, we must weigh the force of the words with which you have been dueling from dawn almost until this evening. Moreover, the discussion will not be from entirely hidden principles, nor (as I believe) through reasons that are not clear for believing. For what else do the spheres of the eyes, shut in the concave openings of the brow, desire than to see? This is their one and proper consolation, without which surely the fruit of the spectacle would be in vain and their gifts idle. If you wish, remember what a clearly quite elegant poem that Phrygian <note>A character in a lost play</note> brought forth in **Euripides** <note>An ancient Greek playwright</note>.

> Whoever that man is, he said, even if he be a slave, he nevertheless rejoices to see the light.

What about that cartilage, softer than bone and firmer than flesh, hanging and bending in the ears of men? Does it not desire to drink in voices? Whence also they think the ears are named <note>The Latin word for ear, *auris*, was etymologically linked to *haurire*, to drink or draw in</note> as **Virgil** <note>The famous Roman epic poet</note> says when he is quoted:

> And I drank in the voice with these ears.
> <note>original: "Vocemque his auribus hausi"</note>

And to include the other instruments for perceiving things: What else is the fragrant power of the nostrils but to smell? What does the tongue crave more than to taste? Therefore, the food upon which the eye feeds is color. So the sound for the ears, the sweet scents for the nose, flavor for the tongue, and all soft things for the touch become their nourishment. I ask next: if there were no eye, nor ear, nor nose, nor finally any tongue or sense, what benefit do you think color, voice, odor, bitterness, sweetness, roughness, or smoothness would bring us? Would not all these things in the world be in vain, considered empty and as nothing? 

Certainly, put this out of your hands and take up not the "arms" of the senses, but their powers and faculties. For unless a sharp judgment is present in them, all things will become indistinct and confused, nor will you assign to each its own object: the eye would "see" a voice, and the ear would "hear" a color. I beg you therefore, **Sidonius**, along with your **Lucretius** <note>The Roman Epicurean poet mentioned on the previous page</note>:

> Can the ears rebuke the eyes? Or touch the ears? Or furthermore, shall the taste of the mouth refute this touch? Shall the eyes refute the nostrils, or will they also overcome the taste?
> <note>original: "An poterunt oculos aures reprehendere?..." from Lucretius, *On the Nature of Things*</note>

It is not so, in my opinion. For...

<summary>Capnion (Reuchlin) begins his discourse by arguing that knowledge is hindered by human frailty and the perceived distance of the gods, but he uses the physical senses to demonstrate that every faculty has a specific purpose and requires judgment to function correctly.</summary>

# ON THE MIRACULOUS WORD

...contradicts himself both here and in many other places. But let us touch upon the heart of the matter. When he <note>The Roman poet Lucretius (c. 99–55 BC)</note> described the sacred rites of Cybele, the Great Mother, and affirmed that armed men followed her chariot because they thought the goddess was foretelling what protection they should use to defend their country, he nonetheless added these words, believing such things were vain to believe:

> For although these things are conveyed well and exceptionally arranged, they are nonetheless far removed from true reason. For it is necessary that the entire nature of the gods, by itself, enjoys immortal life with the highest peace, far removed and separated from our affairs. For, exempt from all pain, exempt from dangers, powerful in its own resources and needing nothing of ours, it neither gains from good merits nor is touched by anger.

From this it appears that the gods grant us nothing out of their mercy, lest perhaps they be affected by the trouble of business. But later, in his third book, he says:

> It must be said he was a god, O illustrious Memmius <note>Gaius Memmius, the patron to whom Lucretius dedicated his poem "On the Nature of Things"</note>—a god who first discovered that reasoning of life which is now called **Wisdom**, and who by his art placed life out of such great waves and such great darkness into such tranquility and such clear light.

This speech can represent nothing except that philosophy is a breath of God <note>original: "spiraculum dei"</note> and a divine illumination. He finally confesses this, forced by the truth itself—something we have barely wrenched from his hands, which we expressed without the threat of the rack <note>original: "fidicularum," a Roman instrument of torture involving cords</note>. I pass over how he asserts at one moment that the mind is mortal, and at another that it returns to its heavenly origin. And when I proceed to say, for the sake of the word, that the soul is mortal, you might believe I am also saying the mind is so. In another poem he says:

> Finally, we are all sprung from celestial seed; that same one is the father to all.

And after a few lines he concludes:

> Likewise, what came from the earth returns back to the earth, but what was sent from the shores of the ether, that the shining temples of heaven receive again.

So much does he not fear being refuted as thoughtless and shameless; but he hoped to deceive us with decorated words, or at least render us doubtful, and just as he himself betrayed his purpose, to touch us as if with the sweet honey of the Muses. But I beg you, let him sing among the poets; do not let him preside over the pulpit of the philosophers. For if I thought you or anyone else were moved by his decrees, his writings, or even by the Epicurean <note>A school of philosophy that held that the gods are indifferent to human life and the world is made of atoms</note> faction—as late as it is impure—I would cry out and contend as much as I possibly could that this sect is the most filthy nurse of all vices, planted by the fanatical dream of a certain Leucippus, later watered by the authority of Democritus. I would rouse philosophers of every kind, even though to uproot this sacrilegious race there is no need for so many, nor for a particularly sharp or brilliant intellect.

Then **CAPNION** <note>The pseudonym for the author, Johannes Reuchlin</note> said: "Put away the insults; let us consider the matter itself. Indeed, I seem to see myself looking at both of you—explorers of the deepest things, endowed with supreme intellects and a singular abundance and faculty of speaking—having contributed many things splendidly, many magnificently, and most things wisely to the matter at hand, and offering, as it were, a most noble tasting of various wares from both of you. But when I think to myself what I myself can bring to your forum worthy of such great litigants, I truly pity and weary of myself, who do not hesitate to thrust a too rustic and inelegant speech into the ears of most learned men, unless this one thing frees me from fault: that I have joined your company deliberately and with focused effort, so that by the comparison of my rudeness and lack of skill, the dignity and glory of most literate men might shine much more sharply, as white is accustomed to do among many...

**BOOK ONE**

...the more clearly it shines, and anything appears more distinct by the presence of its opposite. "Come then," said Sidonius, "I beseech you—and I know this Baruchia is with me—spare the air <note>A figurative way of saying 'do not waste words' or 'do not remain silent.'</note> even in an inopportune matter. For why is there need for the renunciation of praise of which we think you most worthy? We have known this among ourselves for a long time. Therefore, go on, Capnion, and explain your opinion to us. For this very suitable place for philosophy contains only us in our solitude, and we shall not be prevented from hearing by any noise, nor will you be prevented from speaking."

"I will do so easily because you command it," said **CAPNION**, "as long as you do not bear my follies with a troubled mind. And if memory does not fail me, by Hercules, having left all the numbers of enemies behind with their camps, I think the war between you is waged for two reasons. First, that no knowledge of things so high or so low can be acquired by human means, both because of human weakness regarding divine matters, and because of the temporary mutability and continuous alteration of natural things, which is thought to belong to the foundation of perfection. The second reason also stands in the way: that mortals still know nothing by divine means, lest the gods be affected by intolerable annoyance through the daily appetites of men eager for learning. Furthermore, and most importantly, because joining divine operation with human operation—which would necessarily happen in such a science—would turn out no differently than if you strove to connect the most diverse things with a harmonious bond.

To explain all this as briefly as our capacity allows, the force of the words must be weighed, with which you have been dueling almost from dawn until this evening. Moreover, the treatment will not be from entirely hidden principles, nor (I think) by reasons that are not clear for believing. For what do the circles of the eyes, enclosed in the concave holes of the forehead, desire other than to see? This is their one and proper solace, without which the fruit of the spectacle would be in vain and their gifts idle. If you wish, remember what sort of clearly inelegant song that Phrygian <note>Likely referring to a character from a lost play by Euripides, or a generic reference to a Phrygian slave in Greek drama.</note> in Euripides brought forth. 'Whoever that man is,' he said, 'even if he were a slave, he nonetheless rejoices to see the light.' 

What of that cartilage, softer than bone and firmer than flesh, hanging and bending in the ears of men—does it not desire to drink in voices? From this, the ears <note>Latin: *aures*</note> are thought to be named 'drinkers' of sound, citing Virgil when he says: 'And I drank in the voice with these ears' <note>original: *Vocemque his auribus hausi* from Virgil's Aeneid.</note>. And to embrace the other instruments of perceiving things: what else is the fragrant power of the nostrils than to smell? What does the tongue desire more than to taste? The food, therefore, on which the eye feeds is color. Thus the sound of the ears, the sweet smells of the nose, the flavor of the tongue, and all soft things become the fodder of touch. 

I ask next, if there were no eye, nor ear, nor nose, nor any tongue—no sense at all—what benefit do you think color, voice, odor, the bitter, the sweet, the rough, or the smooth would bring us? Would all these things not be in vain in the world, empty and considered as nothing? Certainly, put this out of your hands and take up not the 'weapons' of the senses, but their powers and faculties. For unless a sharp judgment is present to them, all things will become indistinguishable and confused; you would not match the proper object to the sense, and the eye would 'see' a voice, and the ear would 'hear' a color. I beg you therefore, Sidonius, according to your Lucretius: 'Will the ears be able to rebuke the eyes? Or touch rebuke the ears? Or will the flavor of the mouth further prove this touch wrong? Will the eyes refute the nostrils, or will they also overcome taste?' <note>These lines are a paraphrase of Lucretius, *De Rerum Natura*, Book 4, arguing that each sense has its own unique function and cannot judge the others.</note> I think it is not so. For...

<summary>Capnion (Reuchlin) begins his discourse by arguing that human knowledge is limited by the physical senses and the constant change of the natural world, suggesting that senses require a higher judgment to be meaningful.</summary>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the scholar Capnion (Johann Reuchlin) and his companions, Baruchias the Jew and Sidonius the Epicurean. Capnion is responding to the previous debate about whether human reason can grasp divine or natural truths by explaining the mechanism of human perception.</meta>

->### ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD ###<-

To each, a separate power is divided. In this, Baruchias will agree with you, as will all the **Academics** <note>Followers of Plato's Academy, often associated with skepticism in this period.</note> and **Peripatetics** <note>Followers of Aristotle, who "walked about" while teaching.</note>. But what is the occasion for this division?
Let **Epicurus** <note>The Greek philosopher who taught that the senses are the primary source of knowledge, though often misinterpreted as purely hedonistic.</note> say that the eye, and likewise the other instruments of sense, do not themselves know the nature and condition of things. What then? Hear this little verse: *In the end, it is the reason of the mind that must discern this.* <note>original: "Hoc animi demum ratio discernere debet." A quote from the poet Lucretius, *De Rerum Natura*.</note> The matter is settled. For just as a color feeds the sight, or an empty picture feeds the gaze, so the form of truth reflecting in the mind will undoubtedly be the food of reason, which distinguishes one thing from another, with thought—which they call "estimation"—intervening in the middle. I will review the whole cause as briefly as I can. A sensible object <note>A physical thing that can be perceived by the senses.</note> propels from itself on all sides a most thin and incorporeal **simulacrum** <note>An "image" or "likeness" that travels from the object to the eye.</note>, which is easily apprehended by the sense because of the friendly hospitality of the sensitive power. When it dwells in that place, it soon acquires a more eminent dignity, and casting off the present substance of the thing, it becomes an **idol** <note>In this context, a mental representation or phantom, not a religious statue.</note>, while retaining all the other circumstances. Then, therefore, a certain **imagination** receives the likeness of the absent image. Finally, the **phantasy** itself, through certain appearances, both estimates and judges the conditions of the idol, sometimes truly, sometimes deceitfully. Thus, a horse is accustomed to hate masks by discerning them wrongly, and to flee them as if they were future harms. It is no different for birds, which, suspiciously fearing the false idols <note>scarecrows</note> in gardens and fields, often dread traps and grow cautious of snares. Later, though these wrappings are carefully refined, they are still sprinkled with "brute" <note>Physical or non-rational</note> appendages; a worthier virtue called **reason** sifts and weighs them, running from one property to another. By this clear discourse regarding things, reason affirms that "this is crystal," but "that is ice," and disputes among gems whether "amber" or "glass" is mixed in; therefore it observes that "this is chrysolite," "that is called resin," and "this other is called glass." In actions and affairs, however, it judges one thing to be base and another to be becoming. Therefore, reason is always searching, and with diligent and untiring investigation, it scrutinizes sensible things—once stripped of their matter and the presence of the object—even adding various intentions not received by any of the senses, which nevertheless cannot become known without the senses. It is as when I look upon this Baruchias: a man of medium stature, with a long beard, a somewhat sad brow, an aquiline nose, wearing a cloak, standing nearby, and as the **Satirist** <note>The Roman poet Persius (Satire 3.80-82).</note> says: *With head bent down and eyes fixed on the ground, he chews over murmurs and mad silences within himself, and weighs his words with an outstretched lip.* From this, I divine that he is a Hebrew, for whom the **yellow circle** <note>original: "croceus est circulus." In Reuchlin’s time, Jews were often forced to wear a yellow ring or patch as an identifying badge.</note> is a sign; a philosopher, because he is covered by a cloak; and especially a **Pythagorean** or of some other more divine sect, because **Saturn**, the author of secret contemplation, is said to produce images of such a surface. Furthermore, according to the custom of his nation and profession, he is not very friendly to me, except insofar as letters <note>scholarship/literature</note> now reconcile us; likewise, he rejoices in a roof and shade, and many other signs of this kind that accrue to a man as evidence of either vice or praise. Nor does the scrutiny of reason ever cease, but there is an innate desire in everyone to seek diligently, until the sagacious appetite for knowledge is not frustrated in its wish regarding the longed-for truth. Craftsmen investigate this in every art, and nature itself makes anyone who is deceived blush, because they lacked the judgment of right reason between the true and the false. We also naturally hate deceivers as such, and all those who carry one thing on their tongue and another in their breast. And nothing is so modest—

<meta>catchword: modesty (uerecundia)</meta>

<summary>Capnion explains the process of human cognition, moving from sensory input to the rational "sifting" of images. He uses the physical appearance of Baruchias to demonstrate how reason draws conclusions from observed signs, including the historical "yellow circle" badge worn by Jews.</summary>

<keywords>perception, simulacrum, reason (ratio), phantasy, Baruchias, Epicurus, Persius, Jewish badge, yellow circle, cognition, philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse by Capnion (Johann Reuchlin), who is responding to the Epicurean Sidonius. Following the previous page's discussion on how the mind perceives images of objects, Capnion now argues that the human drive for truth is an innate, natural faculty, distinguishing between the certain "knowledge" of eternal things and the "opinion" we hold regarding the changing physical world.</meta>

# BOOK ONE #

Men consider it more shameful to be mistaken than to be modest, especially those gifted with a good intellect and who excel in strength of soul—whom I call the only true "men" whenever we must discuss the sciences. Even infants, hardly torn from the breast, curiously inquire about whatever they see or feel, so that it may be admitted not without reason that a love and capacity for finding the truth is innate in us. Nor should we allow ourselves to be dragged, or at least led, to such madness that we think the nature of the universe—which, as even **Sardanapalus** <note>The last King of Assyria, often used in philosophy as a witness to the purely material or sensory world.</note> testifies, creates, increases, and nourishes all things—has erred in this one instance. We should not think she has impressed such a greed for truth upon us in vain, when elsewhere she is never seen to have acted wrongly. 

For just as you would admit the eyes were created in vain if they were never permitted to perceive light, so too would we think of holy and skillful Nature. She would not have implanted reason in mortal minds, nor instilled such a vigilant effort of inquiring after the truth, unless it were known to her beforehand that the human mind can apprehend truth. Nature intended for this so noble a faculty, condition, virtue, and power to eventually be brought forth into operation, reduced to effect, propelled into action, and aimed at its appropriate object—which, in turn, desires the mind itself. 

For truth is the food of reason just as illuminated color is for the eye, an excited scent for the nostrils, and a harmonious sound for the ears. Therefore, it clearly appears in this structure of the world which we perceive that some truth triumphs, which so often moves, stirs, entangles, and unfolds our reason; so often it troubles the restless and sleepless mind in marvelous ways—not by chance or external accidents, but by its own proper and original allurements of nature. Since all these things are not done in vain, it follows that we shall at last, at some opportune time, embrace the truth of those things that exist. 

Whoever, therefore, truly comprehends that which is—either by his own nature or aided by a divine support—why should I not consider him to have attained its **knowledge** <term>scientia: in this period, a technical term for certain, demonstrable knowledge of unchanging truths.</term>? For thus we shall call "knowledge" the true apprehension of a thing. This is what I said at the beginning: the force of words over which you fight so much must be weighed. In this cause, it can certainly be easily understood how the name of "knowledge" signifies various things, just as the things we say are "known" are multiple. 

Regarding the things from which many philosophers—but especially the **Platonists** <note>Followers of the philosophy of Plato, who distinguished between the physical world and the world of ideal forms.</note>—claim to gather knowledge, they decreed that there are two natures. One is that which can be seen by the eyes and touched by the hand, changeable at almost every moment. Let pardon be given (as **Madaurensis** <note>Apuleius of Madaura, a Roman philosopher; he often apologized for the difficulty of translating Greek philosophical concepts into Latin.</note> says) to the "novelty of words serving the obscurities of things." Since this physical nature cannot persist as the same and one, it is nevertheless received into the guest-chamber of the mind by a specific power, more correctly according to the way it *is* than the way it *is not*, just as the thing is in truth—that is, changeable. 

This power in us, which is receptive to the changeable thing—which ought henceforth to be equally agreed upon between us—I call **opinionated reason** <term>opinabilem rationem: the faculty of the mind that deals with sensory information and forms "opinions" rather than absolute certainties.</term>. From this it proceeds, **Sidonius**, that you contend there is a "knowledge" of natural and sensible things. And I agree with you, for certainly as the things known change, so too will the knowledge change. But it matters little whether you call this "knowledge" or **opinion** <note>original: "opinionem"</note>, since the matter itself is clear. Do you want me to say it in one word? Of all things dwelling under the roof of heaven, there is nothing that we humanly call true knowledge, but rather opinion. But if we ever hear that knowledge is obtained concerning these things, always nevertheless...

<summary>Capnion argues that the human desire for truth is a natural instinct implanted by Nature, asserting that while we can have "opinion" about the changing physical world, true "knowledge" (scientia) relates to higher, unchanging realities.</summary>
<keywords>Nature, reason, truth, knowledge (scientia), opinion (opinio), Platonists, Sidonius, Sardanapalus, sensory perception</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from the previous page where Capnion (Reuchlin) began to explain how the human mind is naturally inclined toward truth, he now distinguishes between the "opinions" we hold about physical objects and the "divine knowledge" of eternal substances.</meta>

->### ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD ###<-

->5<-

the term itself designates "opinion." They assert, however, that the other nature of substances is incorruptible, immutable, constant, and always the same; this nature is not only perceived by penetrating thought but is even seen by the sharp edge of the mind. These are the things we call "divine." For many call the preceding things "daemonic" <note>From the Greek *daimon*. In this context, it refers to a spiritual power or a genius that bridges the gap between the human and the divine, not an evil spirit.</note>. This is why many have nicknamed Plato "divine" and Aristotle "daemonic," because of the excellence of both in writing about such matters. 

Therefore, regarding these things—since they are super-celestial and nowhere meet any of the senses—who among mortals, my excellent men (for I call upon you, **Sidonius** and **Baruchias**), who, I say, could obtain even the smallest degree of knowledge except by divine gift and by no human power? No one, by Hercules, no one! Therefore, truth is the object, the fodder, the food, and the highest pleasure of the reason for lower things, and of the mind for higher things, according to each one's measure and capacity. 

Yet, in the vast space of the world, no constant, complete, and irrefutable science of substances is held except by divine revelation, insofar as it was defined by **Aristotle**; for divine things are not touched by the senses, and worldly things escape the senses at every moment. On one hand, we lack a sufficient knowledge of causes; on the other, we lack the constancy of permanence. What hinders us most is our fragile industry, our late-arriving prudence, and the swiftness of death. 

Thus it happens that what we often think is wisdom or science is actually error, whether it is supported by the observation of the heavens or relies on a lofty suspicion of natural causes that drag matter along with them. This is what that most noble prophet **Isaiah**, writing to the city of Babylon, reproaches the Chaldean philosophers for: *“Your wisdom and your knowledge, it has deceived you.”* <note>original: "sapientia tua & scientia tua ea ipſa decepit te." Isaiah 47:10.</note> *“Let the astrologers of the heavens save you”*—which perhaps we translate better as “the sky-diviners.” (For the *hobre* <note>original: "hobre." A transliteration of the Hebrew *hobrim* (הֹבְרֵי), meaning those who divide or map the heavens for divination.</note> are the astrologers who contemplate the stars and calculate the months) *“from whom whatever is to come shall come to you. You have grown weary in the multitude of your counsels; let them each err in their own way.”* <note>Isaiah 47:13-15.</note> 

He has thus encompassed both: those who examine the causes of lower things, and those who care for celestial influences. One errs by calculating, the other by gathering counsels, both ignorant of the balanced power of operations and the condition of all circumstances. 

Therefore, whether we are wise regarding eternal things (from which the name **wisdom** comes), or we know perpetual things for which there exists a small amount of matter, or we recognize momentary, fleeting, and falling things which are subject to time and the heavens—right faith is always superior to them. Through this faith, the **active intellect** <term>The "intellectus agens," a concept in medieval philosophy describing the part of the mind that makes the world intelligible to us.</term>, positioned at the summit and peak of the human soul—pure, lucid, and clear—gazes upon the states of all things, both mortal and immortal, as they radiate in divine and super-celestial minds and shine back as if in a certain **mirror of eternity**. 

This faith is far more certain and more effective for both contemplating and acting than any rational ordering, discourse, investigation, opinion, or science—even more than any potential, active, or speculative intellect, insofar as that intellect visits things lower than itself. For if it is permitted to compare lower things to higher: just as the soul, according to the senses, becomes one and the same in act with sensible things, and according to the intellect with intelligible things, so also—and even more so—our mind becomes one in act with the heavenly intelligences through faith, and is joined with God. In Him, all the causes of things shine back much more eminently, much more no-

<meta>catchword: -bly than itself (nobilius quã ipſe)</meta>

<summary>Reuchlin argues that true knowledge of divine and eternal substances cannot be reached by human reason or "daemonic" Aristotelian science alone, but requires divine revelation and "right faith." He uses Isaiah's critique of Babylonian astrologers to show that human calculations of the heavens are prone to error compared to the certainty of a soul joined to God.</summary>

<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Capnion (Reuchlin) is concluding his point that the ultimate causes of things reside more deeply in the Creator than in the physical results we see in the world.</meta>

# BOOK ONE #

...more than they can consist in the effects themselves. For the first cause is more intimate to things than second causes. The first cause contains the second ones, just as a fountain contains the waters of the streams. Indeed, like a vast sea, it draws in all the waves at once, and its grasp is most wide-reaching. This is what Agamemnon proclaims in Homer. For he holds all things; hence he is said to have "the greatest power" <note>original: *megiston kratos*</note>. According to the testimony of Orpheus, the origin of every thing and its most powerful force for subsisting is the beginning, the middle, and the end. Believe me, when I turn these things over in my mind, and when I gather and separate the natures of the universe, I seem to see clearly how man stands in relation to those things closest to him.

For although the heavens share matter with fire, fire shares heat with air, air shares moisture with water, and water shares cold with earth—and conversely, earth shares dryness with fire—so too God shares immortality with the Intelligence. This Intelligence we call by the term "daemon" <note>original: *daemon*, here used in the classical sense of a guardian spirit or divine intellect, rather than a "demon."</note> as if it were a *knowing spirit* <note>original: *daimon*</note>. The daemon shares emotion with man, man shares sensation with the beast, and the beast shares with the plant a certain kind of soul that causes it to grow. 

Yet I find at last that the brute differs from man by reason, and man from the daemon by innate wisdom. But man differs most from man by faith. Just as the ignorant person is distinguished from the wise one through the use of reason, so the wise person differs from the prophet through the use of faith. The prophet, too, differs from the daemon by his mortal condition. Now therefore you see that God placed man in the middle of paradise. Paradise is a garden of delights. All delights are ornaments. Indeed, the world (Latin: *mundus*) took its name from "ornament," since in Greek it is called "the cosmos" <note>The Greek word *kosmos* means both "the universe" and "order/ornament," just as the Latin *mundus* means "world" and "clean/elegant."</note>. In civil law, the "woman's world" (*mundus muliebris*) is the collection of things with which women are adorned. 

God therefore placed man in the center of the universe, sharing a nature with both the lower and the higher beings, so that he might hold faith toward the higher ones and reason toward the lower. Among mortals he is like a god through faith; among the heavenly beings he is like a man through reason. Excelling between both through wisdom, he is only slightly surpassed by divinity, yet he is the victor over mere humanity. This we have learned from King David. For he speaks thus of man to the highest and best God:

> "You have made him," he says, "a little less than the *elohim*" <note>original: *elohim*, a Hebrew name for God or divine beings</note>, that is, than God.

Therefore, you can remember what I think about your previous dispute: namely, that there is a certain eternal truth among eternal things, of which the mind itself becomes the vessel; and among mutable things, there is a mutable truth, which is content with the lodging-place of reason. The knowledge of the former can be called "wisdom," and the latter "right opinion." All things are uncertain unless they are strengthened by divine faith. 

There now remains one obstacle of a certain weak argument—if I may say so with everyone's permission—dreamed up by the popular philosopher Epicurus. I believe Sidonius recites this rather than affirms it, given the singular excellence of gravity and learning with which he is uniquely endowed. This argument makes us doubt: could God be troubled if he bestows benefits on mortals, or would he rather be entreated with constant prayers? Epicurus alone, impatient with labor, so loved idleness that he placed leisure in the "highest good" and thought the influence of divinity was a burden. As if we should believe the sun is exhausted by its light and finally fails because it has shone for so long, or that the heavens are fatigued when they provide gentle influences to the lower things. If this long-lasting duty does not trouble, wear down, or weigh upon physical bodies, what should be thought of the supreme author of all things? If he, along with Epicurus, Lucretius, and those other pleasure-seeking parasites, pursued laziness and inactivity...

c

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the speech of Capnion (Reuchlin's persona), who is arguing for the existence of a single, purposeful Creator against the chaotic atomic theories of Epicurus. He moves from discussing the hierarchy of nature to the necessity of a single divine "Prince."</meta>

->### ON THE MIRACULOUS WORD ###<-

...and if He had loved idleness, He certainly would not have poured out from His own inner being this great and wonderful ornament that we behold, nor would He have constructed this grand machine <note>The "machine of the world" (*machina mundi*) was a common Renaissance term for the structured universe.</note> to rule it for all eternity. For although we have experienced that there are specific managers, deputies, tribunes, and governors for various affairs, we always return at last to this one Prince of the universe. We must believe that without Him, managers cannot manage, tribunes cannot vote, nor can governors suffice—no differently than a ray of light is nothing without the sun, or a secondary cause is nothing without the first. For the order of the universe requires something that is first and one. It is a disgrace for a philosopher not to know this, when even common foot-soldiers are aware of it. 

Indeed, this is how **Ulysses** speaks to the army in **Homer**: *“O Achaeans, we certainly cannot all be leaders. For the rule of many is not good; let there be one leader, one king.”* <note>original: *Haud profecto singuli praeesse possumus o Achiui. Non enim bonus est plurium principatus, unus sit princeps, unus rex.* This is a famous passage from the Iliad, Book 2.</note> Where now, **Epicurus**, are your many atoms governing such a kingdom, and the innumerable architects of a single world? If these tiny little bodies rule by reason, they cannot be "simple" and "indivisible" <note>A play on the Greek word *atomos*, which means "indivisible."</note>, since they would then consist of both reason and a physical body. If they rule without reason, then everything must necessarily be carried out recklessly. Nature itself shrinks from such a thought, as does the very name of the **"world"** <note>original: *mundus*. In Latin, *mundus* means both "the world" and "elegant" or "neat."</note>, in which nothing orderly or "clean" could remain standing if everything were thrown into confusion and every part pulled in a different direction.

Therefore, He who is supreme is One; He who is One is simple. Otherwise, if He were composed of parts, He would be two, not one; for this reason, He never admits into Himself any sorrow, weariness, disturbance, or annoyance. If He did, He would become varied and manifold, and—worse yet—subject to change, which does not befit unity and supreme goodness. 

However, when He is said to be "like Himself"—that is, merciful, beneficent, compassionate, and other terms that describe His goodness—these names suit and adorn Him. In that case, He is the same being; He is both the Merciful One and God at the same time. He is "God" when He is called upon, and "Merciful" when He grants what is prayed for. 

Is anyone more insane than the man who thinks it possible that the eternal, "busy rest" and "restful activity" <note>original: *operosa quies & quieta operatio*. A paradox suggesting God is always active yet always at peace.</note> of the divinity could be bothered by the prayers of any suppliants? His highest dignity is to be asked; His highest tranquility is to give. He even invites men to ask, as the Son of God (who is Himself God) taught us. *“No one,”* He says, *“comes to me unless my Father has drawn him.”* It is a pleasure for God to look upon the faces of men who are gazing at Him and admiring Him; it is not a bother or a source of restlessness. 

But your **Lucretius**, O **Sidonius** (for I will not mention your name in connection with such a sacrilegious sect, out of respect for you), and your **Epicurus** as well, dare to claim something about God that would be impious to even think about good men: that they would be annoyed by the prayers of their friends. For what else moves mortals to pray to the divinity except the hope that they are a friend to God? What sustains faith in these pleas except love alone? **Homer**, who is most full of all philosophy, showed this when he said that **Apollo** himself heard **Chryses** praying because he was very much a friend to him. 

It appears, I believe, from these few points I have gathered (according to the modest ability for speaking that I possess), what should be answered regarding the human connection with the divinity. For the question now is not whether one and the same can be God and man, whether born of God or descended from men. Such is the account that the deserter **Julian** <note>This refers to the Emperor Julian "the Apostate," who abandoned Christianity to return to Neoplatonism and paganism.</note> preserved in his book published *Against the Galileans* <note>Julian's polemic against the Christians.</note>, whose words are nearly these, translated by my own hand: *“For Jupiter, in the realms of pure thought, produced Aesculapius from himself...”*

<summary>Capnion argues that the universe requires a single, simple, and unchanging Governor rather than the chaotic atoms of Epicurus. He asserts that God is not annoyed by human prayer but rather finds pleasure in the friendship and devotion of humanity.</summary>

<keywords>monotheism, Epicurus, Lucretius, atoms, Homer, Ulysses, Julian the Apostate, Aesculapius, nature of God, prayer</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the speech of Capnion (Johann Reuchlin's persona), who is arguing for a structured, divine hierarchy against the random atomism of the Epicureans.
