# BOOK ONE #

...begot him, and through the sun’s prolific power destined him for life on earth. This being, having obtained a passage from the heavens to the earth, appeared in human form in a uniform manner around **Epidaurus** <note>A Greek city famous as the center of the cult of Asclepius, the god of healing.</note>. Thus increased there by his successes, he stretched out his soul-saving right hand over the entire world. He passed over to **Pergamum**, then set out into **Ionia** to **Tarentum**, and later to **Rome**. From there he crossed into **Cos**, and finally to **Aegae** and other places by sea and land, so that he might restore ill-disposed souls and bodies that were not faring well. 

Whether **Julian** <note>Likely Emperor Julian "the Apostate," who championed the old gods and specifically wrote about Asclepius as a divine emanation.</note> himself truly saw these things, we shall not strive to explain in this place; nor shall we settle the controversy regarding the immortal gods—how they lived on earth and were seen by men. For instance, how **Apollo** pursued **Daphne**, how he was stationed in **Thessaly** at the house of **Admetus**, how he was recognized in **Phrygia** before **Laomedon**, or how both the high gods feasted in the hut of the poor **Philemon** and **Baucis**, as **Ovid** wrote:

> Jupiter came here in mortal form, and with him 
> the grandson of Atlas <note>original: *Atlantiades*. This refers to Mercury, the messenger god.</note>, the caduceus-
> bearer, his wings laid aside.

Indeed, almost that entire book of transformations <note>Ovid's *Metamorphoses*.</note> is nothing other than a record of the communion—or, what is more ridiculous, the transmutation—of gods with men, or even with beasts, metals, stones, and other things lacking a soul. This must seem quite marvelous to the **Epicureans**, that the poets sing of the highest **Jupiter** mingling with all things, even the most base. For they say he was transformed at one time into gold, another time into a swan; now he was a bull, now an eagle, now the stone called **Abaddir** <note>A Phoenician or Punic term for a sacred stone; in myth, the stone wrapped in swaddling clothes that Rhea gave to Saturn to swallow in place of Jupiter.</note>. He was always more versatile than even **Proteus** <note>A sea-god capable of changing his shape at will.</note>. 

But as for us, whether we speak of the fellowship of man with God or the communication of God with man, insofar as it contributes to our discussion, we call it **Love**, which **Faith** has fathered and **Hope** nourishes and preserves. God is Love, man is Hope, and the bond of both is Faith. Divinity is joined to the mind as the mind is to the intellect, the intellect to the intention, the intention to the image, the image to sensation, sensation to the senses, and the senses finally to physical things. This happens in such a way that the superior power always flows through each inferior level, according to the capacity of any living being, down to the very last things. 

For it often happens that I direct my eyes toward something, yet I do not remember seeing it because my imagination was bound elsewhere. What it is that I "suspect" I am seeing is provided by the intellect, which, having been previously exercised in the focus of the phantasy, works alongside reason. Yet the intellect is not immune from error unless it is illuminated through the mind. The mind does not offer light to the intellect unless the mind itself is shining with God. Since God has the freest will (for otherwise He would be inferior to man), He rains down more or less light into the mind according to the measure of His will; by this light, the other powers of mortals are watered. 

Therefore, although there is no proportion between man and God—for God is infinite and man is finite—nevertheless (I speak with your permission, **Sidonius** <note>Perhaps referring to Sidonius Apollinaris, or a contemporary philosopher used as a foil in the dialogue.</note>), these two are not "most diverse" as you used to say, and yet the one is not the other. They can, however, be joined in an ineffable union, so that one and the same being may be considered both a human God and a divine man. 

I now deal with you, most learned men, familiarly in the **wrestling-ground** <note>original: *palæstra*. A metaphor for a place of intellectual debate or school of thought.</note> of common philosophy rather than secret faith. For in this way, that **Unity** which has neither beginning nor end, and is therefore called "Infinite" in essence, power, and operation, creates Number even though It is not a number Itself (for a number is a heap of many things). It is joined with number, and Its various interactions in the company of numbers also vary the substance of those same numbers; It governs the entire numerical kingdom like a supreme king. This...

<meta>This page continues the speech of Capnion (the philosopher Johann Reuchlin’s persona), who is refuting Epicurean philosophy. On the previous page, he argued that just as an army requires a single leader, the universe requires a single, active Creator rather than a collection of random atoms.</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD #

...and if he had loved laziness, he certainly would not have poured out from his own depths this so great and admirable order which we see, nor would he have built this machine that he might rule it throughout all time. For although we have experienced that there are individual agents, vicars, tribunes, and governors for various affairs, nevertheless one always recurs to this one Prince of the universe. We must believe that without him, the agents could not act, the tribunes could not vote, and the governors could not suffice. It is no different than light without its source, or a secondary cause without the first. For the order of the universe requires both something primary and something unified—a truth which it is shameful for a philosopher to be ignorant of, since even common soldiers know it. 

For thus **Ulysses** speaks to the army in **Homer** <note>Reference to the *Iliad*, Book 2.</note>: "We **Achaeans** <note>A name for the Greeks.</note> certainly cannot all be in command. Rule by the many is not good; let there be one prince, one king" <note>original: "Haud profecto finguli præesse possumus o Achivi. Non enim bonus est plurium principatus, unus fit princeps, unus rex."</note>. Where now are those many **Epicurean atoms** <note>Epicurus taught that the world was formed by the random collision of indivisible particles (atoms) rather than divine design.</note> governing such a great kingdom, those innumerable architects of a single world? If such tiny little bodies rule by reason, they will then not be simple and indivisible, since they would consist of both reason and matter. If they rule without reason, it is necessary that all things be conducted unwisely. Not only nature but the very name "world" <note>The Latin word *mundus* means both "the world" and "neat" or "ordered." Capnion is making a pun here.</note> recoils from this; in such a state, nothing of beauty or order could stand, while individual things would be thrown into confusion and pull in different directions. 

Therefore, he who is supreme is one; he who is one is simple. For if he were otherwise composed, he would be two and not one. For this reason, he never admits into himself any sorrow, weariness, confusion, or trouble. For he would then become varied and manifold, and changeable for the worse—which does not suit unity and the supreme goodness. But when he is said to be like himself—that is, clement, beneficent, merciful, and other terms that describe his goodness, grace, and beauty—he is then that same self, and thus he will be at once both the Merciful One and God. He is God when invoked, and Merciful when he grants our prayers. 

But is anyone more insane than a madman who thinks it possible that the "laborious rest" and "restful labor" <note>A philosophical paradox suggesting God acts without effort and rests without inactivity.</note> of the eternal divinity could be troubled by any prayers of supplication? His highest dignity is to be petitioned; his highest tranquility is to grant. He even invites men to ask, as the Son of God, who is himself God, taught us. "No one," he said, "comes to me unless my Father has drawn him" <note>original: "Nemo inquit uenit ad me niſi pater meus traxerit eum." quoting John 6:44.</note>. It is a pleasure for God to look upon the faces of men who are gazing at and admiring him; it is not a nuisance or a disturbance. 

But your **Lucretius**, O **Sidonius** <note>Sidonius is the character in this dialogue representing the Epicurean view. Lucretius was the Roman poet who popularized Epicureanism in "On the Nature of Things."</note>—for I do not mention your name out of respect while discussing such a sacrilegious sect—and even your **Epicurus** dare to suggest something about God that one should not even think about good men: that they would find the prayers of their friends to be a burden. For what else moves mortals to prayer toward the divine except the hope that they are a friend to God? What provides the faith for supplications except love alone? **Homer**, who is most full of all philosophy, showed this when he said that **Apollo** himself heard **Chryses** <note>In the *Iliad*, Chryses is a priest whose prayer for help is answered by Apollo because of their close relationship.</note> praying, because he was very much a friend to him. 

It is clear, I think, from these things I have briefly covered—according to the meager ability for speaking that lies within me—what must be answered regarding the human union with divinity itself. Nor is the question now whether one and the same being can be both God and man, whether born of God or descended from men. This is like what that deserter **Julian** <note>The Emperor Julian "the Apostate," who rejected Christianity to return to Paganism.</note> recorded in his published book *Against the Galileans* <note>Julian's polemic against the Christians.</note> regarding **Asclepius** <note>The god of medicine.</note>. These are almost his very words, as translated by me: "For **Jupiter**, among his own intelligible realms, begot **Asclepius** from himself..."

<summary>Capnion argues that the universe requires a single, active, and simple Divine Ruler who takes pleasure in human prayer, refuting the Epicurean notion of indifferent gods and random atoms. He uses examples from Homer and Emperor Julian to bridge the gap between pagan philosophy and divine communion.</summary>

He transitions from discussing the widespread legends of gods appearing on earth to a more philosophical explanation of the ladder of being that connects God to man.</meta>

# BOOK ONE #

...begot, and destined life on earth through the sun's prolific power; here, having obtained a progression from the heavens to the earth, he appeared uniformly in human form around **Epidaurus** <note>A city in ancient Greece famous for its sanctuary of Asclepius, the god of healing.</note>. Thus, increased there by successes, he stretched out his healing right hand over the whole earth. He passed to **Pergamum**, then having set out for **Ionia**, to **Tarentum**, and later to **Rome**. From here he crossed to **Cos**, and finally to **Aegae**, and to other places by sea and land, so that he might restore poorly disposed souls and bodies that were not in good health. 

Whether **Julian** <note>Julian the Apostate (r. 361–363 AD), the Roman Emperor who attempted to revive traditional paganism against the rise of Christianity.</note> himself truly saw these things, we shall not strive to resolve the controversy concerning the immortal gods in this place—how they lived on earth and were seen by men, like **Apollo** pursuing **Daphne**, or how he stayed with **Admetus** in **Thessaly**, or was known before **Laomedon** in **Phrygia**, or how both celestial gods feasted in the poor hut of **Philemon** and **Baucis**, as **Ovid** wrote: 

"To this place came **Jupiter** in mortal form, and with his father came the **grandson of Atlas** <note>original: "Atlantiades." This refers to the god Mercury (Hermes).</note>, the wand-bearer, having set aside his wings" <note>original: "Iuppiter huc specie mortali cūqz parente Venit Atlantiades, positis caducifer alis." From Ovid's *Metamorphoses*, Book 8.</note>. 

Indeed, almost that entire book of transformations <note>Ovid's *Metamorphoses*.</note> is nothing other than the communion—or, what is even more ridiculous, the transmutation—of gods with humans, or with beasts, metals, stones, and other things lacking a soul. It must seem even more astonishing to the **Epicureans** <note>Followers of the philosopher Epicurus, who believed that if gods existed, they were distant and entirely uninvolved in human affairs.</note> that the poets sing of supreme Jupiter mingling with all things, even the most base. For he was transformed now into gold, now into a swan; at one moment he was a bull, then an eagle, then the stone called **Abaddir** <note>In mythology, this was the stone wrapped in swaddling clothes that Rhea gave to Cronus to swallow in place of the infant Jupiter.</note>, and he was always more changeable than **Proteus** <note>A sea-god capable of changing his shape at will.</note> himself. 

We, however—whether it be the fellowship of man with God or the communication of God with man, insofar as it pertains to our discussion—call it **love**, which **faith** brought forth, and **hope** nourishes and preserves. **God is love, man is hope, and the bond of both is faith.** Divinity is joined to the mind as the mind is to the intellect, the intellect to the intention, the intention to the image, the image to sensation, sensation to the senses, and these finally to physical things. In this way, a superior power always flows through each inferior level, according to the capacity of any living being, down to the very last. 

For it often happens that I direct my eyes toward something, yet I do not remember seeing it because my imagination was tied elsewhere. However, what it is that I suspect I am seeing is determined by the **intellect**, trained beforehand in the focus of the **fantasy** <note>The "fantasy" or "phantasia" was considered a specific faculty of the mind that processed sensory images.</note>, together with reason. The intellect, unless it is illuminated by the mind, is not immune from error. The mind does not extend light to the intellect unless God shines upon it; and since He has a most free will (for otherwise He would be inferior to man), He rains more or less light into the mind according to His own will, by which the other powers of mortals are watered. 

Therefore, although there is no proportion of man toward God—for God is infinite and man is finite—nevertheless (I speak with your permission, **Sidonius** <note>The Epicurean character in this dialogue.</note>), these two are not as vastly different as you were saying, and yet one is not the other. They can, however, be joined in an unspeakable union, so that one and the same being may be considered both a human god and a divine man. I am now speaking with you, most learned men, familiarly in this <term>palaestra</term> <note>A Greek wrestling school, used here as a metaphor for a place of philosophical debate and exercise.</note> not of secret faith, but of common philosophy. For thus **Unity**, which has neither beginning nor end, and is therefore called an infinite essence in power and operation, although it is not a **number** (for that is a heap of many things), nevertheless creates number and is joined with number. Its varied interaction within the company of numbers also varies their substance, and it governs the whole kingdom of numbers like a supreme king. This...

<summary>Capnion argues that God and humanity are linked through a hierarchy of mental faculties (mind, intellect, imagination, sensation) and can be joined in a "divine-human" union. He uses Ovid's myths of gods walking the earth as a poetic precursor to this philosophical reality.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the speech of Capnion (Johann Reuchlin), who is arguing for the necessity of a single, divine Governor of the universe. He moves from discussing poetic myths of gods on earth to a philosophical defense of monotheism against Epicurean physics, ultimately preparing to discuss the incarnation of the divine.</meta>

### ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD ###

...and if he had loved laziness, he certainly would not have poured out from his own innermost being this great and so admirable **ornament** <note>original: "ornatum." Reuchlin uses this word in the classical sense of the "cosmos"—the ordered and decorated universe.</note> which we behold, nor would he have constructed this **machine** <note>original: "machinam." A common Renaissance metaphor for the structure of the universe.</note> to rule throughout all ages. For although we have experienced that in any business there are individual managers, deputies, tribunes, and governors, nevertheless one must always return to this one Prince of the universe. Without him, we must believe that managers cannot manage, nor tribunes cast their votes, nor governors suffice. It is no different than light without a source, or a secondary cause without a first cause. For the order of the universe requires something that is both First and One—a fact which it is disgraceful for a philosopher to be ignorant of, since even common soldiers know it. Thus does **Ulysses** in **Homer** address the assembly in the army: *We Achaeans certainly cannot all be in charge. A leadership of many is not good; let there be one prince, one king.* <note>A paraphrase of the Iliad, Book 2, line 204.</note> Where are your many atoms now, **Epicurus**, governing so great a kingdom, and the innumerable architects of a single world? If such tiny little bodies rule by reason, they will therefore not be simple and **indivisible** <note>original: "infecabila," a Latin translation of the Greek "atomos" (uncuttable).</note>, since they would consist of both reason and a body. If they rule without reason, it is necessary that all things be carried out recklessly. Not only nature, but the very name of the **world** <note>original: "mundi." In Latin, *mundus* means both "the world" and "neat" or "elegant."</note> shrinks from such a thought; in a state of chaos, nothing of elegance or order could stand, while individual things would be thrown into confusion and pull in different directions. 

Therefore, he who is Supreme is One; he who is One is **Simple** <note>In philosophy, "simple" means not made of parts, and therefore unchanging and eternal.</note>. For if he were otherwise composed, he would be two, not one; wherefore he never admits into himself any sorrow, weariness, confusion, or trouble. For he would then become varied and manifold, and changeable for the worse, which does not befit Unity and Supreme Goodness. However, when he is called "like unto himself"—that is, clement, beneficent, merciful, and the other terms that explain, suit, and adorn his goodness—then he is that very same being; and thus he will be at once One, Merciful, and God. He is "God" when he is invoked, and "Merciful" when he grants his bounty to our prayers. 

But is anyone more insane than a madman who thinks it possible that the "laborious rest" and "restful labor" of the eternal Divinity could be troubled by any prayers of suppliants? His highest dignity is to be petitioned; his highest tranquility is to bestow. He even invites men to ask, as the **Son of God** (who is also God) taught us. *No one,* he said, *comes to me unless my Father has drawn him.* <note>John 6:44.</note> It is a pleasure for God to look upon the faces of men who are gazing at him and admiring him—not a burden, not a disturbance. But your **Lucretius**, **Sidonius** (for out of respect I no longer name you as part of such a sacrilegious sect), and even your **Epicurus** dare to remark that in God which would be impious to think even of good men: that they would take the prayers of friends as a nuisance. For what else moves mortals to prayer toward the Divinity except the hope that they are a friend to God? What provides faith to our supplications besides love alone? **Homer**, who is full of all philosophy, showed this when he said that **Apollo** himself heard the praying **Chryses**, because he was very much a friend to him. 

It appears, I believe, from these points I have briefly encompassed (according to the meager faculty of speaking that lies within me), what answer must be given regarding that human union with the Divinity. For the question now is not whether God and man can be one and the same, whether born of God or descended from men. Such a story did that deserter **Julian** <note>Julian the Apostate, the Roman Emperor who renounced Christianity.</note> commit to memory in his book published *Against the Galilaeans* <note>original: "contra Galilaeos." Julian's polemic against Christians, whom he called "Galilaeans."</note>, whose words are almost these, as translated by my own hand: *For Jupiter, in his own intelligible realms, produced Aesculapius from himself...* <term>Intelligible realms: In Neoplatonic philosophy, the higher world of pure thought and forms, beyond the physical world.</term>

<summary>Capnion argues that the universe must have a single, simple, and divine Governor, refuting Epicurean atomism as chaotic. He concludes by asserting that God delights in human prayer and begins quoting Julian the Apostate regarding the divine origins of Aesculapius.</summary>
<keywords>Monotheism, Epicurus, Lucretius, atoms, divine simplicity, Homer, Ulysses, Julian the Apostate, Aesculapius, Galilaeans, prayer, First Cause</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the speech of Capnion (Johann Reuchlin), moving from a general defense of a single Ruler of the universe to the specific ways the divine has manifested in history and mythology. He transitions from classical stories of gods appearing in human form to a philosophical exploration of how the Infinite God connects to finite Man through a hierarchy of the soul.</meta>

### BOOK ONE ###

...begat, and destined life on earth through the sun’s prolific power; he, having been granted a path from the heavens to the earth, appeared in human form in the vicinity of **Epidaurus** <note>The primary cult center of Asclepius, the god of healing, who was said to have walked the earth as a man before his deification.</note>. Having increased in success there, he stretched out his life-giving right hand over the whole world. He passed over to **Pergamum**, then set out into **Ionia** to **Taras**, and later to **Rome**. From there he crossed into **Cos**, and finally to **Aegae**, and to other places by sea and land, so that he might restore poorly disposed souls and bodies that were not faring well. 

If **Julian** <note>Julian the Apostate (r. 361–363), the Roman Emperor who attempted to revive paganism and wrote extensively on the manifestations of the gods.</note> truly saw these things himself, we shall not labor in this place to resolve the controversy regarding the immortal gods—specifically, how they lived on earth and were seen by men. For instance, how **Apollo** pursued **Daphne**, how he lived in **Thessaly** in the service of **Admetus**, how he was known in **Phrygia** in the presence of **Laomedon**, or how both the high gods feasted in the cottage of the poor **Philemon** and **Baucis**, as **Ovid** wrote: *Jupiter came here in mortal form, and with him his parent, the grandson of **Atlas** <note>original: "Atlantiades." This refers to Mercury, the son of Maia, who was the daughter of Atlas.</note>, his wings and caduceus-bearing staff set aside.* 

Indeed, almost that entire book of *Transformations* <note>Ovid's *Metamorphoses*.</note> is nothing other than the communion, or—what is even more ridiculous—the transmutation of gods with men, or even with beasts, metals, stones, and other things lacking a soul. This must seem quite miraculous to the **Epicureans**, that the poets sing of the supreme **Jupiter** mingling with all things, even the most base. For they say he was transformed now into gold, now into a swan; at one moment he was a bull, at another an eagle, and at another the stone called **Abaddir** <note>A sacred stone, often identified with the one Saturn swallowed in place of the infant Jupiter.</note>; he was always more changeable than **Proteus** himself. 

But as for us—whether we speak of the fellowship of man with God, or the communication of God with man—insofar as it contributes to our mutual discussion, we call it **Love**, which faith has fathered, and hope nourishes and preserves. **God is love, man is hope, and the bond of both is faith.** Divinity is joined to the <term>Mind</term> <note>original: "mens." In Renaissance philosophy, the highest, most divine faculty of the soul.</note> just as the mind is to the <term>Intellect</term> <note>original: "intellectus." The faculty of rational understanding.</note>, the intellect to the intention, the intention to the image, the image to sensation, sensation to the senses, and finally the senses to physical things. This happens always in such a way that the superior virtue flows down through each individual inferior level, according to the capacity of any living being, reaching even to the lowest things. 

For it often happens that I direct my eyes toward something, yet I do not remember seeing it because a deeper imagination has bound me. What it is that I "suspect" I am seeing is provided by the intellect, which, being already practiced in the intention of the <term>Phantasy</term> <note>The faculty that processes sensory images.</note>, works alongside reason. Yet the intellect is not immune from error unless it is illuminated through the Mind. And the Mind does not offer light to the intellect unless God is shining—who, since He possesses a most free will (for otherwise He would be inferior to man), rains more or less light into the Mind according to the measure of His own will. By this light, the other powers of mortals are watered. 

Therefore, although there is no proportion between man and God—for God is infinite and man is finite—nevertheless (I speak with your permission, **Sidonius** <note>A character in this dialogue.</note>), these two are not "most diverse" as you used to say, even if one is not the other. They can, however, be joined in an inexpressible union, so that one and the same being may be judged both a **human God** and a **divine Man**. 

I am now dealing with you, most learned men, familiarly in the wrestling-ground <note>original: "palæstra." A metaphor for a place of intellectual debate.</note> not of secret faith, but of common philosophy. For in this way **Unity**, which has neither beginning nor end (and is therefore called infinite in essence, power, and operation), even though it is not a "number" (for number is a heap of many), nevertheless creates number and is joined with number. Its various interactions within the company of numbers also vary the substance of those numbers, and it governs the entire numerical kingdom like a supreme king. This...

He argues that despite the gap between the infinite and finite, a "divine man" is possible through a mystical union similar to how Unity governs all numbers.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the characters Capnion (Reuchlin), Baruchias (the Jewish scholar), and Sidonius (the epicurean/philosopher). Capnion is currently explaining the Pythagorean and Neoplatonic descent of the Divine into the material world through numbers and geometry, leading toward the power of the "Word."</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD #

...humbles, and exalts this one. And as **Hesiod** <note>Hesiod (c. 700 BCE), an early Greek poet; the quote refers to the power of Zeus to easily change human fortunes.</note> says, "This same one easily lifts up, and easily brings down the lofty." For it drips from the internal immanence since it is infinity, the measure and the limit of all things, appearing like the **first triangle** <note>In Pythagorean thought, the triangle is the first plane figure and represents the beginning of the "procreation" of forms.</note> that brings about the totals of all things, seeing that it is mixed into the births of everything. Moreover, through a second exit, it reaches an **odd prime**, <note>original: "impar primū incompositū." In mathematical philosophy, odd numbers were often associated with the divine or masculine principle.</note> more in harmony with its own nature, of an indivisible sphere; yet this sphere presents a certain face of a composite nature, being composed from the initial seeds of things, both even and odd. Then, in a third stage, it casts rays over a solid body, penetrating sensible things through the circular number of **otherness**, <note>original: "alteritatis." A Platonic term referring to the principle of difference or the material world.</note> and incessantly driving toward the most perfect foundation of the **denary pyramid** <term>Tetractys (the Pythagorean representation of the number ten as a triangle of dots, symbolizing the completion of the universe)</term>. Finally, the divine unity inhabits the admirable middle of every number, which it attempts to lead back to itself by a certain circle of indivisible oddness through a **miraculous reflection**; for which reason it has marked that very thing, along with the superior "second exit," with a common character. For the architecture of the universe arranged this single difference of order between both, with the body of the world intervening in the middle, so that one might see that unity does not shrink from communication with individual things. From this, we may observe that God freely associates with humans. The most eminent argument for this conversation can be that we ourselves are the performers of marvelous works beyond human strength. At the same time, though established in nature, we rule over nature; and we—mortals—produce portents, wonders, and miracles, the signs of divinity, with **one word**—which I dared to explain to you long ago.

Then **Sidonius** said: "Go on then, O **Capnion**! For I desire to know where you are heading. As God may love me, I have never heard anything more pleasant: namely, that it is situated and hidden within us to accomplish whatever our soul pleases—so many things, so marvelous, so difficult, so sudden, so bold—by **words alone**. We consider these words to be the easiest and thinnest to make among all human inventions. Yet we read of **Medea**: *Three times she spoke the words that bring peaceful sleep, which stay the troubled sea and the rushing rivers.* <note>Quoted from Ovid's *Metamorphoses*, describing the sorceress Medea's power over nature.</note> For what can songs and charms not do? Regarding these things, the **Sorceress** <note>Medea.</note> seemed to me to boast greatly in the works of **Naso** <note>Ovid (Publius Ovidius Naso).</note> when she said:

-> I stay the shaken seas, and I shake the calm waters with my song; I drive away the clouds and I bring them on; I banish and summon the winds. I break the viper's jaws with words and charms. I move the living rocks and the oaks torn from the earth. I command the forests to move and the mountains to tremble, and the ground to groan, and the spirits to depart from their tombs. You also, O Moon, I draw down. <-

"Wherefore, we must entreat **Baruchias**, and even offer sacrifices to this **Capnion**, so that we may not remain ignorant of what this **word** is by which he asserts such things are done."

"I agree with you," **Baruchias** replied, "and certainly it will be a matter worthy of philosophers to debate among themselves about such secrets. Although our race of people was once quite curious about that part of religion in their secret books. Nevertheless, I will speak as I feel: there is no Hebrew in this age whom that power of words wishes to accompany. But we murmur the whole night in vain, just as the oracles at **Delphi** have ceased, and the **Pythian priestess** was accused by **Demosthenes** of **'Philippizing'** <note>original: "Philippizin." A term meaning the oracle was taking bribes from Philip of Macedon to give prophecies in his favor, suggesting the divine inspiration had been replaced by political corruption.</note>—unless some sacrilegious person is found who, for the sake of profit, promises a portent to those who are as foolish as they are easy to deceive. I shall speak of other nations first, then I will remember my own, and then others. For..."

<summary>Capnion explains how the Divine descends into the world via Pythagorean numerology, granting humans the power to perform miracles through a "wonder-working word." Sidonius responds with fascination, quoting Ovid's Medea, while Baruchias expresses skepticism about whether such power still exists among modern people.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the characters Capnion (the scholar Johannes Reuchlin) and Baruchias (a Jewish scholar). In the previous section, they discussed the mathematical and metaphysical descent of the Divine. Now, the conversation shifts toward a critique of those who exploit spiritual matters for financial gain, leading into a defense of true, non-commercial miracles.</meta>

### BOOK ONE ###

-> To the wealthy, a Phrygian priest will give answers; and for a small coin, the Jews will sell whatever dreams you desire. <note>original: "diuitibus reſponſa dabit Phryx augur, & ære minuto qualia cũqʒ uoles iudęi ſomnia uendunt." A quotation from the Roman satirist Juvenal (Satire 6), mocking the commercialization of prophecy and superstition.</note> <-

The cursed hunger for gold has always been held in such honor by wicked men that they do not hesitate to contaminate both divine and human affairs with their crimes because of avarice and the desire for gain. Concerning certain priests of your time, Capnion, who boast that they are the purest and, like grandsons of God, most full of divinity, I would not dare to object to anything, lest you think I am attacking our friendship—which more polished letters <note>A humanist reference to "bonae literae" or classical studies.</note> have brought us—by a singular neglect of that sect. For an excellent adage of Terence exists, which must be embraced by a mindful heart, saying: **Truth breeds hatred**. <note>original: "Veritas odiũ parit." From Terence’s play 'Andria'.</note> If I did not fear it, I should bring this into the open: in what manner they profane all sacred things for a price, from the highest to the lowest, so that everything is now for sale at a price. While they are forbidden by the letter of the law to demand payment before a judge, they have sought a safe refuge by imploring the office of the judge so that the ritual of handing over coins does not perish—as if it were not a requested payment, since it is requested by custom—though that which is plotted against God should not be called a custom. But I am silent about what portents, what miracles they preach to the people, so that the offerings might be burdened with gifts and their whole world overflow with coins. Just as Accius <note>Lucius Accius (170–86 BCE), a Roman tragic poet.</note> says:

-> They enrich the ears of others with words, so that they may enrich their own homes with gold. <-

In no different way, this one brings magic chants, and that one brings Thessalian love-potions, and the Jews sell dreams. If greed were to cease, I believe there would be no one hereafter who would promise to perform miracles with words or figures, unless some insane witch, thinking herself powerful in divine matters, lives in the country, who wishes to be believed by every fool to be quite skilled at bringing down the heavens, suspending the earth, hardening springs, dissolving mountains, raising the spirits of the dead, debasing the gods, extinguishing the stars, and illuminating the underworld. This old-womanish madness is sometimes caught wandering in the very scourges of punishment, and is punished with the ultimate penalty of life <note>i.e., the death penalty.</note> not only by Roman laws, but also by the more ancient laws of our people (that is, the laws of the Hebrews). At this, touched as if by a certain irritation, **CAPNION** nevertheless fell into the conversation quite modestly and said: "My most dear Baruchias, where is your speech leading you? Do you dare to persuade us that, unless profit is present, stupendous and wonderful things are not often done by men? Things not born of time but suddenly arisen, not propagated but present and pressing in a brief moment, whose occasion no one grasped before, no one hoped for, and which for that reason are called miracles—whether you do it by command or perhaps by prayer? For so far from being easily done for the sake of riches, that venerable liberality of divine gifts is denied even to those devoted to greed. For although the gods are named the 'givers of riches' in Hesiod, <note>Hesiod, 'Works and Days'.</note> yet the heavenly ones do not wish to be asked to attain them, and the infernal ones ought not to be—the former to avoid a most wicked fellowship, the latter to avoid the baseness of greed. The sound doctrine of the ancient philosophers exists on this matter, by the testimony of Theognis, who says thus:"

-> I do not love riches and I do not seek them, but may it be for me only to live on modest means and have no evil. <note>Theognis of Megara (6th century BCE), a Greek poet known for his moral maxims.</note> <-

"Your Solomon likewise, endowed with no less wisdom and antiquity, expressed the same sentiment with these words, speaking with divinity. I shall say it in Latin as far as I am able, for it would be your part to translate it more justly."

-> Give me neither poverty nor riches; let me take my designated bread. <note>A quotation from the Book of Proverbs 30:8.</note> <-

"Which they commonly say thus,"

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from the previous page, where Baruchias critiqued the commercialization of spirituality. Capnion (Reuchlin’s persona) now responds, shifting the focus to the historical transmission of divine wisdom from the Hebrews to the Greeks.</meta>

### ON THE MIRIFIC WORD ###

> "Give me neither poverty nor riches; grant me only what is necessary for my sustenance." <note>original: "Mendicitatẽ & diuitias ne dederis mihi, tribue tantũ uictui meo necessaria." A quotation from Proverbs 30:8.</note>

But as for those **mirific words** <note>The "mirific" or "wonder-working" words are the central theme of Reuchlin's work, referring to the hidden power of sacred language, particularly the Hebrew name of God.</note> which you say have ceased to serve your Jewish rituals, believe me, there must be a significant reason why they should not be withheld from me. In former times, that art was practiced among your people to such a degree that our ancestors, led by a desire for that philosophy, were drawn away from the sweet company of friends and fellow citizens and from their ancestral hearths. This was done not just through long travel, but through what was almost a perpetual exile to that region. However, I hear that some are unwilling to agree that Thales, Pythagoras, Plato, and the others actually reached the Jews; they claim they only reached the Egyptians and the priests of Memphis. Among their number, Firmianus Lactantius <note>A 4th-century advisor to Constantine and a Christian author known as the "Christian Cicero."</note> revealed himself to be of this opinion in the fourth volume of his book titled *The Divine Institutes*, perhaps following no one but himself. 

But I am easily compelled toward the opposite opinion by the traces of Mosaic reading that I find in their letters and documents. I would say that they bypassed neither Syria, nor Judea, nor even the school of Chaldea. For in its own time, the sacred scripture of the Hebrews began to be held in such honor and in such frequent veneration among the Babylonians that they took care to translate it from its own tongue into the Chaldean language. This occurred nearly in the fifty-sixth Olympiad <note>Approximately 556–553 BCE.</note>, which was the age of Thales. Therefore, since these men were inflamed with such a great desire for tracking down the truth that they sought exile and left their homes so that the most ancient monuments of sacred things might not remain hidden from them, who would be so out of his mind as to doubt that those same men, while seeking Egypt, did not also visit Judea? They likely did so especially so that, having tasted the streams, they might also drink from the fountainhead, and so that they might also look upon the Nabataeans <note>An ancient Arab people whose kingdom was centered at Petra.</note> and be imbued with a purer sense of divinity. 

In the transmission of this divinity, the Jews were more noble than all the nations of the world, by the testimony of the oracle itself. In that age, the oracle was not unknown due to its daily fame, but was sung throughout the crossroads and villages. I shall try now if I can pluck even two verses from it that harmonize with the Greeks. For thus says the oracle:

> "The Chaldeans alone are wise, and the Hebrews too,
> Who care for the self-begotten King and God." <note>original: "Chaldæi sapiunt soli, sapiunt & Hebręi, / Qui seipso genitũ curant regemqz deumqz." A famous Hellenistic oracle often cited by early Christian writers to prove that even pagans recognized the wisdom of the Jews.</note>

There is further evidence that can strengthen my opinion: the small distance between the Egyptians and the Jews. For this reason, you will often find mixed populations in the same place—Egyptians, Arabs, Hebrews, and Phoenicians—especially in Herod's Sebasta (which is also called Samaria), in Philadelphia, in Jericho, and likewise in Galilee. I am speaking of a matter that is hardly obscure to this Sidonius here, who is most skilled and a dear friend to us both, because he was born not far from there. 

**SIDONIUS** replied: "You speak the truth indeed. For one sees a single contiguous border between Judea, Egypt, Arabia, and the Babylonians. Therefore, it does not seem probable to me that those philosophers failed to reach Palestine and the wisdom of the Hebrews, especially since I have discovered that they transferred into their own books not only the ideas themselves but often almost the very words of that nation." 

"But proceed, Capnion," he added. 

"And you judge correctly indeed," I [Capnion] replied. "For regarding Plato it is certain—whom I myself am accustomed to read occasionally for the sake of breathing <note>A metaphor for mental relaxation or spiritual refreshment.</note> after a thousand business matters and the turmoils of the court—and O good God, how many things I find he has fished out from there, for the most part word for word! If you wish to know these things more extensively, read the philosopher Justin—and I believe you have read him—who was a martyr for my religion and a man of no small integrity; perhaps for that reason... su" <note>The text cuts off here as it reaches the end of the page. "Justin" refers to Justin Martyr (2nd century), who argued that Greek philosophers stole their best ideas from Moses.</note>

<summary>Capnion argues that Greek philosophers like Plato and Pythagoras derived their wisdom from Hebrew sources, citing geographical proximity and historical oracles to support the idea of "prisca theologia" (ancient theology).</summary>
<keywords>Plato, Pythagoras, Thales, Hebrew wisdom, Chaldean language, Lactantius, Justin Martyr, geography of Judea, Sebasta, wonder-working words</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Capnion (Johannes Reuchlin's persona), Baruchias the Jew, and Sidonius the Epicurean seeker. Capnion is asserting that the power of sacred words has shifted from the old Jewish traditions to a new, enlightened understanding.</meta>

### BOOK ONE ###

...unless you are compelled to agree with me by the facts themselves, rather than by my mere assertion. I return to the case. This salutary power of words which has deserted you, has chosen Us, accompanies us, and is seen to obey our every nod. This power of perfected religion—offered and given not by human counsel but by divine gift—has changed its location, not by chance, but through its own diligence and industry. Because you have changed the legitimate sacred rites, you murmur in vain and invoke God in vain; for you do not worship Him as He Himself wills, but rather, flattering your own inventions, you hate us, the true worshippers of God, with an undying envy. This is the first thing the Divinity rejects, for He desires to dwell in peaceful human minds. 

Nor will I easily accept our Sidonius unless he can be turned toward the sincere way of believing. For regarding yourself <note>Capnion is addressing Baruchias here.</note>, I have little hope, born of that lineage which has always been "stiff-necked," <note>original: "duræ ceruicis." A common biblical idiom (Exodus 32:9) used here to describe the perceived stubbornness of the Jewish people in sticking to their traditions.</note> unless your brilliant mind, filled with great literature, many studies, innumerable doctrines, and a significant love of learning, might achieve something. 

Then they both said, "We beseech you, **Capnion**, do not despair of us, nor desert us. For that alone is the power of words which surpasses the knowledge of all things. Therefore, we submit our hands to your rod, <note>original: "manum tuæ ferulæ ſubdimus." A classical metaphor for becoming a student, referring to the rod or ferule used by schoolmasters.</note> into whatever kind of discipline we may finally be initiated. I know, as does Baruchias here, that in proclaiming sacred things one must walk a far different path than mathematicians and physicists are accustomed to. For those men think it most convenient first to 'butt horns' and contend among themselves, fighting for both sides of an issue. Then, through certain antecedents—whether you call them axioms or principles—which you cannot refute, they soon conclude by the artifice of a certain combination, by which you are compelled to stand firm. 

But on the contrary, in divine matters silence is desired, contention is rejected, and the syllogism <note>A form of logical reasoning where a conclusion is drawn from two premises (e.g., All men are mortal; Socrates is a man; therefore Socrates is mortal).</note> is mocked. For the Divinity has no 'principle' or beginning; nothing precedes Him. Therefore, whatever is to be concluded must be immediately accepted under a condition far firmer than any science. We have already heard from you what that condition is. Therefore, best **Capnion**, renew your discourse and teach us with what words we might perform certain wonders. For I promise on behalf of our companion whatever must be provided. Is it not so, Baruchias?"

Then he replied, "It is so, Sidonius. For I know you both to be good and learned men, both he who asks and he who promises. Therefore, where I might fear sacrilege from certain other foolish men, I do not fear it now; nor do I fear that some injury might be done to the supreme Divinity through a perverse form of worship. For you both know how wicked that would be, and how unworthy of philosophers."

**CAPNION** then said: "I accept your promise. But I shall prescribe laws for you both, which you shall observe without rebellion. Let this be your repentance: Baruchias, withdraw from the Talmudists; <note>original: "Thalmudim." Referring to the students and teachers of the Talmud, the body of Jewish civil and ceremonial law.</note> and you, Sidonius, withdraw from Epicurus and Lucretius. <note>Epicurus and Lucretius were the foundational figures of Epicureanism, a philosophy often associated in the Renaissance with atheism or the denial of divine intervention.</note> Wash yourselves, and be clean. Hold one God as the creator of all, and the other powers as His ministers. Let there be vows and prayers to the First, and hymns to the lower powers. But if by chance a petition is made to the lower powers, let it not be the intention unless it is done within the mode of the administration delegated by the First. Let there be reverence and trembling toward the Angels, who fly from us to God and from Him to us. Toward them, according to their proven nature, let there be a joyful obedience. Let the sacred things, the observations of which you have come to hear from me, be public to the worthy, but secret to the profane. These are the decrees and the prohibitions..."

<summary>Capnion (Reuchlin) sets the ground rules for his two students, demanding they abandon their previous philosophical biases (Talmudic and Epicurean) in favor of a spiritual path that values divine silence and faith over scientific logic.</summary>
<keywords>Sacred Language, Dialectics vs. Divinity, Talmud, Epicureanism, Lucretius, Angels, Syllogism, Religious Reform, Johannes Reuchlin</keywords>

<meta>The dialogue continues as the speaker (likely Capnion, the character representing Reuchlin) establishes the preliminary rules and ethical standards required for his companions to enter the study of "the wonder-working word" and sacred names. The catchword "plicatu" from the previous page concludes here in the word "supplicatu" (having prayed).</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

I judged that these things should first be written down clearly <note>original: "in album," literally "into the white," referring to a public tablet or clear record</note> so that you might revolve them in your mind with careful weighing, whether they are to be accepted or rejected. For on no other condition have I decided to admit you into the secret academy of sacred names as if I were a schoolmaster, unless you are willing first to agree to such laws. Indeed, when you enter into these things I have proposed with a willing heart, I hope you will most easily reach those things you desire.

Then **SIDONIUS** said: I think you have ordained nothing intolerable so far, unless perhaps it should seem hard to this Baruchia <note>Baruchia is the Jewish scholar in this dialogue</note> regarding that law of the Talmudists <note>original: "Thalmudim"</note>, which I was once persuaded—while reading them—were arrogant factions and conspiracies of many in their own madness, not to be followed, especially wherever it pertains to divinity; since they rather twist sacred things into hatred, slaughter, and plundering of foreign nations, rather than converting them to the salvation of the world. I think, however, he will easily let it go if he records in his own mind that they were men similar to us who committed their inventions and meditations to writing for the sake of ostentation, as are the commentaries of certain scholastics, whom anyone certainly deserts without punishment, just as I became a defector from Lucretius and Epicurus. For I never swore an oath to the words of either of them or anyone else. The remaining decrees are of his own and ancestral possession. Therefore, by my advice, nothing of them will be refused. 

But for me, to speak for myself, why should I not undergo what right philosophy once persuaded me as a boy? For for many centuries now, the entire sect of Sardanapalus <note>Sardanapalus was a legendary Assyrian king associated with decadence; here used to describe materialist or hedonist philosophers</note>—not without its own authors Leucippus, Democritus, Epicurus, and Lucretius—has been frequently exploded by good and most distinguished men. Why should I doubt your other laws which I learned before from many men of various kinds, especially from those teaching magic? 

For regarding the bath, when I was in the assembly of the wisest men of India, whom they call Brahmans <term>Brachmanas: Hindu priests and scholars known to the Greeks for their asceticism and deep wisdom</term>, I turned that custom of washing almost into my own habit, being heavily initiated by the long duration of daily use. I was accustomed to wash daily with them in a spring not unlike a certain one in Boeotia called Dirce, having stripped off my clothes, after first anointing my head with amber drops <note>original: "guttis electreis," likely referring to liquid amber or aromatic resins</note> and odors fit for that purpose. Then, after they thought themselves sufficiently clean according to the rite, we had to go out around midday, as many as stood there for the sacred rites in the form of a choir, dressed in white linen and a white miter, wearing rings on our fingers and carrying staffs in our hands. This cleanliness of body and dress, as they said, was believed to help the purity of the soul in no small measure. 

And this was under the priest Vardanes among the Indians, the fourth from Hiarchas. For after Hiarchas came Sauchnes, after him Sefonchoris, then Bocchoris, so named from the former kings of the Egyptians. I also admitted a similar thing when I was staying among the Gymnosophists <term>Gymnosophists: "Naked philosophers," a term used by ancient Greeks to describe Indian ascetics who practiced extreme self-discipline</term> for the sake of learning, and even more so because it was a sacred rule to be washed with cold water three times by day and twice by night before I could enter the inner sanctums of the sacred rites in as many moments. There was likewise a linen garment daily freshly washed. For they consider wool a profane clothing, since it is pulled from cattle as if it were the excrement of beastly filth. We read also in Hesiod that this kind of precept is especially adapted to divine matters. For he says (as you know) in the book of *Works and Days* almost thus:

> No one at dawn would dare to pour wine to Jove with unwashed hands, nor to any of the immortals. They do not wish to hear such a person and they reject his prayers. 

And in another song thus: 

> When a wicked man crosses a river without having washed his hands, the gods are angry at him, bestowing sorrows thereafter. 

From which Telemachus carefully guarded himself. For in his prayer to Pallas <note>Pallas Athena</note>...

<summary>Sidonius discusses the necessity of physical and spiritual purity when entering sacred studies, citing his experiences with Indian Brahmans and the rejection of materialist philosophies. He justifies these rituals by appealing to ancient authorities like Hesiod and the practices of the Gymnosophists.</summary>
<keywords>ritual purity, Brahmans, Gymnosophists, Hesiod, Epicureanism, sacred names, India, Egypt, Sidonius, Baruchia</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue where the speaker (Capnion) is justifying his "laws" for the study of sacred names by citing classical Greek poets and philosophers, reconciling pagan wisdom with the idea of a single supreme deity.</meta>

# BOOK ONE

...about to pray <note>original: "supplicaturus," completing the word from the previous page</note>, he washed his hands in the sea. Do you see how that custom was not just now devised by you, but was recommended many centuries ago? For our Homer—that blind man who sees all things—did not, I believe, ignore this when he described the mother of Achilles, about to become a suppliant to Jove, as washing away the waves of the sea. Although he seems to have explained another mystery even greater than that, it is nevertheless not relevant to this discourse. 

But what a third law you have brought us! The clearest dogmas of the most ancient authors prove it. Hear what Sophocles brings forth in this Iambic distich—translated by me perhaps not so elegantly, yet I think not ineptly—when he says: "In truth, there is only one God, who created this heaven and the spacious earth." Euripides commands that he be named Jove. Through whose head (as Orpheus sang) all things proceeded into the light; he whom Orpheus praises in his first hymn as the father of all things with a single name, calling him "the beginning and the end."

But as for what follows—that the other powers are to be considered ministers—I would believe you learned that from the poems of Homer, in the eighth volume of the *Trojan Rhapsodies* <note>The Iliad</note>. There, in a splendid speech full of majesty, Jupiter addresses the assembly of the gods, proclaiming himself the highest dispenser of all things and these others to be inferiors; nothing is to be attempted except by his nod, and whoever does otherwise shall be cast into Tartarus <note>the deep abyss used as a dungeon of torment in Greek mythology</note>, so that it may appear how much more powerful Jupiter is than both gods and men. This famous poem exists; translate it from Greek into Latin however you prefer. 

Indeed, from this the fourth law is skillfully derived, as if in the manner of the logicians. For if he is the first who dispenses each thing, it is in vain that one flees to the rest, whether by prayer or by vow. As Nausicaa, the daughter of Alcinous, once persuaded her guest: "Jupiter himself dispenses all things to men from above, whether to the upright or the wicked, according to how he favors each matter." Therefore, Achilles by no means sought out Thetis so that she herself might bring him safety against the king by whom he suffered unworthy violence; rather, weeping with outstretched hands, he prayed in this way: "But you, if you are able, embrace your son’s cause, and climbing to the threshold of Olympus, implore Jove." For this lord and father of the universe—as Menander says in *Diphilus*—we shall deem worthy of this highest honor, he who alone is the inventor and possessor of such great goods. For it is innate in our nature that we can reach the clear vein of the spring just as easily as the streams. 

What then? Shall we, contrary to the institutions of our ancestors, avoid the inhabitants of heaven and seek the aid of Jove alone? And should we not rather, by the warning of Theognis, believe: "A man ought to be daring in hard times, and then ask the gods above to snatch him out"? But perhaps we should use their labor just as we use the help of friends—like the conveniences of neighbors, or of kinsmen, or of wise men—insofar as they are able to benefit us with counsel and consolation. By their learning, their warnings, and their always faithful impulse, we can undoubtedly conform our minds in any matter to the divine will. For they are more skilled in divinity, and they know us better than we know ourselves, along with all the circumstances of our affairs. Therefore, they subordinate us and our affairs more certainly and much more aptly to the immutable decrees of the Sovereign God—like certain polished bodies reflecting rays—so that either things will go well for us, or evils may be declined. This is why, with the gods either being benevolent or turned away from us, we either escape this fate or endure that one. Hence, there was an ancient fame, even in the memory of our fathers and ancestors, that such majesty belongs to the gods...

<meta>catchword: cipiq (likely 'percipi' or 'accipiendam')</meta>

<summary>Capnion argues for the supremacy of a single God (Jove/Jupiter) using citations from Homer, Sophocles, and other Greek poets, while explaining that lesser "gods" act as ministers or intermediaries who help align human will with the divine.</summary>
<keywords>monotheism, Greek poetry, Homer, Sophocles, Jupiter, Achilles, intermediaries, divine will, fate</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue where the speaker (Sidonius) is quoting classical precedents to support the "laws" of sacred study proposed by Capnion (Reuchlin). It transitions from the authority of Greek poets to the dangers faced by those who possess higher knowledge, including both pagan philosophers and Hebrew prophets.</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

...which the fates <note>original: "fata"</note> impose upon mortals. It is the sentiment of a common woman, just about to be overcome by sleep, who addresses Ulysses in the nineteenth book <note>of Homer's Odyssey</note> (if I remember correctly) with these words: "The immortals have appointed for mortals who inhabit the fruitful earth so many and such fates." Thus Socrates proclaimed himself to be warned by a god <note>referring to Socrates' "daimonion" or divine voice</note>, and thus Egeria led Numa <note>Numa Pompilius, the second king of Rome, was said to have been instructed by the nymph Egeria</note>. In this way, no one was ever truly a Pythagorean who did not sense the presence of the gods. 

Truly, honor and reverence are owed to those who are good, as they are portions nearest to the "First Good." For honor and praise follow the good by its very nature, as Aristotle's *Ethics* <note>original: "Moralia"</note> testify. Since honor is deferred to superiors, it acts as a kind of reward for virtue. But the gods are greater; they are not merely our companions, but our rulers, because by their nature they flourish more in reason and intellect, and for that reason, they are sometimes called "Intelligences" <note>original: "intelligentię"; a technical term for celestial or angelic beings in Neoplatonism</note>. 

Furthermore, your following laws are proven by the custom of mathematicians through preceding decrees. Sophocles also showed the last one when he said in *Orestes* <note>While the text attributes this to Sophocles, the play "Orestes" is by Euripides</note>: "One must obey those who are superior in all things." And in *Ajax*, he uses these words: "For believe that he who has both vigorous fear and reverence shall soon have safety." Therefore, Capnion, it will be very easy for me to grant my consent to your commands, certainly in that cause which I have known to be accepted for many thousands of years by learned and good men.

Then **Baruchias** spoke: "Sidonius, it is discovered that even the age of the ancestors sometimes went astray. Do not think that everything from former times granted for learning must also be followed, unless reason first examines whether they are true. I would not wish to assert this led by any rashness, as if I desired to resist these promulgated laws or insidiously distrust your other arguments, which you bring forth abundantly with both your excellent elegance and your expertise in various matters. 

For I myself shall also submit to them, forced by no ready reason except the authority of divine revelation alone. For I agree with you on the assertion of the priest Cotta in Cicero’s writings, which he confidently displayed to Balbus: that in matters of religion one must believe our ancestors without any reason being given—those whom Plato thought to be the sons of the gods, who knew their own parents best. And therefore, when they affirm something as if it were a domestic matter, the highest faith must be offered. 

But the vanity of the common people has often mixed certain falsehoods with the truth, which even the philosophers themselves could scarcely escape without danger to their lives. Just as they accused Diagoras of a crime for writing more things about divine matters than seemed right to the common folk, presenting a capital indictment; but he avoided imminent death by swift flight. Anaxagoras was sentenced to the ultimate punishment at Athens, and Socrates was killed by poison. 

Our own wise men, whom we once called 'seers' <note>original: "videntes"; a reference to the Hebrew term *ro'im*</note> and now call 'prophets,' often underwent the indignation of kings, sometimes of the people, and occasionally death itself for the sake of religion and divine things. You remember that Jeroboam raised his hand against the man of God, which was soon turned dry <note>withered</note>. Isaiah was cut into pieces, Jeremiah was overwhelmed by stones in Egypt, Ezekiel was slain in Assyria by order of the leader, Daniel was condemned to the beasts, Amos was killed by a club, Micah was thrown into a precipice, and Zechariah was slaughtered at the altar. For no other reason did they suffer than that they felt more holily than the unlearned commoners. Therefore, you had added quite justly: 'To learned men, and the same being good.' But nothing is more serious to me, nothing more troublesome now..."

<summary>Sidonius and Baruchias discuss the necessity of following the "laws" of divine study, noting that while ancient poets and philosophers recognized a divine hierarchy, speaking higher truths often led to the martyrdom of both Greek thinkers and Hebrew prophets.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue within Reuchlin’s work, where the character Baruch (representing a Jewish perspective) yields to the request of Capnion (Reuchlin) to set aside his traditional studies in favor of exploring the "Wonder-Working Word." The speaker Sidonius then interrupts to warn of the many fraudulent magical texts circulating under ancient names.</meta>

# BOOK ONE

...oppresses [me], nothing from which I shrink more than to be deprived of such great glory, such splendor, and such a good reputation among our most learned men (whom they call **Thalmudim** <term>Thalmudim: A term derived from the Hebrew *Talmud*, referring here to masters of Jewish law and tradition</term> on account of their learning), not just for a time, but forever. But since you wish it so, Capnion, your will be done; I shall reject even the **Thalmudim**, in whom, from my tenderest years <note>original: "a teneris unguiculis" — literally "from tender little nails," a Latin idiom meaning since earliest childhood</note>, as soon as I began to have understanding, the highest pleasure always resided, making the rest of life more pleasant. I also hope that I shall perform a deed not ungrateful to God if, for His sake, I cast aside what was most dear to my soul. For it happens now—and I know not if it ever did before—that I may easily do without all others, provided that I do not miss out on such holy knowledge of wondrous names which you seem to promise us; or, if I remember correctly, so that I may not be accused of wickedly carping at your words. You said you remembered a word (I know not what) by which we mortals, established in nature, might rule over nature, and perform monsters, portents, and miracles which are the signs of divinity.

Come then, Capnion, let us not remain ignorant of this word. For my fellow countrymen hide many words almost under a bushel <note>A biblical reference to Matthew 5:15, meaning to keep something hidden that should be shared for the benefit of all</note>, and keep them in secret, which they consider to be miraculous, and which I myself have read many times, but have never felt their effect. And that perhaps because I either did not observe the proper manner, or I believed fictions for truths. "You have hit the nail on the head," <note>original: "tetigisti acu" — literally, "you have touched it with a needle"</note> said **SIDONIUS**. "For here the snake lies hidden in the grass; this is something that matters not a little. For I once had a similar passion for certain triflers writing about the magical art <term>ars magica: The "magical art," which in the Renaissance could refer to anything from fraudulent sorcery to high "natural magic" or theurgy</term>, men hardly worthy of having paper and pen supplied to them. Moved by the index of books, I thought I would find there certain wonderful crafts, such as the works of Zoroaster, Oromasis, Zamolxis, Epimenides, Orpheus, Pythagoras, Hostanes, Empedocles, or even the Thessalian factions of the younger ones: Carinondas, Damigeron, Apollonius, and John Dardanus. 

But listen while I read what it is. By Pollux <note>original: "Edepol"; an ancient Roman oath</note>, it is nothing other than that line of Horace: 'The mountains are in labor, and a ridiculous mouse will be born.' <note>Horace, *Ars Poetica* 139. A proverb meaning grand promises often lead to trivial results</note>. These rascals hang splendid titles before the doors of their volumes: now claiming this is the Book of Enoch, which antiquity asserted was more divine than all others; now they lie that it is the work of Solomon, easily creeping into unlearned ears with this notable conjecture which one may guess from the books of Josephus. For he says in the eighth book of the *Antiquities* <note>Josephus, *Antiquities of the Jews* 8.2.5</note>, that Solomon discovered an art against demons for the benefit and cure of men, and instituted incantations against diseases, and even a method of exorcism <note>original: "coniurationum"</note>. To this he adds an experiment which he himself saw. 

Nevertheless, from the sacred histories of the Hebrews, it is well known that Solomon in his time used to discourse on trees and herbs, from the cedar of Lebanon even to the hyssop. Likewise on beasts, birds, reptiles, and fish—all of which could display at least a natural magic. But to one looking more closely, the deceptive trickery of these charlatans openly reveals itself, and the volumes themselves profess to have been forged long ago in our own age, if anyone considers the usage of the words, the force of the sentences, and the very type of script. For nothing will prevent it from being clearly seen that all such books have recently gushed forth from the same wallow of a single conspiracy in our own age. Then, even if they have put into their volumes things from the deeds of the ancients that relate properly to the art, yet because of the crudeness of the writing they can preserve nothing unbroken—not the words themselves, nor...

<summary>The speakers discuss the possibility of a "miraculous word" that can command nature, while Sidonius warns that most books on magic attributed to ancient figures like Solomon or Enoch are modern forgeries.</summary>
<keywords>Thalmudim, Capnion, Sidonius, Solomon, Josephus, magic, forgeries, Horace, wonder-working word</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue where the characters discuss the failure of medieval magicians and the necessity of true linguistic knowledge and faith. It concludes Book One with an agreement to meet the following day at Capnion's (Reuchlin's) country home to begin the actual revelation of the "Wonder-Working Word."</meta>

# THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

<meta>catchword: Capnion</meta>

...numbers nor figures. For they are ignorant that the Egyptians use a double character, one for profane matters, the other for sacred ones. Furthermore, they attest that the language of the Chaldeans and the Hebrews is necessary for magical work; we write them with a similar appearance of characters. Therefore, neither Robert, nor Bacon <note>Roger Bacon (c. 1214–1292), a medieval philosopher and scientist often associated with early experimental science and alchemy</note>, nor Abano <note>Pietro d'Abano (c. 1250–1316), an Italian physician and astrologer accused of magic</note>, nor the *Picatrix* <note>A famous medieval grimoire of astral magic originally written in Arabic</note>, nor the council of masters, were able to grasp or teach any of these things accurately as they ought, mostly due to their ignorance of languages; much less could the hands of scribes avoid errors when copying from double-writing examples, nor could students learn, nor practitioners perform their works. I return, therefore, to agree with Baruchias; although I have read plenty of such secrets, I have never perceived their effects anywhere—perhaps because a suitable teacher was also lacking, upon whose labor and talent those things depended. Again, therefore, do not be reluctant to recite. 

And **CAPNION** said: "It is already evening, and the hour approaches when, because of the night, such a great and divine task—plainly a priesthood—cannot be completed; it is a matter most worthy of wise philosophers and the light itself. For we shall not imitate the Brahmans <note>Indian sages known to the Renaissance through classical sources as 'Gymnosophists'</note>, who contemplate by night and for whom black is the most honored color, because of the frequent heat of the sun which blackens almost all their belongings. Furthermore, this place is unsuitable for such secret ceremonies, unequal to such a heavenly gift, and far too profane for sacred rites; therefore, it seems it must be changed. We should withdraw into a chapel—if that is also your opinion—as such most excellent sacraments may persuade us." 

Therefore, he judges it much better to put the whole matter off until tomorrow, when there will be an opportunity to meet in my suburban house, situated almost within a grove. "In the meantime, remember this tonight: that which we have decided during the delay must be felt above all else, most firmly believed, and most openly proclaimed and praised. For right faith alone is the door to miracles; if you neglect it, you cannot penetrate, and you are justly excluded from the secret inner shrines and the assemblies of the oracles." 

Then **SIDONIVS** said: "The decision, the time, and the place please me very much. But you, take care not to doubt our faith. Led by your integrity as well as your singular learning, I believe whatever you command." 

And **BARVCHIAS** said: "I shall believe no differently than your opinion suggests. For if I did not, Sidonius would cite to me the poems of the ancient Linus <note>A legendary figure in Greek myth, often called the inventor of melody and rhythm</note>, in which he teaches that all things are to be believed. For nothing is incredible, all things are easy for God, nothing is impossible. Word for word, the verses of Linus (as you know) suggest these things. But also Pallas in Homer, whom I remember on this occasion, is introduced consoling Achilles with such speech: 'I have come to quiet your wrath, if you will believe.' <note>original: "Veni ego quietā factura tuā iracundiā si credideris" — a reference to the Iliad, Book 1</note> I would more gladly bring forward some of my own examples, namely the story of Daniel, which was written not in Hebrew but in Chaldean <term>Chaldean: In this context, the term refers to Aramaic, the language used in specific chapters of the biblical Book of Daniel</term>. For only because that holy man believed in the divinity did he escape the dangers of the lions unharmed. For thus the truth of the word holds: 'And no hurt was found in him, because he believed in his God.' <note>original: "Et omnis læsio non est inuenta in eo, quia credidit deo suo" — a quote from Daniel 6:23</note> Therefore, I too shall believe whatever you command, together with Sidonius." 

Then **CAPNION**, soon producing certain tablets, showed them and said: "The matter goes well. Each of you take these home, in which the whole of the true faith is briefly described in the manner of a catalog; I decree that you must believe these first, so that we do not meet for our established assembly in vain. Farewell." And then each, departing, replied: "Farewell happily, Capnion."

<summary>The characters conclude that previous magical writers failed due to linguistic ignorance and a lack of faith. Capnion adjourns the meeting to his country home, emphasizing that faith is the essential "door" to performing miracles.</summary>
<keywords>faith as a door, linguistic accuracy, Roger Bacon, Picatrix, Book of Daniel, Linus, Brahmans, sacred vs profane characters</keywords>
