The participants—Capnion (Reuchlin), Sidonius (the Epicurean), and Baruchias (the Jew)—reconvene to discuss the specific linguistic mysteries of the "Wonder-Working Word."</meta>

# BOOK TWO

### BY JOHANN REUCHLIN OF PFORZHEIM, DOCTOR OF LAWS: CAPNION, OR ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

Since the first day was successfully completed, Holy Prelate Dalberg <note>Johann von Dalberg (1455–1503), the Bishop of Worms and a key patron of German humanism</note>, I pray you listen patiently to how the three philosophers, having awakened after their sleep, meet before dawn and spend the entire day discussing the wonder-working word. Baruchias presides over this day as its leader, just as Sidonius did yesterday. For today, you will learn many more things taken from the books of the Chaldeans and the Hebrews. To seize upon the starting principles, Sidonius began thus:

We are here, Capnion <note>Reuchlin’s Greek pseudonym, meaning "smoky," a translation of his German name "Reuch"</note>, and we have almost outrun the dawn itself in our eagerness. For I see this Baruchias here not just walking, but hurrying, so that he might not miss anything of yours. Shall I? I will apply the spurs to him, even though he is already running. "Hey there, Baruchias, into the chapel!" 

But he, with barely a breath, says: "I am here, Sidonius, and with you. Although I am a little late, I am still early, I think, 'Since dawn has not yet moved the damp shadow from the sky' <note>original: *Cum ne humentem aurora polo dimouerit umbram*—a poetic reference to the early hour</note>. This place is certainly worthy of a philosopher, and I marvel at it, Capnion. Contrary to the custom of your people—or rather now *ours*, since you have made us your own—it contains no idols (which you call *signa* <note>original: *signa*, meaning signs, statues, or standards</note> in Latin), nor any images, unless something lies hidden in the inner chambers or these very sanctuaries. And look, there is the cross itself."

Then Sidonius said: "Let it go for now, Baruchias; do you not know what is on the tablets? Beware of causing us trouble by being the author of a longer delay. But come, Capnion, teach us about the wonder-working word—or if it should be some name that is even plural. For we are not pressing the matter as grammarians do, provided it is that by which we, though situated in nature, might be able to work miracles *above* nature, as you said. Whether this happens through foreseeing (as in prophecies) or by effecting things themselves, the natural cause of which is generally unknown and therefore to be wondered at even by the most skilled men. 

For there are certain things so composed by nature that we marvel at them because they are seen rarely or never, such as the Hermaphrodites <note>original: *Hermaphroditi*, also called *Androgyni*</note> of old, or the Phoenix about whom it is sung:

> Egypt gathers 
> at the miracle of such a sight, and the 
> cheering crowd greets the rare bird.

Herodotus <note>Ancient Greek historian known as the "Father of History"</note> says that although he waited a long time, he never saw it except in a painting. Likewise, there are five children in a single birth, and other monstrous things. And there are those wonders we build with the help of nature itself, as is found in Democritus <note>Greek philosopher often associated with early science and hidden properties of nature</note> concerning the liver of a Chameleon burned on the highest rooftops, which stirs up rain and thunder; or even the wooden dove of Archytas of Tarentum <note>A Greek mathematician credited with building the first autonomous flying machine</note>, which he had designed to fly, such as we ourselves have seen and known how to construct. These and similar things we produce either through nature acting upon our senses, or by the assistance of nature. This physical reasoning is easily proven to unlettered men, to mechanics, and to those of narrow conscience. 

I do not strive to learn any of these things from you. For they are so common that they are lectured upon at every crossroads and street corner, and even in the daily halls of the sophists. Nor do I seek those wonderful machines which the astrologers promise, whether in composing images <note>astrological talismans</note>, or carving rings, or predicting events, or dispensing fortune and misfortune, and applying the altogether incomprehensible power of the heavens to the specific natures of lower things. Since those things are so deceptive and so...

<sig>d</sig>

<summary>Book Two opens with the three philosophers meeting at dawn in Capnion’s chapel. Sidonius asks Capnion to move beyond natural wonders and mechanical tricks to reveal the true "Wonder-Working Word" that operates above the laws of nature.</summary>

<meta>This text appears to be the conclusion of the first day's debate in Reuchlin's dialogue. It transitions from a critique of previous magical authorities to the group's agreement to meet the following day at Capnion’s country estate (which is where the previously translated page begins). It establishes the necessity of "faith" and linguistic expertise for understanding divine mysteries.</meta>

-># ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD<-

...numbers nor figures. For they are ignorant that the Egyptians use a double script <note>original: "duplici charactere"</note>, one for common matters and another for sacred ones. Finally, we write in a similar style of character the languages of the Chaldeans <note>The language of the Babylonians, often associated with ancient magic and the Aramaic portions of the Bible</note> and the Hebrews, which they testify are necessary for magical work. Therefore, none of these men—neither Robert <note>Likely Robert Grosseteste (c. 1175–1253), a scientist and philosopher</note>, nor Bacon <note>Roger Bacon (c. 1219–1292), famous for his works on nature and the occult</note>, nor [Peter of] Abano <note>Pietro d'Abano (1257–1316), an astrologer and physician</note>, nor the *Picatrix* <note>An influential medieval handbook of talismanic magic</note>, nor the council of masters—could accurately grasp and teach these things as they should, most especially because of their ignorance of the languages. Furthermore, the hands of the scribes were not free from errors when copying from examples, students were less able to learn, and practitioners were less able to perform the work. I return, therefore, to the same opinion as Baruchias: although I have read abundantly many secrets of this kind, I have never perceived their effect anywhere, perhaps because a suitable master was also absent, upon whose labor and talent those things depended. Therefore, do not be reluctant to recite them again.

And **CAPNION** <note>The Greek pseudonym for Johann Reuchlin, meaning "smoky"</note> said: It is now evening, and the time is pressing when such a great and divine business—clearly a priesthood, and most worthy of wise philosophers and the light itself—cannot be completed because of the night. For we shall not imitate the Brahmins <note>original: "Brachmanas"</note> who prefer to contemplate by night, and for whom black is the most cultivated color, because the frequent heat of the sun turns almost all their things dark. Likewise, this place seems unsuitable for such secret ceremonies, unequal to such a heavenly gift, and far too profane for sacred rites; and therefore, it seems it must be changed. If it is also your opinion, we should withdraw into the chapel, as such most excellent sacraments might persuade us. Therefore, I judge it much better to put the whole matter off until tomorrow, when there will be an opportunity to meet at my suburban house, situated almost in a grove. In the meantime, remember this tonight: what we have decided during this discussion is most of all to be felt, most constantly believed, and most openly proclaimed and praised. For right faith <term>fides</term> <note>Reuchlin emphasizes "faith" here not just as religious piety, but as a focused mental state or "certainty" required for mystical efficacy</note> alone is the door to miracles; if you neglect it, you cannot penetrate into the just inner sanctums of secrets and the gatherings of oracles.

Then **SIDONIUS** said: The decision, the time, and the place please me greatly. But take care that you do not doubt our faith. Led by your integrity as well as your singular learning, I believe whatever you command.

And I, said **BARUCHIAS**, shall believe no differently than your opinion. If I did not, Sidonius would cite to me the songs of the most ancient Linus <note>A mythical figure of Greek song, often paired with Orpheus</note>, in which he teaches that all things must be believed. For nothing is incredible, all things are easy for God, nothing is impossible. The verses of Linus—word for word, as you know—insinuate these things. But also Pallas in Homer, whom I remember on this occasion, is introduced consoling Achilles with such a speech: "I have come to quiet your anger if you will believe." I would more gladly bring forward some of my own examples, namely the history of Daniel, which was written not in Hebrew but in Chaldean [Aramaic]. For by this alone—that this holy man believed in the Divinity—he escaped the dangers of the lions unharmed. For thus the truth of the word holds: "And no wound was found on him, because he believed in his God" <note>original: "Et omnis læsio non est inuenta in eo, quia credidit deo suo"—referencing Daniel 6:23</note>. Therefore, I too will believe, along with Sidonius, whatever you command.

Then **CAPNION**, soon producing certain tablets, displayed them and said: The matter goes well. Each of you take these home, in which the whole of the true faith is briefly described in the manner of a catalog; I decree these are to be believed by you first, so that we do not gather for our appointed meeting in vain. Farewell. "And you, Capnion, farewell happily," each responded as they departed.

->## BOOK TWO<-
-><sig>d</sig><-

# THE WORK OF JOHANN REUCHLIN OF PFORZHEIM, DOCTOR
# OF LAWS; *CAPNION*, OR *ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD*

Since the previous day was successfully completed, Holy Prelate Dalberg <note>Johann von Dalberg (1455–1503), the Bishop of Worms and a patron of humanists</note>, I pray you listen patiently to how the three philosophers, having woken from sleep, meet before dawn and spend the whole day discussing the wonder-working word. Baruchias <note>The Jewish representative in the dialogue, who provides the Kabbalistic perspective</note> presides over this day, just as Sidonius <note>The pagan/Epicurean representative</note> did yesterday. For today you will learn much more taken from the books of the Chaldeans and Hebrews. To grasp the principles of these matters, Sidonius began thus: We are present, Capnion <note>The Greek pseudonym for Johann Reuchlin, meaning "smoky," a pun on the German "Rauch"</note>, and we have almost arrived before the light itself, perhaps too importunely. For I see this man Baruchias not merely walking but hurrying, so that he might not miss any of your words. Do you wish? I shall apply the spurs, even though he is already running. "Hey there, Baruchias, into the chapel!" But he, with gasping breath, said: "I am here with you, Sidonius, though a little later than intended, yet still early, I think, since the dawn has not yet moved the damp shadows from the sky." Surely this place is worthy of a philosopher, and I marvel at Capnion, who—contrary to the custom of your people, or rather now *our* people, since you have made us your own—keeps no idols (which you call *signa* in Latin) nor any images, unless something is hidden in the inner chambers or these very shrines. And look, it is the Cross itself.

Then Sidonius said: "Let it be for now, Baruchias; do you not know what is on the agenda? Beware lest you cause us trouble by being the author of a long delay. But come, Capnion, teach us about the wonder-working word, or even if it should be some plural name. For we do not press this matter like grammarians; we only care that it be that by which we, though situated in nature, might be able to work miracles *above* nature, as you were saying—whether this happens through foreseeing, as in prophecies, or by effecting the things themselves, the natural cause of which is commonly unknown and therefore admired even by the most expert men." For there are certain things so composed by nature that we marvel at them because they are seen rarely or never, such as the Hermaphrodites <note>Intersex individuals, often a subject of wonder in classical and Renaissance texts</note> (formerly called Androgynes), or the Phoenix of which it is sung:
*Egypt witnessed such great wonders,*
*And the cheering crowd greets the rare bird.* <note>original: "Conuenit Aegyptus tantã miracula uisus, Et raram uolucrem turba salutat ouans."</note>
Herodotus says that although he waited a long time, he never saw it except in a painting. Likewise, the birth of five boys at once, and other monstrous things. And there are those portents we manufacture with the help of nature itself, such as what is found in Democritus regarding the liver of a Chameleon burnt on the top of a roof, which stirs up rain and thunder; or the wooden dove of Archytas of Tarentum <note>A 4th-century BCE Greek philosopher and mathematician credited with building the first autonomous flying machine</note> which he had designed to fly, the likes of which we ourselves have both seen and know how to construct. These and similar things we produce either through our natural senses or by assisting nature—a physical method which easily proves itself to unlettered men, mechanics, and those of small conscience. I do not strive to learn any of these from you. For they are so common that they are read in the streets and crossroads, and even in the daily auditoriums of the sophists. Nor do I seek those wonderful machines which the astrologers promise, whether in composing images, carving rings, predicting events, or dispensing good and bad fortune, or in applying the incomprehensible power of the heavens to the natures of inferior things individually; for these are so deceptive and so...

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from the start of Book Two. Sidonius, the Epicurean philosopher, has begun his discourse. In the preceding section, he greeted Capnion and Baruchias at dawn. Now, he launches into a skeptical critique of the "arts" of astrology and magic, setting the stage for Baruchias to later offer a more "sacred" alternative through the Hebrew tradition.</meta>

->### ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD<-

diversities arise among themselves, so that even the authors of that school oppose one another in turn. They involve such seemingly insurmountable diligence and infinite labor that they cannot be classified as a truthful art <note>original: "sub artem non mendacem," literally 'under a non-lying art'</note>. I would rather speak the truth in peace than deceive anyone with the greatest efforts, having declared a laughable condition that teaches lying on purpose. For the clearest proof of truth is harmony; conversely, for a lie, it is discord.

But what will you find that is consistent, firm, and pure in that art they speak of? I am not speaking of the measurement of the stars—in which nothing is more certain, as both mathematicians and experience itself show—but of the judgment derived from them <note>The author distinguishes between *astronomia* (the science of star positions) and *astrologia* (the "judgment" or divination of human affairs)</note>, whose entire path is deceptive and whose opinion is foolish, especially in those things that exceed physical qualities, the elementary dispositions, the moments of the year, and the signs of the seasons, as your Baruchia Moses <note>A reference to Maimonides (Moses ben Maimon), whom the character Baruchias holds in high regard</note> suggests. 

Look at how they differ among themselves! For Abumassar <note>Abu Ma'shar al-Balkhi (787–886), a famous Persian astrologer</note>, whom they adore as the chief and master of this faculty, when he treats judgments, says that some astrologers believe the planets only signify universal matters, such as the elements and the natural species of things. But concerning individual parts and actions—namely, moving, resting, and the like—they demonstrate nothing at all. Others, however, think the stars show only what is necessary—namely, that fire is indeed hot and water cold—but not things that are contingent, such as whether a person speaks or remains silent. Still others think the planets portend only the changes of the seasons, and how these affect bodies. 

The later astrologers follow the ancients, yet no sayings of the ancients contribute to the actual intent of making a judgment, since they say it would be impossible even for wise astronomers to find anything in the science of judgments. Nevertheless, they dare to introduce a discipline by which they strive to persuade us that they know even the most minute points of human thought, and that they can subvert and restore the entire nature of things. 

The warning of Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century author of the *Tetrabiblos*, the foundational text of Western astrology</note> does not frighten them, even though he asserts that such prediction fails us for the most part. Nothing turns them away: neither the slippery reasoning of their principles—which is very much like a vain fiction, and which the ancients, Ptolemy, Haimon, and Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary father of the Hermetic tradition</note> all interpret differently—nor the infinite assembly of secondary causes that happen to a single thing; nor our great distance from the stars and the innumerable media between us. 

Nor does the uncertain mass of them, that incomprehensible multitude of stars, deter them—even to the point that there are stars they have not yet seen, and those that appear for the first time which they admit they have never looked upon. The power of these stars must necessarily be admitted in the substances that are to be born and ruled, since such a tiny star, which some call the Dog and others Sirius, adds so much heat to the ruling planets in the month of August <note>original: "sextili mense," the sixth month of the old Roman calendar, corresponding to the "Dog Days" of summer</note>, though it is scarcely seen.

Nor do I demand much of you to explain to us in what ways it happens that miracles are performed through magical practice. For I myself have spent no small amount of time in that delusion—vainly, to be sure—yet I do not yet repent of it, since I can more easily turn other mortals away from such great destruction with the strongest arguments. For when you have consumed an entire lifetime in such sacrilegious business, you will finally, with difficulty, extract nothing that truly *is*, but only what perhaps *seems* to be, accompanied by the expectation of the highest danger to life and limb. Thus I myself knew Robert the Englishman <note>Robertus Anglicus, a 13th-century scholar and commentator on astronomy often associated with occult interests</note>, who died miserably among the Swiss <note>original: "apud Heluetios"</note>, and another certain man whose name I shall not mention, who was even more miserably captured and punished in his reputation. I have known many others, most flourishing in that art, who ended most wretchedly...

<summary>Sidonius delivers a scathing critique of astrology and magic, arguing that their practitioners disagree on fundamental principles and that the "art" is both deceptive and dangerous. He cites historical figures like Abumassar and Robertus Anglicus to warn against these "sacrilegious" pursuits.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, judicial astrology, magic, Abu Ma'shar, Ptolemy, Hermes Trismegistus, Robertus Anglicus, Maimonides, skepticism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from the start of Book Two. Sidonius, an Epicurean philosopher, is finishing his critique of the overwhelming complexity and danger inherent in traditional magic and astrology, specifically the Neoplatonic and Hermetic traditions. ->### BOOK TWO<-

I have heard from the most faithful men that they had died to this world. Indeed, the powers of darkness are usually accustomed to bestowing such honor upon their clients. But if there are any who think they practice magic not through the bindings or adjurations of evil beings, but by the quiet help of good spirits <note>original: "dæmonum." In the Renaissance, this term often referred to mediating spirits or "daemons" in the Greek sense, rather than purely malevolent demons</note>—perhaps because it seemed so to them—nonetheless, they have everything so entangled that it is necessary first to observe and mix together all the teachings of the astrologers (which we refuted a few pages back) along with the prescribed rites of their own ceremonies to the letter. This is an excessive, not to say impossible, burden for the human mind. Scarcely a man’s whole lifespan is sufficient just for reading the books of that faculty; Mercurius <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Egyptian sage and purported author of the Hermetic corpus</note> alone is said to have written thirty-six thousand five hundred and twenty-five books embracing the universals of the sacred arts.

What do you think of the rest? Now, finally, I grant that through long vigils, great effort, and diligent observation, you have abundantly attained the admirable secrets of hidden magic along with the inventions of the astrologers—secrets by which the spirits of the dead <term>manes|The spirits of the departed or gods of the underworld</term> obey, the stars are disturbed, the deities are compelled, and the elements serve. Even if you wished to perform an operation free of all clumsiness using the unassailable power of that discipline and the blind violence of compelled deities—or, if you prefer, with the timely help of a bright company of gods—you would then be so thunderstruck and stupefied by the great crowd of spirits who precede as scouts and a kind of procession the very deity you wish to use favorably in your ritual, that you would shrink back in horror. You would leave the work unfinished, resulting in loss to yourself and becoming a laughingstock to others.

For it is known from the authors Iamblichus and Porphyry <note>Two influential Neoplatonist philosophers who wrote extensively on the soul and theurgy (god-working)</note> that no one knows how great a multitude of powers is usually stirred up when the gods descend and are moved, except for those priests who are already experienced through the labor itself. Indeed, he who does not pursue each one with his own specific honor, nor distributes to all what is fitting for each, nor behaves according to their condition with thanks, offerings, and very similar gifts, does not achieve the presence of the deities or the desired effects—just as in a harmony, if one string is broken, the whole suddenly sounds discordant. These are the places, these are the foundations of the discipline and the very methods of the art.

Therefore, Capnion <note>The Greek-style name, meaning "Smoky," used by Johann Reuchlin</note>, you have relieved us of a heavy annoyance and an unbearable burden by your promise; you who have come to free our study from so many stars, so many spirits, so many precepts, so many rites, and such vain observations. For, to omit the heavenly beings—how many and how great they are, being innumerable according to the infinite thoughts or "ideas" of the Primary Mind—of whom they say that ancient Orpheus introduced three hundred and fifty gods by name. Let us consider only the earthly deities closest to us, of whom that most noble writer of equal antiquity, Hesiod, numbered thirty thousand in his book *Works and Days*. To offer sacrifices to each of these is beyond man and exceeds our strength. But now the sun is rising; begin, most accomplished Capnion.

Then he said: "If nature had produced me as somewhat more abundant in speaking and not so irritable, certainly, as much as I reflect upon it, no small thanks would be due to you, Sidonius, who have shown such a forest of wonders from which we might be abundantly supplied with fallen wood for the grand and magnificent illustration of the structure of such a divine work. And if I were one from the school of Chrysippus <note>A leader of the Stoic school known for rigorous logic and dialectic</note>, I would greatly rejoice in these riches of most ornate eloquence proposed by you, from which my own speech could be made more copious. But I am not chosen for that..."

<sig>d ii</sig>

<summary>Sidonius concludes his critique of traditional magic, arguing that its complexity and the sheer number of deities (citing Hermes, Orpheus, and Hesiod) make it impossible for humans to master. He then invites Capnion to present a simpler, more divine alternative as the sun rises.</summary>
<keywords>magic, astrology, Hermes Trismegistus, Iamblichus, Porphyry, Orpheus, Hesiod, Chrysippus, theurgy, Neoplatonism, ritual complexity</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from Book Two, shifting the focus from the failures of traditional magic to the prerequisites for divine knowledge. Capnion (Reuchlin’s persona) begins his response to Sidonius, emphasizing that the "Wonder-Working Word" requires a different approach than the complex systems previously discussed.</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

...of elegance <note>original: "elegantiarum." The sentence likely began on the previous page as "I was neither born nor trained in the flowery arts of elegance."</note> I was neither born nor trained, nor does the matter at hand allow for it. On the contrary, that which is divine and sacred rejects an abundance of speech and sentences colored with flowery words, especially in a teaching dedicated to God alone, where every blemish must be avoided. But in the sacred scriptures we are warned that *in much speaking, sin will not be absent* <note>A reference to Proverbs 10:19</note>; not unlike this, even the more refined letters <note>A humanist term for classical literature</note> command us, as Flaccus <note>The Roman poet Horace</note> says: **Whatever you teach, be brief.** This I shall do both intentionally and by necessity, so that I may complete everything shortly.

Indeed, if I had not already begun to share the greatest intimacy with you, in the manner of the Pythagoreans <note>Followers of Pythagoras, known for strict silence and testing students before admitting them to higher mysteries</note>, I would first have scrutinized each of you thoroughly, sensing each one's nature, diet, study, affection, and age. Truly, I would have required patience of the tongue and ears, and silence. For it is not granted to everyone, as Nazianzenus <note>Saint Gregory of Nazianzus, a 4th-century theologian</note> says, nor always, nor concerning all things, to philosophize about God; rather, it is for certain people, at certain times, and to a certain extent. 

But as for you, the "tablets of yesterday" which you keep at home make you prepared and suitable colleagues for this business. Nor do I demand more from you than what Simo in Terence <note>A reference to the play *Andria* by the Roman playwright Terence</note> sought from Sosia: **loyalty and silence.** For you have reached many years above twenty-five, below which limit—as Baruchias here will testify is true—it is not lawful for any of the Hebrews to touch the scriptures of divine secrets. And there shall be among us the same custom, which is not without praise. 

Furthermore, this most sacred function of ours must not be revealed to the profane multitude, but only to priests. Regarding the highest sacred matters, both Plato warned Dionysius of Syracuse, and Dionysius the Areopagite <note>A 1st-century convert, though here referring to the influential mystical writer "Pseudo-Dionysius"</note> warned Theophilus accurately and diligently. Indeed, without controversy, we are priests—even if no hands of bishops have been laid upon us—provided that we hold divine wisdom along with the practice of religion. For the opinion of Lactantius Firmianus <note>A 4th-century Christian author known as the "Christian Cicero"</note> is true, in those volumes he wrote to the Emperor Constantine: **that those who are teachers of true wisdom are the same as those who are priests of God.** 

Therefore, lest we be considered profane, both the treatment of true philosophy and the reverence for the one God forbid it. Truly, humility alone makes us fit listeners for such a great matter, hope makes us attentive, and faith makes us teachable. Above all things, however, is a fervent love, which preserves us in our work and bestows the effect upon our prayers. 

Therefore, since every art of miracles (if any can be truly named so, as our friend Sidonius here has learnedly and industriously reviewed) is divided into three parts—namely into **Physics**, **Astrology**, and **Magic** (which contains both <term>Goetia|Lower magic involving deals with spirits or sorcery</term> and <term>Theurgia|Higher magic aimed at union with the divine</term>)—and since they are all so joined to one another and related that he who promises Astrology without Physics accomplishes nothing, and he who dares to profess Magic without Astrology and Physics wanders far from true reason; you yourselves cast them all aside on account of their vain outcomes and frustrated results after immense and laborious studies. 

And you have asked me again to enter upon a **fourth way** with you, which we may call "soliloquies," where whatever is proposed succeeds according to the favorable prayers of those who are patient. I shall do as much as is permitted and lies within my power; I will deny nothing that you so diligently request. But know this first, which was established long ago even by the Pythagoreans and Plato himself: the perception of divine things cannot be found by us through any art. Nevertheless, it is revealed from above, as Plotinus <note>A major Neoplatonic philosopher</note> gave and Proclus <note>Another key Neoplatonic philosopher</note> confirmed having received, and to which not only your theologians but also ours attest. It will be fitting for us first to offer and commend ourselves, having been purified...

<summary>Capnion explains that divine wisdom requires brevity, silence, and priestly maturity (age 25+) rather than complex arts. He rejects the traditional triad of Physics, Astrology, and Magic in favor of a "fourth way" of internal revelation called Soliloquies.</summary>

<keywords>Pythagoras, brevity, priesthood, Lactantius, Physics, Astrology, Magic, Goetia, Theurgia, Soliloquies, divine revelation, age of maturity</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discourse of Capnion (Reuchlin's spokesperson) as he transitions from the philosophical requirements of the seeker to the actual practice of approaching the divine. He begins with a liturgical hymn before launching into a theological defense of God as the sole source of true miracles, contrasting this with the "art" of magicians.</meta>

## BOOK TWO

...to the deifying light, and then, while that rain of super-celestial water descends upon our heads with our senses closed, we should expectantly wait with a quiet mind—sometimes praising and sometimes adoring that sea of goodness. From this sea, not only the streams of the wondrous Name and sacred words derive, but even the rivers of all miracles and the most divine currents flow down to us. In the beginning, therefore, as we fall prostrate upon the ground, this hymn must be offered in the Ionian mode <note>A musical mode associated in the Renaissance with lightness or, in sacred contexts, a certain gentleness or honesty</note>, and with the accent due to the purpose of stirring the sacred mind.

->**The Hymn**<-

**Creator and craftsman of every thing. King of those above, light of the spirit, hope of humanity. Terror to the shadows of dark Phlegethon <note>A river of fire in the classical underworld; here used to represent hell</note>. Incredible love of the heavenly dwellers. Invincible dread of the denizens of Tartarus <note>The deepest abyss of the underworld</note>. Renowned worship of the earth-born. Lord, Our Lord, Our God <note>original Hebrew: "Adonai Adonenu Elohenu"</note>. King, Almighty, First-born <note>original Greek: "Basileus pantocrator protogenethlos"</note>. One God, the same God, life-giving God. Coming from above, glide into us.**

Once this hymn had been recited according to custom, Capnion began again: 

"Every miracle of man whose substance is found to be true and not imaginary—whether it be grand, mediocre, or tiny—if the prescribed order of sacred things is observed, it must always be referred back to the glorious God, whose name be blessed for ever and ever. For He alone is the one who, either by Himself or through a delegate (though not without Himself), or through a substitute from Himself, performs such things as we marvel at, and whose adequate cause we cannot know, either why they happen or why they happen in this specific way. This precept stands forth, drawn from the armory of sacred things. For thus the divine hymn <note>Psalm 72:18</note> contains: *Blessed be the Lord God of Israel, who alone does wonders.* If He alone, then not men; if He alone, then not demons; if He alone, then not even angels. Therefore, God alone acts through Himself or through His creatures. For it is a well-established principle of law <note>original: "explorati iuris." Reuchlin was a trained jurist and often applied legal logic to theology</note> that what anyone can do through themselves, they can do through another in their own name. 

And Aristophanes immediately says: *Jupiter rules through some of the gods,* not because the lesser gods rule in the place of the Highest God—for then God would be absent and they would merely fill His place. That shepherd of Virgil <note>A reference to the character in Virgil's Third Eclogue</note> would not permit us to say such a thing, he who sang among the flocks: *All things are full of Jove.* Rather, it is that the angels rule in the name of God, governing their duties and executing the office delegated to them. It is lawful for them to take up these commands, for God dwells in them, is carried by them, and walks upon them. It is no different for us mortals who, as my friend Paul says <note>1 Corinthians 3:9</note>, are *co-workers with God.* As Aratus testifies in his *Phaenomena* <note>A Greek didactic poem on astronomy famously quoted by St. Paul in Acts 17:28</note>, we—along with the angels—are also His offspring, in whom and through whom He acts as He wills. 

Therefore, it is not we who perform miracles, but God, the Best and Greatest, to whom you must confess, *Who alone does great wonders, for His mercy endures forever.* <note>Psalm 136:4</note> You have here the identity of the worker: for He is the God of gods. You have the cause of the operation: for His mercy, His piety, His beneficence, and His grace endure forever—not by law, nor rigor, nor debt, nor any obligation. You have the end of these miraculous operations: for they are for confession, magnificence, glory, honor, and worship, of which the God of gods and Lord of lords is worthy, to whom you should confess because He is good. 

For this reason, some people should be ashamed—even those who think they have served philosophy most excellently—who, while professing many other things that walk the path of no small insanity and madness, strive most especially to persuade us that there is a certain 'art.' They claim that by the rules of this art, the wills of the gods are bound by a certain necessity, either by the constraints of words or the chains of written characters <note>term: "characterū uinculis." Refers to the sigils and symbols used in ritual magic to "bind" spirits</note>. Yet these gods do nothing unless commanded, and he who commands does not..."

<sig>d iii</sig>

<summary>Capnion presents a foundational hymn to the "One God" and argues that all true miracles proceed from God alone, often through angelic or human mediators. He explicitly rejects the "art" of ritual magic that claims to bind divine or spirit wills through symbols or specific words.</summary>
<keywords>divine agency, miracles, liturgy, Adonai, Aristophanes, Virgil, St. Paul, ritual magic, sigils, mercy vs. law, angelic mediation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue in Book Two of Reuchlin's work. Following the introductory hymn, the character Capnion (representing Reuchlin) argues that miracles are the work of God alone, not human "art." This prompts a frustrated rebuttal from Sidonius, who represents the perspective of a scholar seeking practical, almost magical, power through philosophy.</meta>

# BOOK TWO

...to be ruled, but is accustomed to rule, since he is the highest and therefore in no way subject to inferior fictions. Furthermore, while there can be a doctrine of illusions <note>The author distinguishes between 'præstigium' (juggling/stage magic/illusions) and true miracles</note>, there is no doctrine of miracles unless it be a serving union with divinity. In this union, we ourselves do not act through God, but God acts through us, gratifying the wishes of his friends to effect something that others might admire. 

Therefore, it was not without punishment that Moses and his brother Aaron <note>original: "Germanus", meaning his full or biological brother</note> suggested to the people in the desert of Zin that they themselves could produce water from the hardest rock. They did not attribute the act to its true Author [God], but presumed it for themselves to the extent that the fame of their own names might grow among the common people. This was truly foolish; for while they were careful that their own authority <note>original: "gravitas"</note> among their subjects should not be diminished, they allowed the majesty of God to be harmed. For this reason, they were forbidden from entering the land flowing with milk and honey, so that the younger generations might learn that the operation of miracles must be referred to the one highest and greatest God, who alone performs great wonders. Not to us, certainly not to us, but to the name of God must this glory be given <note>A reference to Psalm 115:1, "Non nobis Domine"</note>. 

"Therefore," Sidonius said, "we ourselves do not perform miracles, nor can there be any art by which we might learn to do them. It is in vain, then, that we have waited so long for you to tell us about the wonder-working Name. In vain has the desire to know this thing led me to spend a nearly sleepless night, if—as you declare—there is no art for it, and even if there were, we would not be capable of it. But by Pollux! <note>original: "ædepol", a common Roman oath</note> even this seems most wonderful of all to me, Capnion: that you now deny what you previously so excellently affirmed. You said, if I remember correctly, that we ourselves are the makers of wonderful works; now you tell a different story: that neither the gods nor we ourselves do them, but God alone. 

What is this great inconsistency? One moment you say yes, the next you say no. Yet we can fight most fiercely against you using the deeds of our ancestors and win the battle. For setting aside the poets' tales of the 'little woman' who promises to release minds with her songs, to stop the flow of rivers, and to turn the stars backward, or the thousand magical feats in Lucan—such as how 'love, not sufficiently drawn by nature, flowed into hard hearts through the song of the Thessalian women' <note>Lucan was a Roman poet famous for his descriptions of witchcraft in his epic, the Pharsalia</note>—or the tale of Pirithous, who is said to be the first to draw the moon down from heaven, or the Homeric tales of Circe's omnipotence, or the Virgilian <note>original: "Maronica", referring to Publius Vergilius Maro</note> tales of Proteus’s cunning. 

I prefer to duel with you using histories. Although they are quite innumerable, I will mention a few you dare not deny: that Tarquinius Priscus, following the opinion of the augur Attus Navius (as recorded in Livy), cut a whetstone with a razor; that Pythagoras was in both Thurii and Metapontum at the same moment; that Apollonius was suddenly transferred from Smyrna to Ephesus by a spoken word, and is said to have raised a girl on her wedding day as she was dying in Rome by words secretly pronounced—of which that noble writer Philostratus is witness <note>Philostratus wrote the 'Life of Apollonius of Tyana', a Neo-Pythagorean philosopher and wonder-worker</note>. 

By these examples and with that hope, we trusted that we would be instructed by you in similar learning. We hoped that, omitting the anxieties of magicians, the observations of astrologers, and the investigations of physicists, we might by our mouth alone effect sudden things worthy of admiration, which would prove that true philosophy exists within us. For what does it help to learn many wonders daily if one never performs any? There is a great opinion among the common people regarding our philosophy and our knowledge of all things—for they describe philosophy as 'the knowledge of things divine and human.' But I pray you, best friends, what notable and illustrious satisfaction for our studies shall we provide to the people, while [we perform] such...

<summary>Sidonius challenges Capnion's claim that miracles belong to God alone, citing various historical and mythological figures like Pythagoras and Apollonius who allegedly performed wonders through their own knowledge.</summary>

He argues that if philosophy doesn't grant the power to perform wonders, it is no better than common knowledge or the natural eloquence found in anyone.</meta>

-># ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD<-
->## BOOK TWO<-

d iiii

...divine works are lacking: It is a small thing for us to know letters, which even the lowliest have learned. Being eloquent raises us not at all, as nature has granted this even to simple women. Nor does it matter that we profess to either entangle or disentangle so many and so perplexed scholastic questions. Therefore, the common people consider us fools and madmen rather than wise men—so much so that in our age, the name of philosophy has almost fallen into disgrace. Even pagan rites invite one to live religiously, and morals compel it; wherefore there will be very little to distinguish us from the unlearned masses, unless wonderful works likewise follow our admirable profession. 

Moved by this, Thespion, that leader of the Gymnosophists <note>original: "gymnosophistarum"; these were the "naked philosophers" of India whom the Greeks believed were masters of ascetic wisdom</note>, said to the philosopher of Tyana [Apollonius]—who had come from afar, drawn by the fame of the Egyptian school to explore their discipline—as he sat among the disciples of wisdom in a garden for the sake of disputing, he pointed to an elm tree, the third in order from them, and said: "O wood, speak to the wise Apollonius, so that he may recognize that it is not impossible for wisdom to be possessed by us as well." And so, according to the command, the wood spoke to him with an articulate and feminine voice. They did not think themselves proven philosophers unless they showed a prodigy that exceeded all sense of human judgment. 

Since you, however, attribute this to God alone and deny it to men, I do not know in what way you seek to diminish your own [tradition], who are nevertheless celebrated with the highest praise for performing miracles. I remain silent about the apostles, whose power is said to have been immense throughout the whole world. But what of Hermione—one of the four daughters of Philip, the one who instructed the treasurer of Queen Candace? She could not be harmed despite being beaten with many rods in the sight of Trajan. After that emperor died, she was scourged again by order of Hadrian and sang hymns. Then, placed in a cauldron heated with a great fire, filled with lead and boiling sulfur, she looked up to heaven, and having made the sign [of the cross], quicker than a word, the fire was extinguished and the lead sprang out. And in the presence of everyone, she said, "O King, I call the God of heaven to witness that I feel no more heat than you yourself feel." For she seemed to everyone to be moving almost in dew and among flowers. When the cauldron was lit again, she remained unharmed just as before. 

I could cite the annals and deeds of many, and weave a longer history of a certain man in Atroa named Peter, but I have decided to restrain myself lest I seem to teach you most learned men—as they say, "the sow teaching Minerva" <note>original: "sus Minerva"; a common Latin proverb for an ignorant person attempting to instruct an expert</note>. Yet, regarding this man, I will recall at least that he is written to have crossed the River Halys with dry feet. 

Then Barchias said: "If you understand Capnion, Sidonius, as you most certainly should with an attentive and diligent mind, surely you have spoken many things to us in vain, unless I am mistaken. For he proposed the right things to each of us, though you have taken them wrongly <note>original: "sinistre"; literally "on the left," meaning perversely or incorrectly</note>. Remember what he added yesterday, following the talk of the inestimable conjunction, society, and union of the highest divinity with human weakness: that we are able, as evidence of such divine conversation, to perform wonderful prodigies with a single word. I shall judge it so, as people are accustomed to attribute operations to those things from which we see them emanate daily with our crude sight. Such as this line from the Odes of Horace: *And machines haul the dry hulls* <note>original: "trahuntque siccas machinae carinas"; Horace, Odes 1.4.2</note>. For if you penetrate the force of the speech and do not rather notice the habit of speaking, you will find no "machine" that hauls ships, but rather we...

<summary>Sidonius argues that true philosophy must be accompanied by miracles, citing historical and legendary figures like Apollonius of Tyana and Saint Hermione. Barchias begins to clarify Capnion's earlier point, suggesting that miracles come through a "single word" via a divine union with humanity.</summary>

--- Page 63 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue where Sidonius argues that human agency is often credited for divine actions. He uses the analogy of tools and ships to transition into a discussion about the power of the Hebrew language, eventually leading Baruchias to begin his revelation of "secret names."</meta>

-># ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD<-

We ourselves ingeniously construct certain machines by which we easily move such a great mass into the sea. Thus it happens that we, by means of machines, more rightly pull the keel; yet it better fits the common way of speaking to say, as the poet does: *The machines drag the dry keels* <note>original: "Trahunt siccas machinæ carinas"; a quote from Horace, Odes 1.4.2</note>. In this way, we would also be forced to accuse Homer of a similar fault when he writes such things about Phereclus—both that he was the most skillful craftsman of all, and that he built for Alexander <note>Alexander is another name for Paris, the prince of Troy</note> the ships that were the cause of all evils, which were a ruin to all the Trojans and to Paris himself. Why should we blame the ships of Phereclus, which could do no harm without a pilot? It was Alexander, therefore, who caused the damage, not the ships. 

At this point, Sidonius, you might rebuke the wisest and greatest of poets for saying that the ships were a harm to the Phrygians <note>The Trojans</note>—those ships in which Helen was carried away to Troy—rather than blaming the son of Priam who committed the adultery and the kidnapping, or Troy itself which received Helen. But custom, as in many other things, ought to prevail in this matter even among us; though we speak of divine things in human language, we say that men full of the spirit of God perform miracles, when nevertheless it is primarily God himself who performs them through the man. 

Truly, I would believe you do not have so dull a taste that you have not perceived these things far better than I. And yet, you wished to complain about the slowness of Capnion <note>The Greek-style pseudonym for Johannes Reuchlin, meaning "Little Smoke"</note>, who holds us in suspense with such a great desire for the "wonder-working name"—perhaps to consume us by delaying, so as to make us more eager; though as far as I am concerned, that is impossible. For there is nothing anywhere that I desire more. Or perhaps he thinks that by this delay his neck might be snatched from the halter <note>i.e., he might escape his promise</note> and thus slip away from what was promised. Yet I think he attempts this the less because he values integrity so highly that he would consider it a cause for incredible shame if he held to a wavering faith. 

But there is a type of men who gladly bury their talent <note>A reference to the Biblical Parable of the Talents</note> and hide their knowledge; they prefer to seem more ignorant so that they do not have to teach, rather than creating rivals by teaching. I do not know if he is pretending to be of their number. For my part, in the fellowship of letters, I would wish every individual to be like me—so profuse in putting forth those things which we carry in our minds and which we accumulate for ourselves through erudition. For if by chance, perhaps deceived by self-love, I think I know something, I immediately seek a listener, whom I both pray and sometimes even eventually persuade to learn from me; so much do I delight in the communication and liberality of studies.

"Let us then put Baruchias to the test," Sidonius replied to Capnion, "to see whether he speaks the truth or if he has decided to invent this discourse within himself so that he might more easily force me into a corner. Come, speak of the words that your kinsmen keep in secret, and hide almost 'under a bushel' <note>original: "sub modio"; a reference to Matthew 5:15</note> as you were saying. For you showed that you had read them over many times. I now agree with you earnest students that the Hebrews have contributed not a little to our faculty; we often imitate them more rightly than other nations, and it is well known that in the memory of our fathers their religion possessed a great deal of divinity and they worshipped God with the proper rites."

"And I shall do so without being forced," said Baruchias, "lest I should seem to seek glory by fleeing—by allowing myself to be asked by such great men—or lest I should seem to say one thing and do another. For there are a few things concerning the sacred names that I was taught by the school of the Hebrews, and therefore I shall explain them in a few words. But bolt the door <note>original: "obstruite pessulum"</note> so that none of the servants may overhear, lest sacred things become a laughingstock to the profane, and *pearls be foully trampled by swine* <note>original: "margaritæ a porcis fœde calcentur"; a reference to Matthew 7:6</note>. I will also deliberately attempt to speak somewhat obscurely, provided that you yourselves, by the excellence of your intellect, understand what I intend. The fellowship of the divinity with the human mind occurs because God uses man as an instrument, not otherwise than the Peripatetics <note>Followers of Aristotle</note> speak concerning the activity of the sensitive and vegetative soul..."

<summary>Sidonius argues that humans are instruments of God during miracles, just as ships are tools for sailors. Following this, Baruchias agrees to reveal secret Hebrew names for God, warning that such sacred knowledge must be kept from the "profane."</summary>

<keywords>Johannes Reuchlin, Capnion, Baruchias, miracles, divine instruments, Hebrew names, Tetragrammaton, Homer, Phereclus, sacred secrets, Peripatetics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical argument from Book One, moving from the analogy of physical tools to the internal mechanics of nature and the soul. It establishes the relationship between the "vegetative" and "sensitive" faculties as a mirror for how God interacts with the world and humanity.</meta>

->### BOOK TWO<-

...nature teaches such things. For wherever the <term>vegetative faculty<note>The 'vegetative' or 'nutritive' soul is the part of a living being responsible for growth and nutrition, shared by plants, animals, and humans.</note></term> exists within the <term>sensitive faculty<note>The 'sensitive' soul is the part responsible for feeling and movement, shared by animals and humans.</note></term>, the vegetative acts as an instrument, while the sensitive acts as the primary mover. When the sensitive power begins to act, it does not move toward something lower than itself, but through a middle ground suitable to both, it moves as far as possible toward something equal and agreeable to itself, and toward that with which it can dwell. 

In animals, the power that fosters and nourishes acts through an intermediate heat. This heat—which is the messenger of a certain hidden and digestive property—digests and transforms raw food. For heat itself does not do the transforming; otherwise, you would cook nourishment faster on a hearth than in a stomach, yet we clearly see the opposite is true. Rather, that heat runs between like a prophet, serving as a kind of character and image of a natural agreement for the purpose of digestion. Therefore, the food does not remain as it was, but migrates into another substance. The nourishing power does not transform it into grass or a plant—for that would make it more like a mere vegetable—but rather, it industriously converts it into flesh, which is most suitable for the sense of touch, so that the sensitive mover itself may inhabit it through that touch. 

Thus, God, the most excellent of all, who (according to the testimony of <term>Hermes Trismegistus<note>original: "Mercurio ter maximo"; a legendary Hellenistic figure regarded as the author of the Hermetic Corpus, a series of sacred texts on philosophy and magic.</note></term>) fashioned two images after his own example—the world and man—so that he might "play in the circle of the earth," as it says in the Proverbs, and find his "delights among the sons of men." 

In the world, God plays through wondrous operations, not only by celestial or elementary power, but sometimes by a hidden property. Physicians prove this regarding the wearing of <term>coral<note>In the Renaissance, coral was often worn as an amulet to protect against various ailments or evil spirits, believed to work through 'occult' or hidden natural properties rather than simple heat or cold.</note></term> or other objects; they work not because this or that thing is particularly hot or cold, but because of the specific quality we mentioned regarding animal nutrition—where the transformation of digested things occurs not by superior heat, but through such a hidden quality. And just as natural heat in the digestion of food is like a shadow holding the place of a hidden property, so too in the effects arising from the secret disposition of things—where one individual differs from another—it is not nature itself (as the common people think), but something more obscure that precedes nature in the chain of cause. The very word "nature" carries the likeness and type of this obscure cause in common speech. 

Now you have heard concerning the world. Let us look likewise at man, who is called the <term>Microcosm<note>From the Greek 'mikros' (small) and 'kosmos' (world); the idea that the human being is a 'little world' that mirrors the structure of the entire universe.</note></term> by the Greeks. God himself delights in his interaction with man more than with the world, and he strives to transform man into himself (as far as human capacity allows), just as sensitive nutrition transforms food. On our part, he does this through the "heat of love" which we conceive; on his part, he does it through a hidden property which we do not know—whether you call it a covenant, or an inexpressible pull, or by any other name. 

Now, although God as the sole Almighty could do all these things without any intermediate causes and with fewer observed limits on his actions, he nevertheless wished for many things to participate in his goodness and to work together. Some have been accustomed to call these "secondary causes," others "powers," and some "gods," "angels," or "good spirits." Truly, whatever intermediate thing comes between these effects, it holds an affinity and agreement with the mover, and at the same time is not at an enormous distance from the thing being moved. 

Therefore, the Lord of Eternity, rejoicing in our society, holds the holy mind of man among his delights, as if it were a neighbor to his own nature and origin. When he sees that mind following him with diligent devotion and perpetual religion, he always—like a most ample nourishing power—endeavors (as much as human weakness permits) to transform it into himself by digesting it, not only by the heat of love, but also by a hidden property, so that man may migrate into God and God may dwell in man. But truly, just as that property by which...

<summary>This page explores the concept of 'hidden properties' in nature, using the analogy of digestion to explain how God transforms the human soul. It defines man as a Microcosm and suggests that spiritual growth is a process of being 'digested' into the divine through love and occult natural laws.</summary>
<keywords>vegetative faculty, sensitive faculty, digestion, hidden property, Hermes Trismegistus, Microcosm, heat of love, secondary causes, angels, spiritual transformation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical argument from Book Two, moving from the analogy of physical digestion to the spiritual "digestion" or transformation of the human soul into the divine through the power of sacred words.</meta>

->### ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD<-

...we transition toward God and exceed human nature, the way is secret and hidden to us. Thus, by the highest right, God gave to this path hidden and secret names as well, and placed within them certain covenants. When these are observed, he himself may soon draw near to be present at their proper utterance, according to our prayer. Furthermore, just as in natural digestion there is a power of heat which we recognize, and yet a hidden power of <term>transcorporation<note>The process of changing one substance into the body of another; the biological transformation of food into flesh.</note></term> which we do not understand; so in this most divine transition into God, there are certain words which we know, and certain words which we do not know. 

Indeed, this bond of words is suitable to both parties. For God is spirit, the word is a breath, and man is a breathing being. God is called <term>Logos<note>original: "logos". A Greek term meaning both "Word" and "Reason." Reuchlin uses it to bridge the gap between divine thought and human speech.</note></term>. The Word is designated by the same name, and human reason is expressed by a similar term. God is conceived by our mind and brought to birth by the word; therefore, just as he chose the imperceptible seat of the mind for himself, so he chose a perceptible dwelling place in words—not all words, nor those falling by chance, but those which divinity first established for us, not those which human invention devised. For God "knows the thoughts of men, that they are vain." <note>A reference to Psalm 94:11.</note> But the thought of God is a bringer of safety and health; it is permanent, invincible, and certain. Whatever he thinks, so it shall be; whatever he turns over in his mind, so it shall happen. The Lord of the universe has decreed it, and who shall be able to weaken it? 

Therefore, not all words possess divine power—if you have listened closely to me speaking—but only those which the highest God of the world and of men, or the perceptive angels, have composed in a certain way by the command and providence of God. These words resonate with the harmony of eternity; they are held fast by divinity and breathe with the structure of a beneficial arrangement. They are like perceptible monuments of an imperceptible <term>deification<note>The process of a human becoming divine or god-like.</note></term>, which, relying on the immovable stability of letters and the everlasting firmness of the vocal elements, allow for both an unshakable rhythm of modulation and a swiftness of pronunciation that is not sought after or artificial, but native, simple, and venerable. 

By these intermediaries, God strikes a covenant with man—not with just any man, but with one who shines with holiness, piety, religion, and especially with the confidence of belief. Therefore, if a certain decree of speaking ought to reconcile human mortality with the power of God, it is fitting that those most holy names (or "words," if you prefer) be primary, direct, and ancient. This is true even if you sometimes call them <term>barbaric<note>In the Renaissance, "barbaric" referred to languages other than polished Classical Latin or Greek, specifically Hebrew or Aramaic, which were seen as more ancient and closer to God.</note></term> on account of their unrefined—that is, simple—antiquity. Indeed, what is ancient approaches the age of the world; that age is close to eternity, and eternity is neighbor to God, who reigns above eternity. This is why he is called the <note>original: "antiquus dierū"</note> **Ancient of Days** by the early theologians, because he presides over the kingdom of all ages. 

Therefore, "barbaric" things are more closely related to divinity. For this reason, and not without cause, **Zoroaster**—the first of the ethnic theologians—forbids barbaric words to be changed. By "barbaric," they mean Hebrew or those words derived most closely from it. For the little flowers of speech and the charm of elegancies were devised after the time of **Heber** <note>The biblical ancestor of the Hebrews, from whose name the word "Hebrew" is traditionally derived.</note> and the division of languages by men who were more curious than sincere. But the simple speech—pure, uncorrupted, holy, brief, and constant—belongs to the Hebrews. It is the language in which God is said to have spoken with man, and men with angels, face to face and not through an interpreter; face to face, not through the stream of **Castalia**, nor the cave of **Trophonius**, nor the tree of **Dodona**, nor the **Tripod of Delphi**, <note>These are all references to famous Oracles of ancient Greece, which Reuchlin contrasts unfavorably with the directness of Hebrew revelation.</note> but just as a friend is accustomed to speak with a friend. If you think this is said by me rather to praise my own people than because I truly believe it, then compare the languages. You will find none purer, none more modest. Regarding their antiquity and order, if you trust our historians less, then look at...

<meta>catchword: your</meta>

<summary>Reuchlin argues that Hebrew is the original, divine language through which God communicates directly with humanity, possessing a unique spiritual power that "refined" languages like Greek and Latin lack. He characterizes Hebrew as "barbaric" in its holy simplicity, linking it to the eternal nature of the "Ancient of Days."</summary>
<keywords>Hebrew, Logos, divine names, Zoroaster, Ancient of Days, sacred language, Kabbalah, oracles, philology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument for the antiquity and divine authority of the Hebrew language. Reuchlin is transitioning from the philosophical nature of the "Word" to a historical defense, asserting that Moses predates all Greek philosophers and poets, thereby making the Hebrew tradition the primary source of wisdom.</meta>

-># BOOK SECOND <-

...learn from your own writers that no one among all the authors is found to be more ancient than Moses. Three hundred and ten years are counted from his time until the capture of Troy <note>The fall of Troy was a central chronological marker for Renaissance historians.</note>. At that time, Abeson <note>original: "Abeson"; likely referring to Ibzan, a judge of Israel mentioned in Judges 12:8.</note> ruled over our people, Agamemnon over the Greeks, Vafer over the Egyptians, and Teutamus over the Assyrians. Let someone, meanwhile, unroll for me any other volume recorded in letters to memory before the destruction of Troy, besides the books of the Hebrews. For after one hundred and fifty years, finally the primary Greek prophets, Homer and Hesiod—with Cicero as witness—followed the Trojan era; Orpheus, with his sacred rites, was only slightly before them. But this is nothing compared to our author <note>Moses</note>, who brought forth written laws at a far earlier time. Indeed, in previous centuries, the Greek historian Eupolemus, in the most noble monuments of his works *On the Kings of Judea*, left record that Moses was exceptionally wise and handed down the knowledge of grammar and letters to the Jews, who afterwards bestowed them upon the Phoenicians because of their proximity. Then the Phoenicians distributed the system of characters to the Greeks on account of their mutual commerce. This, I believe, is what moved Pliny to say that he always thought the original letters were Assyrian, although some would have them found among the Egyptians and others among the Syrians; nevertheless, he says Cadmus brought them into Greece from Phoenicia, sixteen in number. The fact that this whole region shares common borders creates this variety of opinion. And perhaps it is also because certain writers, not being very diligent in these matters, called Moses an Egyptian priest, such as Strabo in the sixteenth book of his *Geography*. Whatever the case may be, Moses, whose writing has come down even to us, stands as the more ancient; before him, no monuments of letters are read. Many of the Greeks confess this antiquity, though one says it one way and another another. Our own Philo <note>Philo of Alexandria, a Jewish philosopher who harmonized Greek and Hebrew thought.</note>, and our—or rather your—Fabius Josephus, while he entitles his work *Antiquities*, writes nothing that is foreign to Moses. Likewise Polemon, in the first book of his Greek histories, places him in the times of Ogygus and Inachus <note>Legendary early kings of Greece used as chronological anchors.</note>. Apion, that learned man, says in the time of Amosis, king of Egypt, he was the leader of the fugitives, as mentioned both in his book against the Jews and in the fourth of his histories; and Ptolemy of Mendes mentions it in his histories of the Egyptians. Finally, there are those who are considered illustrious in recording the affairs of the Athenians: Hellanicus, Philochorus, Castor, Thallus, and Alexander, surnamed Polyhistor, and easily the prince of all historians, Diodorus, who himself diligently traveled through nearly all of Asia and Europe with his own eyes. All these have proclaimed the most ample marks of antiquity concerning that miraculous leader and most excellent author of divine matters. Through this it becomes manifest that he stands before all others in age of time, antiquity of religion, and in the admirable power and discipline of divinity regarding secret <term>operation<note>original: "opationis" (likely a contraction of operationis); refers to theurgy or the practical application of divine names.</note></term>. For in vain would you seek from the Greeks words descending from heaven or names contained by divine favor in their syllables, because of their modern art of speaking and their very recent variety of language. For the Greeks, as Iamblichus of Chalcis, the disciple of Porphyry, says, are by nature fond of new things and are carried headlong everywhere like a ship lacking <term>ballast<note>original: "saburra"; the heavy material used to stabilize a ship. Reuchlin uses this to describe the "flighty" nature of Greek philosophy compared to Hebrew stability.</note></term>, having no stability. Nor do they preserve what they have received from others, but they quickly let even this go, and are accustomed to transform everything on account of their instability and the expression of new invention. And for that reason, a certain Egyptian priest is rightly praised for answering Solon in Plato’s *Timaeus* when he said: "O Solon, Solon, you Greeks are always children, nor is any among you an old man from Greece, because you always have a young soul in which there is no ancient opinion derived from the commemoration of antiquity."

<summary>Reuchlin argues for the supreme antiquity of Moses over all Greek poets and historians, citing numerous classical authorities to prove that the Hebrew language and its "secret operations" predated and influenced all other civilizations.</summary>
<keywords>Moses, antiquity of Hebrew, Greek historians, Philo, Josephus, Pliny, Plato, Timaeus, divine names, origins of the alphabet</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument for the antiquity of the Hebrew language. It completes a sentence from the previous page regarding Cadmus bringing the alphabet to Greece, then transitions into the history of the Septuagint translation in Alexandria.</meta>

-># ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD <-

...into Greece. This is no mere opinion but a teaching aged by time. Led by this, perhaps, that famous Ptolemy Philadelphus <note>Ptolemy II Philadelphus (308–246 BCE), king of Egypt, who famously sponsored the Library of Alexandria.</note>, a man of very keen zeal in gathering books of antiquity, when he had filled the Library of Alexandria with nearly seven hundred thousand volumes, and the pillar of antiquity, Moses, and the first monument of old age was missing, it is said he then sent royal ambassadors to Jerusalem. They were to pray that from each of the individual tribes, six men most learned in Greek and most fit for translating the Pentateuch—or rather that volume which we call by the compound name "The Twenty-Four" <note>The Hebrew Bible consists of 24 books in the traditional Jewish arrangement.</note>—should travel with them to Alexandria. 

When they arrived, that king, not in the city itself, but at Pharos <note>An island near Alexandria, famous for its lighthouse.</note> almost seven furlongs <note>original: "stadia"; a Greek unit of distance approximately 185 meters.</note> away, lest they be hindered by the tumult, built seventy-two small cells according to the number of interpreters. Jerome, writing to Desiderius, does not think these stories are fables <note>Saint Jerome was famously skeptical of the miraculous claim that the translators were isolated in cells yet produced identical texts; Reuchlin here argues that Jerome might have changed his mind if he had read Justin Martyr’s account.</note>, if he had more accurately weighed the *Exhortation* of Justin Martyr, who asserts that he saw the remains of the cells still standing. Therefore, the Hebrew scripture was translated into the Greek tongue for the Egyptian king, so that among so many volumes he would not be lacking the first elements of this world, nor, along with Solon being forced by the truth to admit it, that neither he himself nor any other of the Greeks had any knowledge of antiquity. 

This warns us with the greatest reason that for secret operations <note>magical or mystical rites</note>, no ancient names or those related to the divine should be sought from the Greeks, nor even from the Egyptians, despite their supposedly more ancient discipline. For as Socrates philosophizes with Hermogenes in Plato's *Cratylus*, "All divine names certainly were brought forth either first by the gods, or from an antiquity whose beginning is not easily known, or from the Barbarians" <note>In this context, "Barbarians" refers to non-Greeks, specifically the Hebrews.</note>. 

But indeed, the Egyptians fashioned their own gods with their own hands, by the testimony of their father Hermes Trismegistus <note>A legendary Hellenistic figure associated with the syncretism of the Greek god Hermes and the Egyptian god Thoth, regarded as the author of the Hermetic corpus.</note>, wandering concerning the reason of divinity and not attending to the worship and religion of God. What divine thing, then, will you seek in a human clay figure? What sacred things from your inventions, what celestial aids can be hoped for from the works of your hands? In what way will you learn the signs of the most transcendent and super-celestial presence, and the paths of the highest powers, from demons, whom the ancestors of the Egyptians made while sinning against divinity and wandering? 

For the other celestial gods, whose maker is eternal, dwell indeed in the highest places according to the philosophy of the Egyptians, and are therefore called heavenly beings, and they do not mingle with our human enticements. Therefore, those others with whom their daily conversation in the temples occurred, and who were content with human proximity, are not gods but demons, made by the ancestors against the will of God, arisen after the birth of men, and either so foolish that they do not speak but merely nod, or so cunning that they flatter their Egyptian makers in all things, saying whatever might soothe their ears. From this you can draw nothing stable, nothing firm, nothing constant, nothing pleasing to God, to whom even their very generation was displeasing. 

You can understand these arguments from the more excellent philosophers of that region. But neither did the sacred names of divinity flow to this nation from primeval antiquity, although they boast of many years in Plato, when they claim that their deeds are contained in writings of nine thousand years; nevertheless, I consider this whole number—as much as I am persuaded to believe concerning the beginning of the world—to be full of fables...

<summary>Reuchlin recounts the history of the Septuagint's creation under Ptolemy Philadelphus and argues that true divine names and mystical power must be sought in Hebrew antiquity rather than from Greek or Egyptian sources, which he links to man-made demons.</summary>
