# BOOK TWO

...I think. But if they themselves do not consider these things to be false, I demand from them that they also willingly admit what Marcus Tullius Cicero <note>original: "M. Tullius Cicero"; Reuchlin refers to Cicero’s *De Divinatione*, where the author mentions the vast chronological claims of the East.</note> records concerning Babylon and the Chaldeans. They claim to possess monuments covering four hundred and seventy thousand years <note>original: "quadraginta septuaginta millia annorū"; a massive span of time often cited by ancient authors to demonstrate the antiquity of Eastern civilizations.</note>, a number far greater than that of the Egyptians. Moreover, although the Babylonians are sometimes called Assyrians, it is established by the most ancient scholars that they were the same people once called Syrians, who made Babylon and Nineveh the capitals of their kingdom. The illustrious author Strabo <note>Strabo (c. 64 BCE–24 CE) was a Greek geographer and historian.</note> and many other notable writers, in their great zeal for investigating antiquity, have handed this down to posterity. 

In truth, the most noble region of Syria is the territory of Damascus, neighbor to the Persians, from which the race of the Jews took its origin—as attested by Trogus and Justin <note>Pompeius Trogus and his epitomizer Justin were Roman historians who provided accounts of Jewish origins that differed from the Biblical narrative.</note>, as well as many other historians not to be despised. Therefore, the Jews (who were both Syrians and Assyrians) would, in this way, possess monuments of their language much older than those of the Egyptians, even if we were to believe external authors. Thus, the Jews would surpass the Egyptians both in time and in their God. Consequently, the Hebrew names would be more sacred than the Egyptian ones, since they are both older and were handed down under the worship of the one and first God.

I must also add this: the Egyptians, due to their crudeness in much earlier times—before they had invented alphabetical characters—wrote using the images of things themselves <note>Reuchlin is referring to hieroglyphs, which he views as a "cruder" precursor to the alphabet.</note>. They have maintained this form of writing in their so-called "sacred rites" right up to the present, alongside more recently devised elements. It remains to be seen whether Egyptian names, if they were divine and truly sacred, would be useful in secret miracles. Yet, we have already demonstrated (citing Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary figure to whom the Hermetic corpus is attributed.</note>, their own most excellent authority) that their "gods" were actually demons who came after humanity. Nor were these names handed down from an antiquity of scripture that could contain them, for the books of the Hebrews surpass them in every possible way.

Let us now test this further: did these names perhaps arise from the Egyptians themselves as "barbaric" men? (This was the third part of the Socratic discourse). Herodotus, the father of history, teaches us in his second book that the Egyptians refused to be called "Barbarians"—rather, they applied that term to all other nations. But because they so abhor and disdain this word, as if it were a mark of shame upon their region, they were instead called "Barbarians" by their enemies, the Hebrews. 

It is just as when we sometimes say to a prostitute or a common whore as a taunt: "O, noble lady!" Indeed, there were three famous courtesans in Rome at the same time: Cytheris, Origo, and Arbuscula. Sallust <note>Gaius Sallustius Crispus (86–35 BCE), a Roman historian.</note> writes in the same spirit: "He had," he said, "an old habit of debauchery with Fulvia, a *noble* woman" <note>original: "muliere nobili"; Reuchlin is highlighting the ironic use of "noble" to describe a woman of ill repute.</note>. You see how a word that should belong to virtue is thrown at shameful people as a reproach. For a little later, the same Sallust asserts that most of the nobility seethed with envy against Cicero, a <term>novus homo</term> <note>original: "nouum hominē"; literally a "new man," referring to the first person in a family to reach the Roman consulship.</note>, believing the consulship was "polluted" by him. In that instance, the word "nobility" was used more correctly. 

This is how I understand that song: *When Israel went out of Egypt, and the house of Jacob from a barbarian people, Judah became his sanctuary* <note>Psalm 114:1. Reuchlin argues that the Bible calls the Egyptians "barbarians" specifically to mock their pride.</note>. I believe the Egyptians were called "Barbarians" in contempt precisely because they refused to be called so. Yet the word <term>Lo’ez</term> <note>original: "Loes"; a transliteration of the Hebrew לעז (*la'az*), meaning a foreign or barbaric tongue.</note> here designates crudeness and dullness—the letter 'n' being changed into 'l', though <term>Lo'ez</term> is everywhere explained as "barbarian." Let the Egyptians therefore blush at being called barbarians and complain of the insult. And as for the sacred rites of Zoroaster...

He also explains that the term "Barbarian" was applied to Egyptians by the Hebrews as an ironic insult to puncture their national pride.</summary>

He is now moving into a discussion on the term "Barbarian" and why Hebrew should be considered the original "barbaric" language of sacred mystery.</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

...of the prince <note>original: "principis"; likely referring to the Neoplatonic philosopher Porphyry mentioned on the preceding page.</note>, as they say, they will accept this priestly decree when he forbids that barbarian words be changed in sacred rites. Therefore, the barbarian words which we use in sacred rites, unchangeably and without contamination, will not be Egyptian, but rather they will be Mosaic <note>That is, belonging to the tradition of Moses.</note>.

For that Moses confessed himself a barbarian before God—that is, thick-tongued and heavy of speech—when he said: "My Lord, I am not a man of eloquence, but of a heavy mouth and a heavy tongue" <note>A reference to Exodus 4:10, where Moses expresses his speech impediment to God.</note>. The cosmographer Strabo related in his fourteenth book that such men were called "barbarians" from the beginning, a word invented for those who spoke with difficulty, roughness, and hardness, just as we say "lispers" and "stammerers," finding names related to the things themselves, such as murmur, clangor, noise, crackle, and other similar sounds.

And if one wishes to examine all histories precisely, we will find that all Eastern nations resent the name of "barbarian," except for our Moses and his people, who did not deny being harsh-spoken and barbarian, and thus handed it down through letters to memory. It is not fitting for us, who imitate his footsteps and speech, to deny that we are barbarians. Not because our method of speaking is entirely sluggish; for we have a language structured by rule, and we practice the art of grammar. To the Hebrew youths, Moses Kimhi <note>Mose Kymhei (c. 1127–1190) was a famous Hebrew grammarian and brother of David Kimhi.</note>, Master Ephod <note>original: "Magister Ephod"; this refers to Profiat Duran (c. 1350–1415), a Hebrew scholar whose work was titled *Ma’aseh Ephod*.</note>—whom we interpret in the French manner as "I, the perfect one, must endure" <note>A playful etymological pun on Duran's name: *Ephod* and *Durandus*.</note>—and Hasra and other excellent schoolmasters, instruct both the mouth and the pen.

Therefore, it is not that we are speechless and rustic, but that we are the first of men, whom all investigators of the most ancient matters have always asserted were "barbarians." Not considering this accurately, some of our own people dared to teach that "barbarians" are those who do not speak the Holy Tongue, like Solomon Scholasticus in the exposition of the hymn we mentioned, in the interpretation of this word *Loes* <note>original: "Loes"; a Latinization of the Hebrew word *Lo'ez* (foreign-speaking), famously used in Psalm 114 to describe Egypt as a people of a "strange language."</note>, and certain other beginners. But it happens to our people as to other artists: "Untrained and trained alike, we everywhere write poems" <note>original: "Scribim' indocti doctiqʒ poemata paffim"; a quote from Horace, *Epistles* 2.1.117.</note>.

I would truly believe that the more illustrious Egyptian priests mixed into their own rites the names of the Hebrews, which they might use more effectively when they wished to bring some secret into the light; and indeed, they learned these things only through long and hidden study, as you yourselves can learn. Unless these things had once been foreign to their native tongue, they could not have hidden them as much as we see in history. For although Plato and Eudoxus lived with them in Heliopolis for thirteen years, they were barely able at last to gather a few small things from their secret words through long and diligent conversation, which they might scatter in their own monuments.

Regarding the Indian philosophers whom they call Brahmans, there is no need for much dispute, since the fact that they began first from the Greeks is proven to us by infinite other things, especially the many statues there, which are both Greek and worshiped with Greek ritual—such as those of Minerva, of Delian Apollo, of Lemnian and Amyclean Dionysus, and others which Philostratus diligently mentions in the *Life of Apollonius of Tyana*. The Indians also agree with the Greeks in opinions and disciplines. For Iarchas used to speak to Apollonius a theory regarding the human soul, such as Pythagoras handed down to the Greeks and they themselves to the Egyptians, as if the Brahmans were the teachers of the Egyptians; yet this indicates not so much the order of antiquity as the innovation of opinions. Especially since the same Iarchas tells that his grandfather lived at a time when no great philosopher was in India...

philo <meta>Catchword: philo(fophus)</meta>

He further asserts that Egyptian and Greek philosophers, including Plato and the Brahmans of India, were influenced by or borrowed from these Hebrew origins.</summary>

### BOOK TWO

...philosopher, nor did any noble spirit sprout there. But among the Druids of the Gauls, one need not look back through many ages of men, for they all philosophized in Greek, and for that reason they are known to have come later than the barbarians. Most learned and kind men, I have dared to blunt your ears with far too many words out of my own particular vice of talkativeness, while I attempt to somewhat affirm that the most ancient words of the barbarians are to be selected for performing miraculous works—not those of the Phoenicians, nor the Greeks, nor the Egyptians, nor the Indians, nor the Druids. I would not even add the Persians, were the vicinity of Damascus and Persia not so close; rather, I speak only of the Jewish nation and that of the ancient Heber <note>original: "Hebri"; referring to Eber, the biblical ancestor of the Hebrews from whom the language takes its name.</note>, whose speech, during the dispersion of languages, reached all the way to Moses and after him to us, pure and holy. It reflects the first institution of speaking from the world's beginning, which we must judge the first-born humans received not from man, but from God alone, especially regarding the divine names, the positioning of which is beyond human power. On this matter, let us hear Socrates, so that I may use your own authors in this debate rather than mine; he speaks thus in the works of Plato:

> By Jove, Hermogenes, if we had any sense, we would establish it as the highest form of rightness to confess among ourselves that we know nothing of the gods, neither of the gods themselves nor of the names by which they call themselves. For it is certain that they call themselves by their true names. These things are in Plato <note>A reference to Plato’s *Cratylus*, a dialogue concerning the origin of names and language.</note>.

"Indeed, you do not seem to me to be speaking obscurely," Sidonius said, "and you have given us more than you promised, Baruchia. For almost all these things align with our philosophy: that God joins Himself to the human seed and rejoices in us when we have been transformed into Him according to our capacity; and that He works through us whenever He wishes, just as a craftsman does through his tools, and sometimes He does certain things suddenly without any intervening causes. For He can do both, even if He acts through us with some medium occurring between them, which is fitting for both God and man. And you think our words are such because they reach God through the enclosed spirit, and reach man through the perceptible air. This, it seems to me, that famous Epicurus expressed in two words following Democritus—not that I wish to follow those whom I have renounced, but because what is soaked in from tender years often offers itself to memory against our will, and nonetheless, the harmony of opinions is a footprint of the truth. For they say our voice is a *stream of reason* <note>original: "Reuma logon"; from the Greek ῥεῦμα λόγων.</note>. For when reason drips onto the tongue, the air is broken, and a word is made. Thus, reason is the fountain, whose stream is the word; in the fountain it meets with God, and in the stream and river it meets with man. And just as there is nothing in the river that was not first in the fountain, so according to Aristotle the Stagirite, there is nothing in the voice that was not first in the concept of the soul, just as letters are the signs of the voice. Concerning this, Ammonius the Peripatetic wrote thus (forgive me if I do not translate in a manner ornate or worthy of your ears):

> For he says that letters only announce, while things are only announced. But voices and mental concepts both announce and are announced. For voices announce once, but they announce in two ways (namely, first the concepts, and second the things). Conversely, concepts are announced in two ways, but they announce only once. They are announced by both voice and letters, but by the former first, and by the latter second.

This variety demonstrates to us, on the authority of both Aristotle and Ammonius, the nature of our words, insofar as they communicate partly...

He uses Platonic and Aristotelian philosophy to explain how language acts as a "stream of reason" connecting God, the human soul, and the physical world.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue regarding the power of ancient languages. The speaker (Capnion) acknowledges the Neoplatonic argument for "barbarian" (non-Greek) languages but questions if Hebrew is the *only* sacred language, citing the efficacy of Greek and Egyptian rites. # ON THE MIRACULOUS WORD

...partly with spirit, and partly with body, so that it seems to me a certain use of words is the most fitting medium for reconciling man with God. As for why you chose those <term>barbarian<note>In this context, "barbarian" refers to non-Greek and non-Latin languages, particularly ancient Near Eastern tongues, which were thought to possess inherent divine power.</note></term> words as being more closely related to divinity, I do not much object. For now, as **Iamblichus** <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 245–325 AD) who defended the use of "barbarian" names in theurgy.</note> says, the gods approved that speech of the barbarians as being most suitable for sacred rites, especially because that mode of speaking stood out as older and was the first to exist. And since the first people received names from God and mixed them with their own tongue—blending them, as it were, with a voice familiar and agreeable to those deities—they handed them down to us to be kept perpetually. Therefore, we must rightly preserve that very rule of tradition from then on. 

By these precepts, Iamblichus himself leads me toward your opinion, except that you strive to persuade us that those things which **Zoroaster** spoke of—namely, <note>original: "barbara uerba ne commutes"; a famous maxim from the Chaldean Oracles meaning "Do not change the barbarian names."</note> *that one should not change barbarian words*—must specifically be Hebrew. And indeed, **Baruchias**, you have used such genius, such abundance of speech, and so many historical testimonies that you easily move me toward agreement. Nothing would now stand in the way of my believing you entirely, were it not for the singular experience of the ancients. They are said to have performed admirable works of the <term>magi</term> not only with Egyptian and Assyrian words, but truly with Greek words, both in warding off dangers and in accelerating benefits. 

For they report that the famous **Orpheus**—that follower of the Egyptian **Mercury** <note>Referring to Hermes Trismegistus.</note>, chief of the first priesthood, theologian, and seer—when he was one of the Argonauts, averted a cruel power into which they had miserably fallen by means of his sacred rites. He was also the first to demonstrate augury, not only from birds but from other living creatures as well. Yet, he used Greek invocations, as his hymns prove, which even now survive and are sung in Greek. Furthermore, **Pythagoras** and **Plato** assert that they were taught all their sacred mysteries by the Egyptians, some from the Assyrians and **Chaldeans**, and others from various nations; it is certainly incredible what miraculous works they demonstrated to men. Therefore, sacred names belong not only to the Hebrews, but also to other nations.

### THEN BARUCHIAS SPOKE:

Sometimes, he said, we say "sacred" <note>original: "sacrum"</note> is the same as "holy" <note>original: "sanctum"</note>, namely that which is defended and fortified against the injury of men. At other times, as **Martianus** <note>A Roman jurist.</note> replied in civil law, "sacred things are those which have been consecrated publicly, not privately." Or, as **Ulpian** <note>One of the great Roman legal authorities.</note>, most skilled in the law, wrote: "that is sacred which the prince has dedicated, or to which he has given the power of dedicating." In this way, whatever we consecrate to God and withdraw from common use is called sacred, holy, and sometimes <term>sacrosanct</term>, of whatever nation or people it may be. 

Thus, that priest in the *Timaeus* <note>Plato's dialogue describing the origins of the world.</note> once told **Solon** that the deeds of many men worthy of memory are preserved in the Egyptian temples and in their sacred writings. So too, it is customary to say of the consecrated priests of every nation that they are devoted and hallowed to God, as **Virgil** sang of **Helenus**, the priest of Apollo: <note>original: "Exorat pacem diuum uittasque resoluit Sacrati capitis"; from Aeneid 3.370.</note> *“He entreats peace from the gods and unbinds the fillets of his hallowed head.”* And after this, in the eighth book of the *Aeneid*: <note>original: "At Caesar triplici inuectus Romana triumpho Moenia diis italis uotum immortale sacrabat"; from Aeneid 8.714.</note> *“But Caesar, riding into the Roman walls in a triple triumph, was consecrating an immortal vow to the Italian gods.”* 

At another time, and more correctly, we call God—the author of all things—"sacred," as the beginning and fountain of everything sacred. Consequently, through participation in Him, all things after God in which God Himself is naturally involved by His presence and power are sacred. This differs from the previous definition: for in the former, we dedicate ourselves to God, but in this latter sense, God [dedicates] Himself to us [from]...

<summary>The speaker debates whether only Hebrew names possess divine power, citing the Greek miracles of Orpheus and Pythagoras, while Baruchias begins a legal and philosophical definition of what constitutes a "sacred" thing.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from the previous page, where Baruchias begins to define the nature of "sacred" things, moving from general definitions to the specific holiness of the Hebrew language.</meta>

### BOOK TWO

...dedicated, and He illustrates our affairs with His presence. And for that reason, we rightfully call those things **sacred** in which God truly dwells. Not angels, nor spirits <note>original: "dæmones"; in this Neoplatonic context, these are intermediary spirits, not necessarily "demons" in the modern sense.</note>, but God Himself, the highest, best, and greatest. For this reason, we even call the angels themselves "sacred" or "gods," since God also dwells in them, and they are often said to bear His name. Conversely, we call the **profane** "execrable" in a similar way, those from whom God is far removed, as **Statius** <note>A Roman poet (c. 45–96 AD).</note> says: *Fraternal battle-lines and alternating wars fought with profane hatred* <note>original: "Fraternas acies alternaq; bella prophanis Decertata odiis." From the opening of the Thebaid.</note>. For according to the primary origin of the word, just as "sacred" things are dedicated by us to God, so "profane" may be interpreted as being far from the temple <note>original: "procul a phano"; a classic etymology for "profanus."</note> and from religion, living outside of sacred things. Indeed, that which is sacred arises so much from our own actions that even the king who performs the rites is called a *sacrificulus* <note>a "little sacrificer" or priest-king</note>, and the "sacrifice" is that which is made by the *sacrificulus*, and even the act itself is designated as "sacred."

**Ovid**, in the twelfth book of the *Metamorphoses*, writes: *The festival day had arrived when Achilles, victor over the swan, appeased Pallas with the blood of a slaughtered cow. When he placed its entrails upon the warm altars, and the scent, acceptable to the gods, rose into the air, the "sacred things" took their portion, and the rest was given to the tables.* <note>original: "Sacra tulere suam pars est data cætera mensis." Achilles is sacrificing to the goddess Athena (Pallas) after a battle.</note> For he means that the sacred sacrifices consumed their own portion. Therefore, I will not deny that images, characters, letters, and words—whether names or voices dedicated by us to God—are sacred, not only among the **Hebrews**, but also the **Egyptians**, the **Greeks**, and other nations; for mortals have consecrated and vowed these things to the divinity according to human desire. 

Nor can I bring a more convenient example to this business than that which is sung in the hymn to the *Lycian Venus* by **Orpheus**, that ancient priest and seer, a follower of the Egyptian **Hermes** and founder of the Pythagorean philosophy, whom you, **Sidonius**, brought up a little earlier, when he says: *For our leaders, guardians of the divine homeland, established a sacred statue near the town.* <note>original: "Nostre nãq; duces patriæ diuina tuẽtes Oppidulũ propter, sacrũ statuere colossum."</note> Although some have thought this hymn belongs to **Proclus** because he mentions his own homeland. But let us hear another song of Orpheus, beyond controversy, composed to the stars: *Therefore, with sacred voices, I now invoke the pure spirits.* <note>original: "Vocibus ergo sacris iam dæmones inuoco puros."</note>

Therefore, that which either is God, or which He alone—not men first—the best and greatest, has consecrated by His own presence and assistance and infused with His power, that alone above all others ought truly to be called **sacred** and **holy** <note>original: "sanctum"</note>; for God has sanctioned it and reserved it for His own honor, not for human allurements. Hence, we commonly speak of the "holy tongue" of the Jews, and "sacred scriptures" written by the finger of God, and "sacred names" not invented by men, but instituted by God Himself. 

If ever any other nation uses its own voices in miracles, it is proved to be <term>illusion<note>original: "præstigiũ"; refers to stage magic or demonic trickery rather than a true miracle.</note></term> rather than a miracle, and it obtains its effect not by the assistance of God, but by a **pact with demons**. How dangerous that is was discussed between us at length yesterday. In short, the distance between the signs of God and <term>sorcery<note>original: "goetia"; specifically low magic or necromancy.</note></term> or <term>poisoning<note>original: "ueneficio"; often used to mean harmful witchcraft.</note></term> is as great as the difference—to conclude in a few words—between the words of the Hebrews and the words of idolaters in sacred matters. We learned this through **Moses**, who turned his staff into a serpent by the word of God. The Egyptian evildoers, whom they called "wise men," cast similar images before the eyes of the king using the enchanted words of human discipline; but the serpent of Moses devoured them to reveal the trickery. For this reason, it seems to me that a certain noble philosopher in Rome recently proposed, not without learning, that no names in <term>lawful magic<note>original: "Magico licitoq; opere"; Reuchlin distinguishes "natural" or divine magic from forbidden sorcery.</note></term> have as much power as...

<sig>e iiii</sig>

<summary>Baruchias distinguishes between "sacred" things (dedicated by humans to God) and "holy" things (sanctified by God's own presence). He argues that while all nations have sacred rites, only the Hebrew language and names are truly holy, as evidenced by Moses' victory over the illusions of Egyptian sorcerers.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from the previous page, where Baruchias began defining the nature of the "sacred." Here, the interlocutor responds by discussing the power of ancient, "barbarous" languages (like Hebrew and Egyptian) in religious rites, before Baruchias provides a legal and theological definition of what makes a name or object "sacred."</meta>

### ON THE MIRACULOUS WORD

...partly with the spirit, partly with the body, so that you might think some use of words is a most fitting middle ground for me to reconcile man with God. But I do not strongly object to your choosing "barbarous" <note>original: "barbara"; in this context, it refers to non-Greek and non-Latin languages, specifically ancient tongues like Hebrew, Egyptian, or Chaldean, which were thought to hold more primal power.</note> words as being more closely related to divinity. For as **Iamblichus** <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 245–325 AD) who defended the use of traditional, "barbaric" names in theurgy.</note> says, the gods have approved that speech of the barbarians as being most consistent with sacred rites, especially because that manner of speaking existed as the most ancient and primary one. And since the first men received names from the divine and mixed them with their own language—tempering them with a voice that was familiar and agreeable to those [divine beings]—so they handed them down to us to be preserved forever; and we, in turn, ought to duly preserve that very rule of tradition. 

By these precepts, Iamblichus himself leads me toward your opinion, except that you strive to persuade us that those "barbarous words" about which **Zoroaster** <note>The ancient Iranian prophet, often associated in the Renaissance with the "Chaldean Oracles."</note> spoke—saying one should never change them—must be Hebrew. And indeed, **Baruchias**, you have used such great talent, such abundance of speech, and so many testimonies of historians that you easily move me toward agreement. Nothing would now stand in the way of my believing you entirely, were it not for the unique experience of the ancients, who are said to have performed admirable feats of the Magi not only with Egyptian and Assyrian words, but even with Greek ones, and to have excelled greatly in warding off dangers and hastening benefits. 

For they report that the famous **Orpheus**, a follower of the Egyptian **Mercury** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure conflated with the Egyptian god Thoth.</note> and the leader of the first priesthood, being a theologian and a prophet as well as one of the **Argonauts**, averted a cruel power into which they had wretchedly fallen by means of his sacred rites. He was also the first to demonstrate augury, not only from birds but from other living creatures as well. Yet he used Greek invocations, as his hymns prove, which still exist and are sung in Greek to this day. **Pythagoras** and **Plato**, however, assert that they were taught all their sacred rites by the Egyptians, some by the Assyrians and Chaldeans, and others by various nations—all of whom certainly showed incredible and miraculous works to men. Therefore, sacred names belong not only to the Hebrews, but also to other nations.

Then **BARUCHIAS** said: We sometimes call "sacred" the same thing as "holy"—namely, that which is defended and fortified against the injury of men. Sometimes, as **Martianus** <note>Likely referring to the Roman jurist Marcianus.</note> replied in civil law, "sacred things" are those which have been consecrated publicly, not privately. Or, as **Ulpian** <note>A celebrated Roman jurist (c. 170–223 AD).</note>, the most learned in the law, wrote: "sacred" is that which the prince has dedicated, or to which he has given the power of dedication. In this way, whatever we consecrate to God and withdraw from common use is called sacred, holy, and sometimes "sacrosanct," of whatever nation or people it may be. 

Thus, that priest in the *Timaeus* <note>A dialogue by Plato.</note> once told **Solon** that the deeds of many men worthy of memory were preserved in the temples of the Egyptians and contained in their sacred writings. Thus, it is also customary to say of the consecrated priests themselves across every nation that they are devoted and "sacrated" to God, as **Virgil** sang of **Helenus**, the priest of Apollo: *He entreats the peace of the gods,* he says, *and unbinds the fillets of his consecrated head* <note>original: "Exorat... pacē diuum uittaſq̵ reſoluit Sacrati capitis." From Virgil's Aeneid, Book 3.</note>. And after this, in the eighth book of the *Aeneid*: *But Caesar, riding into the Roman walls in a triple triumph, was consecrating an immortal vow to the Italian gods* <note>original: "At Cæſar triplici inuectus Romana triumpho Moenia diis italis uotum immortale ſacrabat." Virgil, Aeneid, Book 8.</note>. 

At other times, and more correctly, we call God—the author of all things—"sacred," as the principle and fountain of everything sacred. Then, by participation in Him, all things after God are sacred in which God Himself dwells of His own accord with His presence and power. This differs from the previous definition: for in the former, we dedicate ourselves to God; in this one, God dedicates Himself to us...

<summary>The speakers debate whether only Hebrew words are truly sacred or if Egyptian, Greek, and Chaldean terms also hold divine power. Baruchias concludes by defining "sacred" through legal and theological lenses, distinguishing between human dedication to God and God’s indwelling presence.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue where the characters discuss the nature of sacred language. Baruchias is explaining how divinity inhabits certain words and objects, distinguishing between truly sacred things—sanctified by God—and "profane" things which are distant from the divine.</meta>

## BOOK TWO

speaks, and He illuminates our affairs with His presence. And for this reason, we rightly call those things **sacred** in which God truly dwells. Not angels, not demons, but God Himself, the highest, best, and greatest. Because of this, we even call the angels themselves "holy" or "gods," since God also dwells in them, and they are often said to bear His name. Conversely, we call those things **profane** and likewise "execrable" <note>Deserving of a curse or loathing.</note> from which God is far removed, as **Statius** <note>A Roman poet (c. 45–96 AD). This quote is from the opening of his epic poem, the *Thebaid*.</note> says:

> Brotherly ranks and alternating wars fought with **profane** hatreds.
> <note>original: "Fraternas acies alternaq[ue] bella prophanis Decertata odiis."</note>

For according to the primary meaning of the word, just as "sacred" things are those dedicated by us to God, so we may interpret "**profane**" as being far from the temple <note>The Latin "prophana" literally means "before or outside the temple" (pro-fano).</note> and from religion, and those things which exist outside of sacred rites. Indeed, that which is sacred arises so much from our own actions that from "performing sacred rites" (*faciendis sacris*) we get the title of the **sacrificial king** <note>original: "sacrificulus rex"; a Roman priestly office that took over the religious duties once held by kings.</note> himself who performs them, the **sacrifice** (*sacrificium*) which is made by him, and even the object itself is called **sacred**. **Ovid** writes in the twelfth book of his *Metamorphoses*:

> The festive day had arrived when Achilles, victor over the swan,
> Appeased Pallas with the blood of a slaughtered cow,
> Whose scorched entrails he placed upon the glowing altars,
> and the scent, acceptable to the gods, penetrated the heavens;
> The **sacred rites** took their portion, the rest was given to the tables.
> <note>original: Ovid, *Metamorphoses*, 12.150-154. The passage describes a sacrificial feast after a battle.</note>

For he means that the sacred sacrifices consumed their own portion. Therefore, I will not deny that images, characters, letters, and words—whether they be names or voices dedicated by us to God—are **sacred**, not only among the Hebrews, but also the Egyptians and Greeks and other nations; for mortals have consecrated and vowed these things to divinity according to human prayer. Nor can I bring a more fitting example to this business than what is sung in the hymn to **Lycian Venus** by that ancient priest and prophet **Orpheus**—a follower of **Hermes the Egyptian** and the founder of Pythagorean philosophy, whom you, **Sidonius**, brought up a little while ago—when he says:

> For the leaders of our homeland, looking to divine things,
> Established a **sacred colossus** near the little town.

Although some have thought this hymn belongs to **Proclus** <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher (412–485 AD); there was often historical confusion between Orphic and Proclian hymns.</note> because he mentions his own homeland. But let us hear another song of Orpheus, composed to the stars, which is beyond controversy: *By these **sacred voices** I now invoke the pure spirits.* <note>original: "daemones puros." In this context, "daemons" refers to intermediary spirits or guiding divinities, not evil demons.</note> Therefore, that which is either God, or which—not men first, but He alone, the best and greatest—has consecrated by His own presence and assistance and infused with His power: that alone, above all else, ought truly to be called **sacred** and **holy** (*sanctum*), which God has sanctioned and reserved for His honor, away from human allurements. This is why we commonly speak of the "**holy tongue**" of the Jews, and of **sacred letters** written by the finger of God, and of **sacred names** not invented by men, but instituted by God Himself. 

If ever any nation uses its own voices in miracles, it is proved to be an **illusion** <note>original: "praestigium"; a trick or stage-magic.</note> rather than a miracle, and it achieves its effect not through the assistance of God but by a **pact with demons**. How dangerous that is was discussed between us at length yesterday. Ultimately, just as there is a great distance between the signs of God and **sorcery** <note>original: "goetia"; black magic or spirit conjuration.</note> or **witchcraft** <note>original: "veneficium"; literally "poisoning," often used for harmful magic.</note>, so there is as much difference (to conclude in a few words) between the words of the Hebrews in sacred rites and the words of idolaters. This we learned through **Moses**, who turned his staff into a serpent by the word of God. The Egyptian sorcerers, however, whom they called "wise men," used the incantations of human discipline to cast similar images before the eyes of the king—illusions which the serpent of Moses devoured to reveal the trickery. For this reason, a certain noble philosopher in Rome seemed to me to have proposed quite learnedly not long ago that no names in a **licit and magical work** <note>A "licit" work refers to "Natural Magic" or "Theurgy," which was considered scientifically or religiously acceptable by some Renaissance thinkers, as opposed to forbidden necromancy.</note> have equal power...

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from Book Two of Johannes Reuchlin’s "De Verbo Mirifico" (On the Wonder-Working Word). The characters—Sidonius (a pagan philosopher), Baruchias (a Jewish scholar), and Capnion (the Christian persona of Reuchlin)—are debating which languages and specific names hold the most divine power. Baruchias has been arguing for the supremacy of Hebrew, while Sidonius is now weighing the value of "barbarous" or foreign words in ritual.</meta>

-> ### ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD <-

partly with the spirit and partly with the body, so that I consider a certain use of words to be the most fitting medium between me and you for reconciling man with God. And since you have chosen those **barbarous** <note>In this context, "barbarous" does not mean "savage," but refers to languages other than Greek or Latin—specifically Hebrew, Egyptian, or Chaldean.</note> words as if they were more closely related to divinity, I do not strongly object. For as **Iamblichus** <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 245–325 AD) who defended the use of "meaningless" foreign names in prayer because they were given by the gods.</note> says, the gods have approved the speech of the barbarians as being most consistent with sacred rites, especially because that mode of speaking was the most ancient and the first to exist. And since the first peoples received their names through divine inspiration and mixed them with their own tongue—tempering them with a voice familiar and agreeable to them—they have handed them down to be preserved by us forever. Thus, we must subsequently preserve that very rule of tradition in a formal manner. 

By these precepts, Iamblichus himself leads me toward your opinion—except that you strive to persuade us that those things of which **Zoroaster** <note>The legendary Persian prophet. A famous "Chaldean Oracle" attributed to him warns: "Never change the barbarous names."</note> spoke (namely, that one should not change barbarous words) must specifically be Hebrew. And indeed, **Baruchias**, you have used such great talent, such an abundance of speech, and so many testimonies of historians that you easily move me toward agreement. Nothing would now stand in the way of my believing you entirely, were it not for the singular experience of the ancients. For they are said to have performed wondrous things using the words of the **magicians**—not only in the Egyptian and Assyrian languages, but even in Greek—and to have excelled greatly in warding off dangers and hastening successes. 

They report, for instance, that the famous **Orpheus**—a follower of the Egyptian **Mercury** <note>Referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of Hermeticism.</note>, a leader of the first priesthood, a theologian, and a prophet—was one of the **Argonauts**. They say he used his sacred rites to turn away a savage power into which they had miserably fallen, and that he was the first to demonstrate augury not only from birds, but from all other living creatures as well. Yet, he used Greek invocations, as his hymns prove, which even now survive and are sung in Greek. Furthermore, **Pythagoras** and **Plato** claim they were taught all their sacred rites by the Egyptians, and some say by the Assyrians and Chaldeans, and others from yet other sources. It is certainly incredible what miraculous works they displayed to men. Therefore, sacred names belong not only to the Hebrews, but also to other nations.

Then **BARUCHIAS** said: Sometimes we say **sacred** <term>sacrum: something set apart for a deity</term> means the same thing as **holy** <term>sanctum: something made "clean" or inviolable</term>; namely, that which is defended and fortified against the injury of men. Sometimes, as **Martianus** <note>A Roman jurist.</note> replied in civil law, "sacred things" are those which have been publicly, not privately, consecrated. Or as **Ulpian** <note>One of the great Roman legal authorities (c. 170–223 AD).</note>, the most learned in the law, wrote: "the sacred is that which the prince has dedicated, or to which he has given the power of dedicating." In this way, whatever we consecrate to God and withdraw from common use is called **sacred**, **holy**, and sometimes **sacrosanct**, no matter the nation or people it belongs to. 

In this manner, that priest in the *Timaeus* <note>A famous dialogue by Plato where an Egyptian priest speaks to the Greek statesman Solon.</note> once told **Solon** that the deeds of many men worthy of memory were preserved in the Egyptian temples and in their sacred writings. Likewise, it is customary to say of the consecrated priests of every nation that they are "devoted and hallowed to God." As **Virgil** sang of **Helenus**, the priest of **Apollo**: "He implores," he says, "the peace of the gods, and unbinds the fillets of his **sacred** head." <note>original: "Exorat pace[m] diuum uittasq[ue] resoluit Sacrati capitis." Quoted from Virgil’s Aeneid, Book 3.</note> And later in the eighth book of the *Aeneid*: "But Caesar, entering the Roman walls in triple triumph, was consecrating an immortal vow to the Italian gods." <note>original: "At Caesar triplici invectus Romana triumpho Moenia diis italis uotum immortale sacrabat."</note> 

At other times, and more correctly, we call God—the author of all things—**sacred**, as the principle and source of everything sacred. Then, by participation in Him, all things after God are "sacred" in which God Himself dwells of His own accord with His presence and power. And this differs from the former definition: for in the first case, we dedicate ourselves to God, but in this case, God [dedicates] Himself to us...

<meta>This page continues the dialogue within Book Two of Johannes Reuchlin’s "De Verbo Mirifico." Having discussed the antiquity of "barbarous" names, the speaker (likely Capnion/Reuchlin) now shifts to defining the "sacred" through the lens of divine presence. He argues that while many cultures claim sacred status for their rites, true sacredness—and true miraculous power—is reserved for that which is instituted by God Himself, specifically within the Hebrew tradition.</meta>

-> ## BOOK TWO <-

-> e iii <-

...speaks, and illuminates our affairs with His assistance. And therefore, by the best right, we call those things **sacred** in which God truly dwells. Not angels, not demons, but God Himself, the Best and Greatest. And for that reason, we also call the angels themselves "sacred," or even "gods," since God also dwells in them, whose name they are often said to bear. 

Conversely, in the same way, we call those things **profane** and **cursed** from whom God is far removed—as **Statius** <note>original: "Fraternas acies alternaqꝫ bella prophanis Decertata odiis." From the *Thebaid*, an epic poem about the conflict between the sons of Oedipus.</note> says: *“Brotherly battle-lines and alternating wars fought with profane hatreds.”* For according to the primary etymology of the word, just as sacred things are dedicated to God by us, so "profane" may be interpreted as being "far from the temple" <term>phano: the Latin word for temple or sanctuary</term> and from religion, and those things which exist outside of sacred matters. 

Indeed, that "sacred" status arises from our own actions to such an extent that from the "performing of sacred rites" (*faciendis sacris*) the title of **sacrificial king** is given to the one who performs them, and the **sacrifice** is named for what the sacrificial king does, and the object itself is also denominated as "sacred." As **Ovid** says in the 12th book of the *Metamorphoses*: 

*“The festive day had arrived when Achilles, victor over the swan, was appeasing Pallas with the blood of a slaughtered cow. When he placed the cut portions upon the hot altars, and the scent, acceptable to the gods, penetrated into the upper air, the sacred portions took their share, and the rest was given to the tables.”* <note>original: "Festa dies aderat q̃ cygni uictor Achilles Pallada mactatæ placabat sanguine uaccæ..." Reuchlin uses this classical example to show how the term "sacra" (sacred things) traditionally referred to the portions of a sacrifice reserved for the gods.</note>

For he means that the "sacred" sacrifices consumed their own portion. Therefore, I will not deny that images, characters, letters, and words—whether they be names or voices—dedicated to God by us are "sacred." This is true not only among the **Hebrews**, but also among the **Egyptians** and **Greeks** and other nations, for mortals have consecrated and vowed these things to the divinity according to human desire. 

Nor can I bring a more convenient example to this matter than that which is sung in the hymn to the Lycian Venus by that ancient priest and prophet **Orpheus**—a follower of **Hermes the Egyptian** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure associated with wisdom and magic.</note> and the founder of **Pythagorean philosophy**, whom you, Sidonius, brought up a little earlier—when he says: *“For the leaders of our fatherland, guardians of the divine, established a sacred colossus near the town.”* Although some have thought this hymn belongs to **Proclus** <note>A late Neoplatonist philosopher (412–485 AD).</note>, because he mentions his own fatherland. But let us hear another poem of Orpheus, composed to the stars, which is beyond controversy: *“With sacred voices, therefore, I now invoke the pure spirits.”* <note>original: "Vocibus ergo sacris iam dæmones inuoco puros." Here, "demons" refers to the Neoplatonic concept of intermediary spirits, not necessarily evil beings.</note>

Therefore, that which is either God Himself, or which God alone, the Best and Greatest—and not humans first—has consecrated by His presence and assistance and into which He has infused His power: that one thing, above all others, truly ought to be called **sacred** and **holy** <term>sanctum: that which is made "inviolable" or "sanctioned" by divine law</term>, because God has sanctioned it and reserved it for His own honor rather than for human allurements. 

From this, we commonly speak of the **holy language** of the Jews, and **sacred letters** written by the finger of God, and **sacred names** not invented by men, but instituted by God Himself. If ever any nation uses its own voices in "miracles," it is proved to be a **trick** <term>prestigium: an illusion or deceptive magic</term> rather than a miracle; its effect is obtained not by the assistance of God, but by a pact with demons. How dangerous that is was discussed between us at length yesterday. 

In short, there is a vast distance between the signs of God and **goetia** <term>goetia: low magic or sorcery, often involving the summoning of spirits</term> or witchcraft. I will conclude in a few words: in sacred matters, there is as much difference between the words of the Hebrews and the words of idolaters as we were taught through **Moses**, who turned his staff into a serpent by the word of God. The Egyptian sorcerers, however, whom they called "wise men," used the incanted words of human discipline to cast similar images before the royal gaze—illusions which the Mosaic serpent devoured to reveal their trickery. For this reason, a certain noble philosopher in Rome <note>This refers to Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494), who argued in his "900 Theses" that no name has as much power in magic as Hebrew names.</note> seemed to me not unlearned when he recently proposed that no names in a magical and lawful work have equal power...

<summary>Reuchlin defines "sacred" as that which contains the presence of God, distinguishing true divine miracles (associated with the Hebrew language) from the deceptive "tricks" of pagan sorcery. He cites Moses's victory over the Egyptian magicians as the ultimate proof that Hebrew names and letters carry a unique, divinely sanctioned power.</summary>

--- Page 77 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue in Book Two of "De Verbo Mirifico." Capnion (Reuchlin's persona) is discussing the unique power of the Hebrew language. He argues that Hebrew words possess a "mirific" (miracle-working) quality because they were the first sounds formed by the divine voice, a power that even pagan philosophers and the Gospel writers recognized by preserving specific Hebrew terms in their works.</meta>

# ON THE MIRACULOUS WORD

...possess, just like Hebrew or those things most closely derived from it, because these were the first of all to be formed by the voice of God. But that in which nature exercises magic most powerfully is the voice of God. Now, Sidonius, I will no longer allow you to hesitate on what is rightly manifest. For I believe many nations mixed Hebrew into their secret prayers or hidden operations for no other reason than to obtain their wishes more certainly. I think this is beyond doubt regarding Orpheus, Pythagoras, and Plato; nothing of their secrets was so carefully hidden or so secluded as the Hebrew names of divine power. They did not wish to reveal these to anyone in writing, as Plato himself suggests to **Dionysius of Syracuse** <note>original: "Dionysio, Syracusano de seipso per epistolã insinuat." Refers to Plato's *Second Letter*, where he speaks cryptically about the "nature of the first" to avoid being understood by the uninitiated.</note> regarding himself in a letter. 

I could ask you, Capnion, to explain the reason why the Evangelists and Apostles, while they translated the other works of Christ into the Greek language, did not also translate those few words which the worker of miracles himself is recorded to have used. Instead, they left them entirely unchanged and untouched, inserting "barbarous" <term>barbara uerba: In a Renaissance context, this refers to non-Greek or non-Latin words, specifically Hebrew or Aramaic</term> words into the Greek text. For instance, when the children cried out to Christ **Hosthiana** <note>original: "Hosthiana, id est salua domine." A philological reconstruction of the Hebrew *Hôšîʿâ-nā*, commonly rendered as *Hosanna*.</note>, which means "Save, O Lord," the word was later corrupted by the lack of skill of scribes and is now incorrectly said as **Hosanna**. Luke, being more skilled in the Greek language and realizing this would happen—knowing he was writing a Gospel for Greeks who could not pronounce this expression according to its proper linguistic character—judged it better to remain silent rather than offer the word to be mocked by those who would corrupt it. Yet the other three Evangelists, fearing nothing of the sort, decided by no means to omit *Hosthiana* because of that remarkable particle **na**, which they say is one of the divine names.

Likewise, **Mark**, in that excellent miracle which Christ performed in raising the daughter of **Jairus**, did not hesitate to write down the very Hebrew words of Christ by which the dead girl was restored to life and light. For taking the hand of the girl lying in the tomb, he said in two words: **Thabiti, Kumi** <note>original: "Thabiti, Kumi." Standard biblical versions read *Talitha koum*. Reuchlin provides a unique transliteration and interpretation here.</note>. The first of these pertains to life, meaning "Look back," and the second pertains to the recovery of health, meaning "Rise." Later, some insufficiently diligent transcriber dared to mix into the fabric of the narrative what he had found in the margin of the history, so that now almost all books hold it as: "Talitha Kumi, which is interpreted: Girl, I say to you, arise." For it is not to be believed that the Evangelist translated what is not actually present in the original words. Therefore, "Girl, I say to you"—all of this has no place here. 

And I greatly wonder at certain men of no small fame and name among you in the study of all sciences, whom you hold in high esteem, who say in this passage that **Thabita** was the name of the maiden. It is as if one should think that Christ—whom I, however much I have been a Jew, have always held greater than **Apollonius of Tyana** <note>A 1st-century philosopher and reputed miracle-worker often cited by pagans as a rival to Jesus.</note> and any of the philosophers—would act such. But now, taught by you, I affirm He is even more excellent than the angels and all gods, superior not only to men, and I believe Him to be and to have been God. Therefore, it is as if one should think He wanted first to search for a name with which to chant a secret incantation, just as all the magicians agree regarding Apollonius. For when he had escaped from the hands of **Tigillinus** (or more truly, **Nero**), he touched a girl encountered on the road during a wedding who was thought to be dead; and first, he diligently asked for the name of the maiden. Having received it, he is said to have whispered something secretly by which the girl revived. The Romans also once observed this custom: whenever they besieged a city, they sought its name...

<summary>Reuchlin argues that Hebrew is the foundational language of divine power, noting that even the Gospel writers preserved specific Hebrew words like "Hosanna" and "Talitha Cumi" for their inherent efficacy. He contrasts Christ's divine authority with the "magical" methods of Apollonius of Tyana, who required a person's name to perform a ritual.</summary>

## BOOK TWO

...accurately sought the [true name] of the city. And recognizing this, they were accustomed to call out all its protecting gods with a certain song <note>original: "quodā carmine," referring to the ritual of *evocatio*, where Romans invited the enemy's gods to leave their city and come to Rome.</note> and finally to conquer them. For this very reason, it happened that no one dared to betray the proper name of the city of Rome, lest they should suffer a similar fate from their enemies. From this we know that the proper names of things are of no small benefit to incantations—far be it from him who utterly removed all worship of demons, lest he should desire to recognize the name of the girl in this work, whom he had never seen before. But the words **Thabiti Kumi** <note>In the Gospel of Mark 5:41, this is usually rendered as *Talitha cumi*. Reuchlin uses a different transliteration to argue for a specific Hebrew root meaning "to look."</note> are the words of Christ, which are interpreted in English as: 

->**Look, Arise.**<-

Look, that you may live. Arise, so that everyone may see you whole with their eyes, and confess that you have been liberated by my command not only from death but also from every sickness. But I preach to you in vain these things which you yourselves have often read among our people by investigating the scriptures. For instance, in the thirteenth Psalm, which begins:
> **How long, O Lord.** <note>Psalm 13:1</note>
In the verse:
> **Look upon me and hear me, O Lord my God.** <note>Psalm 13:3</note>
And in the twenty-second Psalm, which begins:
> **My God, my God.** <note>Psalm 22:1</note>
In the verse:
> **I will count all my bones; they have looked and seen me.** <note>Psalm 22:17</note>
And in the thirty-third Psalm:
> **Rejoice in the Lord, O you righteous.** <note>Psalm 33:1</note>
In the verse:
> **The Lord looked down from heaven.** <note>Psalm 33:13</note>
And in the following Psalm, in the verse:
> **Look to him and be enlightened.** <note>Psalm 34:5</note>
Where your translation <note>The Latin Vulgate</note> has it thus:
> **Draw near to him and be enlightened.**
And in the songs of praise which you chant daily at the hour of Prime. In the section for the letter Gimel:
> **Open my eyes and I will consider.** <note>Psalm 119:18</note>
Where it ought more truly to be placed:
> **I shall look upon the wonders of your law.**
For it is this same word <term>Abita</term> <note>From the Hebrew root *NBT*, meaning to look or behold.</note> about which we must now discuss, since in the second person it becomes **Thabita** or **Thabiti**, that is, "you shall look" or "look." For just as the Latins are often accustomed to use the future indicative for the imperative—as in:
> **In alternating song you shall speak; the Muses love alternating song,** <note>A quote from Virgil's *Eclogues* 3.59, where "you shall speak" (*dicetis*) is used as a command "speak" (*dicite*).</note>
—so also among the Hebrews the same usage is found most frequently, as here: "you shall look" for "look." But **Kumi** is an imperative of the feminine gender on its own, as in the second chapter of the Song of Songs:
> **Behold, my beloved speaks to me: Arise, make haste, my love.** <note>Song of Solomon 2:10</note>
Where the word **Kumi** is placed, meaning "arise." You will find such things corrupted in many places of Holy Scripture by the unlearned, while in this age theologians are more accustomed to notice the dialectic sophistries of **Aristotle** than the words of divine inspiration and the Holy Spirit. Hence, through the zeal for human invention, the celestial tradition itself is neglected, and the talkativeness of men extinguishes the word of God. 

For a similar error of the copyists is caught in the Acts of the Apostles, which has begun to be common among you, even among the learned and indeed the teachers, regarding the raising of **Dorcas** from the family of the saints in Joppa—which is a <term>toparchia</term> <note>A Greek term for a small administrative district or province.</note> of Judea, as **Pliny** says, very near to Lydda. For there they do not write the name of that same little woman correctly, as I have often found it unlearnedly written in both Greek and Latin manuscripts. By this example, I would believe they also failed in the aforementioned Gospel. But the proper name of the faithful disciple was **Tabia**, without any aspiration and without a "t" in the final syllable; her interpretation as **Dorcas** teaches us this. **Tabia** is an Assyrian name <note>Aramaic</note> which signifies a "Gazelle," just as we read in the Chaldean book called **Illen Pithgamai** <note>The *Targum Jonathan*, an Aramaic paraphrase of the Hebrew Bible; the title means "These are the words."</note>. Furthermore, that word now comes to mind which Christ is written to have said in Mark. For they brought to him a man who was deaf and mute, and they prayed that he would lay his hand upon him. And taking him aside from the crowd, he put his fingers into...

<meta>This page continues the dialogue in Book Two of "De Verbo Mirifico." Capnion (Reuchlin’s persona) is explaining that Hebrew words possess a unique power because they were formed by the voice of God. ### OF THE MIRACULOUS WORD

...to have [divine power], just as Hebrew words or those derived most closely from them do, because these were the very first words formed by the voice of God. But that in which nature exerts its magic most powerfully is the voice of God. Now, Sidonius, I will not allow you to hesitate any longer in that which is rightly obvious. For I believe that many nations have mixed Hebrew into their secret prayers or hidden operations for no other reason than to more certainly obtain what they desired. I think this is also beyond doubt regarding Orpheus, Pythagoras, and Plato; nothing else in their secret lore was so carefully hidden or so secluded as the Hebrew names of divine power, which they did not wish to be made known to anyone in writing, just as the same Plato suggests to Dionysius of Syracuse in a letter concerning himself.

I could ask you, Capnion, to explain the reason why both the Evangelists and Apostles, when they translated the rest of Christ's works into the Greek tongue, did not also translate those few words which the worker of miracles himself is recorded to have used? Instead, they left them entirely unchanged and untouched, inserting "barbarous" <note>In the Renaissance, "barbarous" was a technical term for any language other than Greek or Latin—here specifically referring to Hebrew and Aramaic.</note> words into the Greek. For instance, when the children shouted to Christ **"Hosanna"** <note>original: "hoſthiana," which Reuchlin reconstructs from Hebrew to mean "Save, Lord."</note>, that is, **"Save, Lord."** Later, through the lack of skill of scribes, that word was corrupted and is now used improperly as **"Hosanna."**

Luke, being more skilled in the Greek language and seeing that this corruption would happen, knew he was writing his Gospel for Greeks who would be unable to pronounce this word according to its proper linguistic character; he judged it better to remain silent rather than offer the word to be mocked by those who would corrupt it. For the other three Evangelists, fearing nothing of the sort, decided by no means to omit **"Hosanna"** because of that remarkable particle, **na**, which they say is one of the divine names.

Likewise Mark, in that excellent miracle which Christ performed in raising the daughter of Jairus, did not hesitate to add the very Hebrew words of Christ by which the dead girl was restored to the light of life. For taking the hand of the girl lying in the tomb, he spoke in two words: **Thabiti, Kumi.** Of these, the first refers to life, that is, **"Look"** <note>original: "Reſpice." Reuchlin argues that "Thabiti" comes from a Hebrew root meaning "to look or behold," rather than the usual "Talitha" meaning "girl."</note>; the second refers to recovering health, that is, **"Arise."**

Later, some insufficiently diligent transcriber dared to mix into the text of the narrative what he had found in the margin of the history, so that now almost all books hold **"Thabita Kumi,"** <note>Commonly rendered in Bibles as "Talitha cumi."</note> which is interpreted as, **"Girl, I say to you, arise."** For it is not to be believed that the Evangelist translated what is not actually present in the truth [of the original words]. Therefore, **"Girl, I say to you"**—all of this has nothing to do with the matter here. 

And I am quite amazed at certain men of no small fame among you in the learning of all sciences, whom you hold in high esteem, who say in this place that **"Thabita"** is the name of the girl. It is as if one should think that Christ—whom I have always, as much as a Jew can, held as greater than Apollonius of Tyana <note>A 1st-century philosopher and miracle-worker often compared to Jesus in antiquity.</note> and any other philosophers—but now, taught by you, I affirm him to be even more excellent than the angels and all gods, not only superior to men, and I believe he is and was God. 

So, it is as if one should think Christ wished first to seek out the girl's name so that he might chant a secret song <note>term: a magical incantation or charm</note>, just as all the magicians agree regarding Apollonius. When Apollonius had escaped from the hands of Tigellinus, or more truly Nero <note>The Roman Emperor Nero; Tigellinus was the prefect of the Praetorian Guard.</note>, he touched a girl on the road who was thought to have died during her wedding, and first he diligently asked for the girl's name. Having received it, he is said to have pronounced something secretly by which the girl revived. And this was once observed as a custom among the Romans: whenever they besieged a city, they would [seek] its name...

<summary>Reuchlin argues that Hebrew words in the Gospels, like "Hosanna" and "Thabiti Kumi," are not merely linguistic leftovers but essential divine names and commands. He critiques common translations and compares Christ's miracles to the legendary deeds of Apollonius of Tyana to highlight Christ's superior divine nature.</summary>

# BOOK TWO

...of the city they would accurately seek. Knowing this, they were accustomed to summon <note>original: "euocare," a ritual practice of calling out the gods of an enemy city</note> all its protecting gods with a certain chant, and finally to conquer them. For this very reason, it was the case that no one dared to reveal the proper name of the city of Rome, lest they suffer the same from their enemies. From this we know that the proper names of things are of no small use in incantations. Far be such a thing from him who fundamentally destroyed all worship of demons, yet he did not even wish to recognize the girl's name in this work <note>Referring to the raising of Jairus' daughter</note>, whom he had never seen before. But the words **"Thabiti Kumi"** are the words of Christ, which are interpreted in Latin as **"Look, Rise."** **"Look,"** that you may be seen as living. **"Rise,"** so that all may see you healthy with their own eyes—that they might confess you have been freed from not only death but from all illness by my command.

But I preach to you in vain what you yourselves have often read among our people while investigating the scriptures. Just as in the thirteenth Psalm <note>Psalm 13 in modern numbering</note>, whose beginning is, **"How long, O Lord?"** In the verse, **"Look, hear me, O Lord my God."** <note>original: "Reſpice exaudi me"</note> And in the twenty-second Psalm, which begins, **"My God, my God."** In the verse, **"I will count all my bones; they themselves looked and saw me."** <note>original: "ipſi reſpexerunt uiderunt in me"</note> And in the thirty-third Psalm, **"Rejoice, O you righteous."** In the verse, **"From heaven the Lord looked down."** <note>original: "De cœlo reſpexit dominus"</note> And in the following Psalm <note>Psalm 34</note>, in the verse, **"Look toward him and be enlightened."** <note>original: "Reſpicite ad eũ & illuminamini"</note> Where your translation <note>the Latin Vulgate</note> has it thus: **"Come to him and be enlightened."** And in the praises <note>the Divine Office</note> which you sing daily at the first hour. In the section for the Hebrew letter **Gimel** <note>Psalm 119:18</note>: **"Open my eyes and I shall consider."** Where it ought more truly to be placed: **"I shall look upon the wonders of your law."** <note>original: "Reſpiciã mirabilia de lege tua"</note>

For this is the very same word **"Abita,"** <note>Reuchlin connects the girl's name to the Hebrew root 'nabat', meaning to behold or look</note> which we must now discuss, since in the second person it becomes **"Thabita"** or **"Thabiti,"** that is, **"you shall look"** or **"look."** For as the Latins are often accustomed to use the future indicative in place of the imperative—as in "you shall speak by turns, the Muses love alternating songs," <note>A quote from Virgil’s Eclogues</note> instead of "speak!"—so also among the Hebrews the same is found in very frequent use, as here: **"you shall look"** for **"look!"** But **"Kumi"** is in itself an imperative of the feminine gender, as in the second chapter of the Song of Songs: **"Behold my beloved speaks to me: Arise, hurry, my friend,"** where **"Kumi"** is placed, meaning **"rise."**

You will find such things corrupted in many places of the sacred scripture by the unlearned, for in this age theologians are more accustomed to attend to the dialectical sophistries of **Aristotle** than to the words of divine inspiration and the Holy Spirit. Hence, through the pursuit of human invention, celestial tradition itself is neglected, and the talkativeness of men extinguishes the word of God. A similar error of the scribes is detected in the Acts of the Apostles, which has begun to be common among you even for the learned—and indeed for the teachers—concerning the raising of **Dorcas** <note>Tabitha</note> from the family of the saints in **Joppa**, which is a district of Judea near Lydda, as **Pliny** says. For there they do not correctly write the name of that little woman, as I have often found it ignorantly written in both Greek and Latin manuscripts. By this example, I would believe they also failed in the aforementioned Gospel. But the proper name of the faithful disciple was **Tabia**, without any aspiration and without a "t" in the final syllable; its interpretation as **"Dorcas"** <note>Greek for Gazelle</note> teaches us this. And **Tabia** is an Assyrian name meaning **"Gazelle,"** just as we read in the Chaldean book <note>The Aramaic Targum</note> called **"Illen Pithgamai."** <note>Aramaic for "These are the words"</note> Furthermore, that word now comes to mind which Christ is written to have said in the Gospel of **Mark**. For they brought to him a man deaf and mute, and they prayed that he would lay his hand upon him. And taking him aside from the crowd, he put his fingers into...

# ON THE MIRACULOUS WORD

...his ears, and spitting he touched his tongue and looking up into heaven he groaned and said, **Hiphathah**, <note>A variation of "Ephphatha" from Mark 7:34</note> which is **"be opened,"** which ought to be written at the beginning with a smooth breathing <note>original: "psilen," referring to the Greek grammatical mark for a soft 'h' sound</note> and at the end with a stronger aspiration. For it is the passive imperative of the active verb **Pathah**, which means **"he opened,"** and in the future tense is **Ephathah**, that is, **"I shall open,"** as in the 78th Psalm: <note>Psalm 78:2</note> **"I will open my mouth in parables,"** and in the fiftieth Psalm: <note>Psalm 51:15, or Psalm 50 in the Vulgate numbering</note> **"O Lord, you will open my lips,"** and so on for the others. For you, who are well-versed and learned not only in Greek but also in Hebrew literature, know how to form the masculine singular imperative **Hiphathah**—meaning **"be opened"** or **"be thou opened"**—from the passive **Niphathah**, which means **"it is opened,"** just as the powerful word of Christ thundered it. 

Let us come to his most surpassing death; hanging on the wood and already dying, he said that which is found in the twenty-second Psalm according to the Hebrews, but in the twenty-first among you: <note>Reuchlin notes the difference between the Hebrew numbering and the Latin Vulgate numbering of the Psalms</note> **Eli Eli lamah asabathani** <note>"My God, my God, why have you forsaken me?"</note>. For Matthew recites it just as the matter stands, although **Sabathani** is also said corruptly through the lack of the first syllable which ought to be noted by the letter *Ayin*. <note>term: Ayin (a Hebrew letter representing a guttural sound often missed by non-native speakers)</note> However, your scholars observe that Mark is even less corrected in this passage, for he puts **Elohe**, or according to others **Elohi**, less appropriately. For **Elohe** is **"God,"** but **Elohi** is not read by the Hebrews. It could indeed be designated as **"my God"** by **Elohai**, but the words of the prophet are not to be changed. I believe he <note>Christ</note> remembered God while dying and began the Psalter from the beginning of his passion until he departed from life. 

Finally, in the last gasp of his passing, he arrived at the Psalm which is the thirtieth in the order of the Seventy Interpreters, <note>The Septuagint/Vulgate Psalm 30, which is Psalm 31 in modern Bibles</note> so that both the beginning and the end of the passion might be concluded within a single number. For just as the high priests promised thirty pieces of silver to the traitor Judas before Christ was captured, so he himself did not so much promise as pay thirty Psalms to God the Father before he died. You will grant me pardon if it is not permitted to compare evil things with good, but nevertheless consider and recount within your minds how appropriately that blessed man—if it is right to call him a man—completed his entire condemnation and torment with these thirty Psalms. 

He took his beginning from the time of the anxiety endured in the garden across the torrent, of which the entire fifty-fourth Psalm <note>Psalm 55 in modern Bibles</note> makes mention. There it is written in the manner of a prophecy: **"But I will call upon God, and the Lord will save me; evening and morning and at noon I will speak and declare."** <note>Psalm 55:16-17</note> Therefore, he spoke with God during the whole time of his passion, in the evening, the morning, and at noon. Often it was in secret, which was predicted earlier through David: **"My prayer shall be turned into my own bosom."** <note>Psalm 35:13</note> Nevertheless, he sometimes openly pronounced certain parts of those same songs; some of these his followers, hearing from nearby, committed to memory, while others perhaps, being absent, did not hear. Of those things perceived, therefore, we can judge that it was he who said, **"My God, my God, why have you forsaken me?"** and **"Into thy hands I commend my spirit."** <note>Psalm 31:5</note> The title of this latter Psalm refers to an "ecstasy," which most suits one who is dying. But this title is not found among the Hebrews. 

Nor indeed is it lacking in divine mystery that immediately after this same thirtieth Psalm, and not before, there follows the inscription **Maskil**, <note>term: Maskil (a Hebrew term used in Psalm titles, usually meaning a "mask" or "instruction/enlightenment")</note> that is, **understanding**, **knowledge**, or **instruction**. For after the death of Christ, whose final prayer was this thirtieth Psalm, we finally began to understand and see the one whom they pierced and through whom they were transfixed; wherefore he prayed with a special vow that his father [would forgive those] cruci-

<meta>catchword: -fixers (figenti)</meta>

## BOOK TWO

...forgive those who were crucifying him, since they did not know what they were doing. <note>original: "figentibus ignosceret, quoniam nescirent quid agerent," referring to Luke 23:34</note> But while I am laboring to explain those Hebrew words which the Evangelists used firmly while writing, I have somehow slipped into the secret coverings of your religion. This is so you may realize that I have been turned toward your profession by some prior meditation and not so suddenly. Then **SIDONIUS** said: "My dear Capnion, <note>Capnion is the Greek version of Reuchlin's name, meaning "little smoke"</note> who is so skilled in these divine letters, what then shall I bring to our conversation? If I remember correctly, Baruchias has provided me much peace of mind, so that I am not forced to discuss sacred names too deeply. For he teaches us that only Hebrew is counted among the truly sacred languages. He tries to affirm this both from its antiquity and its conversation with the divine, as well as from a certain close covenant between God and the Jews, and because the language is more solid, using many arguments. If these things are true, I—who have spent more time than others on Greek and Latin—will be happy to have found the time simply to listen." 

And **CAPNION** said: "Indeed, I would marvel at his great knowledge of the Gospels, if he had not confessed much earlier that he had spent no small amount of labor on them, as the matter shows and the work indicates. But let us hear him while there is still light. For the day has already passed midday. Come again, Baruchias, do not shrink from bringing forth those things you have received concerning the secret words—hidden, as you said, almost 'under a bushel.' <note>original: "sub modio," a reference to Matthew 5:15</note> We both pray and urge you—and certainly your tongue (let Sidonius say what he likes) delights us greatly, as we are both your students." 

But since you command it so, **BARUCHIAS** said: "I must comply, provided you do not despise the simplicity of my speech, for I am not imbued with any elegances of style. And now, since we are heading toward evening, I will no longer urge you, Capnion, to give the reason why those writing the life and teaching of Christ in the Greek tongue thought it worthy to interweave Hebrew words. After all, you have all become accustomed to concluding every prayer, whether public or private, with a single word: **Amen**, which is also found in the writings of Paul. 

To this **CAPNION** replied briefly: "I will explain so that you do not remain stuck. It is not entirely safe in such an excellent matter for me to use my own invention, so I shall cite Origen, the light of our theologians, of whom it is said: 'where he is good, no one is better.' For he says in his book *Against Celsus* <note>A famous 3rd-century work of Christian apologetics</note> that a wonderful power lies hidden in certain sacred names. Therefore, these names must not be transferred from the Hebrew language into another, but must be preserved in their very own characters. <note>term: "characters" (characteribus) — here referring to the specific shapes and sounds of the Hebrew letters</note> You have what you have long wished to draw out: namely, that there is a marvelous power in divine names and prayers, because of which both the forms of the characters and the words themselves remain incorrupt. Otherwise, just as when a man's physical frame is destroyed, the spirit enclosed in the body and all its power likewise vanishes. Furthermore, as Iamblichus <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher</note> responded to Porphyry: if those names had been established by the agreement and covenant of men, it would make no difference to exchange some for others. But we understood long ago, Baruchias, and we confess it to be true, that they were received from the divine, not produced by human effort. To which that noble follower of Pythagoras, Iamblichus, adds: 'For names do not preserve the exact same meaning when translated into another language; rather, there are certain properties unique to each nation which cannot be expressed to other nations through the voice.' Added to this is the fact that if the properties of meanings..."

<summary>Capnion explains that Hebrew is a uniquely sacred language whose words and letters contain divine power. Citing Origen and Iamblichus, he argues that translating these sacred names into other languages causes their spiritual "virtue" or power to evaporate.</summary>
