Capnion (Reuchlin) is currently explaining the linguistic and mystical significance of the specific Hebrew and Aramaic words spoken by Jesus in the Gospels, arguing that the original sounds hold divine power.</meta>

# ON THE MIRACULOUS WORD

...his ears, and spitting, he touched his tongue; and looking up to heaven, he groaned and said, *Hiphathah* <note>original: "Hiphathah," the Aramaic word usually rendered as "Ephphatha" in Mark 7:34</note>, which means "be opened." This word should be written with a smooth breathing 'h' at the beginning and a stronger aspiration at the end. For it is the passive imperative of the active verb *Pathah*, which means "he opened," and in the future tense is *Ephathah*, meaning "I shall open." We see this in Psalm 78 in the verse, "I will open my mouth in parables," and in Psalm 50 in the verse, "Lord, you will open my lips," and so on in other places. For you, who are well-versed and learned not only in Greek but also in Hebrew literature, know how to form the masculine singular imperative *Hiphathah*—meaning "be opened" or "be thou opened"—from the passive *Niphathah*, which means "it is opened." This was the powerful word that Christ thundered forth.

Let us come now to his most exalted death. Hanging on the wood and already at the point of death, he said that which is placed in the twenty-second Psalm according to the Hebrews, but in the twenty-first among you: *Eli Eli lamah asabathani* <note>original: "Eli Eli lamah asabathani," Christ's cry from the cross: "My God, my God, why have you forsaken me?" Reuchlin is noting the difference between the Hebrew numbering of Psalms and the Latin Vulgate numbering.</note>. Matthew recites it in this way as the matter stands, although *Sabathani* is also said corruptly by the omission of the first syllable, which ought to be marked by the letter *ayin* <note>The Hebrew letter Ayin (ע) often represents a guttural sound that Latin and Greek speakers found difficult to pronounce or transcribe.</note>. However, your people observe that Mark is even a bit more sluggishly corrected in this passage, for he puts *Elohe*, or according to others *Elohi*, which is less appropriate. For *Elohe* is "God," but *Elohi* is not read by the Hebrews. It could indeed be designated as "my God" through *Elohai*, but the words of the prophet must not be changed. I believe the dying God remembered these words and began the Psalter from the start of his Passion until he departed from life. 

Finally, in the last gasp of his passing, he reached the Psalm which is the thirtieth in the order of the Seventy Interpreters <note>The "Seventy Interpreters" refers to the Septuagint, the ancient Greek translation of the Old Testament. The Psalm mentioned is Psalm 31 in modern Bibles (30 in the Vulgate), containing the phrase "Into your hands I commend my spirit."</note>, so that both the beginning and the end of the Passion might be concluded by a single number. For just as the high priests promised thirty pieces of silver to the traitor Judas before Christ was taken, so he himself did not so much promise as "pay" thirty Psalms to God the Father before he died. 

You will forgive me if it is not permitted to compare the wicked with the good; nevertheless, consider and recount in your minds how appropriately this blessed man—if it is right to call him a man—completed his entire condemnation and torture with these thirty Psalms. He began from the time of the agony he suffered in the garden across the brook. The entire fifty-fourth Psalm <note>Psalm 55 in modern numbering.</note> makes mention of this, where it is written in the manner of a prophecy: "But I will call upon God, and the Lord will save me. In the evening, and morning, and at noon I will speak and declare." Thus, he spoke with God throughout the entire time of his passion—evening, morning, and noon. More often he spoke secretly, as he predicted earlier through David: "My prayer shall be turned into my own bosom." 

Yet sometimes he openly pronounced certain things from these same songs, which his followers, hearing from nearby, committed to memory, while those who were absent perhaps did not hear. Of the things perceived, therefore, we can judge that he said: *Eli Eli lamah asabathani*, and "Into your hands I commend my spirit." The title of this Psalm is held by some to be "for the ecstasy," which most suits a dying man, but it is not found among the Hebrews. Nor is it indeed without a divine mystery that immediately after this thirtieth Psalm, and not before it, there follows the inscription *Maskil*, which means "understanding," "knowledge," or "instruction." For after the death of Christ, of which this thirtieth Psalm was the final prayer, we finally began to understand and to see him whom they pierced and through whom they struck <note>A reference to Zechariah 12:10, a prophecy traditionally associated with the crucifixion.</note>; for which reason he prayed with a special vow that the Father might forgive those who were...

<meta>catchword: ...nailing him</meta>

<summary>Reuchlin analyzes the Hebrew and Aramaic words spoken by Jesus (Ephphatha and the Cry from the Cross) to demonstrate their linguistic precision and mystical significance. He proposes a symbolic theory that Christ recited thirty Psalms during his Passion to correspond with the thirty pieces of silver paid to Judas.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue in Book Two of Johannes Reuchlin’s *On the Art of the Kabbalah* (De Arte Cabalistica). The speakers are Capnion (the persona of Reuchlin himself), Sidonius (a philosopher representing the Greco-Roman tradition), and Baruchias (a Jewish scholar). They are discussing the linguistic and mystical significance of Hebrew as the primordial language of God.</meta>

### BOOK TWO

...he would forgive those who pierced him, since they knew not what they did <note>A reference to Jesus’s prayer on the cross in Luke 23:34</note>. But while I am attempting to clarify those Hebrew words which the Evangelists used without change while writing, I have somehow slipped into the secret coverings of your religion, so that you may recognize that I have turned toward your profession <note>Original: *professionem*, referring here to the study of Jewish wisdom or the Jewish faith</note> by some previous meditation and not so suddenly.

Then **SIDONIUS** said: "My Capnion, who is so skilled in divine letters, what then shall I contribute to our conversation? But if I remember correctly, Baruchias has provided me much peace, so that I am not forced to discuss sacred names at length. For he teaches us that only Hebrew is numbered among the truly sacred languages. He attempts to affirm this based on its antiquity, its use in divine conversation, and a certain close covenant between God and the Jews—and finally by many arguments that the speech is more solid <note>Original: *solidior*, implying the language has a more substantial or essential connection to reality</note>. If these things are true, I, who have spent more time than others on Greek and Latin, will be happy simply to find the time for listening."

And **CAPNION** said: "Indeed, I would marvel at his great knowledge of the Gospels, had he not confessed much earlier that he had spent no small effort on them—a fact which the matter itself shows and the work indicates. But let us hear him while it is light, for the day has passed midday. Come again, Baruchias, do not shrink from bringing forth those things you have received concerning the secrets and words hidden, as you said, 'under a bushel' <note>A biblical metaphor (Matthew 5:15) referring to knowledge that is kept concealed rather than shared</note>. We both pray and urge you, for your tongue—let Sidonius say what he likes—delights us greatly, and we are both your students."

But since you so command, **BARUCHIAS** said: "I must comply, provided you do not despise the simplicity of my speech, for I am imbued with no elegances. And now, since we are heading toward evening, I will no longer urge you, Capnion, to give a reason why those writing the life and teaching of Christ in the Greek tongue also deigned to intersperse his own Hebrew words. After all, you have already become accustomed to concluding every prayer, whether public or private, with a single word: *Amen*, which is also found in Paul.

To this, **CAPNION** said: "I will explain briefly so you are not stuck. It is not quite safe for me to use my own invention in such an excellent matter; instead, I shall cite Origen, the light of our theologians, of whom it is said: 'where he is good, no one is better.' For he says in the book *Against Celsus*: 'In certain sacred names there lies hidden a miraculous power' <note>Original: *In quibusdam sacris nominibus mirandam latere uirtutem*</note>. Therefore, these names themselves are not to be translated from the Hebrew tongue into another, but are to be preserved in their very own characters. You have what you have long wished to draw out: namely, that there is a miraculous power in divine names and prayers, because of which the forms of the characters and the words themselves remain uncorrupted. Just as when a man’s frame is destroyed, the spirit included in the body also vanishes, and the whole power likewise evaporates.

Furthermore, as Iamblichus responded to Porphyry <note>A reference to the debate between the Neoplatonists Porphyry and Iamblichus regarding the efficacy of 'barbarian' names in theurgy (magic)</note>: if those names had been established by human pact and agreement, it would make no difference to exchange one for another. But we understood long ago, Baruchias, and we confess it to be true, that they were received divinely, not produced humanly. To which that noble Pythagorean, Iamblichus, adds: 'Neither do names translated into another language preserve entirely the same meaning.' Rather, there are certain properties peculiar to individual nations which cannot be expressed to other nations by the voice if the properties of the meanings..."

<summary>Reuchlin (as Capnion) argues that Hebrew names and words in the Bible possess an inherent, miraculous power that is lost if translated. He uses Neoplatonic and Patristic sources to justify why certain Hebrew terms like "Amen" remain untranslated in Greek and Latin texts.</summary>

...his ears, and spitting he touched his tongue, and looking up to heaven he groaned and said, *Hiphathah* <note>original: "Hiphathah," usually transliterated from the Greek of Mark 7:34 as "Ephphatha"</note>, which is "be opened." For it is the passive imperative of the active verb *Pathah*, which means "he opened"; in the future tense it is *Ephathah*, that is, "I will open," as in Psalm 78 in the verse: "I will open my mouth in parables," and in the fiftieth <note>Psalm 51:15 in modern numbering</note> in the verse: "Lord, you shall open my lips," and so on with the others. (Matthew 27:46)</note>. For Matthew recites it thus as it actually happened, although *Sabathani* is also spoken corruptly through the lack of the first syllable, which ought to be noted by the letter *Ayin* <note>Reuchlin is performing a "philological" or linguistic correction, noting that the Hebrew verb for "forsaken" (azavtani) begins with the deep guttural letter Ayin</note>. You will grant me pardon if it is not permitted to compare wicked things with good, but nevertheless consider and review within your minds how suitably that blessed man completed his entire condemnation and torture with these thirty Psalms—if it is even right to call him a "man." There, in the manner of a prophecy, it is written: "But I will call upon God, and the Lord will save me. In the evening and morning and at noon I will speak and announce" <note>Psalm 55:16-17</note>. From what was perceived, therefore, we can judge that he said: "My God, my God, why have you forsaken me?" and "Into your hands I commend my spirit" <note>Psalm 31:5</note>. The title of this latter Psalm is held by some to be "for ecstasy" <note>A reference to the Greek title "ekstaseos" in the Septuagint for Psalm 30/31</note>, which most fits a dying man. # BOOK TWO

...to forgive those who pierced him, since they did not know what they were doing. This is so you may realize that I have been converted to your profession through previous meditation, and not so suddenly.

Then **SIDONIVS** said: My dear Capnion <note>Capnion is the Greek pseudonym of Johannes Reuchlin, meaning "smoky," a play on his German name "Reuch."</note>, who is so skilled in these divine writings, what then shall I contribute to our conversation? If I remember rightly, Baruchias has provided me much peace of mind, so that I am not forced to discourse at length about the sacred names. For he teaches us that only Hebrew names are numbered among the truly sacred. He tries to affirm this both from their antiquity and from their familiarity with divinity, as well as from a certain close covenant between God and the Jews, and finally through many arguments that this speech is more solid. If these things are true, I—who have spent my life in Greek and Latin more than others—will be glad to have found this time simply for listening.

You have what you have long wished to draw out: namely, that there is a miraculous power in divine names and prayers, because of which both the forms of the characters and the words themselves remain incorruptible. For just as when the framework of a man is destroyed, the spirit enclosed in the body and all its power likewise vanishes.

Furthermore, as Iamblichus <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote on the Egyptian mysteries.</note> replied to Porphyry: if those names had been established by human pact and agreement, it would make no difference to exchange one for another. But we understood long ago, Baruchias, and we confess it to be true, that they were received divinely, not produced humanly. To which that same noble "Pythagoras," Iamblichus, adds: for names translated into another language do not preserve the exact same meaning <note>original: "mentem," literally "mind" or "intent"</note>; rather, there are certain properties belonging to each nation which cannot be expressed by the voice to other nations. ### BOOK TWO

...he might forgive those who fixed him to the cross, since they knew not what they were doing. <meta>This page continues the dialogue in Book Two of "On the Mirific Word." The previous page established that Hebrew is the primary language of the divine covenant. Here, Baruchias takes up the argument, explaining that while the meaning of words can be translated, their inherent "virtue" or supernatural power is lost in the process.</meta>

-># ON THE MIRIFIC WORD<-

...although we can interpret these words through another language, they do not preserve the same power once they have been altered. I would want these authors to answer you on this, but take care not to think that I feel the same way you do about all words—specifically, that they possess that majesty which you spread so widely by fame. For who would doubt that God the Almighty—and, as the Greeks say, the All-Sustainer <note>original: *omnitenentem*, a Latin rendering of the Greek *pantokrator*</note>—is able to adorn words with divine powers? Therefore, the power of God is evident, but His will is not yet shown, and His will alone is the cause of all things. For God has done whatever He willed in heaven and on earth. For this reason, unless you show that God willed this or that specific word to possess such-and-forth power, you have achieved nothing. 

But **BARUCHIAS** said: "I did not promise to teach you, but rather to recount what I once received concerning the secret mysteries from our assembly <note>original: *collegio*, referring to the tradition of Jewish mystical scholars</note>. If I fulfill this duty properly, there will certainly be much for me to rejoice in, and I will not bind myself to any other task. Since yesterday evening we agreed with one voice that in divine matters one must have faith—though I know it will never be a matter of doubt among you that God is capable of all things, a fact which our mind comprehends by its very nature. If God is above all things and the one upon whom everything depends—both existing and non-existing things, as even the most eminent theologians assert—then the supreme power (indeed, a power beyond the supreme and superior to every power) over both existence and non-existence rests with God, and it is bounded by no limits. Otherwise, it would not be supreme or beyond the supreme. 

If He should show that this power dwells within a word or a thing, it makes no difference, so long as He desires His virtue to inhabit that place. We recognize this decree of God and His will clearly through either the experience of past deeds or the speech of divine inspiration. To this end, it is necessary to apply both Holy Scripture and the teachings of contemplative men—through whose mouths the Divine speaks—as well as the heroic histories of the blessed. For who has known the mind of the Lord of Eternity? No one, except the Spirit who is within Him, and finally those to whom He reveals Himself. Just as no creature knows the thoughts within a man except the spirit that is in him, or a friend to whom the spirit (through the man) or the man (through his spirit) has announced or shown them by signs. 

Our ancestors—men both learned and experienced—affirmed that God spoke in this way with Abraham, Moses, and Joshua; that He showed them the powers of divine words and confirmed them with miraculous effects. They committed these things to memory through writing for us, the offshoots of their lineage <note>original: *prosapiae furculis*, literally "the little branches of the family stock"</note>. We, receiving these with a grateful heart, daily experience and perceive them if we so desire. 

But to approach the matter: let those who desire to hear of wonders recall what we have already discussed at length—that only the first and greatest God performs portents, miracles, marvels, and signs, at least those that are true and not mere illusions. At times, when He deems His angels worthy of divine honor, He entrusts them with the performance of miraculous works. He does this, however, in His own name, lest He give His honor to strangers and His glory to another <note>Isaiah 42:8</note>, and lest man turn to the worship of the creature, abandoning the Creator. For this reason, He places His name within the angels, so that we do not fall into the worship of forms—which in Greek we call 'idolatry' <note>original: *idolatriam*</note>—a thing He hates most bitterly of all. Thus He says to His people: *'Behold, I send my angel to go before you, to guard you on the way and bring you to the place I have prepared. Pay attention to him and hear...'* <note>Exodus 23:20-21</note>"

<summary>Baruchias argues that while God is omnipotent, divine power is only attached to specific Hebrew words by His specific will. He explains that God uses angels to perform miracles by placing His Name within them, ensuring that the glory returns to the Creator rather than the "forms" of the angels themselves.</summary>
<keywords>divine names, Hebrew, Baruchias, omnipotence, miracles, idolatry, angels, revelation, Abraham, Moses, Joshua</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the speech of Baruchias. He shifts from general divine power to the specific names of God and the danger of altering divine words, using the example of Eve's addition to God's command as a cautionary tale for his own discourse on sacred names.</meta>

### BOOK TWO

<meta>catchword: lustre</meta>

...[hear]ken to his voice, and do not think to despise him, for he will not forgive when you have sinned. For my name is in him. <note>original: *Est enim nomen meum in illo*, quoting Exodus 23:21 regarding the Angel of the Lord.</note> Since, therefore, wondrous matters are carried out according to our prayers, first by God and secondarily by the angels, it seemed most fitting to me to speak first of the divine name which is known to pertain to God himself, as far as my memory allows; and then of that which also concerns the angels at times, according to the measure of our slight human weakness. In this matter, we shall maintain an order, so that we may first recount to you those names of God whose meaning has long been clear. After those, we shall attempt to speak of those whose meaning we have received as not yet manifest, or revealed only to a few. 

Therefore, many from the memory of our fathers and some from your own people are read to this day who have dared to write about secret names; for this reason, my part in this discussion will be easier if I explain summarily those things which you have seen described at length by various authors. **Jerome** <note>original: *Hieronymus*, the 4th-century scholar and translator of the Bible.</note> commemorates ten names of God in his letter to Marcella. **Dionysius the Areopagite** <note>A highly influential Christian mystic and theologian.</note> mentions nearly forty-five; others seventy-two <note>A reference to the *Shem HaMephorash*, the 72-fold name of God in Jewish tradition.</note>, others more, others fewer, each according to his own capacity. Indeed, what limit can there be to the names of Him who is both unnamable <note>original: *innominabilis*</note> and celebrated by every name? 

For this reason, let us neither thrust ourselves into a corner from which we cannot break free, nor let us plead our cause so slowly and superficially that we are proven to hold no memory of divine honor. Truly, I wish for you to agree with my words no more than I myself would agree, except as the sacred utterances allow—lest I, who am breathing mud and clay, a worm and not a man, should seem to do an injury to God the Best and Greatest, or like another **Uzzah** <note>original: *Oza*; a biblical figure struck dead for touching the Ark of the Covenant without authorization (2 Samuel 6:6-7).</note> should touch the ark and add something beyond what God himself has spoken. 

For I believe the first sin of the human race was that which **Eve**, the mother of all living, added to the words of God beyond what God himself had said—which was indeed the beginning of subsequent crimes and the origin of divine vengeance. For before the woman was created, God said to Adam: "Of every tree of paradise you shall eat, but of the tree of knowledge of good and evil you shall not eat." But let us see how **Eve**, newly created, altered the words of the Creator, saying to the serpent: "Of the fruit of the tree which is in the middle of paradise, God commanded us that we should not eat, and that we should not touch it, lest perhaps we die." 

She added to us a plural pronoun, although God had nowhere commanded Eve not to eat, as she was not yet born. She added, "And that we should not touch it," which God also had not said. Furthermore, as if she wished to make the Lord out to be a liar, she added a particle of doubt and unbelief, saying "Lest perhaps we die," whereas the Creator had threatened Adam alone, singularly and firmly: "For on whatever day you eat from it, you shall die the death." Wherefore the serpent paid the penalty for his counsel, Eve for her assistance, and Adam for his transgression. Therefore, lest I fall into the same vice or crime before the trembling Author of the universe, His supreme mercy must be prayed for, so that He may notice our goodwill and piety rather than our ruin if by chance my foot should slip; in which matter may He deign to help from the most sacred inner chambers of His love toward us, for the glory and honor of His name, blessed forever and ever. Now, what might that so illustrious word be—so effective, so venerable, so salutary—in which our prayer [is more easily granted...]

<summary>Baruchias discusses the various catalogs of God's names and warns against altering divine words, citing Eve's fatal addition to God's commandment as a warning against theological arrogance.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the speech of Baruchias. Having previously discussed the lists of divine names compiled by Jerome and Dionysius, he now turns to the philosophical and ontological nature of God's "essential" name, moving from the multiplicity of names to the singular "name of essence" revealed to Moses.</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

...how we attain mastery <note>original: *compotes euadimus*</note>, since as we have said many names of the supreme God are found, we must consider. For what the prophet **Zechariah** exclaims—*In that day God shall be one and his name one* <note>original: *In die illa erit deus unus & nomē eius unum*, from Zechariah 14:9</note>—reminds us that in this present time there are many names for God, because in the future age there will be but one. Why do we drag out the hours? Why do we waste time in vain? Why do we wait so long, as if much dispute were needed to explain how that which lacks a name can be designated by so many names? Does another name exist for the divinity among those in the heavens than that which exists among mortals? Shall the accustomed naming of this world cease in the future life of our souls? Does God have a name when we consider his immanence within himself? Or indeed, is there then no need for any name for him? 

Dismissing all that pertains no more to the matter than to the delay and slowness of our business, let us explain in a few words. For although the supreme divinity among the immortals, the heavenly ones, and those above the heavens is called, as is likely, by some super-eminent name according to their own custom, nevertheless we shall treat more of those things which concern our own matter. We shall say that according to human contemplation, we attribute certain names from the sacred records delivered to us by God to his **essence**, some to his **power**, some to his **operation**, and the rest to our own **affection** <note>In this context, "affection" refers to the human emotional or devotional response toward God, such as "Merciful" or "Just."</note> toward God. 

By the **essence** of God, however, I mean his separation from all things and his total enclosure within himself—what that noble philosopher of our age <note>Likely a reference to Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494), a key figure in the development of Christian Kabbalah who heavily influenced the author, Reuchlin.</note> calls an "extreme, deep, and solitary retraction in the most remote recess of his divinity, hiding himself most inwardly in the abyss of his own darkness." According to this view, they affirm that God instituted a venerable name for himself among men; for since he alone knows himself, he alone gives himself a name. 

Now let us see by what argument wise men infer this, and what that most sacred name of essence might be. **Moses** said to God: "Behold, I am going to the children of Israel and I shall say to them: 'The God of your fathers has sent me to you.' And they will say to me: 'What is his name?' What shall I speak to them?" And God said to Moses: ***Ehieh***, which is "I shall be." And he said: "Thus you shall say to the children of Israel: ***Ehieh*** [I shall be] has sent me to you." <note>original: *Ehieh q̃ ero*; referencing Exodus 3:14. In Hebrew, *Ehyeh* is the first-person singular imperfect form of the verb "to be."</note> 

**Plato** had learned this name during his long travels among the Assyrians <note>Renaissance scholars often claimed Plato traveled to the East and encountered Hebrew wisdom, which they used to harmonize Greek philosophy with the Bible.</note>, which he finally translated for the Greeks using two letters as ***On*** [Being]. For in the *Timaeus* <note>Plato’s famous dialogue on the creation of the universe.</note> he says: "It is therefore, in my opinion, first to be distinguished what ***On*** is—that is, the being that always is and has no beginning—and what it is that has a beginning, but never truly is. The former is grasped by the intellect with reason as being-in-itself; the latter is perceived by opinion with irrational sense as that which is generated and corruptible, as something that never truly *is*." 

By that name, therefore, Plato distinguishes the world from its Creator, and a great many highly learned men have followed him, except only that some have changed the neuter gender into the masculine. For Plato names the most excellent author of the universe "that which is," using the single term ***To on*** [The Being], but many of his followers call him "He who is," ***Ho on***. This change is consistent with both for the Hebrews. For since they follow a nature that is not "monstrous" <note>The author uses "monstrous" to imply that the Hebrew language follows a natural binary (male/female) without the "unnatural" third category of the neuter.</note>, they have only two genders: masculine and feminine. Therefore, a neuter word among the Latins or Greeks is easily included under their masculine. Wherefore, whether he is called "he who is" or "that which is," he is pronounced by his essential name according to Moses as ***Ehieh***, which comes from the substantive verb ***Haiah*** [to be]...

<summary>This page explores the philosophical transition from God's many names to His singular "essential" name, identifying the Hebrew "Ehieh" (I shall be) with Plato's "On" (Being). The text argues that while God is ultimately nameless and self-contained, He revealed this name of pure existence to Moses to distinguish the Creator from the corruptible world.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discourse of Baruchias. Having previously discussed the various names of God found in the scriptures, he now moves to the "Name of Essence"—the philosophical and mystical core of how God is named. He addresses the tension between elegant classical speech and the "barbaric" technical language required for theology.</meta>

## BOOK TWO

...derive according to the laws of grammar, adding a letter to the previous one. I do not know if it is entirely worthy of pardon for me to speak so **barbarously** <note>The author refers to "barbarism" here as the use of non-classical, technical, or Scholastic Latin that would offend a Renaissance humanist's taste for "elegant" Ciceronian style.</note> in so elegant a cause before you, most eloquent contenders. But though some **Demosthenes** or **Cicero** <note>Famous Greek and Roman orators, used here as symbols of perfect eloquence.</note> might delay, I would surely believe him to be mute and tongueless in the secrets of God. For so your **Origen** <note>A prominent early Christian theologian (c. 184–253 AD) known for his prolific writings and allegorical interpretations.</note>, not inferior to the most splendid orators, speaks in the twentieth homily (if I remember correctly) on **Exodus**, in almost these words: *All men, in comparison to the divine word, are to be thought not only ineloquent but even mute.* <note>original: "Omnes homines ad comparatiōnem verbi diuini, nō solum ineloquentes sed & muti putandi sunt"</note> Therefore, if you can listen to perpetual barbarism without annoyance, I shall gladly proceed.

**SIDONIUS** replied: "Go on, indeed. For it is no secret to us that this province of study is the most difficult of all; and besides, I myself think that divine things can in no way be sufficiently uttered in the human manner, mindful of what histories tell of **Simonides**, a man of outstanding learning. For when the tyrant **Hiero** asked him what God was, he requested a space of one day to deliberate. When Hiero returned the next day and asked the same thing, Simonides asked for two days. Then, as the Tyrant asked more frequently, Simonides always doubled the number of days. When Hiero finally asked why he did so, he said, 'Because the longer I consider it, the more obscure the matter seems to me.' <note>This story, originally from Cicero’s "On the Nature of the Gods," illustrates the impossibility of the human mind fully grasping the divine essence.</note> Therefore, I marvel that this business could be handled by you thus far and so clearly, even in our own language; so do not delay us further."

**CAPNION** <note>The pseudonym for the author, Johannes Reuchlin, representing the Christian Hebraist perspective in the dialogue.</note> also encouraged him in this. Then he [Baruchias] said: "You have the name of the highest God, which some who are not unskillful have thought to be the first of all, since it seems to designate the very **essence** <term>essentia: the fundamental nature or "being" of a thing</term> of God itself, and not to communicate with any other things. For nothing exists outside of God, since all things consist of a certain proportion of composition. God alone is most simple, and therefore He 'is who He is' <note>A reference to the name revealed to Moses in Exodus 3:14.</note>; everything else, however, is indeed that *by which* it is, but not *what* it is. 

Nevertheless, other men, not to be despised and most studious in the contemplation of the most secret things, enter much more closely into the very inner sanctuaries of divinity; these men, with the utmost observance, weigh the greatness of another name, and place above this name **Ehieh** <note>Hebrew: "I shall be" or "I am"</note> or **On** <note>Greek: "The One who is"</note> or another. For as your **Dionysius the Areopagite** <note>A Christian Neoplatonist philosopher whose works, like "The Divine Names," were central to medieval mysticism.</note> explains: *On* itself—that is, 'He who is'—is a certain **super-essential** being of all being according to its power, the founding cause and artisan of being, of subsistence, of substance, of essence, of nature, the principle and measure of eternities and times, the entity and age of beings and the time of those things which come to be, and being for those things which are in any way whatever, and generation for those things which are produced in any way whatever. 

Here it is perceived not obscurely that the first being concurs with flowing things, to which it provides the power of persisting. And although the most simple essence of God—through which He notifies Himself simply and freely, just as He embraces all being in Himself pre-eminently—is such that it is the super-essential [nature] of all being, yet it does not designate God as enclosed and resting, but as looking upon things themselves, and insinuating Himself with things to which He grants subsistence. Just as you easily understand from the words of the same **Dionysius** which you have already heard after sharp meditation. Wherefore, considering the separate withdrawal within Himself of unutterable supereminence, they venerate above all the most holy name **Hu** <note>Hebrew: "He" (HU), often used in Kabbalah to refer to the hidden, transcendent aspect of God (Kether).</note>..."

f

<meta>This page continues the discourse of Baruchias. Having just established that human language is "mute" compared to the divine word, Baruchias now moves to the central investigation of the work: the specific names of God. He transitions from the multiplicity of names used in this world to the single, essential name revealed to Moses, drawing a bridge between Hebrew scripture and Platonic philosophy.</meta>

### ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

...we come to possess these names. Since, as we have said, many names of the highest God are found, it is necessary to consider them. For as the prophet Zechariah exclaims, *In that day there will be one God and his name will be one,* <note>original: "In die illa erit deus unus & nomē eius unum" — Zechariah 14:9.</note> we are reminded that at this time there are many names for God, because in the future age there will be only one. Why do we drag out the hours, why do we waste time in vain, why do we wait long—as if there were need for much dispute—over how that which lacks a name can be designated by so many names?

Does another name for the divinity exist among those in the heavens than among mortals? Will the customary naming of this world cease in the future life of our souls? Does God have a name when we consider his immanence within himself? Or indeed is no name necessary for him then? Putting aside everything that pertains no more to the matter than to the delay and slowing of our business, let us explain in a few words. 

For although the supreme divinity among the immortals, the heavenly beings, and the super-heavenly beings is called by some super-eminent name according to their own custom (as is likely), yet we will treat more of those things which concern our own human condition. We say that according to human contemplation, we attribute something to the **essence**, something to the **power**, something to the **operation**, and the rest to our **affection** toward God, derived from the sacred names divinely handed down to us. 

By "essence," however, I mean God’s separation from things and his total enclosure within himself. It is the extreme—as that noble philosopher of our age <note>Likely a reference to Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494), a famous Renaissance philosopher who integrated Kabbalah with Platonism.</note> says—the deep and solitary retraction in the most remote retreat of his divinity, hiding himself most inwardly in the abyss of his darkness. According to this, they affirm that God established for himself a venerable name among men, for he alone knows himself; wherefore he alone gives a name to himself. Now let us see by what argument wise men conjecture this, and what that most sacred name of essence is. 

Moses said to God, *Behold, I go to the children of Israel and shall say to them: The God of your fathers sent me to you; and they will say to me, "What is his name?" What shall I speak to them?* And God said to Moses, **Ehieh** <note>Hebrew for "I will be" or "I am."</note> which means *I shall be*; and he said, *Thus you shall say to the children of Israel, **Ehieh** sent me to you.* 

Plato had learned this name during his long travels among the Assyrians <note>It was a common Renaissance belief that the Greek philosophers traveled to the Near East and secretly learned the wisdom of Moses.</note>, which he finally translated for the Greeks into two letters as **On** <note>Greek for "Being" or "That which is."</note>. For in the *Timaeus* <note>One of Plato's most influential dialogues, dealing with the creation of the world.</note> he says: 
> *It must first be distinguished, in my opinion, what is **On**, that is, the Being which always is, but has no beginning; and what is that which has a beginning, but never truly is. The former is comprehended by intellect with reason, being always through itself; the latter is perceived by opinion with irrational sense, being generated and perishable, as that which never is.*

By that name, therefore, Plato distinguishes the world from the creator, and a multitude of most learned men followed him—except that some changed the neuter into the masculine. For Plato names the most excellent author of the universe "that which is," with the single word **Toon** <note>Greek: τὸ ὄν (the being/the thing that exists).</note>, but many of his followers call him "He who is," **Oon** <note>Greek: ὁ ὤν (the being/the person who exists).</note>. 

Both of these changes agree with the Hebrew language. For those who follow a natural grammar have only two genders: masculine and feminine. Therefore, a neuter word among the Latins or Greeks is easily included under the masculine. Wherefore whether it be said "He who is" or "That which is," it is pronounced by its essential name according to Moses as **Ehieh**, which comes from the substantive verb **Haiah**...

<summary>Baruchias explains that while God is beyond naming, He is known through names of essence, power, and operation. He identifies the Hebrew name Ehieh (I Am) as the supreme "Name of Essence" and argues that Plato's philosophical concept of "Being" (On) was a Greek translation of this Mosaic revelation.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the speech of Baruchias. He has been discussing the grammatical formation of divine names and now shifts to a humble apology for his "barbaric" Latin before diving into a deep philosophical exploration of God's primary names—"Ehieh" (I Am), "On" (Being), and "Hu" (He)—using the works of Origen and Pseudo-Dionysius to bridge Hebrew and Greek thought.</meta>

## BOOK TWO

...derived according to the laws of grammar, with a letter added to the first. <note>The speaker is continuing a technical explanation of how Hebrew names are constructed grammatically.</note> I do not know if it is entirely worthy of pardon that I speak so "barbarously" <note>original: "barbarice loqui" — Baruchias apologizes for using Hebrew-inflected or non-classical Latin in the presence of experts in rhetoric.</note> in so elegant a cause before you, most eloquent debaters. But though a Demosthenes or some Cicero might delay, I would truly believe that such a man would be mute and tongueless regarding the secrets of God. For thus your Origen, <note>Origen of Alexandria (c. 184–253 AD), an early Christian scholar and theologian.</note> who is not inferior to any of the most brilliant orators, speaks in his twentieth homily on Exodus (if I remember correctly) in almost these words: *Compared to the divine word, all men must be considered not only ineloquent but even mute.* <note>original: "Omnes ho- mines ad comparationẽ uerbi diuini, nõ solum ineloquentes sed & muti pu tandi sunt"</note> Therefore, if you can listen to this perpetual "barbarism" without annoyance, I shall gladly proceed.

"Indeed, proceed," said **SIDONIUS**. "For it is no secret to us that this province of study is the most difficult of all. Moreover, I myself believe that divine matters can in no way be sufficiently expressed in a human manner, mindful of what history relates concerning Simonides, <note>Simonides of Ceos (c. 556–468 BC), a Greek lyric poet.</note> a man of outstanding learning. For when the Tyrant Hiero <note>Hiero I, the ruler of Syracuse.</note> asked him what God was, he requested one day’s time to deliberate. When Hiero returned the next day and asked the same thing, Simonides asked for two days. Then, as the Tyrant asked more frequently, Simonides always doubled the number of days. Finally, when Hiero asked why he did so, he replied: 'Because the longer I consider it, the more obscure the matter seems to me.' Therefore, I marvel that this business could be treated by you in our language thus far and so clearly; wherefore, do not delay us further." **CAPNION** <note>Johannes Reuchlin, the scholar and protagonist of this work.</note> also urged him to this.

Then he said: "You have the name of the highest God, which some who were not unskilled thought to be the first of all, since it seems to designate the very essence of God himself, and does not communicate with any other things. Nothing is outside of God that exists, since all things consist of a certain proportion of composition. God alone is most simple, and therefore **He is who He is**; <note>A reference to Exodus 3:14: "I Am That I Am."</note> everything else is indeed *that by which it is*, not *that which it is*. Yet others, not to be looked down upon and most studious in the contemplation of the most secret things, enter much more closely into the very inner shrines of divinity. They most reverently weigh the greatness of a certain other name, and above this name **Ehieh** or **On** <note>original: "Ehieh" is Hebrew for "I Will Be/I Am"; "On" is the Greek translation "The Being."</note> they establish another. For as your Dionysius the Areopagite <note>Pseudo-Dionysius, an influential 5th-century philosopher who blended Christian theology with Neoplatonism.</note> explains: *The Being (On) itself—that is, He Who Is—is a certain **superexistential** power of the whole of being, the founding cause and maker of the entity, the subsistence, the substance, the essence, the nature, the principle and measure of eternities and times, the entity and the age of beings and the time of those things that are made, and the being for those things which exist in any way, and the generation for those things which are in any way born.*

Here we clearly perceive the meeting of the first Being with flowing things, to which he provides the power of persisting. And although the most simple essence of God—through which he makes himself known simply and freely—just as it embraces the whole in itself, is itself 'being' in a most excellent way, so as to be the **superexistential** <term>superexistential: a term used to describe a reality that transcends the very category of "existence" or "being."</term> of the whole of being; yet it does not designate God as enclosed and resting, but as looking upon the things themselves, and insinuating himself with the things to which he grants subsistence. This you will easily understand from the words of the same Dionysius which you have just heard, after sharp meditation. Therefore, considering the separate retreat of his ineffable supereminence within himself, they especially venerate the most holy name **Hu**. <note>original: "Hu" — Hebrew for "He," used here to represent the hidden, absolute aspect of God.</note>"

f

<meta>Baruchias continues his technical and mystical discourse on the divine names. Having touched upon the grammar of "Ehieh," he now introduces the name "Hu" (He), linking it to the Greek concept of "The Same" (T'auton) and finding echoes of this divine unchanging identity in the poetry of Virgil and the philosophy of Plato.</meta>

### ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

And they place it [the name *Hu*] even before *Ehieh* <term>Ehieh: Hebrew for "I am," the name revealed to Moses at the burning bush</term>. God himself revealed this to Isaiah, the noblest of prophets, saying: "I am the Lord, *Hu* <note>original: "Hu"; Hebrew: הוא (He)</note> is my name; I will not give my glory to another." You see that the aforementioned name contains a light beneath a bushel and indeed restricts its rays, designating God dwelling in solitary and hidden retreats without any outflow of powers, but keeping himself in the most simple and absolute consistency of his own being; not going forth, governing nothing, but permitting all things, accepting what is done well, and likewise not remembering what is done poorly. Regarding this, God speaks later in the same prophet: "I am, I am *Hu*, blotting out your iniquities within myself, and I will not remember your sins." For because *Hu* does not give his glory to another, because he does not remember sins, and because he blots out iniquities within himself and not for the sake of something outside himself, he declares the meaning of the name; since *Hu* denotes God as so separate from things that he either acts, permits, or rests all things within himself.

And this name is often placed before following negative statements, as God spoke a little earlier: "That you may understand that I am *Hu*; before me no god was formed, and after me there shall be none." This so august and distinguished name was once called *T’auton* <note>Greek: τὸ αὐτό — "the same" or "the self-same"</note> by the Greek philosophers, especially the Platonists, and by the Latins it is called "The Self-Same" <note>original: "idem ipsum"</note>. For the hundred and first Psalm <note>Modern Psalm 102:27</note> says this of God: "And you are *Hu*, and your years shall not fail." You chant in Latin during your sacred assemblies: "But you are the self-same."

And Virgil transferred the same name to Jove [Jupiter] with a similar understanding, when Jupiter promulgated an oracle, saying: "Whatever fortune each man has today, what hope each man pursues, whether he be Trojan or Rutulian, I shall hold them with no distinction—whether the camp is held in siege by the fates of the Italians, or by Troy's wretched error and sinister warnings. Nor do I release the Rutulians; his own beginnings shall bring to each man labor and fortune. King Jupiter is the **same** <note>original: "idem"</note> for all." You see this name "The Same" attributed to King Jove. Therefore, a word of such great majesty, so protective and helpful as *T’auton*, which is *Hu*, is super-essentially eternal, unchangeable, remaining in itself, existing according to the same things and in the same way, similarly present to all things, and firmly and purely placed according to itself within itself in the most beautiful boundaries of substantial identity; untransmutable, inflexible, invariable, unmixed, immaterial, most simple, lacking nothing, incapable of increase or decrease, unbegotten—not as if it were not yet born, or as if imperfect, or because it was not begotten by this or that, or because it exists nowhere and in no way; but as being unbegotten above all things and absolutely unbegotten, always existing and perfect in itself, and being the "same" according to itself, and limited by itself in a uniform and identity-forming manner.

I fear here that in translating the Greek I may badly offend learned ears. But perhaps I do not have to deliver a speech before a magistrate, nor do you sit as listeners in a forum or on the benches of a courtroom, but in the Academy of the most secret philosophy, which desires naked truths more than ornate words—though if it were possible for me, I ought indeed, according to Horace Flaccus, <note>The Roman poet Horace (65–8 BC)</note> to speak words that are both pleasant and suitable alongside the facts. However it may be, I would fix the sublime dignity of that name at the very summit of all things, were it not that sometimes—indeed, quite often—it flows down into these sublunary <term>sublunary: the physical world below the moon, subject to change and decay</term> realms and communicates with things in such a way that it takes a surname for itself from them...

<summary>Baruchias explains the divine name "Hu" (He), identifying it with the philosophical concept of "The Same." He argues that this name represents God's absolute, unchanging essence, citing support from Isaiah, the Psalms, Plato, and Virgil.</summary>
<keywords>Hu, Ehieh, T'auton, Divine Names, Platonism, Virgil, Isaiah, Identity of God, Secret Philosophy, Reuchlin</keywords>

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>Baruchias continues his exploration of the divine name "Hu" (He), arguing that it represents the absolute identity and sameness of God. He bridges the gap between Hebrew scripture, Greek philosophy (specifically the "Tauton" or "The Same" of the Platonists), and the poetic language of Virgil to show that this "sameness" is the source of all existence.</meta>

### BOOK TWO

Whatever we consider to exist not as something else, but as the self-same thing, occurs not only in substances—as when Dido says to Aeneas, "Because of you, the same <note>original Latin: *eundem*</note>, my shame is lost"—but also in the consequences of those substances. For instance, Anna, the sister of Dido, says: "If you had called me to the same <note>original Latin: *eadem*</note> fate, the same <note>original Latin: *idem*</note> grief and the same <note>original Latin: *eadem*</note> hour would have taken both of us by the sword." Likewise, Virgil says of the companions of Aeneas: "The same <note>original Latin: *idem*</note> passion holds them all at once." 

It seems to me that Isaiah explained this participation of things in the "same" through the word by which God speaks: "I am *Hu* <note>The Hebrew pronoun for "He," used here as a divine name implying absolute existence</note>, I am the first and I am the last." And a little later, in the fifty-first chapter: "I am, I am *Hu* who comforts you." From this, it becomes clearly known to us that this *Hu* is involved in the beginning, the end, and the middle of all things. 

To demonstrate that your thinkers also agree with ours, listen again to the Areopagite <note>Dionysius the Areopagite, a Christian theologian and philosopher whose writings blended Neoplatonism with mystical Christianity</note>, who says this concerning *Hu*—which he calls *Tauton* <note>Greek: τὸ αὐτό — "The Same"</note>: "The Same itself, from itself, irradiates all those who are fit to participate in it, connecting some things to others by an abundance and origin of identity within itself; it possesses opposites in an 'identical' way through the one and singular, pre-eminent cause of all identity." 

Thus, like a king, He penetrates all things—the celestial, the worldly, and the infernal—by His mere nod. Nor is there any power in heaven, on earth, or in the underworld that is able to resist the power of this name. Therefore, He is called the "Lord of Powers" (or Hosts) because every power and potency is subject to Him. The prophet asks in the twenty-third Psalm: "Who is *Hu*, that King of Glory?" The spirit within the prophet responds: "The Lord of Powers is *Hu*; He is the King of Glory forever."

Something else comes to mind which I recall solely by the mention of the aforementioned name. Among the seven names consecrated at the same time through Moses, there is also placed *Esth* <note>Reuchlin's transliteration of the Hebrew *Esh* (אש), meaning "Fire"</note> alongside *Hu*, in a single context of speech in that repetition of the Law which you call Deuteronomy—which in Latin we call *ignis* (fire). For this reason, He is very often heard in fire, as with Abraham, Elijah, David, and others of whom histories exist, as if in a matter consistent with divinity. 

Moses saw God in the fire, and an angel in a flame of fire. Ezekiel’s divine vision was the splendor of fire, and lightning coming out of the fire. Likewise, Plato maintained that the supreme God exists in a fiery essence. His ungrateful disciple, Aristotle—as he often does elsewhere—now also criticized this not without some malice in a certain small book written to Alexander, King of the Macedonians. There, he discovered—or rather learned from the Brahmans <note>original: *Brachmanibus*; ancient Indian philosophers/priests</note> of India—that the "fifth essence" is the dwelling place of divinity. He says, "We do not say, as some who wander into error concerning the divine, that God is in a fiery essence." 

Then, he tries to cite a witness of truth better than Plato when he adds: "For so even Homer says: 'The lot of Jove is heaven, he sits in the broad ether among the clouds.'" Therefore, the prophet Zechariah spoke better—and certainly much better, as one who heard God Himself speaking from the mouth of an angel: "I will be to her," said God, "a wall of fire round about, and for the honor of *Ehieh* <note>Hebrew: "I Am"</note> in her midst." 

Regarding this, even to please our friend Sidonius by citing philosophers, that man Plutarch—a man singularly learned in many arts—makes mention of it in his collection of physical dogmas. He says: "They describe the knowledge of God as an intellectual and fiery spirit having no form, but transforming itself into whatever it wishes and equaling itself to all things." Whence Zoroaster says: "All things are generated from one fire." You too, Capnion <note>The Hebrew-derived nickname for the author, Johannes Reuchlin, meaning "Smoky"</note>...

<sig>f ii</sig>

<summary>Baruchias explains the divine name "Hu" (The Same) and "Esh" (Fire), linking Hebrew mysticism to Greek philosophy and classical poetry to argue that God is the singular, fiery source of all identity and existence.</summary>

<meta>Baruchias concludes his discussion on the divine pronoun "Hu" (He) and transitions into the nature of God as fire, bridging Hebrew thought with Christian scripture and classical Greek philosophy. The dialogue continues between the characters Capnion (representing Reuchlin), Sidonius (the Epicurean/Classical scholar), and Baruchias (the Jewish mystic).</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

Indeed, we Christians—for I do not wish to abandon the profession I have begun—commonly name God "fire," along with Paul of Tarsus, who, writing to the Hebrews, said: "God is a consuming fire" <note>Hebrews 12:29; Paul the Apostle is referred to here as "Paul of Tarsus."</note>. Nevertheless, you worship this name under a certain distinct property of divinity attributed to the Holy Spirit, which John the Baptist taught in the books of both Mark and Luke, saying: "I indeed baptize you in water for repentance, but he who comes after me is stronger than I, whose sandals I am not worthy to carry; He will baptize you in the Holy Spirit and fire" <note>Matthew 3:11, though the text mentions Mark and Luke. Reuchlin is harmonizing the Gospels here.</note>. This is expressed through the figure of *Hendiadys* <note>original Latin: "Endyadis." A rhetorical figure where two words joined by a conjunction express a single complex idea; here, "Spirit and fire" are one.</note> as "in the Holy Spirit," that is, "in fire," in whose likeness He appeared to the apostles after Christ was taken up.

**Capnion** said: "That recitation of yours pleases me greatly, because I believe it aligns significantly with our religion. First of all, there is nothing I have ever received with greater pleasure than the way you have arranged these divine names—not by human impulse, I believe—so that, contrary to the opinion of many, even among our own people whom we crown as theologians, you have placed first not *Ehieh* <note>Hebrew: "I am"</note> as they think, but *Hu* <note>Hebrew: "He"</note>. Thus the order is *Hu*, *Ehieh*, *Efth* <note>Hebrew pronouns: He, I am, Thou. Reuchlin correlates these to the three persons of the Trinity.</note>, as if someone were speaking to the one ineffable Trinity and the triune Unity. You are the 'Same Fire,' just as we speak concerning the nature of visible things: substance, power, and operation, all within the same single individual."

"And perhaps," said **SIDONIVS**, "just as Orpheus arranged the sacred hymns according to consecrated names: Night, Heaven, Ether <note>original: "Nyx, Vranos, Aethir"</note>. Or Homer: Zeus, Athena, Aphrodite. Of the first, he says: 'Jupiter darkening the clouds and reigning in the ether.' Of the second: 'If wise Minerva should grant me beauty.' For we call her the God of Great Counsel because of the head of Jove, from whose brain she is said to have been born; you Hebrews call this <term>Hochma</term> <note>Hebrew: Wisdom, the second Sephirah in the Kabbalistic Tree of Life.</note>. Of the third, it is said: 'Having spoken thus, the father of men and gods laughed, and called upon shining Venus, to whom he spoke: "My daughter, the command of war is not left to your care, but rather to kindle the gods with soft embrace and flames."' Concerning this Triad, I might sing that line of Virgil <note>original Latin: "Maronnicum," referring to Publius Vergilius Maro.</note>, or rather Pythagoras: 'God delights in an odd number.' For I seem to see that you have encompassed the entire majesty of the divine essence in a super-eminent triad. From this, perhaps, He creates beings, seizes what is created, and perfects what is seized. But why do I delay Baruchias? Come, if you have anything further."

Then Baruchias said: "I still have a very few things which I long ago skillfully impressed upon my memory, yet they are nothing compared to this class of names which I have just reviewed. For I cannot imagine that among all the things I have either read or learned by hearing, there remains any other name besides those I have already brought forth. For we embrace the divine essence by these three names alone: *Hu*, *Ehieh*, *Efth*, which would signify the essence of God even if there were now no world, whether archetypal or physical. For then God would be the same as Himself, and would be His own essence, and would be of a fiery nature. Not indeed as elemental fire is, nor as stellar or celestial fire, but as an inexpressible splendor within Himself, compared to which all other fires are mere shadow and darkness. This is what the monuments of the Chaldeans indicate in Greek verses found in Synesius <note>Synesius of Cyrene, a 4th-century philosopher-bishop who integrated Neoplatonism with Christianity.</note>: 'Do not look down upon the black world without light, for a deep and dark gloom is spread beneath it; it is squalid and likewise obscure, and likewise it feeds the idols <note>original: "idola." In this context, "idols" refers to fleeting, illusory images or shadows of reality in the physical world.</note>.'" For even that world, if there is any archetype, is a shadow and an idol...

<meta>catchword: idola (idols)</meta>

<meta>The previous page ended with Baruchias discussing the proper order of divine names, arguing that "Hu" (He) precedes "Ehieh" (I am). This page continues the comparison between divine light and the material world, moving into a deep exploration of "Hochma" (Wisdom) as a divine emanation.</meta>

# BOOK TWO

f iii

[nothing] will be in comparison to the divine light within itself; how much more so this material and dreg-filled world of ours, which is called the shadow of the higher world and a certain mere image. But Sidonius whispered something into my ear about <term>Hochma: Hebrew for "Wisdom" (חכמה), often personified in Jewish mysticism as the first emanation of God</term>, as if it were a name among the consecrated aspects of the divine essence. I confess, indeed, that since nothing "happens" to God <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, "accidents" are qualities that happen to a thing but are not part of its essential nature; Reuchlin argues God has no such accidents.</note>, nothing foreign can stick to Him. Nothing is simpler or more unified than He. Every day, however, we cry out to God with David: "In wisdom you have made them all" <note>Psalm 104:24</note>. Therefore, before all things that were made, Wisdom was with God. This Wisdom was not outside of God, otherwise it could not have existed before every creature; it was therefore the essence of God. For we have shown above that the word for "essence" is the same. Therefore Sidonius—moved because he was harassed by the reproach that he might be found among the <note>original: "Vsias," a Latinization of the Greek *Ousia*, meaning "beings" or "essences"</note>—feared that I, through some sluggish negligence, might omit *Hochma*, that is, Wisdom.

But when one meditates in their soul that God is nothing other than His own essence, it will not be difficult to know how we distribute the names signifying God—namely, according to human custom. For we know the most high and excellent nature of divinity improperly and imperfectly, and therefore we also name it imperfectly. If any names have been handed down to us by divine authority, we arrange them according to the grasp of our understanding. We think some signify God according to Himself, stripped of all garments, naked, and separated from individual applications—just as we see ourselves "happening" to other things. This is how He is when He is that supersubstantial fire; when He is that unique "being" encompassing the eternal past, present, and future, bound together into one <term>ocean of eternity: a poetic metaphor for God's timelessness, where all points of time coexist</term> by a unifying bond; and when He is "the Same itself," mixed with no proportion of "otherness." These three things designate a certain pure nature of substance, even according to our mortal and weak knowledge of things. 

But Wisdom is of the kind that we perceive "happening" to humans; for this reason, we call one person wiser than another. Similarly, we attribute it to God, and its name is *Hochma*. Although it is judged to be of no small efficacy among the sacred names signifying God, it is nevertheless not permitted to name God *Katauton* <note>Greek: "According to himself"</note>, that is, in His absolute self, but *Periauton* <note>Greek: "About himself"</note>, that is, concerning what surrounds God. The sacred words are evidence of this: "The foolishness of God is wiser than men" <note>1 Corinthians 1:25</note>. Thus, Wisdom itself reveals this mystery about itself in the Book of Proverbs when it says:

> The Lord possessed me in the beginning of his way, before he made anything from then. From eternity I was poured out from the head before the earth. The abysses were not yet when I was conceived, nor yet the heavy-laden springs of water. Before the mountains were established, before the hills, I was conceived. <note>Proverbs 8:22-25</note>

Afterward it follows: And <term>Ehieh: The Hebrew name "I am," here identified with the divine craftsman</term> was the master builder according to itself, and *Ehieh* was the daily delight. You read this more elegantly as:

> I was with him, arranging all things, and I was delighted day by day. <note>Proverbs 8:30</note>

Now, with the veil removed, you surely see that the wisdom of God is a certain emanation. Although produced before the constitution of the world, it nevertheless arose from the head of the most high God through the contemplation of future things. Through it all things were made, and without it nothing was made—as if it were a concomitant habit and industry of *Ehieh* itself (that is, of the "First Being"), whom it calls the "master builder" here. I speak in a human manner because of our weakness. For in such a cause

Using Proverbs 8, the text establishes Wisdom as the 'master builder' through which the world was created, existing before time in the 'ocean of eternity.'</summary>

<meta>The previous page established that while God's essence is one, we speak of His attributes like "Wisdom" (Hochma) using human language to understand His actions in the world. This page transitions from the philosophical concept of divine essence to the specific system of the ten Sephiroth, the emanations through which God reveals Himself.</meta>

# ON THE MIRACULOUS WORD

...we sometimes understand what we cannot express in words. Indeed, I believe that wisdom was a living substance and an energy operating according to the will of the Architect of the universe, providing for all. Although it is a holy opinion that this was God Himself, my mind nevertheless compels me to understand it as a specific property of God there, rather than His own bare immanence. Therefore, I think <term>Hochma: Hebrew for "Wisdom" (חכמה), the second of the ten Sephiroth</term> is among those things that accompany the hidden closure of God within Himself—just as the "strength" of God, which together with the previously mentioned "wisdom," contributed no small amount to the machine of the world. This is according to the testimony of <note>original: "Hieremiæ"</note> Jeremiah concerning the Creator, who "made the earth by His strength, and prepared the world by His wisdom, and by <term>Binah: Hebrew for "Understanding" (בינה), the third Sephirah</term>—that is, by His intelligence or providence—He stretched out the heavens." <note>Jeremiah 10:12</note>

Here again, you have a religious name that the Hebrews place after the "Crown" and "Wisdom" among the ten "numerations" <note>original: "numerationes," referring to the Sephiroth</note> attributed to God—not as He is incommunicable, but as He is radiant; not as He is enclosed, but as He is apparent; not as He is abstract, but as He is emerging. The first of these is the inaccessible abyss of divinity, a sea of majesty, and the infinite power over all things that are as well as those that are not. No contradiction can escape this, no entanglement can prohibit it, and no dialectical reasoning of philosophy can moderate it—neither sophisticated perplexities nor logical phantasms. Finally, this <term>Kether: Hebrew for "Crown" (כתר), the highest and most hidden of the Sephiroth</term>, a kingdom hanging entirely from the divinity, penetrates through all broken barriers. They call its name *Kether*.

There follow nine more "garments" of God. Whenever the mind of the blessed touches the fringes of these garments, God responds: "Someone has touched me, for I also felt power go out from me." <note>A reference to Jesus’s words in Luke 8:46, here applied to the soul's contact with divine emanations.</note> For these are not so much the enjoyments of the nine beatitudes themselves as they are the *objects* of those enjoyments, which finally correspond to the minds of the blessed within the nine hierarchies <note>Referring to the nine choirs of angels</note> and allow for a perpetual enjoyment of bliss for each. If they should take up that first numeration once again, they will correspondingly fulfill the total of the "Ten Words" written by the finger of God. <note>The Ten Commandments</note>

Concerning *Hochma* and *Binah*, Solomon says in the Proverbs: "Blessed is the man who has attained wisdom and who flows with understanding." <note>Proverbs 3:13</note> And in the book of Job, Elihu the son of Barachel answered: "I said, days will speak and a multitude of years shall know wisdom; but surely it is the spirit in men, and the inspiration of the Almighty God that gives them understanding." <note>Job 32:7-8</note> Concerning <term>Nezah: Hebrew for "Victory" or "Endurance" (נצח), the seventh Sephirah</term>, the prophet Samuel wrote in his book, which you call the First Book of Kings: "The Triumphant One in Israel will not spare, and will not be turned by repentance; for He is not a man that He should repent." <note>1 Samuel 15:29. In the Vulgate, 1 Samuel is titled 1 Kings.</note>

After this divine name, King David, giving thanks to God for the gathered expenses of the future Temple, placed another name near the end of the first book of the "Words of the Days" <note>original: "uerborum dierum," the literal translation of the Hebrew title for Chronicles, *Divrei Hayamim*</note> when he said: "Yours, O Lord, is the magnificence, and the power, and the glory, and the victory, and <term>Hod: Hebrew for "Splendor" or "Majesty" (הוד), the eighth Sephirah</term>," which they translate variously as "praise" or "confession." <note>1 Chronicles 29:11</note> This very garment of divinity will be manifested by the 103rd Psalm, in which God is extolled with these words: "You have put on *Hod* and beauty, clothed with light as with a garment." <note>Psalm 104:1 (numbered 103 in the Vulgate)</note> Following this confession of praise and glory is another holy and venerable name, an illustrious name full of heraldry, which is placed near the middle among the ten Sephiroth like the heart in a living being: <term>Tiphereth: Hebrew for "Beauty" or "Adornment" (תפארת), the sixth Sephirah</term>. So it is found in the book of the "Words of the Days" already cited, which they call "Of Things Left Out" <note>original: "Paralipomenon," the Greek name for the books of Chronicles</note>...

<summary>Reuchlin introduces the ten Sephiroth (divine emanations) of the Kabbalah, explaining how God moves from a hidden, inaccessible state (Kether) into radiant attributes like Wisdom (Hochma) and Understanding (Binah). He aligns these mystical concepts with biblical verses from Jeremiah, Job, and the Psalms to show their presence in scripture.</summary>

<meta>The previous page concluded with a discussion of the Sephiroth as the radiant and emerging aspects of God, beginning with the "Crown," "Wisdom" (Hochma), and "Understanding" (Binah), describing the infinite power of the divine abyss that no logical entanglement can escape.</meta>

# BOOK TWO

...as is said in the song recorded in the book:
> Now therefore, our God, we thank you and praise your glorious name, <term>Tiphereth: Hebrew for "Beauty" or "Glory" (תפארת), the sixth Sephirah, representing balance and harmony</term>. <note>original: "nomen Tiphereth inclytum tuum." Reference to 1 Chronicles 29:13.</note>

Psalm ninety-five treats both:
> <term>Hod: Hebrew for "Splendor" or "Majesty" (הוד), the eighth Sephirah</term> and beauty are in his presence; strength and Tiphereth are in his sanctuary. <note>Psalm 96:6 in modern numbering; 95:6 in the Vulgate.</note>

You may contemplate the power of this name in the eighty-eighth Psalm, where it is said:
> For you are the God of the Tiphereth of their strength and valor. <note>Psalm 89:17; 88:18 in the Vulgate.</note>

And in Isaiah sixty-three:
> So you led your people to make for yourself a name of Tiphereth. <note>Isaiah 63:14.</note>

But after all these things, let us speak of the august belt of the high God, which He Himself is said to have used in the ordering of the world, together with other garments worthy of a prince. For even in civil law, a "belt" <note>original: "cingulum"</note> is the name of an ornament. Concerning this, it is sung in the sixty-fourth Psalm:
> Hear us, O God our Savior, the hope of all the ends of the earth and of the sea afar off; who prepares the mountains by your strength, being girded with <term>Geburah: Hebrew for "Strength" or "Power" (גבורה), the fifth Sephirah, representing divine judgment and constraint</term>. <note>Psalm 65:5-6; 64:6-7 in the Vulgate.</note>

When, therefore, such great majesty emerges from its hiding place, adorned with the crown, wisdom, intelligence, victory, proclamation, and beauty—each of which He girds upon Himself by His own power—what else is this than a King proceeding into His kingdom?
> For God is our King before the ages; He is the Lord of virtues and the King of Glory, the great King above all gods, the King of all the earth; He shall reign forever and beyond.

Wherefore arises that consecrated name <term>Malchuth: Hebrew for "Kingdom" (מלכות), the tenth and final Sephirah, representing the physical world and divine sovereignty</term>, which regards such an illustrious kingdom and such a luminous empire, of which there shall be no end in the eternal course of the vast world. Concerning this, it is written:
> Your kingdom is a kingdom of all ages. <note>Psalm 145:13.</note>

Two insignias of the King follow: the scepter and the sword, that is, mercy and fear. The name of the former is <term>Hesed: Hebrew for "Kindness" or "Mercy" (חסد), the fourth Sephirah</term>, which the royal prophet remembers, saying:
> O Lord, your mercy is in the heavens. <note>Psalm 36:5; 35:6 in the Vulgate.</note>

And not only this, but even above the heavens, as in Psalm one hundred and seven. And Moses, in that divine invocation of the highest Creator among the most sacred adjurations, did not hesitate to bring forth even this name devoted to holiness, saying:
> O Lord, <term>Tetragrammaton: The four-letter name of God (YHVH), considered too sacred to pronounce</term>, God, merciful and gracious, patient and of much mercy,

which is *Hesed*. Finally, there is the principle of divine worship and perfect religion which, if we rightly examine the individual properties of contemplation regarding the outflow of majesty itself, follows last toward us. But when we direct our gaze from ourselves toward the clothing and adornment of the supreme deity, it certainly—not without reason due to the turning of the path—holds the first seats and is called <term>Pahad: Hebrew for "Fear" or "Dread" (פחד), another name for the Sephirah Geburah</term>, which is to say, "fear." From this, that common triviality is quite elegantly sung:
> Fear first made the gods in the world. <note>A famous line from the Roman poet Statius (Thebaid 3.661), often used to discuss the origins of religion.</note>

And there are some, including noble grammarians, who derived the word for "gods" from "fear." For <note>original: "$\phi \acute{o} \beta o \varsigma$"</note> *phobos* in Greek is "fear" in Latin. However, this is not the fear by which the guilty dread torture. For that is worthy of the Furies, harmful demons, and adverse spirits of the dead, of whom those in the underworld complain in Virgil:
> Each of us suffers his own ghost. <note>Virgil, Aeneid 6.743. Usually interpreted as each soul facing its own particular retribution.</note>

That is not the divine and salvific fear, nor does it proceed from God; rather, it is the fury and the worm of a guilty conscience, which daily gnaws at and burns the evil mind and the evil soul. Marcus Tullius Cicero, in his usual manner, argues this excellently and copiously against Piso:
> Do not think, he says, O Conscript Fathers, that as you see in plays, wicked men are terrified by the burning torches of the Furies at the impulse of the gods; rather, it is one's own fraud, one's own crime, one's own wic...

f iiii

The author distinguishes between a holy, constructive fear of God and the destructive, psychological torment of a guilty conscience as described by classical authors like Virgil and Cicero.</summary>
