# ON THE MIRACULOUS WORD

<meta>catchword: things di</meta>

Justice, by its own audacity, disturbs one from health and mind. These are the furies of the impious, these are the flames, these are the torches. But <term>Pahad: Hebrew for "Fear" or "Awe" (פחד), often associated with the Sephirah of Geburah</term> is an internal fear, more excellent than love. Thus it is that a certain most noble philosopher of our age <note>Likely a reference to Giovanni Pico della Mirandola, a contemporary of the author Reuchlin.</note> seemed to me to have correctly said that just as external fear is inferior to love, so internal fear is superior to love. You yourselves surely understand this to be true without any teacher; how much better then will you recognize that such a holy, such a worshipful, such a venerable name was breathed into men by the divine spirit, not only as something consecrated but as something divine, effective, and the firmest sacrament of religion. 

If you doubt this, read the monuments of the secrets, read the holy scriptures written concerning the patriarch Jacob. For Jacob swore by the <term>Pahad</term> of his father Isaac, which is interpreted as "Fear." <note>Genesis 31:53.</note> And he said to his father-in-law: "Unless the God of my father Abraham and the <term>Pahad</term> of Isaac had been with me, you would have sent me away empty-handed." <note>Genesis 31:42.</note> You have heard briefly what those most observant names of ineffable consistency are, which finally—both within themselves and derived outwardly—signify the ten emanations <note>The Sephiroth</note> and their praise, honors, glory, dignities, and sacred titles, indicating the manner of the disposition of the divine essence, and chosen from the most sacred letters themselves. 

To us, therefore, now with such majesty, the whole <term>Elohim: A Hebrew name for God (אלהים), plural in form but treated as singular, often associated with the creative power</term> has gone forth into the universe of things, surrounded by so many ornaments and so many garments; and for that reason, by the plural voice *Elohim*, the one God is named. But when we were treating the words of essence, it was necessary to briefly commemorate that which truth holds: that no name explained by us from those same three <note>Possibly referring to the three highest Sephiroth or the primary divisions of the names.</note>—insofar as it designates the sole and proper essence of God—neglects created things. Rather, any such name, with its own ornament, beauty, surname, and title, invests and names any creature depending on the Highest.

And to begin from the highest, which is "the Same" <note>original: "idem ipsum"</note>: whatever is contained within itself and its own boundaries, whatever has obtained its own nature, an uncommunicated place, a peculiar dimension, or whatever has obtained a distinguished mark of difference and a separate condition of status, is said to be nothing else but "the Same." Concerning that which "Is" <note>original: "quod est"</note>, however, we see how great is its communication into individual created things. For not only does that which rests upon its own substance strive to usurp the title of "Being" <note>original: "entis"</note>, but even that which adheres to another, so that eventually all things whatsoever that depend on the First are said to "Be." 

But what shall we say of fire, when there is nothing anywhere that can be said to lack its spark, to such an extent that <term>Heraclitus: A pre-Socratic Greek philosopher known for his doctrine that fire is the fundamental material of the universe</term> taught that all things were born from fire? Wherefore, to our intellect as well, the distinguished, adorable, and most excellent name of the supersubstantial deity offers itself: the **Innominable** [Unnameable], by which—besides the one, sole, first God—nothing else can be named. It is called among the Greeks the <term>Tetragrammaton Anecphoniton: Greek for "the unutterable four-letter [name]" (τετραγράμματον ἀνεκφώνητον)</term>. Because it works all things, it names nothing of its works after itself, it calls nothing, it affects nothing with its own name. For the interpreters themselves, finding no word equal to it, called it "Lord" <note>The title "Adonai" is traditionally substituted for the Tetragrammaton during reading.</note> at least on account of its super-eminence. This is not because the letters produce this sound—since they do not form any syllable—but signifying the most Simple, they refuse to be combined; and existing as images of an incomprehensible voice, the individual vowels are **Ihuh** <note>The four Hebrew letters Yod, He, Vav, He (יהוה), transliterated here with Latin vowels.</note>. The Chaldeans <note>Here referring to practitioners of ancient Near Eastern wisdom or Kabbalists.</note> are accustomed to depict this properly with only four points arranged in order, lest such holy characters...

### BOOK TWO

...things written by the finger of God might become cheap through daily mortal use. In the same way, the Hebrews have now adopted a custom, especially when they attempt to write on paper. At such times, they either do not write the four letters in full, or they introduce a Chaldean <note>Original: "Chaldaicam." In the Renaissance, the Hebrew square script was often referred to as "Chaldaic."</note> figure. Furthermore, there are those who use only three dots connected by a semicircle, and others use different methods. All of this was invented out of reverence for so great a name, lest the sacrament of the divine word, brought forth by such majesty, be held in contempt through frequent handling.

Now, at last, you have received the most secret seals of all mysteries: the Name <term>Tetragrammaton: The four-letter, unpronounceable name of God (YHVH).</term>, never discovered by man but handed down by the one God. A name holy and to be honored. The one name by which God alone is to be blessed according to ancestral religion. This is the most powerful name which those in the heavens worship, those below observe, and the nature of the universe kisses. When it is absorbed by constant worshipers and takes root in priestly minds, it is said to grant miraculous powers to human capacity. This name was known to the first parents of our lineage, given by divine inspiration along with the very breath of life. For concerning her firstborn, Eve said: "I have possessed a man through the Tetragrammaton." <note>Genesis 4:1. The author notes that the name appears in the Hebrew text here even before it is formally "revealed" to Moses.</note> For so the holy characters contain it, though before this point neither of them is found to have uttered it.

This name was thereafter passed down into the good and fruitful generation of the <term>protoplasts: A term for the "first-formed" humans, Adam and Eve.</term>. For when a most joyful son was born to Seth—who himself was formed in the image and likeness of Adam—on that day the name Tetragrammaton began to be invoked. This was because an invisible race of men, descended from the line of Cain, previously inhabited the earth, with whom God did not have much conversation. Thus Seth, because he was a worshiper of divinity, rejoiced so greatly over his offspring that he invoked the name of God, as taught by his parents, together with his wife and family.

Yet there are some who teach in this passage that Enos, the grandson of Adam, was the first of all to begin the calling of the name Tetragrammaton. Perhaps they are captured by an error of the scribes, not fearing what the <term>Stagirite: A title for the philosopher Aristotle, referring to his birthplace, Stagira.</term> warns in his *Sophistical Refutations*: that those unskilled in idioms are easily deceived. We see this happen to those who would rather be entangled for a lifetime in logical trifles and "spider webs" than spend a few years on divine literature. For when <term>Eusebius Jerome: Saint Jerome, the 4th-century translator of the Bible into Latin (the Vulgate).</term> translated it in this way: "But to Seth also a son was born whom he called Enos; *here* [hic] the name of the Lord began to be invoked," some judged the word "*Hic*" to be a pronoun <note>Meaning "This man (Enos) began..."</note>. In fact, it is an adverb of time, to be pronounced with a circumflex accent, designating the same thing as "at that time." 

This is just as in the works of <term>Maro: The Roman poet Virgil (Publius Vergilius Maro).</term>, who wrote: "*Here* [hic] the almighty father, thrice clear from high heaven, thundered," meaning *then* the almighty father thundered from heaven. Similarly, where it indicates a sign of place, by the same custom it sometimes signifies *when*, as in: "where there is time, I will wash everyone in the fountain." Therefore, as soon as a son was born to Seth, *at this time* [hic] it began to be invoked—that is, *then* the name Tetragrammaton was called upon. 

Indeed, we have now come to such a misuse of the word that the pronoun "*iste*" [that one] is commonly read in place of the adverb "*hic*" [here/then], as if "that" Enos first began to invoke the name of the Lord. Whether that is true, you yourselves can understand both from the sacred letters and from the Chaldean exposition. Thereafter, this most holy name, most worthy of worship, passed by a certain right of inheritance and perpetual succession, not through the generation of the reprobate Cain, but through the diligent religious devotion of Seth...

...[the devil], by his audacity, drives them from sanity and reason. These are the furies of the ungodly; these are the flames, these are the torches. However, *Pahad* <term>Pahad: Hebrew for "Fear" or "Dread," associated in Kabbalah with the Sephirah of Gevurah (Strength/Judgment).</term> is an internal fear, more excellent than love. Thus it is that a certain most noble philosopher of our age <note>Likely referring to Giovanni Pico della Mirandola, a key influence on Reuchlin.</note> seemed to me to have correctly said that just as external fear is inferior to love, so internal fear is superior to love. You yourselves surely understand this to be true without any teacher; how much better then will you recognize that such a holy, worshipful, and venerable name was inspired into men by the divine spirit—not only as something consecrated, but as something divine, effective, and the firmest sacrament of religion.

If you doubt this, read the monuments of the mysteries; read the holy scriptures written concerning the patriarch Jacob. For Jacob swore by the *Pahad* of his father Isaac, which is interpreted as "Fear." And he said to his father-in-law: "Unless the God of my father Abraham and the *Pahad* of Isaac <note>Original: "Pahad Isaac." From Genesis 31:42 and 31:53. Reuchlin emphasizes that Jacob uses this specific title as a divine name.</note> had been with me, you would have now sent me away empty-handed." You have heard briefly what those names of inexpressible consistency are which must be most strictly observed—those ten emanations <term>Sephiroth: The ten attributes or emanations in Kabbalah through which the Infinite reveals itself.</term> derived both within themselves and outwardly, along with their praise, honors, glory, dignities, and the sacred words that signify the manner of the arrangement of the divine essence, chosen from the most holy letters themselves.

For us, therefore, the whole *Elohim* <note>Original: "Elohim." A Hebrew name for God that is grammatically plural but used with singular verbs in the Bible to denote the one God's majesty.</note> has now gone forth into the universe of things with such majesty, clothed in so many ornaments and so many garments; and for that reason, by the plural word *Elohim*, the one God is named. But when we were discussing the words of the essence, we had to briefly commemorate what the truth holds: that no name explained by us from those same three <note>Referring to the three primary names of God discussed in this section of the book.</note>—insofar as it designates the sole and proper essence of God—neglects created things. Instead, it invests and names every creature with its own ornament, beauty, title, and any appellation depending on the Highest.

To begin from the highest, which is "the same itself": whatever is contained within itself and its own limits, whatever has obtained a proper nature, an incommunicable place, a peculiar dimension, a distinguished mark of difference, and a separate condition of status—that is said to be nothing else but "the same itself." Regarding that which "is," we see how great is its communication into each created thing. For not only that which rests upon its own substance, but even that which adheres to another, strives to use the title of "being," so that finally all things whatsoever that depend on the First are said to "be."

But what shall we say of fire, since nothing anywhere is said to lack its spark—so much so that Heraclitus <note>A pre-Socratic Greek philosopher known for his doctrine that fire is the fundamental element of the universe.</note> taught that all things were born from fire? Therefore, to our intellect also, the distinguished, adorable, and most excellent name of the supersubstantial deity offers itself: the Unnameable. By this name, nothing else can be denominated except the one, lone, and first God. It is called among the Greeks the *Tetragrammaton Anecfoniton* <note>Original: "Tetragrammaton Anecfoniton," meaning the "four-lettered unpronounceable name."</note>, that is, the "ineffable four-letters." While this name works all things, it names none of its works after itself; it calls nothing, it affects nothing with its own name. For the translators themselves, finding no word equal to it, called it "Lord" at least on account of its supereminence. This is not because the letters themselves produce this sound—for indeed they form no syllable—but, signifying the most simple thing, they refuse to be compounded. Existing as images of an incomprehensible voice, they are individual vowels: *Ihuh* <note>Original: "Ihuh." A transliteration of the Hebrew YHVH (Yahweh/Jehovah).</note>. The Chaldeans <note>Original: "Chaldaei." Here meaning Hebrew scribes or Kabbalists.</note> are accustomed to depict this properly with only four points arranged in order, lest such holy characters...

## BOOK TWO

...things written by the finger of God might become cheap through daily mortal use. The Hebrews have now adopted a similar custom regarding these matters, especially when they attempt to set them down on paper. In those instances, they introduce either incomplete versions of the four letters or use the Chaldean <term>Chaldean: Here referring to the Aramaic square script used for Hebrew, as opposed to the older paleo-Hebrew script.</term> script. Furthermore, there are some who use only three dots connected by a semicircle, and others in other ways. All this was invented out of reverence for so great a name, lest a sacrament brought forth from the divine word of such majesty be held in contempt through frequent handling.

Now, you have received the most secret seals of all mysteries: the name *Tetragrammaton* <note>original: "Nomen Tetragrammaton." The four-letter name of God, YHWH.</note>, never discovered by man, but handed down by the one God. A name holy and to be honored. The one name by which God alone is to be blessed according to the religion of the fathers. A most powerful name which those above worship, those below observe, and the nature of the universe kisses; a name which, when drank in by tireless worshippers and tasted by priestly minds, is said to grant miraculous powers to human faculty. 

This name was known to the first parents of our race by divine inspiration immediately along with the breath of life. For concerning her firstborn, Eve said:
> I have possessed a man through the Tetragrammaton. <note>original: "Possedi hominem per Tetragrammon." A variation of Genesis 4:1, where Eve exclaims she has brought forth a man with the help of the Lord.</note>

For so the holy characters contain it, which neither of them is found to have uttered before. Thereafter, the name was passed down into the good and fruitful generation of the first-formed. For when a most pleasant son was born to Seth himself (who was formed in the image and likeness of Adam), they began on that day to invoke the name Tetragrammaton. This was because previously, a hateful race of men born from the lineage of Cain inhabited the earth, with whom God did not have much conversation. Seth, therefore, because he was a worshiper of divinity, rejoiced so greatly over his offspring that, together with his wife and family, he invoked the name of God as taught by his parents. 

And yet, there are those who teach in this place that Enos, the grandson of Adam, was the first of all to begin the calling upon the name Tetragrammaton. They are perhaps caught in an error of the scribes, not fearing what the Stagirite philosopher <note>referring to Aristotle, who was born in Stagira.</note> warns in his *Sophistical Refutations*: that those unskilled in idioms are easily deceived. We see this happen to those who more willingly entangle themselves their whole lives in logical trifles and spider webs than in divine literature for a few years. For since Eusebius Jerome <note>Saint Jerome, the translator of the Vulgate Bible.</note> translated it in this way:
> But to Seth also a son was born, whom he called Enos; *here* [Hic] began the name of the Lord to be invoked,

some judged that this part of the speech, "Hic," was a pronoun [meaning "this man"], when it is actually an adverb of time to be pronounced with a circumflex accent. It signifies the same as "then." Just as in Maro <note>referring to the poet Virgil (Publius Vergilius Maro).</note>:
> *Then* [Hic] the almighty father, thrice bright from the high heaven, thundered, <note>original: "Hic pater omnipotens..." from the Aeneid.</note>

that is, *then* the almighty father thundered from heaven. Just as when a sign of place sometimes signifies "when" by similar custom, as he says:
> *where* [when] the time comes, I shall wash them all in the spring. <note>original: "ubi tempus erit..." from Virgil's Eclogues.</note>

Therefore, as soon as a son was born to Seth himself, *here* [at this time] it began to be invoked—that is, *then* the name Tetragrammaton was called upon. But now we have come to such a misuse that the pronoun "that man" [iste] is commonly read instead of the adverb "here" [hic], as if *that* Enos first began to invoke the name of the Lord. Whether this is true, you yourselves understand both from the sacred scriptures and the Chaldean <term>Chaldean exposition: Referring to the Targum, the Aramaic translations and paraphrases of the Hebrew Bible.</term> exposition. Next, this most holy name, most worthy of worship, [was passed] by a certain right of inheritance and perpetual succession, not through the rejected generation of Cain, but through the diligent religious zeal of Seth himself...

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the antiquity of the divine Name. Having previously established that Eve knew the Name, the speaker now traces its lineage through the Patriarchs before addressing a theological contradiction raised by the character Capnion.</meta>

# ON THE MIRACULOUS WORD

...and through diligent faith toward God and integrity of virtue, the Name descended through a continuous line of descendants all the way to Abraham. When he went into the land of Canaan, he built an altar to God and there invoked the name Tetragrammaton <note>original: "nomen Tetragrammaton." The four-lettered name of God, YHWH.</note>. To him, a little while later, God appeared again, saying:

-> I am the Tetragrammaton <note>original: "Ego sum Tetragrammus." Literally "I am the Four-Lettered One."</note> who led you out of Ur of the Chaldeans. <-

His son Isaac, near Beer-sheba <note>original: "Bersaba," the "well of the oath."</note>, imitating his father, also established an altar to God and likewise invoked the name Tetragrammaton. Moreover, when his son Jacob had lain down in Haran, God appeared to him leaning upon a ladder, which, raised from the earth, touched heaven; to him He said in a dream:

-> I am the Tetragrammaton, the God of Abraham your father, and the God of Isaac. <-

When he had awakened, he said, "Surely the Tetragrammaton is in this place." Therefore, when he struggled with God during a later night in the camp and sought His name, God responded with a rebuke:

-> Why do you ask my name? <-

For He judged it superfluous for him to seek what he had previously possessed. Then **CAPNION** <note>Capnion is the Greek-style pseudonym used by the author, Johann Reuchlin; it means "smoky," a pun on his German name (Rauch).</note> said: With your permission, I must interrupt this disputation, for you seem to be led by the force of your so-copious oratory through pathless ways and nearly toward a precipice. Do you not remember that God said to Moses concerning this name:

-> I am the Lord who appeared to Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob as God Almighty, but my name Tetragrammaton I did not reveal to them. <note>A reference to Exodus 6:3.</note> <-

Yet you dare to teach us that they knew such a hidden name, as if it were an inheritance derived to them from the first mother of the human race. If that were so, the writers of the commentaries—great, weighty, and distinguished theologians—would not have correctly passed down to us that it was first revealed to Moses alone, and was not known to the fathers before; nor would it have been instituted by God, but rather devised by Eve and bequeathed to her posterity. You recite true things, **BARUCHIAS** replied, but you conclude false ones. In turn, you will grant me pardon if, beyond my usual modesty, I employ the custom of the logicians in speaking. To be sure, the sacred letters testify that Eve pronounced this name. But I would hardly agree with you that she first "devised" it or "fashioned" it or "instituted" it, as if it were a human invention and a new beginning. And if you hold it in memory, I removed that possibility by saying—so far as my opinion goes—that it was breathed into our first parents along with the breath of life and known by divine means. This is thought to be greatly confirmed by that most noble herald of divine secrets, Isaiah, when he says:

-> You are the Tetragrammaton, our father, our redeemer; from of old, or from eternity, is your name. <-

Therefore, before the time in which our earthly fathers were created, that name existed. As for the words of God you bring forward—He who says He appeared to the forefathers in a former age as God Almighty and did not reveal the name Tetragrammaton to them—it is worthy of investigation so that I may untie this knot. Let us, therefore, make an attempt at it. Who is there who, upon hearing Ezekiel speaking of precious stones, does not remember having seen one of them? And what wealthy merchant is there to whom was never known the Sardius, Topaz, Jasper, Chrysolite, Onyx, Beryl, Sapphire, Carbuncle, Emerald, or any one of these, or many, or all—or even those which Moses added: the Ligure, Agate, and Amethyst? Likewise, who is there who does not know many and various species <note>original: "species." The text breaks here; "cies" is the catchword for the next page.</note> from the lilies of the field, the flowers of the plain, and the herbs of the gardens?

<meta>catchword: cies</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the transmission of the Tetragrammaton from Eve through the Patriarchs (Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob) and addresses the theological tension between this tradition and Exodus 6:3, where God claims the name was not revealed to them. Baruchias begins to resolve this "knot" by arguing for the Name's eternal existence and using an analogy of precious stones.</summary>

# BOOK TWO

...as he noted them with their names and shapes: here is the garden herb, here the beets spreading wide their arms, as the poet says <note>The author is quoting a Latin poetic description of a garden, likely from Columella or a similar Roman source.</note>, and fertile sorrel, mallows, and green violets; here the chickpea and the leeks that owe their name to the head <note>original: "capiti nomen debentia porra." A play on 'caput' (head) and 'capitatus' (forming a head), referring to the bulb of the leek.</note>. Here also the poppy, cold and harmful to the brain, and lettuce, the welcome rest from noble meals, and the heavy gourd hanging down with its wide belly. Every one of us, even the ordinary crowd, knows these things. But only truly excellent and exceptional men know, besides the names, the virtues and powers of each individual thing—things taught by some physician or shown through the practice of an Empiric <term>Empiric: A practitioner of medicine who relies on practical experience and observation rather than solely on theory.</term>. 

Therefore, even a common man does not lie when he says he knows Coral and Emerald <note>original: "Smaragdus."</note> if some merchant showed him how to distinguish them by color, shape, and price. For he knows that Coral is not Emerald, and vice versa. But whoever professes to truly know these stones must be thoroughly learned in their powers and operations, and why each one is of such great value: that an Emerald hung around the neck turns away the occasion of Epilepsy, and Coral repels stomach pain, as well as the certain law for hanging them and other things belonging to that craft. 

Whoever, therefore, has not been instructed in such things—whoever he may be—even if he has named and chosen the gem, I would rightly say that I have not yet "indicated" the gem itself to him, nor the herb, nor any other thing. For I have not yet shown him its property, its power, and its operations. In this same way, God spoke to Moses about this name, because He appeared to the fathers as God Almighty. In His omnipotence in previous years, God Himself performed miracles. But when the time for the liberation of the Israelites came, He kindly wished to delegate His divine and miraculous power to men in the hidden virtue of this name, the Tetragrammaton <term>Tetragrammaton: The four-letter Hebrew name of God, YHWH, considered too sacred to pronounce.</term>, which was never before revealed to any mortal. For long ago, by a singular pact, He included in that word the majesty of energy and miraculous power, which was hidden from the patriarchs and our ancestors until the time of Moses. 

There are, therefore, three things: there is the name, there is the meaning, and there is the divine pact or confederation. The name lifts the human mind. The meaning clings to the lifted mind. Our ancestors knew these two—both the name and the meaning—and therefore they invoked God in that same name, and He was favorable to them in their lives. But the third thing, which is much greater, in which the surpassing power of the word delegated to man by the Most High God is contained, was shown to Moses and not to the fathers. 

This is weighed more clearly from the Hebrew speech. For where we read "I appeared," the Hebrew letters contain *Nodaethi* <note>original: "Nodaethi." A grammatical discussion of Exodus 6:3. Reuchlin argues the word means "I caused to be known" rather than simply "I was known."</note>, that is, "I made it to be known." For God did not cause His name Tetragrammaton to be known by the previous age, nor let it be understood what great wonders lie hidden within it, entrusted to human fragility; rather, it was known and worshipped and observed generally, like the other divine names. But now, God has set this name forward and placed it at the peak above all other names as something wonderful, great, and terrible—known by the miracles performed by God through man. Therefore Isaiah says: 

-> O Tetragrammaton, you are my God, I will exalt you, I will praise your name, for you have done wonderful things. <note>original: "Tetragramme deus meus es tu... feciſti mirabilia." Referring to Isaiah 25:1.</note> <-

This is what it means to know the name Tetragrammaton: when not only the characters and the word itself do not escape us, but also its pronunciation, which is hidden, and its secret virtue.

The author uses analogies of botany and gemology to distinguish between superficial recognition and deep, functional knowledge.</summary>

<header>->ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD<-</header>

...through study and constant faith toward God, and endowed with integrity of virtue, it descended through a continuous line of posterity all the way to Abraham. When he went into the land of Canaan, he built an altar to God and there invoked the name Tetragrammaton <note>original: "Tetragrammaton." The four-letter Hebrew name of God, often rendered as YHWH.</note>. To him, a little while later, God appeared again, saying: "I am the Four-Lettered One <note>original: "Tetragrammus."</note> who led you out of Ur of the Chaldeans." His son Isaac <note>original: "Ishac."</note>, near the Well of the Oath <term>Well of the Oath: Beersheba, from the Hebrew 'Beer' (well) and 'Sheba' (oath or seven).</term>, imitating his father, also established an altar to God and similarly invoked the name Tetragrammaton. But Jacob, his son, when he had lain down in Haran, saw God leaning upon a ladder which, raised from the earth, touched the heavens; and God said to him in a dream: "I am the Tetragrammaton, the God of Abraham your father, and the God of Isaac." When Jacob awoke, he said: "Surely the Tetragrammaton is in this place." Therefore, when he wrestled with God in the camp on a later night and asked His name, God replied with a rebuke: "Why do you ask my name?" For He judged it superfluous for him to seek what he had already possessed. 

Then, "By your leave," **CAPNION** <note>Johann Reuchlin, the author, uses the pseudonym "Capnion," which is a Greek translation of his German name "Reuchlin" (from 'Rauch', meaning smoke).</note> said, "I must interrupt the discussion. For you seem to be led by the force of your so copious speech through trackless paths and nearly over a precipice. Do you not remember that regarding this name God said to Moses: 'I am the Lord who appeared to Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob as God Almighty, but my name Tetragrammaton I did not reveal to them'? Yet you dare to teach us that those men knew so secret a name, as if it were an inheritance derived to them from the first mother of the human race <note>Refers to Eve.</note>. If that were so, the writers of the commentaries and the great, serious, and distinguished theologians would not have rightly handed down to us that it was first revealed to Moses alone, and was not known to the fathers before him; nor would it have been established by God, but rather devised by Eve and bequeathed to her descendants."

"You indeed recite true things," **BARVCHIAS** said, "but you conclude false ones. In turn, you will grant me pardon if, setting aside modesty, I use the custom of the logicians <term>logicians: original "dialecticorum," referring to those skilled in formal argument and debate.</term> in speaking. Certainly, the sacred letters testify that Eve pronounced this name. But that she first devised it, or fashioned it, or established it—as if it were a human invention and a new beginning—to that I shall by no means agree. And if you recall, I removed that occasion by saying that, in my opinion, it was breathed into our first parents along with the breath of life and was known divinely. This is something the most noble herald of divine secrets, Isaiah <note>original: "Esaias."</note>, is thought to strongly confirm when he says: 'You, O Tetragrammaton, are our father, our redeemer; your name is from the age' or 'from eternity.' Therefore, before the time in which our earthly fathers were created, that name existed. Furthermore, as for the words of God you bring forward—He who says He appeared to the forefathers in a previous age as God Almighty and did not reveal the name Tetragrammaton to them—it is worthy of investigation so that I may untie this knot. Let us therefore make a trial of this matter. 

Who is there who, upon hearing Ezekiel speaking of precious stones, does not remember having seen at least one of them? And what wealthy merchant is there to whom the Sard, Topaz, Jasper, Chrysolite, Onyx, Beryl, Sapphire, Carbuncle, Emerald <note>original: "Sardius, Topazius, Iaspis, Chrysolithus, Onyx, Beryllus, Sapphirus, Carbunculus, Smaragdus."</note>—or some of them, or many, or all, or even those which Moses added: the Ligure, Agate, and Amethyst <note>original: "Ligurius, Achates, Amethistus."</note>—was never known? Likewise, who is there who does not recognize many and various species among the lilies of the field, the flowers of the countryside, and the herbs of the garden...

<summary>This page discusses the historical transmission of the Tetragrammaton from Eve to the Patriarchs, debating a biblical contradiction where God tells Moses the name was previously unknown. Baruchias argues that while the name was spoken by ancestors, its true divine power and essence remained a hidden revelation.</summary>

### BOOK TWO

...as if one had noted the species along with their names and appearances: Here is the garden herb, here the beets spreading their arms wide—as the poet says—and the fertile dock, the mallows, and the green violets; here the chickpea, and the leeks that owe their name to the head <note>original: "capiti nomen debentia porra." The Latin word for leeks, *porrum*, was often etymologically linked to the "head" because of its bulbous shape.</note>. Here also is the poppy, cold and harmful to the brain, and the lettuce, a pleasant rest after noble meals, and the heavy gourd hanging down into a wide belly. All these things, even the commonest among our flock know; but only excellent and distinguished men know, besides the names, the virtues and powers of each, which either a learned physician or an experienced Empiric <term>Empiric: A practitioner of medicine who relies on observation and experience rather than just theoretical dogmas.</term> has indicated. 

Therefore, even a rustic man does not lie when he says he knows Coral and Emerald, if some merchant has shown him the method of distinguishing them by color, shape, and price. For he knows that a Coral is not an Emerald, and vice versa. But he who professes to know these stones without pretense must be thoroughly learned in their powers and operations—what sort of thing and how great each one is. For example, he must know that an Emerald hung from the neck wards off the onset of epilepsy, and Coral repels stomach pain, as well as the certain law for hanging them <note>The author refers to "astrological" or ritual timing for wearing amulets to ensure their effectiveness.</note> and other things consistent with that craft. Therefore, if anyone has not been instructed in such things, whoever he is, even if he has named and chosen a gem, I would rightly say—as I am teaching—that I have not yet truly shown him the gem itself, nor the herb, nor any other thing. For I have not yet shown him its property, its power, and its operations.

In this way, God spoke to Moses about this name, because He had appeared to the fathers as God Almighty <note>original: "deo omnipotente." Referring to the Hebrew name *El Shaddai*.</note>. For in His omnipotence, in former years, God Himself performed miracles. But when the time for the liberation of Israel arrived, He kindly wished to delegate His divine and miraculous power to men through the hidden virtue of this name, the Tetragrammaton <note>original: "Tetragrammati." The four-letter name of God (YHWH).</note>, which had never been revealed to any mortal before. For long ago, by a unique covenant, He enclosed within that word a majesty of energy and a miraculous power that remained hidden from the patriarchs and our ancestors until the time of Moses. 

Therefore, there are three things: there is the name, there is the thing signified, and there is the divine pact or confederation. The name elevates the human mind. The thing signified adheres to the elevated mind. Our ancestors knew these two—both the name and the thing signified—and therefore they invoked God by that same name, and He, in turn, was favorable to them in their lives. But the third thing, which is much greater, by which the supereminent power of the word is contained and delegated to man by the highest God, was indicated to Moses and not to the fathers. 

This is more clearly considered from the Hebrew language. For instead of what we read as "I indicated," the Hebrew text contains *Nodaethi* <note>original: "Nodaethi." From the Hebrew root Y-D-A (to know). Reuchlin is discussing the nuances of the Hiphil or Niphal forms in Exodus 6:3.</note>, which is "I made to be known." For God did not make His name Tetragrammaton known by the previous age, nor the great wonders that lie hidden deep within it—which are now entrusted to human fragility—but rather it was acknowledged, worshiped, and observed generally, like other divine names. 

But now, God has placed this name in the lead and set it at the summit above all other names as wonderful, as great, as terrible, and as known from the miracles performed by God through man. Wherefore Isaiah says: "O Tetragrammaton, you are my God, I will exalt you, I will confess your name, for you have done wonderful things." <note>Isaiah 25:1. The author substitutes the Tetragrammaton for "Lord" (Dominus).</note> This is what it means to know the name Tetragrammaton: when not only the characters and the word do not escape us, but also its pronunciation (which is hidden) and its secret virtue...

<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous section: just as knowing the name of a plant or gemstone is superficial compared to knowing its hidden medicinal or magical virtues, knowing the name "Tetragrammaton" as a mere word is secondary to understanding the divine power and "mirific" (wonder-working) efficacy it contains.</meta>

### THE MIRIFIC WORD

And the signs of miracles that follow, which will become manifest both from the fourth chapter of Exodus and from the very speech of God hereafter. "I," says the Lord, "will lead you out of the workhouse <note>original: "ergastulo." This refers to the slave-prisons of Egypt.</note> of the Egyptians, and I will rescue you from servitude, and I will redeem you with an exalted arm and great judgments; and I will take you to me as a people, and I will be to you as a God, and you shall know that I am the Tetragrammaton <term>Tetragrammaton: The four-letter Hebrew name of God (YHWH), often considered too sacred to pronounce.</term> your God." You shall know now the Tetragrammaton. By what teaching? Because "in an outstretched arm"—behold the miracles! This is the knowledge that the Peripatetics <term>Peripatetics: Followers of Aristotle’s philosophy, who emphasized learning through observation and logic.</term> call "posterior knowledge from the sign," from which faith arises. You shall know; but by what capacity of our fragility? Because "in great judgments." For judgment belongs to God, and the sentence of God, which the entire nation must obey without any preparation—behold, here is hope. You shall know; but what will be the manner of knowing? Surely, the covenant of God with man: "Because I will take you to me as a people, and I will be to you as a God"—behold, here is love and affection on both sides. 

For there is a need not only for a common obligation and bond, but we must necessarily enter into a new covenant with God based upon the Word—the Tetragrammaton—if we desire its power to work within us. For the Lord of all things spoke to Moses about this very future covenant of society with the Jewish people, saying: "Seeing, I have seen the affliction of my people who are in Egypt." Therefore, they had been adopted as the people of God beforehand. And what else is it that he says now, "I will take you to me as a people," except for this pact, this new fellowship which contemplative men enter into at God’s warning, and strike a covenant most recently upon the Tetragrammaton? 

This is what the messenger of the Tetragrammaton <note>original: "nuncius Tetragrammus." This refers to the Angel of the Lord.</note> replied to Manoah when he asked for his name: "Why do you ask my name?" But it is "mirific" <note>original: "mirificum." Meaning wonder-working or miraculous.</note>; by which response, he had recognized the name, and upon it he sacrificed a young goat, which among the ancients was a sign of a covenant, as you also read in Homer. Therefore it follows: "And he placed the offering upon the rock, offering it to the Tetragrammaton, who does wonders." 

From this arose what Manoah said to his wife: "We shall surely die, because we have seen the Tetragrammaton." He would not have feared to see angels, whose frequent conversation with our ancestors was within the memory of the fathers. But because he had understood the voice of God through Moses saying, "No man shall see me and live," he therefore dreaded that he had seen God, until he was comforted. To the Patriarch Jacob, the name was not revealed, even when he asked; instead, this alone was answered: "Why do you ask my name?" and it is not added "which is wonderful." But to this man [Manoah], because he had seen the miracle of the burning offering, the answer is given concerning the name which is "mirific," by which it could be known that it was the Tetragrammaton. 

Although you read in that passage of the book of Judges, "My name which is wonderful" <note>original: "mirabile."</note>, it does not hold the same meaning here as it does in the eighth Psalm: "O Lord our Lord, how wonderful <note>original: "admirabile."</note> is your name in all the earth," that is, worthy of admiration and glorious—which the word *Adir* <gloss>Hebrew: 'addir (majestic/mighty)</gloss> signifies—but here it is *Peli* <gloss>Hebrew: pil’i (wonderful/incomprehensible)</gloss>, which refers to an operator of miracles. 

You have here the origin of so great a name, its form, its significance, and its power, which fits only God, the best and greatest; which among all names is called "Great" by way of excellence. Just as Joshua spoke to God: "What will you do for your great name?" which in Deuteronomy is called "Terrible," and in the Psalms "Good," "honorable," "holy," "laudable," "eternal," "beatific," and in...

### BOOK TWO

In Isaiah, the Name is called Excellent, ancient, and from eternity; in Jeremiah, it is Invoked over us; in Ezekiel, it is Gone out into the nations; in Daniel, it is Blessed; in Amos, the Tetragrammaton is His name; and in Malachi, it is Great among the nations from the rising of the sun even unto its setting, and it is terrible among the peoples. I shall, most learned men, intentionally omit those childish little questions of the grammarians in this so excellent a cause and in this so healthy a business—questions in which great theologians involve themselves with such zeal at this point that they have even descended (it is a shame to hear) into empty contentions. 

The only thing that remains for me is that celebrated and most powerful pronunciation which is said to be most secretly hidden in the abyss of mysteries. If this name possesses that pronunciation, it is not "ineffable" <note>Ineffable: Something that cannot be spoken or expressed in words.</note>; if it does not possess it, it is not a name. What then shall we do? Shall we deny that it is a name, when we have brought forward such a great number of witnesses against us? Shall we deny it is ineffable, which all—both Greeks and Latins—call it? Regarding this, Solomon says in Proverbs: "Who has gathered the waters as in a garment? Who has established all the ends of the earth? What is his name, and what is the name of his son, if you know?" <note>original: "Quis colligauit aquas quasi in uestimento... quod est nomen eius..."</note> It is as if he were to say: "Speak the name itself if you know it, and utter that which is unutterable," as if he considers it difficult for us to hold the explanation of that pronunciation. 

I shall cite for you your Sidonian Hermes, that Trismegistus <note>Hermes Trismegistus: A legendary Hellenistic figure often identified with the Egyptian god Thoth, regarded as the author of the Hermetic Corpus, which explores alchemy, magic, and philosophy.</note>—that is, "thrice-greatest"—in the dialogue of *Aesculapius*. For he says: "I do not hope that the creator of all majesty and the father or lord of all things can be called by a single name, however much it may be composed of many." However, the God of our fathers—not of the profane, nor of the poets, nor of the philosophers, nor of the pagans, but the God of Abraham, the God of Isaac, and the God of Jacob—imposed a proper name upon His own nature, which is both unknown and unnamable. He is also the institutor and teacher of that "impossible pronunciation." He taught Moses how to utter the unutterable name when Moses was complaining about his stuttering tongue <note>original: "ineptitudine linguae." Referring to Moses' heavy tongue mentioned in Exodus 4:10.</note>. 

By now, that pronunciation is known to you from that passage, which is comprised of three clauses under a single period. I would not dare to step beyond the boundaries of these mysteries, lest some unworthy person standing by the wall should overhear. Yet, because a name—as Plato says in his *Cratylus* <note>Plato’s *Cratylus* is a foundational philosophical text on the nature of language and whether names naturally fit the things they describe.</note>—is an imitation of the essence itself, this happens to us just as it does to painters or musicians through a manifold mixing and various distinctions, until we grasp what we wish to imitate. It is as if we desire to look into a noble structure through sharp investigation: first we resolve its individual parts, then we rebuild it as it was first constructed, and finally we preserve the structure itself in our mind. 

The "distinction" or resolution of names is made into syllables, and syllables into letters, and letters into their properties and accidents. When the letters are entwined again, the complexity of the word expresses the thing itself. Therefore, our ancestors, relying on divine zeal and heavenly light, obtained the most full exposition of the graces of this name in a similar way, and deigned to hand it down to us, their descendants—this you call the *Semhammaphoras* <term>Semhammaphoras: A Latinization of the Hebrew "Shem HaMephorash" (the Explicit Name). In Kabbalah, this refers to a 72-fold name of God derived from three verses in Exodus.</term>. I have known many who, trusting in this foundation of secrets, attempted things that nature itself would not have been able to achieve. But lest you be without this very thing yourselves, I will complete it in a few words. The Lord of the four letters <note>A reference to the Tetragrammaton (YHWH).</note> leaned upon the top of the ladder rising from the earth and touching the heavens with its summit; Jacob lay on the ground, resting in sleep. The angels of God first ascend,

## ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

...descend afterward. To Jacob himself the promise is brought; he wakes up, affirms that the Lord is in this place, trembles and is afraid, offers sacrifice at a raised altar, and vows a vow. Let us complete the ladder with its steps, for in the Song of the sons of Korah it is said: "According to your name, O God, so also is your praise." Therefore, the *Schemhammaphoras* <term>Schemhammaphoras: From the Hebrew "Shem Ha-Mephorash," meaning the "Explicit Name." In Western occultism, it refers to a 72-fold name of God derived from three verses in Exodus.</term> will be the praise of God; therefore, the steps of the ladder are divided according to His praises. Now, Hebrew praises are called *Thillim* <note>original: "Thillim," referring to the Hebrew "Tehillim," the Book of Psalms.</note>, by which name the Book of Psalms is titled. Therefore, the *Schemhammaphoras* will be a revealed exposition of the divine name from the Book of Psalms.

Each individual verse marks one step of the ladder, and each one of them contains three sacred characters <note>The 72 names are each composed of three Hebrew letters, extracted from Exodus 14:19-21.</note>. From these, by the suggestion of the Spirit of God, one distinguished seal is forged, to be cherished and observed, formed of three letters, in which they say a divine angel is included, carrying with him the majesty of the divine name. These, moreover, are the 72 angels who ascend and descend. Into their powers the venerable name of the divinity is resolved, and by these same angelic syllables it is at last restored. It is not that the name has ceased to be when resolved, nor begun to be again when restored, but it is just like the sun through the multiplication of its rays and poured-out light. For the most contemplative of Greek philosophers <note>A reference to Plato and his analogy of the sun in "The Republic."</note> says that what the sun is to sensible things, God is to intelligible things. This is the psalm-chanting of the seventy-two verses, which I shall pronounce in English <note>Latin in original.</note> according to the custom of your church, and I shall point out each sacred seal with my finger; therefore, pay attention most diligently. Wherever the four-letter name, that is, the Tetragrammaton <term>Tetragrammaton: The sacred four-letter name of God (YHWH), often considered too holy to speak aloud.</term>, is placed, there I shall borrow the interpretation of "Adonai" <term>Adonai: A Hebrew title meaning "My Lord," traditionally spoken aloud wherever the Tetragrammaton appears in text.</term> in the manner of the ancients.

# BOOK TWO

The Lord guards you; the Lord is your protection at your right hand. <note>Psalm 121:5. Each of these verses serves as an invocation for one of the 72 angels of the Schemhammaphoras.</note>

They who fear the Lord have hoped in the Lord; He is their helper and their protector.
And my soul is troubled exceedingly; but You, O Lord—how long?
From the rising of the sun unto its setting, the name of the Lord is worthy of praise.
The Lord is just in all His ways, and holy in all His works.
Blessed be the name of the Lord, from this time forth and forevermore.
See that I have loved Your commandments, O Lord; enliven me according to Your mercy.

<sig>g ii</sig> <note>original: "g ii"; this is a signature mark used by early printers to ensure the pages were bound in the correct sequence.</note>

**Vocabulary:** Lord, Psalms, justice, mercy, forever (eternity), thoughts, generation, lifts up, blessed.

<meta>This page continues the list of 72 specific biblical verses associated with the names of the angels of the Shem HaMephorash, followed by a philosophical defense of their non-sequential order. It builds on the previous page's list of verses, moving from the invocations themselves into a dialogue regarding their mystical mathematical structure.</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD <note>original: "DE VERBO MIRIFICO"</note>

> Serve the Lord in gladness; enter into his presence with exultation. <note>Psalm 100:2</note>
> Behold, the eyes of the Lord are upon those who fear him, and upon those who hope in his mercy. <note>Psalm 33:18</note>
> Turn, O Lord, how long? And be gracious toward your servants. <note>Psalm 90:13</note>
> Forsake me not, O Lord my God; do not depart from me. <note>Psalm 38:21</note>
> Delight in the Lord, and he will give you the petitions of your heart. <note>Psalm 37:4</note>
> Give thanks to the Lord, for he is good; for his mercy endures forever. <note>Psalm 106:1</note>
> The Lord is the portion of my inheritance and of my cup; you are he who will restore my inheritance to me. <note>Psalm 16:5</note>
> In the beginning, God created the heaven and the earth. <note>Genesis 1:1</note>
> I will praise the Lord greatly with my mouth, and in the midst of many I will praise him. <note>Psalm 109:30</note>
> Turn, my soul, into your rest, because the Lord has dealt bountifully with you. <note>Psalm 116:7</note>

Do not let this seemingly mixed collection of verses disturb you or lead you into amazement, as if it were without order or accidental, consisting not by divine fate but by human will and clever artifice. For what both Plutarch and other students of poetry write concerning the poems of Homer—that they were not in the beginning composed in the numbered order they are now, but were scattered and distracted, and finally brought into order by the grammarians who lived around the time of Pisistratus and Aristarchus—something not unlike this our own people say regarding the hymns of the Psalms, as do even your own men most learned in the Sacred Scriptures. For example, Hilary <note>Saint Hilary of Poitiers (c. 310–367 AD), a prominent bishop and theologian</note>, a leader and bishop, says in his commentaries on that book: "It must not be ignored that among the Hebrews the numbering of the Psalms is indistinct, and they were written without the notation of order. For there, first or second or third or fiftieth or hundredth are not pre-noted, but they are mixed together into one without any prescription of order. For Ezra <note>original: "Esras"</note>, as ancient traditions report, collected them when they were uncomposed and dispersed according to the diversity of authors and times into a single volume." Since, therefore, that tradition of the 72-fold Name <note>original: "Semhammaphoras", the Kabbalistic name of God derived from Exodus</note> existed before the seventy-two translators—who are said to have established both the number and order of the Psalms—and indeed long before, you can certainly judge that it is not without reason that these verses, conceived by divine inspiration, do not serve human number and order. Rather, they are subject to a divine number, specifically their own, which is innumerably numerable and infinitely finite, from which they finally proceeded. Furthermore, they are referred back into the numbers of the same adorable name, the Tetragrammaton <note>the four-letter name of God, YHWH</note>, in a manner known only to the wise.

Then **SIDONIUS** said: You seem to me to have clearly pointed out what you promised with your finger. But I do not quite understand what you wish the number of that Name to be, into which the seventy-two are referred, unless it be the fourfold grouping of letters <note>original: "quaternionem literarum"</note> of which so celebrated a name is said to consist, and for that reason is called the Tetragrammaton. I certainly find that the most ancient philosophers and princes of philosophy always valued this more than the other secrets of any religion whatsoever. Most especially Pythagoras himself and the noble Pythagoreans, who likewise call that which you name the Tetragrammaton—according to the custom of their own discipline—the Tetractys <term>Tetractys: A mystical triangular symbol consisting of ten points in four rows, representing the organization of the universe</term>. This they prefer above all gods and virtues of the gods, and they describe it as the fountain of perpetual nature flowing into our souls. A witness to this is the poem they call the "Golden Verses of Pythagoras," in which the Tetractys is used in place of an oath, as the highest thing upon which faith and the credibility of things might rest with...

<meta>This page continues the discourse in Book Two of "On the Wonder-Working Word," moving from the structure of the biblical verses to a deep philosophical exploration of the number four (the quaternary) as the foundational pattern of the universe, linking classical philosophy, mathematics, and Hebrew mysticism.</meta>

# BOOK TWO
->g iii<-

...with the perfect definition of its own substance. The most excellent poets, just as they followed other parts of his philosophy, also imitated this most subtle and sharp kind of contemplation: those who divided the chaos of the world into four elements; the heavens into the four parts of the year; the air into the East, West, South, and North winds <note>original: "Eurum, Zephyrum, Austrum & Boream"</note>; and the earth into four spaces according to five zones. They distinguished divinity into the common, the noble, the celestial, and the royal palaces; and humanity into the flesh, the spirit, the departed souls <note>original: "manes"</note>, and the shadow. We read of these things partly at the beginning of Ovid’s *Metamorphoses*, and partly they are shown in the poems commonly circulated:

*Man has twice two things: the ghost, the flesh, the spirit, and the shadow.*
*These twice two places receive those four.*
*The earth covers the flesh, the shadow flutters around the tomb.*
*The underworld <note>original: "Orcus"</note> holds the ghosts, the spirit seeks the stars.*

The Stoics praise the splendor of our life based on the four virtues. Mathematicians find the first solid demonstration in the number four <note>original: "quaternario"</note>. The Physicists divide the judging powers into four parts: intellect, reason, opinion, and sense. Arithmeticians call "one" the power of the square <note>original: "tetragonum"</note>. For when the "one" breaks out into action, it is a square number. It is the first to fulfill the entire difference of numbers, perfectly completing the number ten <note>term: "denarium" (the decad), which is the sum of 1+2+3+4</note> under four terms. For one, two, three, and four give birth to the substance of the number ten; because of this, I might more easily believe those ten *Sephiroth* <note>term: "Sephiroth" are the ten attributes or emanations in Kabbalah through which the Infinite reveals itself</note> which you dared to introduce after the name *Tetragrammaton* <note>term: "Tetragrammaton" refers to the four-letter Hebrew name of God, YHWH</note> (seeing as it is a matter unusual to the common people). 

What more? That very same number, within those same four limits, contains all the harmony of music; indeed, it gathers together the whole of mathematics: the point, the line, the surface, and the body. It embraces all of physics, in which the power of nature’s nursery is taught first; second, the natural sprouting; third, the maturing form; and fourth, the composite whole. Finally, it suggests the whole of metaphysics: essence, being, power, and action. This triplicity surely unfolds a marvelous four-fold nature <note>original: "quaternitatem"</note>, so that in the beginning there is the point, the seed, the essence. In the second, the line, the sprouting, the being. In the third, the surface, the form, the power. In the fourth, the body, the composite, the action. 

These seem to me to be the twelve stones which you, before Baruchia, recited by their names, and which your Moses reduced into a four-fold order. Therefore, in the entire university of the world and in its individual parts, that divine four-fold nature shines back. In the higher realms (as it is in Orphic theology), the Muse, Dionysus, Apollo, and Venus—those celestial deities—instill God-making attractions into our minds: the Muse instills poetry, Dionysus mystery, Apollo prophecy, and Venus love. In the heavenly bodies, there are four triplicities. Beneath the heavens, four seasons. Beneath time, four qualities. Beneath the qualities, four elements. Beneath the elements: substance, quality, quantity, and motion. Finally, beneath substance itself: the corporeal, the vegetable, the sensitive, and the intelligent. Under quantity: the point, length, width, and depth (just as we say of number: one, two, three, four). Under quality: hot, cold, dry, and moist. Under motion: ascending, descending, proceeding, and circular. 

We have spoken of things above the heavens, eternal, temporal, and mixed. Let us see in what way the human soul has associated itself with this companionable four-fold nature. And indeed, our soul ascends through four steps to the ultimate spirit within it: through sense, imagination, phantasy, and intelligence. You hold, therefore, the nature...

<summary>This page argues that the number four (the Quaternary) is the underlying structure of all reality, from Ovidian poetry and Stoic virtue to the Hebrew Tetragrammaton and the Kabbalistic Sephiroth. It maps this four-fold pattern across mathematics, physics, metaphysics, and the levels of human consciousness.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical defense of the number four as the foundation of the cosmos, transitioning from classical elemental theory to the mystical significance of the Tetractys and the Hebrew Tetragrammaton.</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD
->q<-
<meta>catchword: q</meta>

...that the universe consists of a quaternity <note>A grouping of four things; here, the fundamental structure of reality.</note>, which the Architect of all things would not have established in this way unless there were some "Idea" of the quaternary within Himself before He turned to the work of creation. It is for this reason that the Spartans <note>original: "Lacedaemonii"</note> seem to me to have correctly depicted the highest Jupiter with four ears, since he himself is the supreme quaternity—that is, according to the Pythagoreans, the <term>Tetractys</term> <note>A triangular figure of ten points arranged in four rows, which the followers of Pythagoras used to symbolize the harmony of the universe.</note>. Its emanation is found through "form" in the angels, through "true participation" in rational souls, through "imagination" in those lacking reason, and through "shadow" in the shapes of physical bodies. In its usual manner, it flows down like an eternal rain from the heights to the depths, watering all things.

"I listen to you most gladly, Sidonius," said **BARUCHIAS**, "regarding what you apply to our own discipline. For as I see it, everything squares correctly with the rule. Indeed, I believe this is that famous river that goes out from that celebrated place of pleasure <note>Eden</note> to water paradise with four heads. This is what Orpheus and Homer call 'Oceanus,' the origin of divine and human things: first, always existing; second, flowing into the generation of the gods; third, watering earthly things; and fourth, dripping into every fluid nature. This is that River Chebar <note>original: "fluuius Cobar"</note> beside which Ezekiel, touched by the hand of God, saw the likeness of four living creatures, and four within four. In that same river were also four cherubim on four wheels, and a wheel within a wheel. This is the same living creature he had seen by the River Chebar. This is the very 'great sea' in which, according to the vision of Daniel, the four winds of heaven fought, and four great beasts ascended from the sea. They wished the divinity of this power to be called Neptune, who is said in the Orphic hymns to drive a four-horse chariot."

Therefore, the mystery of the <term>Tetragrammaton</term> <note>The four-letter Hebrew name of God, YHWH, considered too sacred to pronounce.</note> was known to the most ancient wise men, even among foreign nations; although it was not used publicly, it was not unknown in private. These men, handing down all the disciplines of the best things under a veil of mystery, appointed certain most skillful craftsmen in this business to appropriately carve the whole universe of things as if with a chisel, forming it into a single image for our sight. They did this so there would not be a frequent need for the work of this supreme Name, which they feared might be looked upon with contempt if it became common. Indeed, they called this a "sacred image," and its carvers were for that reason called <term>hieroglyphs</term> <note>Literally "sacred carvings," Reuchlin here refers to the practitioners of symbolic writing.</note>. It was represented in this way: First an eye, second a staff, third a shield, and fourth a serpent.

See how aptly these figures are visible even to the uninitiated and the unrefined. But there exist numbers that are much simpler and "formal" <note>Relating to the "Form" or essence of a thing in the Platonic sense.</note>, which we who are sharp-minded contemplate more clearly than physical shapes, and which we believe approach closer to divine things. For this reason, this very famous Name is much more desirable to us philosophers, for it embraces at once the sacred mysteries of geometry through the shapes of its characters, and of arithmetic through the forms of numbers. So that I may perform the duty of a mathematician for all, I might most correctly say: "One, the even-odd, the whole, the reduction." Nothing is prior to the infinite, nothing prior to the one, and nothing prior to the point; and this itself is the principle of all things. Then, all things exist up to the "one" and up to the "point," and beyond the one and the point there is nothing. Here, therefore, is the end of all things. Now you hold the first letter of the Tetragrammaton, which reflects the shape of a point, and is called <term>Iod</term> <note>The Hebrew letter Yod (י), the smallest letter, often associated with a point or the start of creation.</note> (that is, the beginning), and designates the "decad" <note>The number ten; the letter Yod has the numerical value of 10.</note> (that is, the end of number), and indeed includes every number.

<summary>This page explores the "quaternity" (the number four) as a universal pattern, linking the Pythagorean Tetractys, the four rivers of Eden, and the visions of Ezekiel to the Hebrew Tetragrammaton. Reuchlin argues that ancient sages used symbolic "hieroglyphs" to represent God's name and explains the mystical significance of the letter Yod as both a starting point and the number ten.</summary>

<keywords>quaternity, Tetractys, Tetragrammaton, Pythagoras, Ezekiel’s vision, River Chebar, hieroglyphs, Yod, sacred arithmetic, Baruchias, Sidonius</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discourse on the mystical properties of numbers, moving from the fourfold nature of the universe to the primary nature of the Monad (the One) and the procession of being from unity into multiplicity.</meta>

## BOOK TWO
->g iiii<-

...it penetrates most simply, since it carries the mark of unity. Unity, moreover, is the common measure of all numbers. And as the mathematicians <note>original: "Arithmetici"</note> write, it holds the place of the point; for while the point is the beginning of an interval and of length, it is itself capable of neither interval nor length. Just as a point placed upon a point becomes nothing but a point—as if you were to join nothing to nothing—so it happens that unity multiplied by itself produces nothing other than itself. For instance, if you say "once one," it is nothing but one; or again, "once one" does not wander any wider, but remains in the one. Indeed, it is always the same and immutable substance, the leader of the odd number sequence, and in a way the effecter and form of "oddness." It is the beginning, the communication, and the end of all things—above number, above the whole, free of division, and lacking parts. 

For this reason, it most suitably demonstrates the supreme author of this world to our "sick eyes" <note>A common philosophical metaphor for human perception being clouded by material concerns.</note> and describes the nature of divinity to human weakness—the source from which, out of which, through which, in which, and toward which all things exist. Let us use, if you wish, the testimony of the most divine theologian Dionysius the Areopagite <note>A 5th-6th century Christian Neoplatonist whose writings on the "Celestial Hierarchy" were highly influential in the Renaissance.</note>, who writes thus concerning being and the "exemplars" <note>Divine archetypes or ideas in the mind of God.</note>: *For in the monad every number exists uniformly; unity possesses every number uniquely within itself, and every number is indeed joined together within the monad. But as far as it proceeds from the monad, so far is it discerned and multiplied.* 

We have spoken of immanence; let us now speak of procession. Just as the motion of a point makes a line, and the motion of a line makes a surface, so the motion of unity creates the "other," and "otherness" creates the dyad <note>The number two, representing the first step away from unity toward division and matter.</note>. For as Boethius says, among Pythagoras and the heirs of his wisdom, "the other" <note>original: "Alterum"</note> is ascribed to no other number but the dyad. Whence, according to the grammarian Servius, the word "other" is only used when speaking of two things, but the binary indicates a certain composition. For it is a "heap of unities," and composition cannot be understood without the suspicion of material proportion. 

Material proportion cannot exist without formal proportion, just as in truth matter cannot exist without form. Aristotle rightly judges form to be something divine. The odd number—and indeed the ternary <note>The number three.</note>—is asserted to be most proper to divine things. Not unlike this is what Alphesibaeus sang in the *Sorceress* <note>The 8th Eclogue of Virgil.</note> of Maro <note>The poet Virgil, whose full name was Publius Vergilius Maro.</note>: *God delights in an odd number.* 

Therefore, everything that first flows out from God, as soon as it is no longer God himself, becomes "other" than Him and becomes equal to the first multitude <note>The binary/dyad, representing the first step into plurality.</note>. And so that it may nonetheless have being, it turns back again toward the divinity, whose sign is the odd number. For every procession (as Abucaten <note>Likely a reference to a medieval Arabic philosopher, possibly Al-Ghazali or Avempace.</note> said) is effected through the likeness of the secondary things to the first. Thus, the angelic nature is to be signified by an odd number: by a "number" because it is a multitude that has proceeded from the One, but by an "odd" number because it is closest to God and relies on participation in Him. For angels are like a small portion of God, such as the prophetic Apollo once replied at Colophon <note>An ancient Greek city in Ionia, home to the Oracle of Apollo at Clarus.</note> to one who questioned him. 

Here, clearly, is what the philosophers say they have experienced: for where a higher nature touches a lower one, it reaches the highest point of the lower from its own lowest level. But soon, when one reaches the summit—that is, unity—it will necessarily be the opposite: that the highest of the lower nature aspires to the whole condition of unity, since that which is one and most simple has no degrees. The whole condition of unity is to be the same...

<summary>This page explores the Neoplatonic and Pythagorean concept of the Monad (Unity) as the source of all being, explaining how multiplicity (the dyad) proceeds from it and how the angelic nature remains "odd" due to its proximity to God.</summary>
<keywords>Monad, Unity, Dyad, Ternary, Pythagoras, Dionysius the Areopagite, Boethius, Aristotle, Virgil, Angelology, Procession of Being</keywords>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD
### BOOK TWO

...for the entire universe consists of a quaternity <note>A group or set of four things; here referring to the four elements and the four-fold structure of reality.</note>, which the Maker of all things would not have produced in this way unless there were already an Idea of the quaternion within himself before it turned toward the work of creation. This is why the Lacedaemonians <note>The Spartans of ancient Greece.</note> seemed to me to be correct in depicting the supreme Jupiter with four ears, since he himself is also the supreme quaternion—that is, according to the followers of Pythagoras, the Tetractys <note>The Tetractys is a triangular figure consisting of ten points arranged in four rows: one, two, three, and four. It was a sacred symbol for Pythagoreans representing the organization of the universe.</note>. Its emanation is found through "form" in the angels, through "true participation" in rational souls, through "imaginary participation" in those lacking reason, and through "shadowy participation" in the shapes of bodies; it flows down from the heights to the depths in its usual manner, like a certain eternal rain, watering all things. Sidonius <note>One of the characters in this dialogue, representing an Epicurean or pagan philosopher.</note>, I listen quite gladly to what you apply to our discipline. For as I see it, everything squares correctly with the rule.

->B A R V C H I A S<-
<note>Baruchias is the Jewish scholar in the dialogue, representing the Kabbalistic and Hebrew perspective.</note>

Indeed, I believe this to be that famous river that goes out from that celebrated place of pleasure <note>Eden</note> to water paradise with its four heads. Orpheus and Homer call this the "Ocean," the origin of divine and human things: first, as always existing; second, as flowing into the generation of the gods; third, as watering earthly things; and fourth, as dripping into all fluid nature. This is that river Chebar <note>original: "Cobar." A river in Babylon where Ezekiel experienced his visions (Ezekiel 1:1).</note> by which Ezekiel, touched by the hand of God, saw the likeness of four animals, and four within four. In the same river, he also saw four cherubim on four wheels, and a wheel in the middle of a wheel. It is the very animal that he had seen by the river Chebar. This itself is the great sea in which, according to the vision of Daniel, the four winds of heaven fought, and four great beasts ascended from the sea. They wished for its divine power to be called Neptune, who is said in the Orphic hymns to drive a four-horse chariot. 

Therefore, the mystery of the Tetragrammaton <note>The four-letter Hebrew name of God, YHWH (Yahweh).</note> was known to the most ancient sages, and even to foreigners. Although it was not used publicly, it was nonetheless not unknown privately to those who, handing down the disciplines of the best things under a riddle, appointed certain most skillful craftsmen for themselves in this business. These craftsmen were to carve the entire universe of all things as a concealment <note>original: "celte," likely referring to a chisel or the act of carving/hiding.</note>, forming it into a single image for our sight, so that there would not be frequent need for this supreme Name, which they feared would be held in contempt through commonness. They called this sacred image "hieroglyphs," and its carvers were called "hieroglyphics" for that reason. 

It was formed in this way: first an eye, second a staff, third a shield, and fourth a serpent. See how aptly these figures are visible even to the uninitiated and the unrefined. But those same numbers exist as something much simpler and "formal," which we contemplate more sharply than figures, and which we believe approach closer to divine things. For this reason, this very famous Name is clearly much more desirable to us philosophers, as it embraces at once the geometric sacraments in the shapes of the characters and the arithmetic secrets in the forms of the numbers. 

And so that I may perform the duty of a mathematician indiscriminately, I would most rightly say: One, even-odd, the whole, reduction. Nothing is prior to the infinite, nothing prior to the one, nothing prior to the point; and it is itself the principle of all things. Then, all things exist up to the one and up to the point, and beyond the one and the point, there is nothing. Here, therefore, is the end of all things. You now hold the first letter of the Tetragrammaton, which reflects the figure of a point, and is named *Yod*—that is, the "beginning"—and designates the number ten, which is the end of numbers, and [contains] every number...
