<meta>This page continues the discourse by Baruchias on the mystical and mathematical foundations of reality, moving from the significance of the number four to an exploration of the Monad (the number One) and its relationship to the divine and the sequence of numbers.</meta>

# BOOK TWO

...penetrates most simply, since it bears the mark of unity. Unity, however, is the common measure of all numbers. And, as the Arithmeticians <note>Mathematicians specializing in the theory of numbers.</note> write, it holds the place of a point; while it is the beginning of an interval and of length, it is itself capable of neither interval nor length. Just as a point placed upon another point becomes nothing but a point—as if you joined nothing to nothing—so it happens that even unity multiplied by itself produces nothing other than itself. If you were to say, "once one," it is nothing but one; or again, "once one once" does not wander further, but remains as one. For it is always the same and immutable substance, the leader of the odd sequence, and in a certain way the producer and form of oddness. It is the beginning, the communication, and the end of all things, above number, above the whole, devoid of division, and lacking parts. Wherefore it most suitably demonstrates to our weak <note>original: "egrotis" (sickly or ailing)</note> eyes the supreme author of this world, and describes the nature of divinity to human frailty—from which, and out of which, and through which, and in which, and toward which all things are.

Let us use, if you wish, the testimony of the most divine theologian Dionysius the Areopagite <note>A 5th-6th century Christian theologian and philosopher whose works were influential in medieval mysticism.</note>, writing thus concerning being and the exemplars: "For indeed, in the monad <term>monad: from the Greek 'monas,' meaning unity or the number one</term> every number exists uniformly, and unity holds every number uniquely within itself; and every number is indeed joined within the monad. However much it proceeds from the monad, so much is it distinguished and multiplied."

We have spoken of immanence <note>The state of remaining within; here, the divine staying within itself.</note>; let us speak of the process <note>The "procession" or flowing out of the divine into creation.</note>. Just as the motion of a point makes a line, and the motion of a line makes a plane, so the motion of unity creates "the other," and this otherness creates the binary <note>The number two.</note>. For "the other," according to Pythagoras and the heirs of his wisdom, says Boethius <note>An influential 6th-century philosopher and author of 'The Consolation of Philosophy'.</note>, is ascribed to no other than the binary alone. Whence, according to the grammarian Servius <note>A late 4th-century commentator on the poet Virgil.</note>, "the other" <note>original: "Alter"</note> is said only of two things, whereas the binary indicates a certain composition. For it is a heap of unities. Composition is not understood without a suspicion of material proportion. Material proportion cannot exist without formal proportion, just as in truth matter cannot exist without form. Aristotle <note>The famous Greek philosopher.</note> rightly considers form to be something divine.

The odd number, and indeed the ternary <note>The number three.</note> especially, is asserted to be proper to divine things. To which Alphesiboeus <note>A character in Virgil’s Eclogues.</note> sang something not dissimilar in Maro’s <note>The Roman poet Virgil (Publius Vergilius Maro).</note> poem *The Sorceress*: "God delights in an odd number." Everything, therefore, that first flows out from God, as soon as it is no longer God itself, becomes "the other" from him and equal to the first multitude. And so that it may nonetheless have being, it turns back again toward the divinity, whose sign is the odd number. For every process (said Abucaten <note>Likely referring to the philosopher Avicenna or a similar Scholastic authority.</note>), is effected through the similarity of the secondary things to the first. Therefore, the angelic nature must be signified by an odd number: by a number, indeed, because it is a multitude that has proceeded from the One; but by an *odd* number because it is closest to God and relies on participation in Him. For angels are like a small portion of God, such as the prophetic Apollo once answered at Colophon <note>An ancient Greek city in Ionia, home to a famous oracle.</note> to one who inquired. Here clearly there is no lack of what philosophers say they have experienced: for where a superior nature touches an inferior one, it reaches the highest point of the inferior from its own lowest level. But soon, when the highest—that is, unity—is reached, it will necessarily be the case that the highest of the inferior nature aspires to the total condition of unity, since the one and most simple thing has no levels. Moreover, the total condition of unity is to be the same...

<sig>g iiii</sig>

<summary>This page explores the mystical properties of the number One (the Monad) as a representation of the Divine, explaining how it remains unchanged by multiplication. It further discusses the transition from Unity to the number Two (alterity/matter) and the sacred nature of odd numbers, particularly the number Three, in relation to angels and the divine.</summary>
<keywords>Monad, Unity, Binary, Ternary, Dionysius the Areopagite, Pythagoras, Boethius, Aristotle, Virgil, Angelic Nature, Neopythagoreanism, Sacred Geometry</keywords>

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse by Baruchias on the mystical and mathematical foundations of reality, moving from the significance of the number four (the quaternion) to its manifestation in the divine name and ancient symbols.</meta>

-> **ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD** <-

...that the whole universe consists of a fourfold nature <note>original: "quaternitate"</note>, which the Maker of all would not have brought about unless there were some Idea of the fourfold [the quaternion] within Himself before He turned His hand to the work. Hence the Spartans [Lacedaemonians] seem to me to have rightly depicted the highest Jupiter with four ears, since he is himself also the supreme fourfold—that is, according to the Pythagoreans, the **Tetractys** <term>Tetractys: A mystical symbol consisting of ten points arranged in a triangular shape in four rows; it was sacred to the followers of Pythagoras as a representation of the organization of the universe.</term>. The emanation of this Tetractys is found through its "form" in the angels, through "true participation" in rational souls, through "imagery" in those lacking reason, and through "shadows" in the shapes of physical bodies. In its accustomed manner, it flows down from the heights to the lowest depths like a certain eternal rain, watering all things. 

Baruchias said: "I listen quite willingly, Sidonius, to these things which you apply to our discipline. For as I see it, everything fits perfectly to the rule. For this seems to me to be that famous river which goes out from that celebrated place of pleasure [Paradise] to water the garden with four heads. This is what Orpheus and Homer call the **Ocean**, the origin of all things divine and human—first, as always existing; second, flowing into the generation of the gods; third, watering the things of the earth; and fourth, trickling into all fluid nature. This is that River Chebar <note>original: "Cobar"</note> near which Ezekiel, touched by the hand of God, saw the likeness of four animals, and four within four. In that same river, he saw four cherubim upon four wheels, and a wheel in the middle of a wheel. It is the very living creature he had seen by the River Chebar. This very thing is the great sea in which, according to the vision of Daniel, the four winds of heaven fought, and four great beasts ascended from the sea. They wished its divine power to be called Neptune, who is said in the Orphic hymns to drive a four-horse chariot. 

The mystery of the **Tetragrammaton** <term>Tetragrammaton: The four-letter Hebrew name of God (YHWH), considered too sacred to be spoken aloud by ancient sages.</term> was therefore known to the most ancient wise men, and even to foreigners. Although it was not used publicly, it was nonetheless not unknown in private. These sages, passing down all the disciplines of the finest things under the cover of a riddle, established certain most skillful craftsmen for themselves in this business, so that they might fittingly carve the entire universe into a single image for our sight. They did this lest the work of this supreme Name should frequently need to be spoken, for they feared it would be looked upon with contempt if it became common. Indeed, they called this a "sacred image," and its carvers were for that reason called **hieroglyphs** <note>literally "sacred carvers."</note>. It was represented in this way: first an **eye**, second a **staff**, third a **shield**, and fourth a **serpent**. 

See how aptly these figures are visible even to the uninitiated and the unrefined. But the numbers themselves exist in a much simpler and more formal way; we contemplate them more sharply than we do figures, and we believe they approach closer to divine things. For this reason, this very famous Name is much more desirable to us philosophers, as it embraces at once the geometric secrets in the shapes of its characters and the arithmetic secrets in the forms of its numbers. And so that I may perform the duty of a mathematician without distinction, I might most rightly say: **One, even-odd, the whole, the reduction.** <note>This refers to the Pythagorean progression where 1, 2, 3, and 4 (the Tetractys) add up to 10, the "reduction" or return to unity.</note> Nothing is prior to the infinite, nothing prior to the one, nothing prior to the point; and it is itself the beginning of all things. Then, everything exists up to the "one" and up to the "point," and beyond the one and the point, there is nothing. Here, therefore, is the end of all things. You hold now the first letter of the Tetragrammaton, which reflects the figure of a point, and is named **Iod** <note>The Hebrew letter Yod (י). It is the smallest letter, often compared to a mathematical point or the seed of creation.</note>; it designates the "ten" (which is the end of number) and encompasses every number...

<summary>This page explores the mystical significance of the number four (the Tetractys and Tetragrammaton), linking Pythagorean mathematics to biblical visions and ancient Egyptian hieroglyphics. Baruchias explains how the Hebrew letter Yod represents the beginning (a point) and the completion (the number ten) of all things.</summary>
<keywords>Tetractys, Tetragrammaton, Ezekiel's Vision, River Chebar, Hieroglyphics, Pythagoreanism, Yod, Jupiter, Neptune, Sacred Geometry</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical dialogue regarding the nature of the divine and the mathematical structure of the universe, moving from the fourfold nature (the Tetractys) back to the foundational principle of Unity (the Monad) and its subsequent emanations into the Dyad and Triad.</meta>

### BOOK TWO ###

...it penetrates most simply, since it bears the mark of unity. Unity, moreover, is the common measure of all numbers. And as the **Arithmeticians** <note>In the Renaissance, "arithmeticians" often referred to philosophers like the Pythagoreans who saw numbers as the building blocks of reality.</note> write, it holds the place of a point; for while it is the beginning of an interval and of length, it is itself capable of neither interval nor length. Just as a point placed upon a point results in nothing but a single point—as if you were to join nothing to nothing—so it happens that even unity, multiplied by itself, produces nothing other than itself. For if you say "once one," it is nothing but one; or again, "once one" does not wander further, but remains within one. For it is the same always and is an immutable substance, the ruler of the odd-numbered order, and in a certain way the creator and form of "oddness." It is the beginning, the communication, and the end of all things, above number, above the whole, devoid of division, and lacking parts.

For this reason, it most conveniently demonstrates the supreme author of this world to our "ailing eyes" <note>A metaphor for the limitations of human perception when trying to understand the divine.</note> and describes the nature of divinity to human weakness—from which, out of which, through which, in which, and toward which all things exist. Let us use, if you will, the testimony of the most divine theologian **Dionysius the Areopagite** <note>Pseudo-Dionysius, a 5th-6th century Christian philosopher who blended Christianity with Neoplatonism.</note>, writing thus concerning being and exemplars: *For in the monad every number exists uniformly, and unity holds every number uniquely within itself; every number is indeed joined together within the monad.* <note>original: "Etenim in monade omnis numerus uniformiter constat..."</note> However, as much as it proceeds out of the monad, so much is it discerned and multiplied. 

We have spoken of "immanence" <term>Immanence: The state of remaining within oneself; here, the divine staying as a single, unified point.</term>; let us speak of "procession" <term>Procession: The movement or flowing forth from a source; how the Many come from the One.</term>. Just as the movement of a point creates a line, and the movement of a line creates a surface, so the movement of unity creates the "other," and "otherness" creates the **dyad** <note>The number two.</note>. For "the other," according to **Pythagoras** and the heirs of his wisdom, says **Boethius** <note>A 6th-century philosopher whose works on music and math were standard textbooks in the Middle Ages.</note>, is ascribed to no other thing but the dyad alone. Whence, according to the grammarian **Servius**, "the other" <note>original Latin: *alter*</note> is said of only two things, whereas the dyad indicates a certain composition. For it is a heap of unities. Composition cannot be understood without the suspicion of "material proportion." Material proportion cannot exist without "formal proportion," just as, in truth, matter cannot exist without form. 

**Aristotle** rightly judges that "form" is something divine. An odd number, and indeed the **triad** <note>The number three.</note> most of all, is asserted to be most proper to divine things. Not unlike this, **Alphesiboeus** sang in the *Pharmaceutria* of **Maro** <note>The poet Virgil (Publius Vergilius Maro). Alphesiboeus is a character in his Eclogues.</note>: *God delights in an odd number.* <note>original: "Numero deus impare gaudet."</note> Everything, therefore, that first flows outside of God—as soon as it is no longer God himself—becomes "other" than Him and becomes equal to the "first multitude" <note>The number two, the first step away from unity into plurality.</note>. And yet, so that it may nonetheless have being, it turns back toward divinity, whose sign is the odd number. Every procession (as **Abucaten** <note>Likely a reference to the philosopher Avicenna/Ibn Sina.</note> said) is achieved through the likeness of the secondary things to the first. 

Therefore, the **angelic nature** is to be signified by an odd number: by a "number" because it is a multitude proceeding from the One, but by an "odd" one because it is closest to God and relies upon participation in Him. For angels are, as it were, a small portion of God, such as the prophetic **Apollo** once answered to one inquiring at **Colophon** <note>An ancient Greek city in Ionia, home to a famous oracle of Apollo.</note>. Here, clearly, there is no lack of what the philosophers say they have experienced: for where a superior nature touches an inferior one, there it touches the highest point of the lower from its own lowest degree. But soon, when one arrives at the highest point—that is, at Unity—the opposite will necessarily happen: the highest point of the inferior nature aspires to the total condition of Unity, since the One, being most simple, has no degrees. The total condition of Unity is to be the same...

It concludes by framing the angelic nature as the "highest point" of created reality that touches the "lowest point" of the divine Unity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Reuchlin's exploration of the divine name (the Tetragrammaton) through the lens of Pythagorean numerology. Having discussed the Monad (1), he now moves to the significance of the letters He (5) and Vav (6) as they relate to the structure of the cosmos and the nature of God.</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD #

...and always possesses a likeness to itself and an agreeing immutability, in which alone the **odd number**—as the **Arithmeticians** <note>Renaissance philosophers who studied the mystical properties of numbers.</note> say—most greatly participates. Truly, whichever number has a midpoint of one is an odd number. But that number whose midpoint is two receives the nature of "evenness" and can be divided into two equal parts. Therefore, you see that oddness is more akin to God, while evenness is more dissimilar. For this reason, the angelic, ideal, or entire nature of the archetypal world <note>The "archetypal world" refers to the realm of pure ideas or divine plans that exist before physical creation.</note> should more rightly have been designated not by an even number, but by an odd one.

Yet, since God alone does not admit a multitude within Himself, while all other things—however simple—are a kind of accumulation and are constructed from "act" and "potentiality." <note>A Scholastic philosophical distinction: 'Act' is what a thing is right now; 'potentiality' is what it has the power to become.</note> Therefore, the first stream of divinity ought not to be celebrated by some simple mark of oddness, but by one that consists of both an even and an odd number. Even, so that it might be something; odd, so that it might be "just so much"—namely, that which had flowed out and that which would flow back. For "being" is different from "that which is." 

Thus, from the first **binary** <note>the number 2, the first even number</note> and the first **ternary** <note>the number 3, the first true odd number</note>, the **quinary** <note>the number 5</note> is formed. This demonstrates the first ray into the intellectual world, produced from the supreme light, by a single sacred character: that is, the letter **He** <note>The Hebrew letter ה (He), which has the numerical value of 5.</note> itself. The mathematicians have taught that this is the first "uncomposite" of numbering, and the Hebrew grammarians have taught that it designates the breath of utterance and the "hypostasis of essence" <note>The fundamental reality or 'standing under' of a thing; Reuchlin notes that in Hebrew, 'He' often serves as the definite article 'The', marking the specific essence of a noun.</note> through its role as an article.

The most wise **Solomon** seems to me to have skillfully painted this for unrefined and unlearned eyes. For he made two **cherubim** over the Ark upon which the name of the Lord of Hosts might sit, so that one wing of a cherub was five cubits, and the other wing of a cherub was five cubits—as if it were an image of the super-celestial essence and nature just beneath God. 

I also wish to briefly explain the third character of this ineffable and wondrous name, which they maintain is **Vau** <note>The Hebrew letter ו (Vav), which has the numerical value of 6.</note>. Although this is a copulative conjunction <note>In Hebrew, 'Vav' is the word for 'and'.</note>, it nevertheless changes the tense of verbs in continuous speech and signifies the number six in mathematics. 

Most aptly, therefore, it is the little mark of heaven and earth—that is, of all things joined together from matter and form. For **Vav** couples and joins the parts of speech, from which it is called a "conjunction." Furthermore, it transforms the tenses in verbs, just as nature does in things. "What is that which has been?" says **Ecclesiastes**, "The same that shall be. What is that which has been done? The same that is to be done." <note>A reference to Ecclesiastes 1:9, suggesting the cyclical and connected nature of time.</note> Therefore, whatever bodily things exist both in heaven and on earth, and those situated between them, are subject to the mutation of time. 

But let us pass on to the **Pythagorean mysteries** and the secrets of sacred things. I have drawn from the volumes of the ancients that certain most skillful men of the Scythians or other nations were accustomed to carve the nature of the universe in a single image—and for that reason, they were called **hieroglyphs**—which contained an eye, a sword, a shield, and a serpent. 

According to these, certainly after the eye (that is, the point) and the rod or sword (that is, the linear plane), there follows in equal order the **hexagonal shield**: that is, the solid body which, because of its length, width, and height, is enclosed by six angles. For it exists in length as "front" and "back"; in width as "left" and "right"; in height as "upward" and "downward." For this **senary** <note>the number 6</note> is so established in the nature of solids that it always moves around every body in an inseparable conjunction. Therefore, since there are three intervals of solids, each of which has two movements, it is no wonder if after **Iod** <note>The Hebrew letter י, value 1</note> (that is, one) and **He** (that is, two and three), **Vav** now [follows] in its place...

<summary>This page explains the mystical and mathematical significance of the Hebrew letters He (5) and Vav (6) within the Divine Name. Reuchlin links these numbers to the structure of the physical and intellectual worlds, using examples from Solomon's Temple and Pythagorean geometry.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the mystical properties of the letters in the Tetragrammaton. Having previously discussed the number five and the letter He, Reuchlin now transitions to the number six and the third letter, Vav.</meta>

## BOOK TWO ##

...follows, that is, six. This number arises from the multiplication of the preceding numbers, whether you say one times two times three, or conversely three times two; indeed, it is a number most perfectly suited to material things, as it is born from the alternating multiplication of the preceding numbers. Moreover, in the mutual multiplication of things, matter itself provides the greatest opportunity. I will remain silent on how it obtains its own order here from the units that come before it. For from one, two, and three, the **number six** <note>original: "senarius"</note> is gathered, so that you may know by the best right that immediately after the letters **Iod** and **He**, the letter **Vav** itself ought to follow. 

Added to this is the fact that, just as the nature of the entire corporeal universe is asserted to be perfect in every respect—even by the authority of **Aristotle** <note>The Greek philosopher whose works on physics and metaphysics were foundational to Renaissance thought.</note>—neither abounding in superfluities nor lacking in necessities, so too does the number six provide the seal of the entire corporeal world. In the whole context of numbers from the first unit up to ten, it alone is so perfect that, in comparison to its parts, it is neither impoverished by want nor diminished, nor is it excessive with immoderate fullness. For it has a middle part, which is three; a third part, which is two; and a sixth part, which is one; these, when reduced into a single sum, bring forth the whole body of the number six. 

Indeed, other numbers lack this perfection. For the half of the **number eight** <note>original: "octonarii"</note> is four, its fourth is two, and its eighth is one; the collection of these brings back a sum smaller than the whole body, namely seven. Thus we find in the rest that the sums of the parts, when piled together, either exceed or fall short of the trunk of the number; the number six alone is perfect in a totality of this kind. For this reason, not without cause, it alone designates this whole and perfect world.

This opinion of ours must be fortified by a rampart of **Holy Scripture** <note>The Bible, specifically the Hebrew text.</note>, lest you suspect a hollow fantasy or my own invention; and that which few notice, I shall reveal to you, my dearest companions. Assuredly, although very many "generations" are usually recited in the Holy Scriptures, you will nowhere find in the entire volume of sacred letters any **generations** <note>original: "generationes"</note> to which our character **Vav** has been added, except only when we read of the generation of the heaven and the earth: *These are the generations of the heaven and the earth* <note>Genesis 2:4. In Hebrew, the word is 'Tholdoth' (תולדות).</note>, where **Generations** is written with a **Vav** at the end through a singular gift of divinity. 

Indeed, this letter is subtracted from that same word in all other places, except for the one genealogy of **Christ** <note>Referring to the Davidic line leading to the Messiah.</note> begun near the end of the **Book of Ruth**: *These are the generations of Perez* <note>Ruth 4:18. 'Phares' is the Latinized form of the Hebrew 'Perez'.</note>, which lineage was certainly more perfect than any other generation. Therefore, this third letter of the most sacred Name warns us by the number six that the whole universe is perfect, since on the **sixth day** God saw all that He had made and they were "very good." Therefore, the heavens and the earth and all their adornment were perfected. 

It remains for us to speak briefly concerning the third progress of things, that is, the fourth letter. For after the **subsistences** <note>Purely spiritual beings or realities.</note> truly lacking matter, and the **substances** truly possessing matter, there follow—not without justice—those things which, while they are the forms of bodies within matter, are at the same time separated from bodies and materials. To this class belongs the **rational soul** <note>term: rational soul — in Renaissance philosophy, the part of the human mind that bridges the gap between the physical body and the divine spirit.</note> which possesses a *fiery vigor*, as **Virgil** says, *and a celestial origin, so far as harmful bodies do not retard them, nor do earthly limbs and moribund members dull them.* <note>Quoting Virgil's 'Aeneid', Book VI, lines 730-732, describing the divine spark within humans.</note> Although this soul, according to its more excellent essence, walks in an equal gathering with the angelic nature, nevertheless, as far as its being...

<meta>catchword: ...is [Latin: esse]</meta>

<summary>This page explores the mystical and mathematical perfection of the number six, linking it to the Hebrew letter Vav and the completion of the physical world. Reuchlin highlights the unique spelling of the word "generations" in Genesis and Ruth to argue for the divine perfection inherent in the structure of the universe and the lineage of the Messiah.</summary>

--- Page 123 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of the mystical significance of the Hebrew letters, specifically focusing on the letter He (ה) and its relationship to the human soul. Although the previous page concluded a discussion on the number six and the letter Vav, Reuchlin here elaborates on the soul's "middle" position between the divine and material realms, which identifies it with the number five and the letter He.</meta>

->### ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD ###<-

...pertains to the lower realm, it becomes secondary as it is immersed in corruptible things and joined with those sharing in the body. Moved by this very fact, the **Royal Prophet** <note>King David, the traditional author of the Psalms.</note> said: *You have made him a little lower than the angels.* <note>original Latin: "Minuisti eum paulominus ab angelis," from Psalm 8:5.</note> My friends, I shall do you a very great favor, as you are weary from listening now at evening, for I will conclude the argument as briefly as possible. In the book of **Plato** <note>The influential Greek philosopher (c. 427–347 BCE).</note> titled ***Timaeus***, this doctrine stands out: that in the beginning, God first created fire and then earth. But these two alone, being so contrary, could not fit together properly without a third element. Therefore, a medium between both remained, which would share partly with the higher and partly with the lower. 

Thus, since there are **archetypes** <note>term: archetypes (original patterns or models from which all things are made)</note> like certain fiery lights created first, and then the entire corporeal world embracing the dregs of matter, it was fitting that some substance should come to be which would neither depend entirely on matter nor exist without it. This was so that, regarding the operation of its powers, it might dwell with the body, and regarding the power of its essence, it might dwell with the angels, and by one or the other virtue, it would not be absent from either world. Therefore, the **human soul** is the most perfect middle ground, which, while being neither of the two, participates in both.

Because of this, **Hermes Trismegistus** <note>A legendary figure to whom a collection of mystical and philosophical texts, the Hermetica, were attributed.</note> says: *O Asclepius, man is a great miracle, a living being to be adored and honored.* For he passes into the nature of God as if he were himself becoming a god. He knows the race of **daemons** <note>In the Platonic sense, these are intermediary spirits or guiding geniuses, not necessarily "demons" in the modern sense.</note>, as one who recognizes that he shares the same origin with them. He looks down upon the part of human nature within himself, trusting in the divinity of his other part. Oh, how much more happily balanced is the nature of man! Joined to the gods by a kindred divinity, he despises that part of himself which is earthly. All other things to which he knows he is necessary by heavenly disposition, he binds to himself with a bond of love, and thus he looks up to heaven. Thus, he is placed in a happier middle position, so that he may love those things which are below him, while he himself is loved by those above. 

You hear that the **rational soul** is the medium between the invisible world and the visible world, yet most closely related to the higher beings. For both these reasons, it could not be designated by any mark other than the **number five** <note>original Latin: "numeri quinarii."</note>, which is called the letter **He** [ה]. It is undoubtedly the most just middle of the entire decimal numbering system. For if you bind together four above and six below on either side of the number five, they come to ten, of which five is the middle. Again, three and seven, then two and eight, and finally one and nine—you will always find ten, and you will find that only the number five is the middle. 

If we inquire more accurately, the essence of the heavenly beings is surely similar, and should be shown by a similar character, and nothing but order creates the difference. For as the **angelic world** remains more absolute, pure, and firm, it must be shown to our eyes by the first letter **He** and in a more eminent place. However, the **human mind** is shown by the latter character, though it is the same letter, because it is attached to the body and began to exist at the same time as the body. Yet, because it will not cease with the destruction of the body—provided it follows its own nature and is not weighed down by foreign allurements—it returns immediately and ascends, whether remaining in the body or flying outside the body, to a heavenly dwelling suitable to it, which I have called the **ascent** <note>original: "reductionem," literally a "leading back" or restoration to a former state.</note>. Concerning this, **Zoroaster** <note>The ancient Persian prophet, often cited in the Renaissance as a source of "primeval theology."</note> said: *You must ascend to the light itself and to the rays of the Father, from whence the soul was sent to you, clothed in much mind.* And **Pythagoras** <note>The Greek philosopher and mathematician famous for his theories on the soul and numbers.</note> handed it down in these words: *If you, leaving the body, [go] into the free ether...*

<summary>Reuchlin argues that the human soul is a "great miracle" because it serves as the essential middle point between the material and divine worlds. He links this mediating role to the number five and the Hebrew letter He, citing authorities like Plato, Hermes Trismegistus, and Pythagoras to support the soul's divine origin and eventual ascent.</summary>
<keywords>human soul, letter He, number five, quinarius, Plato, Hermes Trismegistus, mediation, microcosm, Tetragrammaton, Zoroaster, Pythagoras</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discourse on the mystical properties of the Hebrew letter He (ה), which Reuchlin associates with the number five and the final letter of the Tetragrammaton. The speaker concludes a lengthy explanation of divine names and angelic hierarchies before the party adjourns for dinner.</meta>

->### BOOK TWO ###<-

...you pass into the free realm, you will be an immortal god. <note>original Latin: "rem liberum transieris, eris immortalis deus." The speaker is describing the soul's ultimate liberation.</note> This is indeed the final leading back of man to divinity; this is the end, this is the boundary, this is the goal, this is the limit and the perfection of this name so sacred, so adorable, so powerful, so wonderful, which God mentioned in the presence of Moses: *I will show all good to you, and I will call the name Tetragrammaton before you.* <note>original Latin: "Ego ostendam omne bonum tibi, et uocabo nomen Tetragrammaton coram te," referencing Exodus 33:19. The Tetragrammaton is the four-letter name of God, YHWH.</note> This is the fourth letter, and in Daniel, the appearance of the fourth is like the son of God. <note>A reference to the story of the fiery furnace in Daniel 3:25.</note> For God gave men the power to become sons of God to those who believe in his name. The final **He** <note>The letter ה, the fourth letter of YHWH.</note> of this name is, as it were, the fourth face of the eagle in the wheels, which in Ezekiel walked continually and did not turn back. By the power and strength of this letter, **Abraham**, at a hundred years old, fathered a child by **Sarah**, a ninety-year-old and sterile woman, contrary to the laws of nature, and he grew into nations. Because of this, he was named *Abraham*, whereas before he was called *Abram*. <note>In Genesis 17:5, God changes Abram's name by adding the letter 'He' (ה), symbolizing a divine covenant.</note>

Why say more? All our happiness is expressed by this character of the number five, by which **Alpha** is united with **O** <note>The beginning and the end (Alpha and Omega).</note>, and the waters under the heaven with the waters which are above the heavens. By this, we are able to do what we wish, and we wish for what the beings above wish. For this reason, **Asher** was allotted the fifth part, because blessedness is most abundantly fulfilled in the letter **He**. For we interpret *Asher* as "happiness" or "blessedness."

Night rushes on, excellent men; I have treated in a very long discourse those names which seemed to signify the essence of God. Learn the rest for yourselves from **Dionysius the Areopagite** <note>A 5th-6th century Christian theologian and philosopher.</note> and **Jerome** <note>Saint Jerome (c. 347–420), known for translating the Bible into Latin.</note>, whether they signify the power of God, his operation, or our affection toward him. Although there are some explained neither by them nor by other Greek and Latin authors, but only by our own people, such as this name **Isth** <note>likely referring to *Ish* (אִישׁ), the Hebrew word for "man," which Reuchlin connects to the divine.</note>, which suggests both man and God, with whom Jacob alone is recorded to have wrestled in the book of Genesis. And it follows: *Your name shall no longer be called Jacob, but Israel, because you have prevailed with God and man.* 

There is also the word **Na** <note>A Hebrew particle of entreaty, often translated as "please" or "I pray."</note>, which we use in times of mental distress and troubles. Just as Moses says in the Book of Numbers: *Forgive, Na, the sin of your people,* <note>Numbers 14:19.</note> which you have translated as, "Forgive, I pray." and in the hundred and seventeenth Psalm: *A na Lord, save us, na. A na Lord, give us prosperity, na,* <note>Psalm 118:25 (numbered 117 in the Vulgate).</note> which you more elegantly translate as, "O Lord, save me; O Lord, grant good prosperity." For you also read **Iah** in *Halleluiah*, and **Zabaoth** <note>term: Zabaoth (Lord of Hosts)</note> in the sacrifices, and **Adonai** in Exodus, and **El** in *Immanuel*, and **Ischyros**, which is **Sadai** <note>term: Sadai/Shaddai (Almighty)</note>. And "before the sun, **Innon** is his name," which you say as, "His name remains." Also **Elion** <note>term: Elion/Elyon (Most High)</note> and many others not very common, such as **Macom** and **Caphu**. 

But there are also certainly many names of angels according to their kinds, such as the **Hasmalim**, **Haioth**, **Ophanim**, **Tharsisim**, and the rest. <note>These are traditional classes or "choirs" of angels in Jewish mysticism.</note> Then individually, not a few, such as **Uriel**, **Asariel**, **Zuriel**, **Abir**, **Metattron**, and those similar to them. You now hold in your memory what I have committed to you by your hearing regarding the sacred names, which is certainly very brief and very small. Although there are some other things of that kind which I would nonetheless propose and explain, however much larger or more moderate they might be than these, the sun has set long ago, and you are tired from listening. I delegate the remaining parts to **Capnion** <note>The Greek nickname for Johannes Reuchlin himself, meaning "Smoky."</note> for tomorrow. Now let us go inside from here to dinner.

Then **SIDONIUS** said, "Let it be so."

And **CAPNION** said, "Let it be so. For a sumptuous meal is ready."

<summary>Reuchlin concludes his lecture on divine names, specifically the letter He and its power to grant immortality and perform miracles, before the characters adjourn for the evening.</summary>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD
<meta>catchword: commentary</meta>

## JOHANN REUCHLIN OF PFORZHEIM, DOCTOR OF
## BOTH LAWS, *CAPNION*, OR ON THE
## WONDER-WORKING WORD

<margin>i</margin>
Now the third day dawns, venerable Prelate, on which—after the sacred names of the ancient philosophers of the Greek nation and of the Hebrews have been explained—it is time for the final, continuous discourse of Capnion to be heard. Capnion is a native of Pforzheim by birth and a Christian by profession. <note>Capnion is the Greek pseudonym used by the author, Johann Reuchlin; it is a translation of his German name, which means "smoke" (Rauch).</note> However, before his beginning, Sidonius—again moved by a great fire for investigating the discipline—begins before the others as follows. <margin>A mark and vertical line accompany this section</margin> "It seemed to me, my dear Capnion," Sidonius says, "that this whole bedroom shook tonight, and the very walls themselves trembled from those portents of words which Baruchias recited so learnedly to us yesterday in that speech of his. To us listeners, they were not only most pleasant, but truly most admirable. I believe that from the sharpest and most attentive thinking (as often happens), the unheard-of novelty of such terms seized my mind as it was close to sleep and pulled it in different directions, so that my imagination manufactured certain movements of the walls. Or, if what he thinks is true—and I do not strongly reject it—that a great choir of the heavens could easily have followed those symbols of divinity. For none of the gods would dare, according to Homer’s verses, to remain in their seats once Jove is moved. <note>original Latin: "moto Ioue." Sidonius refers to Zeus/Jupiter in the Iliad, suggesting that when the supreme deity acts, all other powers must submit.</note> But come, Capnion, it is now time for the wonder-working word; do not frustrate our hope."

To this, Capnion said: "Do you think that anything was left over from yesterday by this preacher Baruchias for me to use up today? He is so full of a variety of names, it would be a wonder if he did not pour out every provocation of astonishing deeds." And Sidonius replied, "I certainly do think so." <margin>Marginal bracket and vertical line</margin> "For he himself strongly testified that, until now, none of these words have prevailed through force and power beyond the common course of nature, nor were any others of his people ever able to do so." <margin>Marginal bracket with circular flourish</margin>

Then BARUCHIAS said: "Sidonius reports truly. Therefore, Capnion, see whether you consider it concerns your honor to fulfill what you promised us, so that you may explain both that certain word by which miracles are performed, and at the same time its infallible ritual, and keep our minds in suspense no longer."

Then he replied: "Since you strip me so much of the freedom to refuse, I shall do—not perhaps as much as you wish—but as much as I am able. Since I believe it is now established for you that there was some name or word among our ancestors by which they were once held to have performed portents, miracles, and wonders, there will certainly remain few things which our great friendship does not allow me to deny you. For if I name the word itself and hand down the ritual of its use, I shall have sufficiently fulfilled the duty you demand by my own right." <margin>Marginal bracket and lines</margin> "Although all these things may be ready and at hand, nevertheless, the matter itself seems to me to need to be sought a little more deeply first. Reflect within your minds and diligently recall the first day of our discussion, when it was agreed among us according to the opinion of each—and I clearly do not know if anything else has sat deeper in the minds and judgment of us all, or stuck more firmly, or stood more solidly—that whether we humans possess a certain wisdom of eternal things, or a science of perpetual things, or a knowledge of momentary things, nonetheless a right faith is always superior and more certain for them, as it does not rely on our empty..."

<margin>faith?</margin>

Capnion begins his discourse by emphasizing that faith is superior to human reason or scientific knowledge in the performance of miracles.</summary>

### BOOK THREE

[The study does not rely on human] commentaries, but leans entirely upon divine revelation; from this source, one must seek the origin of the world and everything that proceeds from God into the world.

<margin>Paul of Tarsus <note>The original text contains a typo, "Tarfensis," for Tarsensis.</note></margin>
This is supported by the distinguished testimony of Paul of Tarsus, who says in his Letter to the Hebrews: "By faith we understand that the worlds were framed by the word of God, so that things which are visible were made from things which are invisible." <note>Hebrews 11:3</note> What the royal prophet <note>Referring to King David, the traditional author of the Psalms.</note> revealed to mortals through divine revelation in this song: "By the word of the Lord the heavens were created, and all their beauty, power, and host by the breath of his mouth." <note>Psalm 33:6</note> And the Lord is faithful in all his words, wherefore let no one dare to change the word of God. For it remains forever, and it is necessary for all created things to obey it, because "He spoke, and they were made." <note>Psalm 33:9</note> For even fire, ice, and the stormy winds fulfill his word. <note>Psalm 148:8</note> That word which melts the crystal, gives life to man, heals the sick, and strikes down transgressors—all this the sacred scriptures have brought forth. Therefore, this Word is set over nature and established as if in a vast empire; according to John the Theologian and Evangelist, "In the beginning it was with God, and all things were made through it," and what is even greater, "the Word was God." <note>John 1:1</note> Nothing, therefore, shall be more powerful to us than that Word, which is full of the greatest force, vehement strength, and supreme power. What the power of God is when he wills to perform any work—or rather, the very will of him who desires it—we are unable to judge while we dwell in this fragile skin. We cannot know how great and how spacious the field of its strength is for that will and that word, concerning which that prophetic oracle stands: "Whatever the Lord pleased, he did in heaven and on earth, in the sea and in the depths." <note>Psalm 135:6</note> Here you have, first of all, what is most important to use in miracles that exceed the accustomed habit of nature, the well-worn path, and the established limits and order of the universe. For then the work of that divine will—which is the Word of God—will not be of a limited and narrow virtue (such as is the nature of all existing things), but of infinite power in a most ample space of dominion and a very wide field. This is rightly written in the Apocalypse <note>The Book of Revelation 19:16.</note> as "King of kings and Lord of lords," who holds the empire of omnipotence over those things that are as well as those things that are not. This speech might be considered foolish by the Peripatetics <note>The followers of Aristotle, known for their focus on physical and natural observation rather than mystical theology.</note> who profess Physics, but to contemplative men it is established as a fitting and true discourse. For the Apostle says, "God calls those things which are not as though they were." <note>Romans 4:17</note> Dionysius, <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a highly influential Christian mystic and philosopher.</note> a follower of this discipline, says: "We shall also dare to affirm this with reason, since even that which is not participates in the beautiful and the good." Whether you call this "of the word" or by any other name, I do not object, provided that the first emergence of the divinity within itself is understood from its immanence—like light coming to light, and the radiance of light back to the light, or the mind to reason and vice versa in that order. This is indeed called by the Greeks either the "rational word" or "verbal reason," for <note>original: "λογος"</note> *Logos* <term>Logos: a Greek term meaning both "word" and "reason" or "plan." In Christian theology, it refers to the Second Person of the Trinity (Jesus Christ) as the creative agent of God.</term> means both "reason" and "word."

<margin>Logos <note>original: "λογος;"</note></margin>
Indeed, that ancient author Zeno <note>Zeno of Citium, the founder of Stoicism.</note> calls the maker of the universe and the arranger of nature "Logos," and Hermes, the most ancient of philosophers, writes to his son (for I shall interpret speech from speech in such a necessary cause): "For there is," he says, "O son, a secret word of wisdom, the holy word of the only Lord of all and the pre-intellected, which to speak is beyond man." <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure associated with the Hermetic corpus of mystical writings.</note> Plato, in the *Epinomis*, wished all things that happen in the world to consist of this Word, when...

<meta>This page continues the philosophical and theological exploration of the "Word" (Verbum/Logos), specifically looking at how Platonic philosophy aligns with the concept of the Divine Word as the ordering principle of the universe and its relation to the Father.</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

...when he discussed the fellowship and equal dignity of divine things in this way, he said, "and let us render honors, not assigning a year to some, a month to others, and no portion to others, nor any time in which it runs through its own circle, completing the world itself, which the most divine Word <note>original: "uerbum"</note> of all things has ordained." <note>This refers to a passage in Plato’s *Epinomis* (986c), discussing the divine order of the heavens.</note> The fortunate man first admires this Word, and then conceives a love for learning about it, as much as a mortal nature can bear.

<margin>Comparison between the human and the divine</margin>

Indeed, you shall not judge that Word according to our human manner, as if it were a messenger of air broken between the teeth, which is how they describe the voice of mortals. But if it is necessary to compare fragile things to eternal ones, earthly to super-celestial, or human to divine, at least one must allow the internal word of man—which you call the supreme reason <note>original: "rationem"</note> within us, proceeding from the mind—to be compared to that. This reason is neither entirely the same as the mind, nor is it altogether different from it. For human reason flowing forth from the mind is not without cause judged to be distinct from its source of intellect, but when it leads the mind out into the open, nothing else can be seen by the mind. Therefore, since the nature of the mental substance is the same, it is judged to be different based on its underlying duties.

After the fashion of this shadowed image, one may perceive within the inner sanctuaries of the most august deity the Word or Reason <note>original: "uerbum siue rationē"</note> of the paternal mind itself. Because it is able to subsist through itself, it is therefore distinguished from the Father from whom it simultaneously has its existence. Yet, since it clearly shows within itself all the things that we contemplate as being in God or around God, we then affirm that it is the same and one with the substance of the paternal mind. Nor is there anything anywhere else that indicates a distance between God speaking and God the Word, except the nature of the utterance alone. For all other things in both are equal, or rather, the same. Each is God, each is eternal; each, therefore, is before all time. This one always brought forth, and this was always brought forth—a Word equal in power to the one bringing it forth, and a Father bringing forth the Word.

This is what Plato himself, no idle searcher of divine matters, suggests in his religious letters in the *Epistle to Hermias, Erastus, and Coriscus*, when toward the end he says: "It is fitting for all three of you to read this letter, but especially when you are gathered together. If not, then at least two together as much as is possible. Finally, using it as a covenant and a proper law as is right, with an oath interposed—not without elegant study and that study's sister, discipline—witnessing the God who is the leader of all things, both present and future, and the Lord and Father of that leader and cause, whom, if we philosophize rightly, we shall know." <note>This is a citation of Plato's *Sixth Letter* (323d). Reuchlin interprets the "leader" as the Son/Word and the "Father of the leader" as God the Father.</note>

Do you not clearly see that the Word which Plato taught in the *Epinomis* had disposed the order of the world is, in this place, called the leader and cause of all things, and that he has a Father? Therefore, the Son is the Word whose Father is named Lord. Now I would like us to recall that discourse which Baruchias <note>A character in Reuchlin's dialogue representing Jewish mystical tradition.</note> gave yesterday around noon, both learnedly and at length, in which he wisely began instructing us about the Ineffable Name <note>Referring to the Tetragrammaton, the four-letter name of God, YHWH.</note> that most closely belongs to the highest God alone, claiming that translators have placed "Lord" <note>original: "dominū"</note> in the place of the Tetragrammaton because they could find no equivalent word for it in any language.

Join to this, if you will, that speech which I myself gave three days ago, in which I remember affirming to you that Thales, Pythagoras, and Plato traveled through Syria to be instructed in Hebrew disciplines, from where not a few...

<summary>This page explores the Platonic roots of the "Logos" (the Word), arguing that Plato's philosophical writings prefigured Christian doctrine regarding the relationship between God the Father and the Divine Word (the Son). It also connects Greek philosophy to Hebrew wisdom through the travels of ancient philosophers to Syria.</summary>

--- Page 128 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of the "Word" (Verbum) by identifying it as the Son of God. It bridges Platonic philosophy—which describes a divine "cause" and "guide"—with Christian scripture, particularly the prophecies of Isaiah and the Psalms, to argue that the "Word," the "Hand of God," and the "Only-Begotten" are all titles for the same divine person who shares one substance with the Father.</meta>

### BOOK THREE

...of those who have compared his writings with their own, you see a trace here. Plato calls the Lord the father of the guide and cause of all things. <note>Referring to Plato’s *Epinomis* or *Letters*, where he discusses the "Governor" of the universe.</note> Therefore, since that God is the guide and cause of all things present and future because he ordained the world, who will now doubt that, according to Plato, this is that most divine Word <note>original: "uerbum"</note> by which anyone who is caught up in the admiration, love, and perception of it shall be happy? The Word, therefore, is the Son of the Lord, and for that reason is God, and the guide, and the cause of the world. Thus he is named by many titles, in various ways by many, and differently by others. But among so many names of that most divine Son, one stands out as the most choice for performing wonders <note>original: "ad miracula electissimum"</note>, in which the Supreme Father was most well-pleased. Regarding this matter, he himself made us more certain through prophecy in his spirit. Hear, I beseech you, the sacred oracle of Isaiah:

> A child is born to us, and a son is given to us, and the government is placed upon his shoulder, and he called his name Wonderful. <note>Isaiah 9:6. The Latin "mirificum" carries the sense of "wonder-working" or "maker of miracles."</note>

But if his names are so many and so varied, we ought to seek accurately which one among them all is that distinguished name which the Father called "Wonderful." For he is called by John the "Only-begotten Son" who is in the bosom of the Father.

<margin>Is the Father the Son? Only-begotten? Why?</margin>
First, although the title "Son" may apply to other men, it fits this most divine Word more properly, while it applies to mortals only by a less perfect likeness. For this Son is the same as the Father, and not only that, but he is also "from" the Father. For the Father is he who provided the beginning of being according to a nature similar to himself; the Son, however, is he who took the origin of being by birth from another. Therefore "Only-begotten" <note>original: "unigenitus"</note> is added because it is most proper, peculiar, and unique to this Son—not only because he is the one born of the One, but also because he is born in a singular and unique way, very different from corporeal nature.

<margin>The Bosom of the Father? Who is he?</margin>
Next, let it be determined what the "bosom of the Father" <note>original: "sinus patris"</note> is intended to mean for us. And rightly he is first said to be "He who is," so that we may recognize his co-eternity with the Father—he who commanded Moses:

> Thus you shall say to the children of Israel: "He Who Is" has sent me,

as if the Son were in him from eternity just as if in a bosom. For this reason he adds: "Who is in the bosom of the Father." By this speech, he declares the intimate and very familiar birth of the Son from the Father.

<margin>The Hand?</margin>
By another name, David calls him the "Hand" <note>original: "manum"</note> when he speaks to the Father in this way:

> Why do you turn away your hand and your right hand from the middle of your bosom?

Therefore, the Hand in the bosom of the Father is the Son, and the Son in the bosom is the Hand of the Father, through whom he made all things, who says:

> I myself, I am the first and I am the last; my hand also founded the earth, and my right hand measured the heavens.

Nor shall I omit to show you the certainty of this cause. For that this "Hand" took its beginning from the Father is a truth ready at hand from the hundred-and-eighth Psalm. <note>Psalm 109 in modern numbering.</note> For thus the prophet prays:

> Help me, O Lord my God; save me according to your mercy; and they shall know that this is your hand, and that you, Lord, have made it.

But that the Father and the "Hand" are the same, of one substance, one God, and the same Lord, will be easy to see in the forty-third Psalm <note>Psalm 44 in modern numbering.</note> if the verse is read according to the Hebrew custom. For it says:

<margin>You are your hand? Psalm 79.</margin>
> You are your hand, you have scattered the nations and planted them.

For what follows shortly thereafter <insert>❧</insert> concerning the "illumination of the face" is no small secret of the divine Trinity.

<margin>Logos</margin>
Let us return again to the Son, called the "Word" <note>original: "uerbum"</note> by the Latins, but called "Logos" <note>original: "λόγος"</note> by the Greeks, which is much more significant and perhaps more correct. For "Logos" <note>original: "λόγος"</note> signifies reason, definition, the word, and discourse. Therefore, having obtained pardon, let us compare our fragile things to the divine.

<sig>h ii</sig>
<insert>❧ The illumination of the face, etc.</insert>

<summary>This page argues that the "Word" mentioned by Plato is identical to the biblical Son of God, using titles like "Only-begotten," "Bosom of the Father," and "Hand of God" to explain the Son's eternal relationship and shared substance with the Father.</summary>

--- Page 129 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical and theological defense of the "Word" (Logos) as the Son of God. While the previous page established the titles of the Son through scripture, this page employs Aristotelian logic and Platonic thought to explain the relationship between the Father (the Mind) and the Son (the Reason or Word).</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

<margin>A Comparison.</margin>
For just as human reason relates to the mind, so the subsisting reason of God relates to the Father—not only because the "generation" <note>The theological term for the "birth" or origin of the Son from the Father.</note> of both happens without suffering or change, but also because of their cohesion. For there can be no reason without a mind, nor any mind without reason.
<margin>The duty of the Word?</margin>
Since the duty of the Word is to announce the inner contents of the mind—as the Stagirite <note>Aristotle (384–322 BCE), born in Stagira, often referred to simply as "the Stagirite" in Renaissance texts.</note> observes in his book *On Interpretation*—and to draw forth secrets from the depths of thought and reveal the will of the speaker, for that reason this <margin>The Word?</margin> Son is most aptly judged to be the "Word" of the Father. For as John says, "No one has ever seen God," but "the only-begotten Son, who is in the bosom of the Father, he has revealed him to us." He is the "Messenger of Great Counsel," and for that reason is also called an "angel" <note>original: "angelus." In Greek, *angelos* means "messenger." Reuchlin is linking Christ to the "Angel of Great Counsel" mentioned in the Greek Septuagint version of Isaiah 9:6.</note> who reveals the heavenly meditations of God to mortals. <margin>Definition?</margin> Regarding the "definition" <note>original: "diffinitio"</note> of this Word, it is no secret to you that it must be of such a nature that it seems to "eviscerate" or lay bare the entire nature of the thing being defined. <margin>Reason?</margin> Furthermore, "Reason" <note>original: "Ratio." In Latin, *ratio* means both "reason" and the "definition" or "proportion" of a thing.</note>—which is what the name signifies according to Aristotle's *Metaphysics*—is the definition itself. In this way, this most divine and substantive Word—or rather, this <term>supersubstantial reason</term> <note>A philosophical term meaning a reality that exists beyond or above the level of physical "substance" or matter.</note>—might rightly be called an evident demonstration of the Father's nature. Its total, adequate, and universal "product" is a silent and certain description of the one who generated it, through which we immediately recognize with a clearer intuition the one whom we were ignorant of just a moment before. <margin>He who sees me?</margin> Therefore, when he says, "He who sees me sees my Father also," he is calling the "sight" of such a thing the "knowledge of the intellect." <margin>The eye of the soul?</margin> For the mind itself is the eye of the soul. Therefore, the "vision" of the mind is the knowledge of things, which becomes most known when the whole substance is made open in a brief statement, which the experts in logic <note>original: "dialectici"</note> call a "term." For if this is what it means to be "begotten"—namely, to be produced from the substance of the parent as a child similar in nature—then the Father will no longer be hidden when the Son is shown, who is the "perfect image" of the Father. Paul expressed this in his letters when he urged the Colossians to give thanks to God the Father, who "transferred us into the kingdom of the Son of his love, who is the image of the invisible God." And yet, I ask you, wise men: in what way or how can an image of an *invisible* thing consist? By what outlines, what sketches, what likeness, or what color shall it imitate that example which no one has ever seen, and which no one knows except the Son? But consider what follows: "No one knows the Father except the Son, and anyone to whom the Son chooses to reveal him." He is therefore a *living* and *willing* image, the Son himself. He is not a likeness made with colors by Apelles, nor carved by Pyrgoteles, nor cast in bronze by Polykleitos <note>Apelles, Pyrgoteles, and Polykleitos were the most famous painters, gem-carvers, and sculptors of Greek antiquity.</note>; he is not cast from any craftsman’s mixture, nor sculpted with a chisel, nor painted with a needle, nor fashioned from clay or wax. He is not like the longed-for and imaginary "little Aeneas" of Dido <note>A reference to Virgil's *Aeneid*, where Queen Dido wishes for a "small Aeneas" to play in her halls to remind her of her lover.</note>, who would resemble his father only in his face. Nor is he like Seth, born in the image of Adam, nor like the other sons of our own propagation, whom mothers desire to be as similar as possible to their husbands in appearance. <margin>To sons and descendants: shadows</margin> <margin>Fatherhood?</margin> For "Fatherhood" is said first and more truly of divine generation; regarding us and our descendants, it is said only later and in the manner of a kind of shadow. For the Apostle writes to the Ephesians: "For this reason I bow my knees to the Father of our Lord Jesus Christ, from whom all fatherhood in heaven and on earth is named." To be sure, among these mortal things, that which imitates its model either is not of the same substance with it (even if it is of equal shape), or if by chance it is of one substance, it will not be entirely equal in its remaining powers...

<summary>Reuchlin argues that the Son is the "Reason" or "Word" of God, acting as the perfect intellectual definition and "living image" that makes the invisible Father known to the human mind. He distinguishes this divine relationship from mere artistic representation or human biological reproduction, which he calls a "shadow" of the true divine Fatherhood.</summary>
<keywords>Logos, Aristotle, Stagirite, Divine Generation, Image of God, Imago Dei, Christology, Apelles, Paternitas, Reason, Intellectual Vision</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between the Father and the Son, specifically focusing on the Son as the "Word" (Logos). The previous page established the philosophical link between Mind and Reason; here, the author moves into the theological implications of the Incarnation and the specific titles of Christ, such as "Seal" and "Character," while beginning to connect these concepts to the practice of working miracles through the divine Name.</meta>

# BOOK THREE

...is the same, similar in counsel, equal in power, and equal in motion. Indeed, that divine likeness is so close to the Father—as close as anything can possibly be—that it would be more correct to call it "the same" rather than merely an "image." Where, however, the image is of the same substance <note>original: "homousios" — a Greek theological term meaning "of the same essence."</note>, there the thing itself is perceived entirely in its likeness, even if in its own nature it can be neither seen nor touched. 

<margin>Seal. <image-desc>A small ink drawing representing a signet ring or seal.</image-desc></margin> They also sometimes call this very image, which is of one substance with the highest Father, a "seal," following the same interpretation as mentioned above. For just as a signet ring works the form of a seal linearly through time, here the Father expresses Himself in His Word substantially, with eternity, and in a far more excellent way. 

<margin>Character.</margin> But regarding the "character" <note>original: "charactere" — a Greek term meaning a stamped mark or engraved impression, used here to describe the Son as the "express image" of the Father's person.</note>, since the Son is also honored with this name, I shall hold another opinion. I will soon explain this to you, since my own boldness—or to speak more truly, perhaps my excessive confidence in your kindness toward me—has not only led me into much speaking but has even surrendered me to it. Nevertheless, however it may be, I will gladly explain it, provided I first remind you that all the names of the most divine and wondrous Word ought to be understood in a three-fold way <note>original: "trifariam"</note>, according to the different conditions of Him whose names they are.

<margin>The names of the Word are understood in three ways.</margin> For it has become openly known to you, I believe, from our previous discussion—which, though brief, was clear—that He is the Word, the cause of the universe, God, and the arranger of nature; and furthermore, that in the beginning He was with God, like a hand in the bosom of the Father. Now it also remains for you to know what will be most important of all for our purpose of performing miracles: that this same God, the Word of God the Father, by His own Spirit proceeding from the Father, was finally incarnated in recent years from an incorrupt virgin, just as the prophecies of the seers many centuries before had predicted. Let us read the oracle of Jeremiah:

> Because the Lord Tetragrammaton <note>original: "Tetragrammus" — the sacred four-letter Name of God in Hebrew (YHWH).</note> has created a new thing on earth: a woman shall encompass a man.

This is astounding, this is new, this is admirable: that the female sex, without intercourse and without the embraces of a man—but by God alone, not merely making but creating—carried in her womb and surrounded with her womb not an embryo, but a perfect man. Because of this, Isaiah says:

> The Lord himself shall give you a sign: Behold, a virgin shall conceive and bear a son, and she shall call his name God-with-us.

<margin>Immanuel.</margin> For what else did the prophet wish to express by that name "Immanuel" except this alone: namely, the incarnate Word? Thus, because He is the Word, He is God; and because He dwells among us, He appears incarnate. Otherwise, it would be a sign of no value—one so greatly to be sought by a king, or one for which he was believed to have feared tempting God for no reason—if God had only promised to be with us according to His divine nature alone, by which He fills heaven and earth. 

<margin><unclear>Regarding the nature by which God fills the earth and promised to dwell with us</unclear></margin> 

<margin>Note: three things are to be considered.</margin> For no place is empty of God; but there were three things in that excellent sign to be weighed, not at all without great wonder by men. First, that a virgin would conceive; second, that a virgin would give birth; and finally, that the one born from her would be both God and man at the same time—individual points which have been found to be signified even to the spiteful and unwilling Hebrews. The universal Church of our ancestors will be the witness, who were present for such a great event and followed that "theanthropos" <note>original: "theanthropum" — a Greek term meaning "God-man."</note> (that is, the God-man) on foot. Therefore John, His disciple, made it known through his Epistle in this way:

> That which was from the beginning, which we have heard, which we have seen with our eyes, which we have looked upon, and our hands have handled, concerning the Word of life...

<column-break/>

It cites the prophecies of Jeremiah and Isaiah as proof of the Incarnation, emphasizing that Christ is both fully God and fully man (theanthropos).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the theological exploration of the "Word" (Logos), transitioning from the philosophical definitions of the Son as the "Seal" and "Character" of the Father to the mystery of the Incarnation. The author here uses the metaphor of leaven to describe how the divine nature permeates humanity.</meta>

### BOOK THREE

...and the life was made manifest, and we have seen and testify and announce to you the eternal life which was with the Father and appeared to us <note>original: "uita manifestata est... apparuit nobis" — a quotation from the First Epistle of John 1:2.</note>. And not only for that reason is that sign given by God esteemed so supereminent and admirable—because it appeared to us through bodily sight, and indeed in a human body—as if the angels had not once shown the same to the patriarchs, with whom they also lived and conversed in a human manner, and at times even the Lord God Himself. Rather, it is because He stained the entire mass of human substance by His arrival, just as a more excellent ferment <note>The author uses "fermentum" (leaven or yeast) as a metaphor for how the divine presence transforms the whole of humanity.</note> is said to permeate the entire mixture and every fate. This is that which excels every miracle, which we rightly value most highly, and which we pursue with unceasing proclamation. For the Deity of this divine man—that is, the incarnate Word—united to the whole ferment, which is our nature, has so elevated us by His permeation that, once the barrier of our ancestors' condemnation was removed, we are able to become divine children and be numbered among the gods through His name.

You have, therefore, three states of the Word: He is God, He is the Son, and He is Incarnate. For each of these, we find most religious titles in the sacred scriptures, either negating or affirming. For we announce God by nothing else more surely than by either a privative particle or a negative word <note>This refers to "apophatic" or negative theology, the idea that God is best described by what He is *not* (e.g., infinite/un-finite) because His essence exceeds human language.</note>, although not even that is certain, since He Himself, as far as His substance is concerned, is subject to no name; for whatever the symbols of affirmation assert concerning God, it is all neither His essence nor His nature, but that which is believed to be nearest and around it. Whence that saying of Hermes: "Since His name cannot be spoken by a human mouth, because to speak it is beyond man." <note>original: "Hermetis" — referring to Hermes Trismegistus, a legendary Hellenistic figure associated with hermetic philosophy.</note> And also that in the revelation of our theologian: "And on His head were many diadems, having a name written which no one knows but Himself, and He was clothed in a garment sprinkled with blood, and His name was called the Word of God." <note>A reference to Revelation 19:12–13.</note>

<margin>
6
Kether <term>Crown</term>
Head.
Hand?
Arm
Progenitor without time.
6 Revelation?
</margin>

In this place, the most sacred divination of that exile <note>Refers to St. John the Apostle, who was exiled to the island of Patmos where he wrote the Book of Revelation.</note> can instruct us clearly regarding this Son of God: both when He has a proper name and when He does not. Here, I hope, the prayer of Baruchia <note>original: "Baruchiæ" — likely referring to Rabbi Berechiah, a figure often cited in Renaissance Kabbalistic texts.</note> may come to our memory, in which he spoke of the first emanation from God, which according to the philosophy of the Hebrews is called *Kether*, that is, the "Diadem" or "Royal Majesty." Because it is the highest and most ample, it therefore contains within itself many diadems which are on the head—that is, in His multiform wisdom. Therefore, this first emergence of such great majesty from the Father of lights, since it is ineffable, cannot be assigned a peculiar name. For in the spirit, Isaiah exclaims: "Who shall declare His generation?" <note>Isaiah 53:8.</note> For if the prophet will not declare it, who shall declare it, since we perceive nothing of divine matters except by the prophetic spirit—that is, through a revelation fallen upon us from above? Wherefore a little before, he says: "To whom has the arm of the Lord been revealed?" <note>Isaiah 53:1.</note> Now it is clearly established from the preceding what the sacred letters mean by the "hand" or the "arm"—namely, nothing else in God than the substance whose nature, subsisting through itself, always depends with another and from another. For just as the Father is always whole and never lacking, so He was never sterile, but always a generator, because He is always a Father. But He is a generator without time; therefore, there is a perpetual Father of a perpetual Son. Such a distinction we conceive in the mind, under the obscure cloud of thought.

<vocab>Kether: The "Crown," the highest of the ten Sephirot in Kabbalah, representing the divine will. Baruchiæ: Refers to Rabbi Berechiah. Esaias: Isaiah. Brachium domini: Arm of the Lord. Verbum dei: Word of God. Hermetis: Hermes Trismegistus. Apocalypsis: The Book of Revelation.</vocab>
