<meta>This page continues the exploration of the divine "Word," moving from the general concept of the Son’s divinity to the specific ways that divine secrets are hidden within the Hebrew language. The author is now demonstrating how the mystery of the Trinity is encoded in the very names and verbs of the Old Testament, which remained "enigmas" until the coming of Christ.</meta>

# ON THE MIRIFIC WORD

But those things which we see through a glass darkly <note>original: "per speculum in ænigmate" — a reference to 1 Corinthians 13:12</note>, and only in part, we certainly cannot express with any proportionate, clear, or complete name. For this reason, in the Book of Revelation, the one wearing many crowns upon his head has a name that no one knows but himself <note>original: "nomen habet in Apocalypsi, quod nemo nouit, nisi ipse" — referring to Revelation 19:12</note>. This was rightly said concerning his own proper and adequate name, which encompasses the total definition of the thing itself. Indeed, no one knows this regarding the entire Trinity—not even the angels—except insofar as the Divinity itself has freely revealed of itself, according to our own measure; not as great as it is, but as great as we can grasp. Do you not see clearly enough that the one wearing the crowns lacks an equal name among us? But do you see how, once he is clothed in a blood-stained garment <note>original: "sanguinolentam uestem indutus" — referring to the image of Christ in Revelation 19:13</note>, he begins to be called the "Son of God" by men? For we held nothing stable, nothing firm, and nothing known regarding the Son before the arrival of the incarnate Word and his garment sprinkled with blood. Whatever we had been taught by prophets, philosophers, and poets, we judged to be entirely full of smoke and darkness. "The Lord said to my Lord, sit at my right hand," and "You are my son, today I have begotten you" <note>quoting Psalm 110:1 and Psalm 2:7</note>. Also, "His coming forth is from the beginning, from the days of eternity" <note>quoting Micah 5:2</note>. And, "The stone which the builders rejected has become the head of the corner. This was made by the Lord," or more truly, "this was from the Lord; it is marvelous in our eyes" <note>quoting Psalm 118:22–23</note>. Indeed, these are shadows in our eyes—these enigmas, these doubtful oracles. For who in that former age could then have shown me the Father and the Son in the "stone," which the Holy Scripture expressed in Hebrew as **Aben**?

<margin>Aben!</margin>
It is well established that in that language, *Ab* signifies "Father" and *Ben* signifies "Son." These three letters <note>In Hebrew, the word "Aben" (אבן) consists of three letters: Aleph (A), Bet (B), and Nun (N). Reuchlin is showing how the word for "Father" (Ab, אב) and "Son" (Ben, בן) are physically combined within the word for "Stone."</note>, joined into two syllables, form **Aben**, which is to say, "a stone." This is a mystery; this is a secret which I think I would not have perceived back then—I would have been a simpleton rather than a riddle-solver <note>original: "Dauus non oedippus" — a classical proverb meaning "a common man, not a solver of riddles."</note>. Similarly, in the word **Bra** <note>original: "Bra," meaning "he created" (ברא), the first verb in the Hebrew Bible</note>—which means "he created" and with which Moses’s book begins—I would not have sought a knot in that bulrush <note>original: "nodum in scirpo" — a proverb for looking for a problem where none exists, though here the author means looking for hidden depths</note> so as to examine letters of that kind, in which I might discover that "A" stands for the Father, "B" for the Son, and "R" for the Spirit. Nor would I have guessed that the voice of **Bra** itself was the first syllable in the beginning. By this argument, it would be evident either that the most Holy Trinity was the beginning of the world, or that the Father created heaven and earth in the Son—which happens in this manner: If anyone joins the last of the three letters of **Bra** to the first, **Ab** will immediately remain, which we have shown to be the "Father." Then, doubling the "B" and adding the remaining "R," it will sound as **Bebar**, which is to say, "in the Son" <note>Reuchlin is using a Kabbalistic technique of rearranging letters to reveal hidden meanings: "B-R-A" (Created) is manipulated to show "Ab" (Father) and "Be-Bar" (In the Son).</note>.

<margin>The Rule?</margin>
For as you know, among the Hebrews, vowels are not placed as such within the row of letters. Finally, if one takes the whole word, he will certainly perceive this entire mystery hidden in a single syllable: namely, "the Father created in the Son." Oh, what a marvelous thing! But it is one that requires a prophetic spirit or divine inspiration. In this manner, infinite things are contained within these most sacred characters—not only the fulfillment of past events but also certain foretelling of things to come—provided there is someone present who can not only interpret them, but prophesy them. For in this way, the spirits of the prophets of a past age become subject to future prophets. This is why the Apostle says: "I wish you all to speak in tongues, but even more to prophesy. For he who prophesies is greater than he who speaks in tongues" <note>quoting 1 Corinthians 14:5</note>. And as Plato says in the *Timaeus*, some are seers, and others are the interpreters of seers. Because truly, the same Paul, the teacher of the gentiles, even deigns to call poets by that title...

<sig>h iiii</sig>

Reuchlin argues that these hidden meanings remained "enigmas" until the coming of Christ provided the key to their interpretation.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Reuchlin's argument that the mysteries of the Christian Trinity were hidden not only in Hebrew prophecy but also in the ancient "theology" of Greek poets like Orpheus. He is bridging the gap between classical mythology and Christian revelation.</meta>

-> # ON THE MIRIFIC WORD <-

<margin>
Concerning the poets or prophets in the fortified writings. Night? (Greek: *graphē* — variant reading).
</margin>
Even regarding the poets, let us say this much of the prophets: Who, before the Word was incarnate, would have dared to apply the Orphic mystery of **Night**, **Heaven**, and **Aether**—which you also, Sidonius, recently mentioned—to the most simple Trinity of the highest God? Who would dare, with Orpheus, to say of the Father: "Night is the generation of all things," <note>original: "Nox generatio uniuerforum"</note> and therefore Night is the Father? Listen to Malachi: *Have we not all one father? Has not one God created us?* <note>Malachi 2:10</note> And according to the testimony of the Psalms, He *made darkness his secret place; his pavilion round about him.* <note>Psalm 18:11</note> Nor would that mystery be sufficient regarding the birth of his only Son, unless he also followed that same Orphic hymn of Night with: "Night, you who send forth light." <note>original: "Nox quæ lucem emittis"</note> Wherefore the prophet directs his prayers to God the Father thus: *Send out your light and your truth: let them lead me; let them bring me unto your holy hill, and to your tabernacles*—that is, to you, the Father, whose tabernacles (as appeared before) are darkness and a secret place. Both of these led me: your Son, who spoke in the treasury, saying, *I am the light of the world*, and the Spirit of truth who proceeds from you, the Father, as it is written in John; they have led and 
<margin>
See the high contemplation of Moses.
</margin>
brought me into a sublime kind of contemplation. For in this way we speak of the "mountain" which Moses ascended to reach God. Do you wish to hear the Apostle [Paul] teaching much more clearly? *For God, who commanded the light to shine out of darkness, hath shined in our hearts, to give the light of the knowledge of the glory of God in the person of Jesus Christ.* <note>2 Corinthians 4:6</note> For you should know that the embodied Word is hereafter called according to the Apostle’s custom, to which he added *prosopon*—that is, a "person"—which he intended to be the seal of the threefold distinction [of the Trinity]. 

Proceed now to hear what the ancient poet Orpheus sings concerning **Heaven**, whom he likewise calls the parent of all. If Night begat all things, as the previous hymn demonstrates, she also begat Heaven; therefore Night is the parent of Heaven. Yet she is not like a common parent of all things, but as one sending forth a celestial light from herself.

For just as Night is said to be the 
<margin>

Thus, what David sang is true: *For the darkness shall not be dark to you, and the night shall shine as the day; as its darkness is, so also is its light.* <note>Psalm 139:12</note> Consider now the consubstantial equal power of Night and Light. For "as its darkness is, so also is its light." Both are the creator of all, both are God, both are light: the Father in the Son, and the Son from the Father, as light from light, and the brightness of eternal light. Concerning which our Ennius <note>Quintus Ennius, an early Roman poet</note> says: "Behold," he says, "this sublime brightness, whom all invoke as Jove [Jupiter]." He spoke not of the Stygian [underworld] Jove, but of the celestial one, just as Euripides does in these verses: "You see the high, infinite Aether that embraces the earth everywhere with moist arms; believe this is Jove, consider this to be God." This is that sublime brightness of which Ennius taught us, whom the ancients

### BOOK THREE

-> and the last. <- Therefore, concerning our heaven, whom they considered in the Orphic mystery to be the son, a similar strain is sung in the second verse of that same hymn: *Aged by birth, the beginning of all, and the end of all.* <note>original: "Natiuitate senex, principium omnium, omniumque finis"</note> Indeed, he likewise says of Jupiter: *Jupiter is the first-born, and the latest, and the prince of lightnings.* You yourselves know the reason for this, since indeed the Son is God, and although he is God, he was nonetheless born as a man. Concerning him, it is written to the Corinthians: *The first man is of the earth, earthy: the second man is the Lord from heaven, heavenly.* <note>1 Corinthians 15:47</note> The conjecture regarding the **aether** is easy, which Orpheus names *Pyripnon*, that is, a **fiery breath**. <note>Greek: "pyripnoun"</note> I interpret the first verse of his hymn in this way—for he supplicates the aether, exclaiming in this manner: *O you who possess the imperial power of Jupiter, always insuperable.* Do you not now perceive the equality of power? Do you see the Jovian union—that is, the fellowship with heaven? For according to **Hermes Trismegistus**, <note>A legendary Hellenistic figure regarded as the author of the Hermetic Corpus, a series of sacred texts on philosophy and magic.</note> Jupiter is the *Ousia* <note>Greek: "ousia", meaning essence or substance</note> of heaven; through heaven, Jupiter provides life to all. Wherefore the Greeks call him **Zina** by a fitting name.
<margin>
Zēna (Zeus)
To live (zēn)
Dia (Zeus/Through)
</margin>
For we say *Zin* means **to live**. *In him* (says our theologian) *was life.* <note>John 1:4</note> Or also **Dia**, just as in Plato’s *Cratylus*, because it signifies the preposition **through** (per). For all things were made *Dia*, that is, **through him**. <note>John 1:3</note> He embraces in his moist arms both the spirit and the corporeal nature—that is, the aether and the
<margin>
Dionysius <unclear>abniẽ</unclear>
</margin>
earth—according to the opinion of **Euripides**, which you heard before. For since the only-begotten Son of God is the water of life, as the sacred utterances hand down, *descending like rain into the fleece, and as the drops that water the earth*, <note>Psalm 72:6</note> it is clear that he embraces with his moist arms, with his shining river, with that outflow of his, the aether—that is, that spirit nourishing all things within and that *fiery vigor*, as **Virgil** says, <note>Aeneid VI.730</note> and the celestial origin proceeding from the paternal substance. This proceeds not from that heaven which we perceive with our bodily eyes, but from the invisible—that is, the divine Son and the "heaven of heavens" from within himself. On account of this embrace, therefore, the Hebrews call heaven **Schamaim**,
<margin>
Eschamaim?
Aether?

as if it were *Esh-umaim*, that is, **fire and water**. <note>Reuchlin is using a mystical etymology connecting the Hebrew words "Esh" (fire) and "Mayim" (water) to form "Shamaim" (heaven).</note> For **Aether** is so named as if it were *aeth-aer*, from *aetho*, that is, "I burn," and *aer*, that is, "spirit"—a name which, according to Greek grammar, signifies a **burning spirit**. From Night, therefore, according to Orpheus, **Heaven** is born; from Heaven proceeds **Aether**. *And the evening and the morning were the first day.* <note>Genesis 1:5</note> This matter is very like a miracle and scarcely credible—or rather incredible—unless the Word of God had been incarnated, who would have instructed us in such great mysteries. It was one thing that was simultaneously evening, morning, and day, three days before the sun, moon, and stars were born. It was one light, distinguished into morning, evening, and day, which was the same as that same Disposer who said: **Let there be light**. Consider the words; weigh the mysteries. These are the words: *In the beginning God created the heaven and the earth.* <note>Genesis 1:1</note> Behold the briefest summary of the entire creation. But what was it that was made, and what was in the beginning before anything was made, and in what manner was that which was in the beginning, and who or of what condition was he who had already begotten the "beginning"? Then it follows: *Because the earth was a vast deformity.* Finally, heaven was like a garment which surrounded and concealed the earth, and wrapped it like a close-fitting cloak. And it was called the **abyss**, as in the hundred-and-third Psalm: *Who laid the foundations of the earth, that it should not be removed for ever. You covered it with the abyss as with a garment.* <note>Psalm 104:5-6 (Vulgate 103)</note>

He specifically links the Hebrew word for heaven, "Shamaim," to a combination of fire and water, mirroring the Greek definition of Aether as a burning spirit.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue in Book Three of Reuchlin's *On the Wonder-Working Word*. The previous page discussed the Orphic and Hermetic concepts of God as life and light. Here, the speaker (Capnion/Reuchlin) connects these pagan mysteries to the Genesis creation narrative and the Incarnation, transitioning into a systematic classification of divine names.</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD
<meta>catchword: consu</meta>

<margin>
clear/bright
clear sky
</margin>
...of him. Above the heaven was darkness, after the darkness water, and over the water the Spirit of the Lord. And so, God made the Word to flash forth by speaking, and there was light. And he divided the light from the darkness, and he called the light Day, and the darkness Night; and there was evening and there was morning, one day. Indeed, "darkness" and "evening" represent the **Orphic Night**. <note>In Orphic cosmogony, Night (Nyx) is a primordial deity from which all creation emerges; Reuchlin here aligns this with the darkness of Genesis.</note> "Light" and "day" represent heaven. But "morning" represents the **aether**. <note>The aether was considered the highest, purest part of the atmosphere, often associated with divine fire or the fifth element.</note> For the most ancient of the Latins used the word *manum* to mean "clear," just as the Greeks use the word *aethrion*. <note>original: "manum" and "æthrion"</note> From this, the word for "morning" [*mane*] is derived. Indeed, the day proceeds out of the night, and the morning depends upon both—not only before the creation of heaven and earth, but even before all eternity. These things have perhaps fallen from our lips too rashly, yet for the sake of our discussion, it seems to me quite opportune. I say this so you do not think there was ever a time in the memory of our fathers when our ancestors could have disentangled these and many similar things so accurately, so properly, and with such firmness of opinion before the Son was incarnated. But after that **Word of God** came into this flesh of ours to illuminate mankind, then the eyes of our mind were opened. Now, we recognize many things proclaimed by divine inspiration long ago—perhaps even more clearly than their own authors did—especially whatever pertains to the divinity of the Word. 

But let us return again to his names, from which it is necessary to select the **one wonder-working name** <note>original: "verbo mirifico"</note> in which alone we mortals, though situated in nature, may rule above nature and above fate, and may perform wonders, portents, miracles, and the very signs of divinity. And so that you may understand that various names have been attributed to that Word, I have decided to recall those which he holds in common with the Father and the Spirit. Then, those which are considered proper to his own divine subsistence; thirdly, those of the flesh; and lastly, those of the incarnate Word. 

In the beginning, my **Sidonius**, <note>Sidonius is one of the three interlocutors in the dialogue, representing a curious, secular, and somewhat Epicurean viewpoint.</note> you cannot help but marvel at how much convenience **Baruchias** <note>Baruchias is the Jewish scholar in the dialogue, representing the wisdom of the Hebrew tradition and the Kabbalah.</note> provided with his speech yesterday. I know this one thing: you will congratulate me when you see me relieved of such a vast enumeration of names. For all those names signifying divine ineffability, essence, abstraction, and relation—as well as those signifying power, operation, likeness, and whatever follows or adheres to the supreme nature—are predicated of the Son (whose name is wonder-working) and simultaneously of the entire Trinity. We have decided to separate them in this way: some of them do not indicate what the nature of the divinity *is* (for this would be impossible according to human instruction), but rather what exists *around* it. These pertain to the **Essence** which you, my Baruchias, have in my opinion already completed quite copiously and abundantly. 

Let us refer the others to the various management of the **Divine Economy**. <note>term: Divine Economy. From the Greek "oikonomia," referring to God's plan and actions in the world, particularly the Incarnation, as opposed to "Theologia," which refers to God's internal nature.</note> This latest root grows into two branches: into the arrangement of things to be carried out without a body, and into the condition of the assumed flesh—that is, of the man. The former are attributed to the Word in common with the Trinity, the latter specifically to the Word. For the term **Essence** <note>original: "Vsias" (Ousia)</note> and all the names that fit that united One are common to the supersubstantial and united Trinity. Therefore, leaving behind those things that pertain to the Essence (as we were sufficiently taught about them in yesterday’s debate), let us now attempt to recite in summary those which are believed to represent dignity and power. For instance: **He Who Is**, and **I Myself**, and **Fire**...

# BOOK THREE

...you consume, and the **Tetragrammaton** <note>The sacred four-letter name of God in Hebrew (YHWH), which Reuchlin considers the most powerful of all words.</note>, which signifies that which cannot be spoken, seem to me to have been sufficiently explained by you, Baruchias, and by the sharpness of your more skillful studies—in which you are more gifted than I—regarding those things which you considered to be of substance. <margin>God: Damascene, Isidore, and Gregory</margin> We cannot deny that the titles **Lord**, **King**, and **God** signify an imperial majesty, if we follow John Damascene. <note>John of Damascus (c. 675–749), a Syrian monk and scholar who wrote "The Orthodox Faith."</note> Gregory Nazianzen <note>A 4th-century Archbishop of Constantinople known for his "Theological Orations."</note> certainly places these among the names that are relative—that is, they refer to something else. For we read "Lord of lords," "King of kings," "Holy of holies," and "God of gods." Only **He Who Is** <note>original: "Ωn" (The Being)</note> is judged to be the most powerful name for God in and of Himself. And although grammarians say that the word "God" <note>original: "deus"</note> is an altered version of the Greek word *Theos*, which is believed to be derived from *aithein* <note>original: "Aethin"</note>—according to Plato—meaning "to burn," because God is a consuming fire, and from this it might seem to be a mark of His substance; nevertheless, it is not of such a kind that it explains what something is in itself without comparison to another.

For the Lord said to Moses: *Thus you shall say to the children of Israel, the Lord God of your fathers, the God of Abraham, the God of Isaac, the God of Jacob has sent me to you; this is my name forever.* That voice, therefore, expects some others to whom He is "God," just as a lord is a lord over subjects, and a king is placed over inferiors; thus He alone is the most high and exalted. *I said, you are gods and all of you sons of the Most High,* and the Sublime One inhabiting eternity. The Cause, the Prince, the Leader, the First and the Last, the Alpha and the Omega, the Beginning and the End, the Head, and the Eye of Man—each of these is understood in relation to something else. In His power, however, God is wonderful, almighty, great, terrible, living, seeing, eternal, the Ancient of Days, enduring, strong, everlasting life, and the Lord of Virtues. <margin>God from "to run" <note>original: "theos a theo"</note></margin> Some judge the word "God" to display energy, as they are accustomed to derive *Theos* from *thein* <note>the Greek verb for "to run."</note>, meaning to run through, to move, or to penetrate. For this reason, the Lord speaks in Exodus: *Do not worship a foreign god; the Lord, "Jealous" is His name, He is a jealous God.* But God also possesses titles derived from other works, which Dionysius <note>Dionysius the Areopagite, a Christian Neoplatonist who wrote "On the Divine Names."</note> calls "effects" and Nazianzen calls "attributes." From the greatness and beauty of His deeds, it is reasonable for the Maker to be estimated. For the highest Creator is not easily made manifest to us from His own substance, which has no form, but from these things that are present in the created things themselves. In this way He is called: Maker of Wonders, Warrior, Destroyer of Wars, Protector, Giver of Life, Father of All, Creator, Redeemer, Savior, and others of that kind. <margin>Sea of goodness, source of life, Sun: God</margin> A similarity follows which is broad in scope and arrives from several comparisons; for instance, from all good things—nourishing, bodily, rational, legal, mental, and angelic—gathered into one Author, He is called the **Sea of Goodness**. From all conditions of living gathered into one origin, He is called the **Source of Life**. And from all brightness, splendor, and light thrust into one clarity, He is named the **Sun**. For the divine philosopher wrote that what the sun is to sensible things, God is to intellectual things. Another kind of similarity is taken by the comparison of one to one and equal to equal, just as if I wished to compare the most divine Trinity to the clarity of the sun, I would then compare any light of the sun to any subsistence of the Trinity. For in this way I have become accustomed to name the **hypostasis** <term>hypostasis: the distinct personhood of the Father, Son, or Holy Spirit as opposed to their shared divine essence.</term>, as you will hear more clearly hereafter.

meum

### ON THE MIRACULOUS WORD

<margin>
morning
bright
clear sky
</margin>
...of it. Above the heavens there was darkness; after the darkness, water; and above the water, the Spirit of the Lord. Thus God, by speaking, caused the **Word** to flash forth, and there was light. And He divided the light from the darkness, and called the light Day, and the darkness Night; and there was evening and there was morning, one day. 

Indeed, "darkness" and "evening" represent the **Orphic Night** <note>In Orphic mythology, "Night" is the primordial source of all creation, which Reuchlin here links to the "darkness" before the creation of light in Genesis.</note>. "Light" and "Day" represent heaven, while "Morning" represents the **aether** <note>The "aether" was considered the pure, upper air or the fifth element that fills the universe beyond the terrestrial sphere.</note>. For the most ancient of the Latin speakers used the word *manum* <note>original: "manum"</note> to mean the same thing as "clear" or "bright," just as the Greeks use the word *aethrion* <note>original: "æthrion", meaning clear sky or fair weather</note>. From this, the word for "morning" <note>original: "mane"</note> is derived. Day indeed proceeds out of night, and morning depends upon both—not only before the creation of heaven and earth, but even before all eternity. 

These words have perhaps escaped our lips too rashly; yet, for the sake of our discussion, I believe they appear quite opportunely. I say this so that you do not think there was ever a time in the memory of our fathers when our ancestors could have disentangled these matters—and many others of this kind—with such accuracy and such firm conviction before the Son became incarnate. But after that **Word of God** came into this flesh of ours to enlighten humanity, then the eyes of our mind were opened. Now, through divine grace, we may understand many things that were proclaimed long ago—perhaps even more clearly than their original authors did—especially whatever pertains to the divinity of the Word.

<margin><unclear>scribal mark</unclear></margin>

But let us return to His names. Among these, it is necessary to choose the one **mirific** <note>The word "mirific" comes from the Latin *mirificus*, meaning "wonder-making" or "miraculous." It is the central concept of this book.</note> name, in which alone we mortals, though situated in nature, may rule above nature and above fate, and be able to perform signs, portents, miracles, and the very marks of divinity. 

And so that you may understand that various names have been attributed to that Word, I have decided to recount those which He holds in common with the Father and the Spirit. Then, we shall look at those which are considered proper to His own divine subsistence; thirdly, those of the flesh; and finally, those of the Incarnate Word. 

At the start of this, my dear Sidonius, you cannot help but marvel at how much benefit Baruchias <note>Baruchias is the Jewish interlocutor in the dialogue who represents the wisdom of the Hebrew tradition.</note> has brought us with his speech yesterday. I know this one thing: you will congratulate me when you see me relieved of such a long enumeration of names. For all things that signify divine ineffability, essence, abstraction, and relation—in addition to distinct power, operation, and likeness, and whatever follows or adheres to the supreme nature—are predicated also of the Son of God (whose name is **mirific**) and simultaneously of the entire Trinity. 

We have decided to separate these in the following way: some do not indicate what the nature of the divinity *is* (for that would be impossible according to human understanding), but rather what exists *around* it. These pertain to the **Essence** <note>original: "Vsias" / "Ousia", the Greek term for being or substance.</note>, which you, my Baruchias, have in my opinion already completed quite copiously and abundantly. Others we shall refer to the various workings of His providence. This latter "root" rises into two branches: 
1. The arrangement of things conducted without a body.
2. The disposition of the assumed flesh—that is, the human condition. 

The former are attributed to the Word in common with the Trinity; the latter are attributed to the Word specifically. For all the names that fit the body united to Him are common to the supersubstantial and unified Trinity. Therefore, leaving behind those things which belong to the Essence—as we were sufficiently taught about them in yesterday's discussion—let us now attempt to recite in summary those which are believed to represent His dignity and power. These include **He Who Is**, and **I Myself**, and **Fire**...

...**consuming fire**, and the **Tetragrammaton** <note>The sacred four-letter name of God in Hebrew, YHWH.</note>, which signifies His ineffability, seem to me to have been sufficiently explained by you, Baruchia. Indeed, they have been handled with the sharpness of your more expert studies—more than I myself possess—which you considered to be matters of substance. Yet we cannot deny that "Lord" signifies imperial majesty, as do "King" and "God," if we follow the authority of **John of Damascus** <note>An 8th-century theologian who summarized the teachings of the early Church Fathers.</note>. Certainly, the famous **Gregory of Nazianzus** <note>One of the four great Greek Doctors of the Church, known as "The Theologian."</note> places these among the names that are used relatively <note>That is, names that describe God's relationship to His creation rather than His inner essence.</note>. For we read "Lord of lords," "King of kings," "Holy of holies," and "God of gods." Only *Ho On* <note>original: "Ων", Greek for "He Who Is"</note>, that is, "He Who Is," is considered to be the most primary name for God's being in itself.

And although grammarians teach that the word for "God" <note>original: "deus"</note> is an altered form of the Greek *theos* <note>original: "theos"</note>, which is believed to have descended from *aethin* <note>original: "Aethin"</note> according to **Plato**—meaning "to burn"—because God is a "consuming fire," from which it might seem to be a mark of His substance; nevertheless, it is not the kind of name that explains what He is in Himself without comparison to another. For the Lord said to Moses:
> Thus shall you say to the children of Israel: The Lord God of our fathers, the God of Abraham, the God of Isaac, the God of Jacob has sent me to you; this is my name forever.

Therefore, that voice expects certain others to whom He is "God." Just as a lord is a lord of subjects, and a king is set over inferiors, so He alone is the Most High and the Exalted.
> I said, "You are gods, and all of you are children of the Most High," and the Lofty One inhabiting eternity.

"Cause," "Prince," "Leader," "First and Last," "**Alpha and Omega**," "Beginning and End," "Head," and "Eye of Man"—all these are understood as being related to something else. But in His own power, God is wonderful, omnipotent, great, terrible, living, seeing, eternal, the **Ancient of Days**, abiding, strong, eternal life, and the Lord of Hosts.

<margin>God from "to run"</margin>
Likewise, God is judged by some to exhibit **Energy** <note>original: "Energiam"</note>, specifically those who are accustomed to derive the word *theos* from *theein* <note>original: "thein"</note>, which means "running through," "moving," or "penetrating." For this reason, the Lord speaks in Exodus:
> Do not worship a foreign god; the Lord, "Jealous" is His name, He is a jealous God.

But from His other works, which **Dionysius** <note>Dionysius the Areopagite, a Christian Neoplatonist author.</note> calls "effects" and Nazianzus calls "attributes," God Himself possesses further titles. From the greatness and beauty of His deeds, it is possible to reasonably estimate the Craftsman. For the supreme Creator is not easily made manifest to us from His own substance—which has no form—but rather from these attributes and from the very things He has effected. In this way He is called:
> Doer of wonders, a Man of war, Destroyer of battles, Protector, Life-giver, Father of all, Creator, Redeemer, Savior, and others of that kind.

fountain of life

Then follows "Likeness," which is a broad category. It arises from several comparisons, such as when He is called a **sea of goodness** <note>original: "pelagus bonitatis"</note> by gathering all good things—nourishing, bodily, rational, legal, mental, and angelic—into one Author. Or, by collecting all conditions of living into one origin, He is called the **fountain of life**. And by pressing all brightness, splendor, and light into one clarity, He is named the **Sun**. For the divine philosopher wrote that what the sun is to sensible things, God is to intellectual things. Another type of likeness is taken from the comparison of one to one, and like to like. For example, if I wished to compare the most divine Trinity to the brightness of the sun, I would then compare any light of the sun to any subsistence of the Trinity. For this is how I have become accustomed to name a **hypostasis** <note>original: "hypostasim", a term for the individual "persons" or distinct realities within the one Godhead.</note>, as you will hear more clearly hereafter...

<meta>This page continues the discourse of Capnion (Reuchlin) in Book Three. He is refining the terminology used to describe the Divine essence, moving from technical philosophical distinctions to a poetic and sweeping list of the names and attributes of God and the Divine Word (the Son).</meta>

### ON THE MIRACULOUS WORD

<margin>
God [as] Sun:
Sun, sphere, ray, light.
God [as] Father, Son, Spirit.
Soul [as] Mind, Reason, Sense.
</margin>

...in divine matters, I have chosen to use not "substance" as **Boethius** <note>An influential 6th-century philosopher and theologian who helped define the terminology of the Trinity.</note> did, but—as it seemed to me more fitting to the subject—"subsistence," <note>original: "subsistentia"</note> so as not to coincide with *ousia* <note>original: "Vsia"; a Greek term for "essence" or "being."</note>, which almost every translator routinely renders as "substance." For I have determined to be not a mere maker of words, but an imitator of established ways; nor will the matter be at great risk as long as the thing itself is made clear by common usage. 

You have certainly heard that God is called the **Sun** by the philosophers. Just as the sun consists of the sphere, the ray, and the light, and all these are one sun; so God is the Father, the Son, and the Spirit, and all these are one God. Let the same be judged concerning the soul. For "God is a mind to us," as the poems say <note>A reference to a common Pythagorean or Orphic sentiment found in classical literature.</note>. There is also the human soul, which consists of mind, reason, and sense. The mind illuminates the reason, and the reason flows into the sense—all are one soul. It is the center of nature, the middle of all things, the sequence of the world, the face of all, the measure and the bond of the world; by this means, even if under an obscure image, one may nonetheless be compared to God. 

Furthermore, many attributes accompany the divine nature from which divinity takes its name, such as unity, truth, and goodness. God is also said to be alone, always, and everywhere, understanding the infinite, understanding Himself first and each individual thing, acting through His own being upon all things. He is named **love** and **charity**, immortal providence; He is proclaimed as wisdom, beauty, justice, peace, and perfection. Finally, there is a vast number of terms that describe God much more truly through privation or negation <note>This refers to "apophatic theology," the idea that we describe God best by saying what He is NOT (e.g., "infinite" or "not-finite"), because His excellence exceeds human language.</note>—not because of any lack in the thing itself, but because of His super-excellence, for God exceeds all speech. 

The purpose of this whole enumeration is so that you might consider all these titles to belong not only to God the Father and the Spirit, but also to the **Word**, who is the most beloved Son of God, the best and greatest, the eternal God, ineffable, incomprehensible, immovable eternity, who is one [with] all, and the beginning of all that exists. For He Himself is the creator of all, and through Him the Father created all things; and He is the same as the **consuming fire**, and the **Tetragrammaton** <note>The sacred four-letter Hebrew name of God, YHWH.</note>, light, day, Olympus <note>Used here as a poetic synonym for the highest heaven.</note>, King of kings and Lord of lords, God of gods, Holy of holies, the God of Abraham, the God of Isaac, the God of Jacob. He alone is the most high, exalted, sublime, inhabiting eternity, the prince, the leader, the first and the last, **Alpha and Omega**, the beginning, the middle, and the end. 

The head and the eye of man, perfection, peace, justice, beauty, wisdom, immortal providence, charity and love, understanding the infinite, understanding Himself first and each individual thing, acting through His own being upon all things, always and everywhere. Unity and truth and goodness, mind, reason, sun, ray, the fountain of life, the sea of goodness, the cause, the father of all, creator, protector, savior, redeemer, a man of war <note>original: "uir pugnator"; a biblical title for God (Exodus 15:3).</note>, performing wonders, crushing wars. The life-giving Lord, jealous <note>original: "zelotes"</note>, and an envious God, God Almighty, wonderful, great, terrible, living and seeing me, the Ancient of Days, remaining, strong, the Lord of virtues, and eternal life. 

With these and similar praises, the Word is celebrated in common with the other persons of the Trinity; but by this very fact He is specifically celebrated as being the **Son**, begotten without beginning—since He is said to have a Father but not a beginning, and to be begotten from the "always-Father" always and without suffering; one from one and uniquely, whom the whole shows the whole, and adequately demonstrates His nature. Whatever words aspire to this, whatever words suggest such a distinction, will be of the kind that investigate the most divine Word—which in the present discourse is worthy of the highest praises...

<meta>catchword: ...investigate (muspe)</meta>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page, where Capnion (Reuchlin) is refining the theological vocabulary used to describe the Trinity. He is specifically addressing the linguistic confusion between Greek and Latin terms for "being," "substance," and "person."</meta>

### BOOK THREE
i

<gloss>Being</gloss> <gloss>hypostasis</gloss> <gloss>Substance</gloss> <gloss>subsistence</gloss> <margin>person?</margin>

...will explain with a special ornament, just as the only-begotten in the bosom of the Father, of whom we spoke before, and the mark <gloss>hypostasis</gloss> <note>hypostasis: A Greek term meaning "underlying reality," used in theology to denote the distinct persons of the Trinity.</note> of his person of which we promised to speak. For this purpose, it is clearly read in Paul today: "Who, being the brightness of glory and the image of his substance," <note>A reference to Hebrews 1:3. In the Latin Vulgate, "hypostasis" was often translated as "substantia" (substance).</note> perhaps by that custom where *hypostasis* is translated from "standing under" as **substance**, and not from "subsisting" as <gloss>Subsistence</gloss> **subsistence**, even by certain learned men. This interpretation is established in the book *On the Two Natures*, which is attributed to **Boethius** <note>An influential 6th-century philosopher whose definitions of "person" and "nature" became standard in Western theology.</note>. Yet, even as he was turning the volumes of **Aristotle** into Latin, he likewise calls *Ousia* <note>original: "Vsia"; the Greek word for "essence" or "being."</note> "substance," thus transgressing his own rules. This inconsistency pours a significant cloud over the eyes of many, so that almost no students of theology can bear this variety with a calm mind. It is full of weariness, or rather hatred, to interpret different conditions with a single explanation, especially in a matter not to be adorned with oratorical polish and the little flowers of speech, but with the strength of truth and the firmness of discipline. 

I pray you, listen attentively and diligently to what the issue is. The senate and the faithful people of the Christian republic <note>A metaphorical way of referring to the Church councils and the body of believers.</note> have decreed that it must be so felt and so confessed: that there is only one true God—eternal, immense, unchangeable, incomprehensible, omnipotent, and ineffable—Father, Son, and Holy Spirit; three persons indeed, but one essence, substance, and nature, entirely simple. These are the very words of the decree; this is the law, this is the edict, this is the true rule according to religion by which we are taught that substance, essence, and nature are one and the same thing. 

However, *hypostasis* in the highest divinity is not slightly different [from these], for it belongs to the one and only essence, nature, and substance. There are three *hypostases*, which in Latin even that same Boethius translated as "persons," and in one single *hypostasis* two substances consist. <note>This likely refers to the two natures (divine and human) in the one person of Christ.</note> Therefore, *hypostasis* and substance will not be the same thing to say or used in the same way, just as "person" and "substance" are not. For the Catholic faith is this: that we worship one God in Trinity and Trinity in Unity, neither confounding the persons nor dividing the substance; in which we recognize that "substance" signifies one thing, and *hypostasis* another. 

For the Father, by generating the Son from eternity, gave him his own substance and nonetheless retained it; and the Son, by being born, received the substance of the Father. But neither did the Father transfer his own *hypostasis* or his own person to the Son, nor did the Son assume it from him. For they are both of two *hypostases* and two persons, yet truly of one and the same substance. For this reason, I could easily believe that **Jerome** <note>Saint Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), the translator of the Latin Vulgate Bible.</note> translated *hypostasis* in this place not as "substance" but rather as "subsistence," especially in a matter of such weight, where he perceived no small difference to lie between *hypostasis* and *Ousia*. This same *Ousia* is found to be correctly translated as "substance" in the first volume of Luke and the fifteenth chapter of his book. For in our manner, *Ousia* should be called "substance," but *hypostasis* should be called "subsistence." Thus, when the Apostle says "the character of the hypostasis," we should understand it as "the figure of the subsistence." For how could the word "character" be a seal or figure of the Father’s substance, when it is not merely similar but is truly that very whole substance of the Father, and nothing else besides it? Therefore, if the character is different from that whose form it expresses, as **Chrysostom** <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407 AD), an influential Early Church Father and Archbishop of Constantinople.</note> testifies, it was necessary...

<vocab>Hypostasis (person/subsistence), Ousia (essence/being), Substantia (substance), Subsistentia (subsistence), Persona (person), Trinitas (Trinity), Boëthius (Boethius), Aristoteles (Aristotle), Hieronymus (Jerome), Chrysostomus (Chrysostom)</vocab>

<summary>This page analyzes the technical Latin and Greek vocabulary used to describe the Trinity, arguing that "hypostasis" should be translated as "subsistence" rather than "substance" to maintain the distinction between the shared divine essence and the three distinct Persons.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological and theological exploration of the names of Christ, specifically focusing on the distinction between the Greek terms "hypostasis" (person/subsistence) and "ousia" (substance/essence), and how these relate to Christ's identity as the Image, Wisdom, and Way.</meta>

### BOOK THREE

The Son is said to be the <term>character</term> <note>original: "characterem"; here meaning an engraved mark or exact representation, as on a coin.</note> of the Father’s <term>hypostasis</term> <note>original: "hypostaseos"; referring to the distinct "person" or "subsistence" of the Father.</note>—that is, of His subsistence—but not the seal of His <term>Ousia</term> <note>original: "Vsias"; the Greek word for essence or "being."</note>, which is to say, His substance. For the Father, by begetting, marked the person of the Son, just as the shape of a signet ring expresses the form of the seal, as He Himself taught concerning Himself: "Work," He said, "not for the food that perishes, but for that which remains unto eternal life, which the Son of Man will give you; for God the Father has sealed him." He is named, moreover, the splendor of clarity or glory, because He is Light from Light, as if indicating a communion of one essence, through which two subsistences might more truly become known to us.

The Image, however, or <term>Icon</term> <note>original: "Icon"; the Greek word for image or likeness.</note>, although it is the full manifestation of the Father—as we revealed a short moment ago—must by no means be understood as a physical thing, but rather alongside the reality; for it is not artificial but natural, born without time, the cause of eternity.

Next follows "the power of God and the wisdom of God," the name by which our sun, Paul, writing to the Corinthians, designates Him. "We preach," he said, "to those who are called, both Jews and Greeks, Christ the power of God and the wisdom of God." For He possesses all power within Himself in the highest degree, and possesses it beforehand as the author of all power, producing all things through a power that is most firm and not limited. When the prophet had contemplated this with the eyes of his mind, he exclaimed: "Be exalted, O Lord, in your power."

What shall we say of His wisdom, whose "foolishness" is wiser than men? For He is full of all wisdom and understanding, and "His wisdom is beyond measure." But He is also placed transcendently above all reason, mind, and wisdom, being the giver of all wisdom and the cause of all intelligence; He proceeded from the head of God <note>A classical allusion to the birth of Athena, used here to describe the eternal generation of the Word from the Father's mind.</note>. In Him, truly, all the treasures of wisdom and knowledge are hidden.

Furthermore, since the Son of God calls Himself the Way, the Truth, and the Life, it will certainly befit us to venerate those names with religious worship; they are the closest signs of the Almighty Creator, urging us toward the supreme blessedness of our souls. "Blessed," says the prophet, "are the undefiled in the way." For they obtain the vision of God in a pure heart, which is the only blessedness of men, the only happiness, which is granted to us through this one Way. Let us hear from His own mouth: "No one," He says, "comes to the Father except through me."

The Way, therefore, by which one goes to the Father is the Son; and for that reason He is the Life, because this gate, this path, this door is our science and knowledge of the Father. He is, therefore, the Truth; for otherwise there would be error, not science, not knowledge. He is also the Truth because He is One; for what is false is many, not one. Yet perhaps these last two names are common to the entire Trinity. But "the Way" seems to be the Son's own proper name. Many such forms of naming are contained in the Sacred Scriptures, differing in appearance but praiseworthy in kind, which all harmonize at this goal: that the Word of God—that is, the Son—was alone begotten from the substance of the Father without beginning, the Only-Begotten. He is the mark and figure of the Father’s subsistence, the splendor of glory, the filial hypostasis and person of the Word, the seal of the Father, Light from Light, the Image or Icon, the power of God and the wisdom of God, the Way, the gate, and the door.

Now it remains to commemorate very briefly those things which follow His human nature. This Word, therefore, although He is the eternal Son of God, begotten from the substance of the Father before the ages, was nevertheless born in time from the substance of a virgin—perfect God and perfect man, consisting of a rational soul and human flesh...

e

### BOOK THREE

i ii

...subsisting. Therefore, since He is man, you should think that nothing human is absent from Him except for sin; indeed, having become all things for all people, He became whatever we are, sin excepted. For this reason, He does not shrink from, nor disdain, nor refuse the manners and titles of a man. He is the Son of Man, a shepherd, a high priest, obedient, a servant, a deputy, one sent, speaking nothing of Himself, judging no one, not granting seats at the right or the left, not doing His own will, not knowing the day of fulfillment, subject to the one who subjected all things, spending the night in prayer, praying on the mountain, asking the Father, questioning where they had placed Lazarus, growing in wisdom, age, and grace before God and men, sleeping in the little boat, hungering after fasts, weary from the journey, weeping, anxious, strengthened by an angel, thirsting, carrying the cross, crucified, dead.

I now pass to the flesh: that He was even a worm and not a man, a serpent in the desert, a grain falling into the earth, a vine, a lamb, a sacrifice, wrapped in a linen shroud, buried. You, therefore, who have this concern and care, and who are eager for the performing of miracles, choose from this assembly, from this army, from this great <term>legion</term> <note>The author uses military imagery ("army," "legion") to describe the many names and titles attributed to Christ in scripture.</note> of sacred names, which one should be used most effectively in such operations.

Then **SIDONIUS** said: "I willingly and gladly escape this duty of the priesthood and transfer it all to you, who preside over the order of today’s disputation; or, if this man **Baruchias** desires it, the pulpit should be granted to him. For I have been initiated into other sacred rites since my earliest youth <note>original: "a teneris unguiculis," literally "from tender fingernails," a Latin idiom meaning from earliest childhood.</note>. I have known no majesty, no god, no excellence, except for those brightest lights of the world which we see: the sun, the maker of the day, and the moon, the ornament of the night; or perhaps those other little lights which you call constellations and stars. And later, whenever I wished to be most deeply religious, there occurred those names of invisible powers which Ennius <note>Quintus Ennius (c. 239–169 BC), an early Roman poet often called the "father of Roman poetry."</note> duly encompassed in these two verses: **Juno, Vesta, Minerva, Ceres, Diana, Venus, Mars, Mercury, Jove, Neptune, Vulcan, Apollo.**

Or those which the most ancient Orpheus <note>The legendary Greek musician; here the author refers to the *Orphic Hymns*, a collection of poems addressed to various deities.</note>, writing to Musaeus, enumerates more extensively: Earth, Proserpina, Liber <note>Another name for Bacchus or Dionysus.</note>, the Foam-born <note>original: "Spumigena," a title for Aphrodite or Venus.</note>, Hebe, Hercules, the Nymphs, Pan, Memory, the Muses, the Graces, the Seasons, the Year, Latona, Dione, the Curetes, the Corybantes, Castor, Pollux, Themis, Night, Day, Faith, Justice, Rhea, Saturn, Tethys, Ocean, Eternity, Time, Styx, Providence <note>original: "Pronoea," from the Greek word for foresight or divine providence.</note>, heavenly spirits, spirits of the air, water, earth, underworld, and fire, Semele, Bacchus, Ino, Leucothea, Palaemon, Victory, Adrastia, Aesculapius, Pallas, the Winds, Thunders, Month, Adonis, the Furies <note>original: "Eumenides."</note>, Hecate, and others whom that same grave author mentions in his hymns. However, while reading at times when other matters were not pressing, I happened to slip into foreign subjects and crept like a scout into enemy camps. Thus I read the Gospels and the books of the Christians."

"And I as well," said **BARUCHIAS**. "For those demons of yours, Sidonius—although I read of them with horror and trembling—I have nevertheless sometimes read of them, though I always judged them not so much worth reading as worth neglecting. This is because they stood far apart from the instruction of our homeland, and it did not agree with reason that something made by another should be named a god. Thus, I more easily accepted that the Word of God is God, since it is the same with God and without beginning, born together with the Father and from the Father before all eternity, and before all things..."

### ON THE MIRACULOUS WORD

...ages. For that which was born of the virgin was not so much a matter of essence as of dispensation <note>The author distinguishes between Christ’s eternal "essence" as God and the "dispensation" or arrangement of His becoming human.</note>. For He willed it so, and therefore it was so; He could have assumed another nature if He had willed. For "the Lord has done all things whatsoever He willed in heaven and on earth" <note>original: "Omnia... dominus fecit"; a citation of Psalm 135:6.</note>. However, since you give me the choice of choosing among so many names, as to which might be most effective in the working of miracles—though I have previously confessed that I know almost nothing about them—the power of the sacred names has not deigned to accompany my ancestors down to this present age. This is despite the fact that in the memory of our fathers, we read of great works performed beyond nature and beyond fate in the name of the <term>Tetragrammaton</term> <note>The four-letter Hebrew name of God (YHWH), held to be unpronounceable and possessed of immense spiritual power.</note>. Perhaps this power is lost because there have been found among the Hebrews (as elsewhere in the world) sacrilegious, poisonous, and superstitious exorcists, whose minds are obstructed by evil spirits in every nation. Therefore, we pray you, **Capnion** <note>Capnion is the Greek name used by Johannes Reuchlin, the author, as his persona in the book. It means "smoky," a play on his German name "Reuchlin" (Rauch means smoke).</note>, proceed so that we may not remain ignorant of which among all the most sacred names is more powerful for miracles, which is more prompt, and which is most ready at hand. For **Sidonius** <note>The third character in the dialogue, representing the Christian viewpoint.</note> desires this along with me. "Indeed," he says, "I desire nothing else, and I pray you again and again to proceed."

At this, **CAPNION** said: "It is clear that neither the words of our voice nor the signs of written lines have any force, power, or command—not only over those effects which occur above nature, but not even over those whose mother is nature—unless they are joined by the virtue of a nobler operation. For Aristotle, in the book he published *On the Soul* <note>original: "De Anima"</note>, left it sufficiently proven to the <term>Peripatetics</term> <note>The followers of Aristotle’s philosophy, named for his habit of walking while teaching.</note> that neither sound nor scent possesses any dominion over a body or works any effect upon substances, except through some accidental quality. For we are composed of primary, not tertiary, qualities. Therefore, the nobler virtue by which words bring force to things beyond—or rather above—nature and the perpetual course of the world, is not a natural power, but one that is also above nature. This is because anything that acts under the guidance of nature works either upon something similar or something inferior, but never above its own species. Thus, since we agreed yesterday without controversy that the omnipotence of God alone is the creator of miracles—for "Blessed be the Lord God of Israel," as the King <note>Referring to King David in Psalm 72:18.</note> prophesied, "who alone does great wonders"—we shall not be far from the truth if we believe that God Himself has bestowed a singular ray of His omnipotence upon every miraculous word. This ray is the power that works a wonderful effect, brought down to us through the ministry of angels, just as we have discovered through the testimony of Holy Writ regarding certain figures or secret preparations."

> Make a bronze serpent and set it up as a sign; whoever is struck and looks upon it shall live. <note>A reference to Numbers 21:8, where looking at a bronze serpent healed those bitten by snakes.</note>

Likewise:

> If the spirit of jealousy arouses you <note>A reference to the "Ordeal of the Bitter Water" in Numbers 5.</note>, after the offering is made, the priest shall take holy water in an earthen vessel, and shall cast into it a little earth from the floor of the tabernacle. And when the woman stands in the sight of the Lord, he shall uncover her head and place upon her hands the sacrifice of remembrance and the offering of jealousy. He himself shall hold the most bitter waters, into which he has heaped curses with an execration, and he shall adjure her and say: "If no strange man has slept with you, and if you have not been defiled by deserting your husband’s bed, these most bitter waters into which I have heaped curses shall not harm you. But if you have turned aside from your husband and are defiled, and have lain with another, you shall be subject to these curses: May the Lord give you..."

<summary>In this passage, the characters discuss why the ancient power of sacred Hebrew names, specifically the Tetragrammaton, seems to have vanished. Capnion (Reuchlin) explains that words and symbols have no inherent natural power but act as vessels for a "ray of divine omnipotence," citing biblical examples like the bronze serpent.</summary>

# BOOK THREE

...into a curse and an example among all his people; may He make your thigh rot, and may your swelling womb burst. May these cursed waters enter into your belly, and as your womb swells, may your thigh rot. And the woman shall answer, Amen, amen. Then the priest shall write these curses in a little book and shall wash them out with the most bitter waters into which he has gathered the curses, and he shall give them to her to drink. When she has drained them, the priest shall take from her hand the sacrifice of jealousy <term>sacrifice of jealousy</term> <note>In Hebrew, *Minchat Kena’ot*; an offering described in Numbers 5 brought by a husband who suspects his wife of adultery.</note>, and he shall lift it up before the Lord and place it upon the altar; and so he shall give the woman the bitter waters to drink. When she has drunk them, if she is defiled and guilty of adultery by having treated her husband with contempt, the waters of the curse shall pass through her, and as her belly inflates, her thigh shall rot; and the woman shall be a curse and an example to all the people. But if she has not been defiled, she shall be unharmed and shall bear children. And let us hear the divine teaching for a third time:

> Thus shall you bless the children of Israel, and you shall say to them: The Lord bless you and keep you. The Lord show His face to you and have mercy on you. The Lord turn His countenance toward you and give you peace. And they shall put my name upon the children of Israel, and I will bless them. <note>This is the "Priestly Blessing" or *Birkat Kohanim* from Numbers 6:24–27, central to Jewish and Christian liturgy.</note>

By such force of words, by that power of figures, by the virtue of such compositions, and by these songs and arranged words of either curses or blessings, the thing itself is fashioned with marvelous artifice—not by us, but through us. For if it is necessary for a divine virtue to be enclosed within these names, and for an infallible divinity to hasten to our aid, and for a power that both surpasses and penetrates all laws of nature to lie hidden within, then it will be necessary not to remain in ignorance. First, we must know which name is superior to the others—indeed, which is the only one remaining out of all of them by whose secret energy and hidden assistance this whole business of wonder is accomplished. Next, we must know how it is recognized that it is that very name and no other. Finally, we must know by what path we should approach that which we desire. This is indeed a great matter, and I know not if it be the greatest; yet it is easy enough, by proceeding step by step with a prudent and mature investigation, to thin out the cause of names like carding wool, until you grasp that which lies hidden and immediately prove it to be that which you desire. For from the vast heap of sacred terms, you have received some that are ineffable <term>ineffable</term> <note>From the Latin *ineffabilia*, referring to names of God like the Tetragrammaton that are considered too holy to be spoken aloud.</note> and some that are utterable <term>utterable</term> <note>From Reuchlin's Latin *sabilia*, meaning names that can be safely pronounced or known.</note>—a hard thing, if not impossible. Yet it is difficult for a voice to be formed for us out of the ineffable that we might use obediently, or which might be ready and at hand for us in any situation, in any state of mind, or in whatever dangers. Therefore, it remains for us, led by necessity, to turn with sharp judgment to the investigation of the named voices, provided that we first investigate as briefly as possible, with somewhat deeper study, the cause of such vast power and such great virtue in names. For this reason, it seems right to act more openly now. For just as the mercy of the heaven, the rays of the stars, and the eternal fires of the constellations are believed to effect no small change in sublunary things <note>The "sublunary" world refers to the Earth and everything below the Moon, which ancient and medieval science believed was governed by the influence of the stars.</note> by their downward flow:

> Hence we can predict storms in the doubtful sky,
> Hence the day of harvest and the time for sowing,
> And when it is fit to strike the treacherous marble sea with oars,
> When to launch the armed fleets,
> Or when to fell the pine tree in the woods at its proper time.
> Not in vain do we watch the settings and the risings of the signs,
> And the year divided equally into four diverse seasons.
> <note>original: "Hinc tempestates... quattuor annum"; Reuchlin is quoting Virgil’s *Georgics*, Book 1, lines 252–258. He uses classical poetry to argue that if the stars have "influence" over nature, sacred names have "influence" over the spiritual world.</note>

This is the work of nature: this influence of rays, this descent of light, motion, and heat, penetrating all things with a subtle vitality. Hence...

<meta>This page continues the discourse by the character Capnion (representing Reuchlin himself), who is arguing that just as nature governs the physical world through observable qualities, God governs the spiritual and material worlds through the power of His Word and His Names. He is transitioning from the "ineffable" (unpronounceable) name of the Old Testament to the "enunciable" name of the New Covenant.</meta>

As the same Mantuan poet <note>The Roman poet Virgil, born near Mantua.</note> says:

> The appearances of their spirits are changed, and their breasts now conceive some emotions, and then others, as the wind drives the clouds; hence that chorus of birds in the fields, the joyful cattle, and the exulting throats of the crows. <note>original: "Vertuntur species animorum & pectora motus Nunc alios, alios dum nubila uentus agebat Concipiunt, hinc ille auium concentus in agris, Et letæ pecudes & ouantes gutture corui." — Virgil, *Georgics* I, 417-423.</note>

To summarize in a few words: just as the nature of the universe acts naturally upon individual things according to its prescribed order, so too does the pre-eminent mistress of nature and the cause of all things—the Divine Will—act supernaturally upon each and every thing through its limitless grace. And just as nature acts evidently through the medium of sensible qualities, so the Divine Will acts at times through the medium of intellectual names <note>Reuchlin refers here to the "Names of God" used in Kabbalistic and magical traditions as tools of divine power.</note> in a way that is binding, yet without proportion, without similarity, and without any measurable relationship between the cause and the things caused. God taught us this through a heavenly revelation in the book of Isaiah:

> For as the rain and the snow come down from heaven, and do not return there, but soak the earth and permeate it, making it sprout and provide seed for the sower and bread for the eater, so shall my word be, which goes out from my mouth: it shall not return to me empty, but it shall do whatever I have willed, and it shall prosper in those things for which I sent it. <note>Isaiah 55:10-11.</note>

Since, therefore, it is clear to us from the higher matters (as I believe), that the Word of God—which by another name is called *Reason*, since both are expressed by the Greeks with the single word *logos* <note>original: "λόγος"</note>—has appeared as that by which God made all things and works all things in heaven, on earth, and in all the depths. Regarding this, I pray you, hear even the mathematician Marcus Manilius in his *Astronomica* addressed to Augustus, who says in his poem:

> This work, constructed from the vast body of the world, and the members of nature established in diverse forms—of air, fire, earth, and the level sea—is ruled by a divine vital force; God breathes through the sacred passage and governs with silent reason, dispensing many covenants throughout every part. <note>original: "Hoc opus immenſi conſtructum corpore mundi... Vis animæ diuina regit, ſacroq[ue] meatu Conſpirat deus & tacita ratione gubernat..." — Manilius, *Astronomica* I.247-254.</note>

And since that Reason and that same Word has put on human flesh, so that we might better attain our advantage through its saving touch—when we might see with these eyes and embrace with every sense that very thing which could no longer be named by an ineffable circumscription of letters as before, but by an enunciable voice and a comprehensible sound—it was fitting that every individual pact of God made with our ancestors, every alliance based on the ineffable Tetragrammaton <term>Tetragrammaton</term> <note>The four-letter Hebrew name of God (YHWH), considered too sacred to pronounce in Jewish tradition.</note> name, and finally every promise should cease and be transferred afterward into the enunciable name of the visible Word itself. It is through this name that He dispenses His covenants, just as you heard a moment ago from the poems. Not only was it fitting for it to happen this way, but God willed it so, and predicted it long ago, which I shall affirm from the oracles of the prophets if you will listen with an even mind:

> Behold, the days are coming, says the Lord, and I will strike a new covenant with the house of Israel and the house of Judah; not according to the pact that I made with your fathers on the day that I took their hand to lead them out of the land of Egypt, a pact which they themselves made void. <note>Jeremiah 31:31-32.</note>

These things are read thus in Jeremiah. From this we clearly understand that the pacts entered into with God in the memory of our fathers became empty and void in later years, being transferred into another covenant. Now, out of all the laws, edicts, and prohibitions numbering six hundred and thirteen <note>A reference to the *613 Mitzvot* or commandments in the Torah.</note>, the chief pact regarding the ineffable name is this one which God entered into with the sons and daughters of Israel, this promise, this covenant:

> I am, He said, the ineffable Tetragrammaton, your God, a strong and jealous God. <note>A variation of the opening of the Ten Commandments from Exodus 20:2-5.</note>

And it follows: You shall make an altar of earth for me, and you shall offer...

<summary>Capnion argues that God's "silent reason" (Logos) has transitioned from the unpronounceable Old Testament name (Tetragrammaton) to a pronounceable, "enunciable" name through the Incarnation, fulfilling the prophecy of a New Covenant.</summary>

He is using Old Testament prophecies to demonstrate that God intended to replace the specific covenant associated with the four-letter name (the Tetragrammaton) with a new name through the Messiah.</meta>

### BOOK THREE

...through it your burnt offerings and peace offerings, your sheep and your oxen, in every place where there shall be a memory of my name, I will come to you and I will bless you. These things are promised in the twentieth chapter of Exodus. <note>Exodus 20:24. This refers to the original promise tied to the sanctuary and the Divine Name.</note> But listen again in Isaiah to how this covenant was broken: 

"Thus says the Lord: Heaven is my throne, and the earth is my footstool; what is this house that you will build for me, and what is this place of my rest? My hand made all these things, and all these things were made, says the Lord. But to whom shall I look, if not to the poor and contrite in spirit and him who trembles at my words? He who slays an ox is as if he killed a man; he who sacrifices a lamb as if he broke a dog’s neck; he who offers an oblation as if he offered swine’s blood; he who remembers incense as if he blessed an idol." <note>Isaiah 66:1–3. Reuchlin uses this to show that physical sacrifices and the old temple-based worship had lost their divine favor.</note>

Concerning the ineffable <note>The "ineffable" name refers to the Tetragrammaton (YHWH), which Jewish tradition forbids pronouncing.</note> name, the Lord says through Jeremiah: "Behold, I have sworn by my great name, says the Lord, that my name shall no longer be called by the mouth of any man of Judah saying, 'The Lord lives,' in all the land of Egypt." <note>Jeremiah 44:26.</note> Since, therefore, we have shown that the covenant which God had with his sons and daughters of the people of Israel regarding the Tetragrammaton name has now been reduced to nothing by God's own judgment, it will now be necessary to explain that the name was changed into something else—something which possesses either equal or greater power than the ineffable name. 

On this point, let us again consider the prophecy of Isaiah, where the Lord says to the Eunuchs: "Those who keep my sabbaths, and choose the things that please me and hold fast my covenant, I will give them in my house and within my walls a place and a name better than sons and daughters; I will give them an everlasting name that shall not perish." <note>Isaiah 56:4–5.</note> It is as if he were saying: "To my sons and daughters I gave a name that has ceased, but now to the Eunuchs who castrate themselves for the sake of the kingdom of heaven <note>A reference to Matthew 19:12.</note>, I will give a better and more permanent name that shall never cease." 

Regarding the fact that the translator rightly rendered "place" in this prophecy, know that in Hebrew a great mystery is contained, which has been revealed to us Christians. 

<margin>Yad <note>original: "Iad"</note>, that is, hand.</margin>

For there, the Hebrew word *Yad* <note>original: "Iad"</note> is placed, which signifies "hand." From this we judge, not without reason, that the Son of God—who is the "Hand of the Father"—is promised to chaste men, and that his name is also to be given. Concerning this transfer of the name, the Lord speaks again through Isaiah to the Jews: "And you shall leave your name as an oath to my chosen, and the Lord God will slay you, and will call his servants by another name." <note>Isaiah 65:15.</note> In which he who is blessed on earth shall be blessed in the God "Amen"—that is, in the God who is not only the "Hand" as mentioned above, but also the Truth, the incarnate Word of God who said, "I am the way, the truth, and the life." <note>John 14:6.</note> 

Do you see how the prophecy we brought forward at the beginning fits here: "A child is born to us, and a son is given to us, and the dominion was upon his shoulder, and his name shall be called *Pele*," <note>Isaiah 9:6.</note> that is, "Wonderful." 

<margin>Pele, that is, Wonderful <note>original: "Mirificum"</note>.</margin>

This is the name we seek. This is that wonderful name of the wonderful Word which we await, which is God before eternity and above eternity, and was made man. A thing truly admirable: God and man, one person, two natures; or what is greater, in one small body, the Lord of nature and nature itself. He was born, but he also existed before he was begotten. From the female sex, which is human, but also from an incorrupt virgin, which is divine. From one side without a father, from the other without a mother—both are of divinity. Carried in the womb, but...

<meta>catchword: carried</meta>

<summary>In this passage, Reuchlin argues that the Old Testament Tetragrammaton has been superseded by a new "everlasting name" through Christ. He uses linguistic analysis of Hebrew terms like "Yad" (hand) and "Pele" (wonderful) to identify the Messiah as the fulfillment of Isaiah's prophecies.</summary>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

...as the same Mantuan poet says, "The appearances of their minds are changed, and their breasts conceive now some movements, now others, as when the wind drove the clouds; hence comes that chorus of birds in the fields, and the joyful cattle, and the crows exulting in their throats." <note>original: "Vertuntur species animorum..." Reuchlin is quoting Virgil’s *Georgics* (1.415-423) to show how nature responds to unseen shifts in the atmosphere, just as the human soul responds to divine shifts.</note> And to summarize in a few words: just as the nature of the universe acts upon each thing according to its prescribed order naturally, so the preeminent mistress of nature and cause of all things—the divine will—acts upon each and every thing through its unlimited grace supernaturally. And just as nature acts through the medium of sensible qualities evidently, so the divine will acts sometimes through the medium of intellectual names in a binding way—without proportion, without similarity, and without a measuring of the cause to the effects. God taught us this by a heavenly revelation through Isaiah. "As the rain and snow," he said, "descend from heaven and do not return there, but soak the earth and saturate it and make it germinate, and give seed to the sower and bread to the eater, so shall my word be which goes out from my mouth; it shall not return to me empty, but shall do whatever I have willed and shall prosper in those things for which I sent it." <note>Isaiah 55:10-11.</note> 

Since, therefore, from the higher matters, as I believe, the word of God (which by another name is called "reason," since both are expressed by the Greeks with the single word <term>logos</term> <note>original: "λόγος". In Greek, *logos* means both 'word' and 'reason/logic'. Reuchlin uses this to link the Greek philosophical concept of universal reason with the Christian Word of God.</note>) has clearly appeared to us as that through which God makes all things and works all things in heaven, on earth, and in all the depths. Regarding this, I pray you, hear even the mathematician Marcus Manilius in his *Astronomica* addressed to Augustus, who says in his poem: "This work of the immense world constructed in its body, and the members of nature established in various forms—of air and fire, of earth and the lying sea—a divine power of the soul rules; and God breathes through a sacred passage and governs by silent reason, and dispenses many covenants throughout all parts." 

And since that reason and the same Word was clothed in human flesh, so that we might more easily obtain our benefit through its saving contact, when we might see with these eyes and embrace with our individual senses that very thing which we could no longer name in an ineffable way as before through a circumscription of letters, but now by a pronounceable voice and a comprehensible sound, it was fitting that the individual pacts of God with our ancestors—every alliance based on the ineffable and <term>Tetragrammaton</term> <note>The four-letter Hebrew name of God (YHWH), which was considered too holy to pronounce.</note> name, and finally every religious duty—should cease and be transferred afterward into the pronounceable name of the visible Word itself, through which He dispenses covenants, just as you heard a little earlier from the poems. 

And not only was it fitting to happen thus, but God willed it so, and predicted it so long ago, which I will affirm from the oracles of the prophets if you will bear it with an even mind. "Behold, the days are coming, says the Lord, and I will make a new covenant with the house of Israel and the house of Judah; not according to the pact I made with your fathers on the day I strengthened their hand to lead them out of the land of Egypt, which they themselves made void." <note>Jeremiah 31:31-32.</note> These things are read thus in Jeremiah. From this, we clearly understand that the pacts entered into with God in the memory of the fathers become empty and void in later years, and are transferred into another pact. Now, out of all the laws, edicts, and prohibitions numbering six hundred and thirteen <note>The 613 *mitzvot* or commandments found in the Torah.</note>, the chief pact is this one concerning the ineffable name which God entered into with the sons and daughters of Israel, this promise, this covenant. "I," He says, "am the ineffable <term>Tetragrammaton</term>, your strong and jealous God." And it follows: "You shall make an altar of earth for me and offer..."

<summary>Reuchlin argues that the unpronounceable Hebrew name of God (the Tetragrammaton) was a temporary covenant, now replaced by the "pronounceable" Word (Logos) made flesh. He uses Virgil, Manilius, and the prophet Jeremiah to show that nature and scripture both point toward this transition from silent reason to a vocal, human savior.</summary>
<keywords>Logos, Tetragrammaton, Virgil, Manilius, Jeremiah, New Covenant, 613 commandments, Divine Will, Nature vs Supernatural, Word of God</keywords>
