### BOOK THREE

->i iiii<-

...through it, your burnt offerings and your peace offerings, your sheep and your oxen; in every place where there shall be a memory of my name, I will come to you and I will bless you. These things are promised in the twentieth chapter of Exodus. <note>Exodus 20:24.</note> But listen again in Isaiah to how this covenant was broken: "Thus says the Lord: Heaven is my throne, and the earth is my footstool; what is this house that you will build for me, and what is this place of my rest? All these things my hand has made, and all these things have come to be, says the Lord. But to whom shall I look, if not to the poor and contrite in spirit, and to him who trembles at my words?" <note>Isaiah 66:1-2.</note> He who sacrifices an ox is like one who kills a man; he who slaughters a sheep is like one who breaks a dog’s neck; he who offers an oblation is like one who offers swine’s blood; he who burns incense is like one who blesses an idol. <note>Isaiah 66:3.</note> Concerning the ineffable name, indeed, through Jeremiah: "Behold, I have sworn by my great name, says the Lord, that my name shall no longer be called out from the mouth of any man of Judah saying, 'The Lord lives,' in all the land of Egypt." <note>Jeremiah 44:26. Reuchlin argues that the privilege of the Tetragrammaton was revoked due to the people's unfaithfulness.</note> Therefore, since we have shown that the covenant which God held with his sons and daughters from the people of Israel regarding the <term>Tetragrammaton</term> <note>The sacred four-letter name of God, YHWH, which was considered too holy to pronounce.</note> name has been reduced to nothing by God's own judgment, it will now be necessary to explain that this name has been changed into something else, which possesses either equal or greater power than the ineffable name. On this point, let us again consider the oracle of Isaiah, for thus says the Lord to the eunuchs: "Those who have kept my sabbaths, and have chosen the things that I willed and have held to my covenant, I will give to them in my house and within my walls a place and a name better than sons and daughters; I will give them an everlasting name that shall not perish." <note>Isaiah 56:4-5.</note> It is as if he were to say: "To my sons and daughters I gave a name that has ceased; but now to the eunuchs, who have made themselves so for the sake of the kingdom of heaven, I will give a better and more permanent name which shall never cease." <note>Reuchlin links Isaiah's "eunuchs" to the celibate or spiritually pure followers of Christ mentioned in Matthew 19:12.</note> However, regarding the fact that the translator rightly rendered the word "place" in this prophecy, you should know that a great mystery is contained in the Hebrew which has been revealed to us Christians. <margin>Jad, i.e., hand.</margin> For there, the Hebrew word *Jad* is placed, which also signifies a "hand." <note>The Hebrew word *Yad* (יָד) means both "place/monument" and "hand."</note> Whence we judge, not without reason, that to chaste men is promised the Son of God, who is the "hand" of the Father, and that his name is also to be given. Concerning this transfer of the name, the Lord speaks again to the Jews through Isaiah: "And you shall leave your name as a curse for my chosen ones, and the Lord God shall slay you, and he shall call his servants by another name." In which, he who is blessed upon the earth shall be blessed in God, Amen—that is, in the God who is not only the "hand," as stated above, but who is also the Truth: the incarnate Word of God who said, "I am the way, the truth, and the life." <note>Isaiah 65:15-16 and John 14:6. Reuchlin interprets the Hebrew "Amen" as a name for the God of Truth.</note> Do you not see how this leads to the oracle we brought forward at the beginning? "A child is born to us, and a son is given to us, and the government was upon his shoulder, and he shall call his name <margin>Pele, i.e., Mirific.</margin> *Pele*, that is, 'Mirific' or 'Wonderful.'" <note>Isaiah 9:6. *Pele* (פֶּלֶא) is Hebrew for "wonder" or "miracle."</note> This is that name which we seek. This is that wonderful name of the wonder-working Word which we await, who before eternity and above eternity is God and was made man. A thing first admirable: God and man, one person, two natures—or what is greater, the Lord of nature and nature itself in one small body. He was born, yet he was also begotten before time. From the female sex, which is human; but also from a virgin undefiled, which is divine. From one side without a father, from the other without a mother—both characteristics of divinity. Carried in the womb, but in the womb...

<meta>The catchword "pora" refers to the next page's starting word "porat", likely part of "portat" or "incorporatio".</meta>

<summary>Reuchlin argues that the Old Testament covenant involving the Tetragrammaton has been superseded by a "new name"—the Wonder-Working Word (Jesus)—as prophesied by Isaiah. He uses Hebrew philology, specifically the words 'Jad' (hand) and 'Pele' (wonderful), to bridge the gap between Jewish prophecy and the Christian Incarnation.</summary>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

<insert>
God?
Man made Christ <note>original: "Χρ̄ος" (Christos). Reuchlin uses the Greek abbreviation for Christ.</note>
</insert>

...known by the prophets, and by [John the Baptist] exulting before the Word by whom he was born. Wrapped in rags in a hovel, but also unwound from the linen and the shroud, he rose from death. Laid in a manger, but glorified by angels, pointed out by a star, adored by the magi. He fled into Egypt, but put to flight the idols of the Egyptians. He had neither form nor beauty among the Jews, but he was beautiful above the sons of men before David. <note>A contrast between the "Suffering Servant" of Isaiah 53 and the "Fair King" of Psalm 45.</note> On the mountain he shone transfigured like the sun, and revealed with what majesty he would come in judgment. Baptized as a man, forgiving sins as God; not needing to be cleansed by the waters, but so that he might cleanse the waters. Tempted as a man, but victor as God, and commanding us to be confident since he conquered the world. He hungered, but fed many thousands, and as the living bread he descended from heaven. He thirsted, but also cried out, "If anyone thirsts, let him come to me and drink," and he promised to everyone believing in him that rivers of living water would flow from his belly. He was weary, but promised rest to those who came to him in their weariness. He was heavy with sleep, but when a storm arose, waking up he commanded the winds and the sea. He paid the tax, but from a fish. Called a "Samaritan" in insult, but he healed the wounds of the man left by the priests and Levites. <note>Reuchlin interprets the Parable of the Good Samaritan as an allegory for Christ healing humanity where the Old Law failed.</note> Said to have a demon, but recognized by the Gadarene demoniac as the son of the most high God, from whom he cast out a legion of demons and drowned them in the sea. He is the one who saw Satan, the leader of demons, falling like lightning from heaven. Even though he was to be overwhelmed with stones, nevertheless he hid himself, passing through the midst of them. He prayed, but he also heard prayers. He wept, but he wiped away every tear. He asked where Lazarus was, for he was a man; yet he raised Lazarus, for he was God. Sold for a little, namely thirty silver pieces, but he redeemed the world with a great price. He was led like a sheep to the slaughter, but he rules—that is, he feeds—Israel and the whole world. A silent lamb, but also the Word and the voice of one crying in the desert. He is bruised and wounded, but he heals every infirmity and every sickness. Hanging on the wood <note>The cross.</note> he is killed, but by wood he restores life and saves the thief, and brings darkness upon death. He is given vinegar to drink, but he also changed water into wine. He gives up his spirit, but he also has the power to take it back. And the veil is torn, because the things that are above are revealed. Rocks are broken, the dead rise. He dies, but he gives life, and by death he unbinds death. He is buried, but he rises. He descends to the lower world, but brings back souls, and ascends into heaven, and is coming to judge the living and the dead.

Rightly, therefore, his name is called **wonder-working**, since he himself is most full of marvelous wonders. And know that the Lord has made this holy one of his—who is the Holy of Holies—wonder-working, so that he may hear us when we cry out to him. You have [now] what the wonder-working word is. But how is his name pronounced? And is it one of the many previously listed? Or do you ask if there will be another besides? We truly ought to imitate Holy Scripture, which teaches us about hidden and secret things. Therefore, if we embrace in our memory the earlier conversations of our dispute, the Word is found in the Father, and the "Hand in the breast," and the invisible God, and the ineffable name—like oil in a lentil. <note>Reuchlin uses the "oil in a lentil" metaphor to describe the divine name hidden within the Old Testament, which only becomes "extracted" and useful through the Incarnation.</note> But after it was incarnated, the Word went out from the Father, bowed the heavens and descen[ded]—

<summary>Reuchlin presents a poetic series of antitheses contrasting Christ's human suffering with his divine power, concluding that his name is truly "wonder-working." He begins to explain how this secret name, once hidden in the Father, was revealed through the Incarnation.</summary>
<keywords>Christology, Wonder-working Word, Incarnation, Miracles, Paradoxes of Christ, Ineffable Name, Reuchlin</keywords>

### BOOK THREE

...and the Lord sent forth his hand from on high, and God was seen on earth and lived among men. And a new name was given to him by an articulate voice, which the mouth of the Lord named, and his name is as "oil poured out." <note>A reference to Song of Solomon 1:3.</note> The vial was opened in the shape of a lentil, with a smooth circumference, and the "horn of the son of oil"; a vineyard was made for the beloved, in which the son himself is the true vine, and his name is "oil poured out"; therefore God anointed him with the oil of gladness above all his participants and all his companions.

<margin>Messiah, Christ</margin>
By what oracle, in what inner shrines, and by what divine voice do we conclude that "Messiah"—which is translated from "anointing" as "Christ"—is that name, or perhaps something else that signifies Christ the Son of God with a more intimate and peculiar sound? For "Christ" is not a proper name but a common word and a title of power and kingship. Individual kings and all priests, whoever were anointed, are "christs." <margin>I anoint, Anointed <note>original: χρίω χριστὸς</note></margin> For <term>chrio <note>original: χρίω</note></term> is translated as "I anoint" and <term>christos <note>original: χριστὸς</note></term> as "anointed." Therefore, we must accept a more proper name for him, in which we may easily obtain everything we desire. 

We are commanded to search for this again in the sacred characters, not without careful diligence, at the urging of the very one whose name is being discussed: "Search the scriptures," he says, "they are what bear witness of me." <note>John 5:39</note> What do the holy books contain regarding our cause? I beg you, excellent men, listen without weariness and lift up your spirits. For now at last we have approached nearly to the very doors of finding the name. 

The history of the most holy Gospel <note>original: Euãgelium</note> is this: In the sixth month, the angel Gabriel was sent by God to a city of Galilee named Nazareth, to a virgin betrothed to a man named Joseph, of the house of David, and the virgin’s name was Mary. And having entered, the angel said to her: "Hail, full of grace, the Lord is with you, blessed are you among women." When she heard this, she was troubled by his speech and pondered what sort of greeting this might be. And the angel said to her: "Do not fear, Mary, for you have found favor with God. Behold, you will conceive in your womb and bear a son, and you shall call his name **Ihsuh**; <note>Reuchlin uses this specific five-letter spelling—adding the Hebrew letter 'Shin' into the four-letter name of God—to argue that the 'Ineffable Name' (YHWH) has become pronounceable and human in Jesus.</note> he will be great, and will be called the Son of the Most High." 

Truly, I will not recount the whole series of events, but will begin again from where our business pertains. And after eight days were completed so that the child might be circumcised, his name was called **Ihsuh**, which was called by the angel before he was conceived in the womb. O happy hour, O auspicious moment of this light, most distinguished men and most beloved companions of studies, in which we have found such an excellent, so powerful, so victorious a name! At its nod and jurisdiction, not only is the vast circuit of the earth governed, but even this entire machine of the world and that famous celestial region, both the heavenly powers and the spirits of the dead, are ruled. 

He of whom Moses wrote in the law and the other prophets, we have found: **Jesus** <note>original: "ihsũ"</note>, often desired, long sought, diligently investigated, with the most pleasant fruit of delight and with an infallible bond of charity between us, which I believe will endure for all time. This is the highest and most surpassing name, a religious name, holy and to be honored, the name to which all sacred names are to be referred, which is above every name that is named in heaven and on earth, even in the age to come. It is a miraculous and wonderful name, a name pronounceable by the sound of the voice, no longer "ineffable," <note>The Tetragrammaton (YHWH) was considered too holy to be spoken aloud; Reuchlin argues that the name of Jesus breaks this silence.</note> not a <term>Tetragrammaton <note>The "four-letter" name of God</note></term> but a <term>Pentagrammaton <note>The "five-letter" name, which Reuchlin identifies as the name of Jesus/Ihsuh</note></term>. Nor is there any power in heaven or on earth which [resists] the name **Ihsuh**...

<meta>catchword: resists</meta>

<summary>In this passage, Reuchlin explains that while "Messiah" and "Christ" are general titles for any anointed ruler or priest, the specific name "Ihsuh" (Jesus) is the unique, five-letter "Pentagrammaton" that fulfills and makes pronounceable the hidden name of God.</summary>

...known by the prophet <note>Referring to John the Baptist recognizing Jesus from the womb.</note> even while he was being conceived, and leaping for joy before the Word by whom he was being born. Wrapped in swaddling clothes in a hut, but also unwrapped from the linen shroud and the face-cloth when he rose from death. Tempted as a man, but victor as God, and commanding us to have confidence because he has conquered the world.

Rightly, therefore, was his name called "Wonder-working" <note>original: mirificum</note>, since he himself is most full of wonderful wonders. And know that the Lord has made this holy one of his—who is the Holy of Holies—"wonder-working," <note>original: mirificavit</note> so that he may hear us when we cry out to him. You now have the sense of what the "wonder-working word" is. 

But how is his name pronounced? And is it one of the many previously listed? Or do you ask if there is another besides those? Truly, it behooves us to imitate the Holy Scripture, which teaches us concerning hidden and secret things. Therefore, if we hold in our memory the earlier conversations of our dispute, the "Word" is found in the Father, and the "Hand" in the bosom, and the "Invisible God," and the "Ineffable Name," just like oil in a lentil. <note>A metaphor for something tiny or enclosed containing great value or essence.</note> But after he was incarnated, the Word went out from the Father, bowed the heavens and descended, and...

<meta>This page continues the speaker Capnion’s (Reuchlin’s) argument. Having established the "paradoxes" of Christ's nature, he now moves to the core of his linguistic mystery: why the name "Christ" is a mere title and why the specific name "Jesus" (spelled here as the five-letter Pentagrammaton) is the true "Wonder-Working Word."</meta>

# BOOK THREE

...and the Lord sent forth his hand from on high, and God was seen on earth, and dwelled among men. And a new name was called upon him by an articulate voice, which the mouth of the Lord named, and his name is as "oil poured out." <note>A reference to Song of Solomon 1:3, traditionally interpreted as the spreading of Christ's fame.</note> The vessel of lentil-shape was opened, with its smooth circumference, and the horn of the "son of oil" <note>An idiom for "the anointed one."</note>; it was made a vineyard for the beloved, in which the son himself is the true vine, and "oil poured out" is his name; therefore, above all his partners and above all his companions, God anointed him with the oil of gladness.

<margin>
Messiah:
Christ
</margin>
By what oracle, in what inner shrines, by what divine voice do we conclude that "Messiah" (which is interpreted from "anointment" as "Christ") is that name, or perhaps something else which might signify Christ the Son of God with a more immediate sound and more specifically? For "Christ" is not a proper name but a common noun and a title of power and kingship. 

<margin>
<note>original: χρίω</note> to anoint:
<note>original: χριστός</note> Christ
</margin>
Individual kings, all priests, whoever was anointed, are "Christs." For "chrio" <note>original: χρίω, the Greek verb</note> is interpreted as "I anoint" and "Christos" <note>original: χριστός</note> as "anointed." Therefore, we must accept a name more proper to him, in which we can easily obtain all that we desire. Once again, we are commanded to search through the sacred characters <note>The Hebrew letters of the scriptures</note>, not without careful diligence, at the urging of this very person whose name is being discussed: "Search the scriptures," he says, "for they are what bear witness of me." <note>John 5:39</note> What do the holy books contain regarding our case? I beg you, best of men, listen without weariness; lift up your minds. For now we have finally come close, and we stand almost at the very doors of finding the name. 

The history of the Most Holy Gospel is this: In the sixth month, the angel Gabriel was sent from God to a city of Galilee named Nazareth, to a virgin betrothed to a man named Joseph, of the house of David, and the virgin's name was Mary. And the angel, having entered to her, said, "Hail, full of grace, the Lord is with you, blessed are you among women." When she heard this, she was troubled by his word, and wondered what kind of salutation this might be. And the angel said to her, "Do not fear, Mary, for you have found favor with God. Behold, you shall conceive in your womb and bear a son, and you shall call his name ihsuh. <note>The author uses this specific spelling—IHSVH—to show how the silent name of God (YHWH) becomes pronounceable by adding the letter 'S' (Shin).</note> He shall be great, and shall be called the Son of the Most High." 

Truly, I will not recount the entire series of events, but I will begin from the point that concerns our business. And after eight days were completed so that the boy might be circumcised, his name was called ihsuh, which was called by the angel before he was conceived in the womb. O happy hour! O auspicious moment of this light, most illustrious men and most loving companions of study, in which we have found so excellent, so powerful, so victorious a name! At its nod and command not only the vast circuit of the earth, but also this entire machine of the world and that famous super-mundane region, the heights and the depths, are governed. He whom Moses wrote of in the Law and the other prophets, we have found: ihsum <note>The grammatical object form of the name ihsuh.</note>, often desired, long sought, diligently investigated—found with the most delightful fruit of pleasure and with an infallible bond of charity among us, which I believe will endure for all time. 

It is the supreme and most surpassing name, a religious, holy, and honorable name, the name to which all sacred names are to be referred, which is above every name that is named in heaven and on earth, and even in the age to come. A miraculous and wonderful name, a name pronounceable by the sound of the voice, no longer "ineffable" <note>Meaning "unpronounceable," a common term for the four-letter Hebrew name of God, YHWH.</note>, no longer the Tetragrammaton <note>The "Four-Letter" name</note> but the Pentagrammaton <note>The "Five-Letter" name</note>. Nor is there any power in heaven or on earth that can resist the name ihsuh.

<summary>Capnion explains that "Christ" is merely a title, revealing instead the specific, pronounceable name of the Messiah: the "Pentagrammaton" (IHSVH). He frames this discovery as the culmination of scriptural searching, linking the Gospel of Luke's Annunciation to the mystical transformation of God's hidden name into a powerful, vocalized Word.</summary>
<keywords>Messiah, Christ, Pentagrammaton, Tetragrammaton, Gabriel, Nazareth, ihsuh, YHWH, naming of Jesus, sacred linguistics</keywords>

--- Page 153 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse of Capnion (Reuchlin's persona) in the third book of De Verbo Mirifico. In the previous section, he argued that while "Christ" is a general title, a more specific and potent name is required to capture the essence of the God-Man.</meta>

...dares to stand. Nor is there any other name that stands more holy or more religious, whose letters are God, whose syllables are spirit, whose utterance is God and man. Why do you stand there astonished? Why do you look at me with fixed eyes? Why do you hold your mouths intent? If you wonder at this, I will add this: what was previously for so long a time—indeed, for so many centuries—ineffable <note>In Hebrew tradition, the four-letter name of God (the Tetragrammaton) was considered too holy to pronounce.</note>, has now been made effable <note>Pronounceable</note> by the joining of a single letter, not a vowel but a consonant. And if you still wonder, I will add further: when the Word descended into the flesh, then the letters passed into voice. See how openly and faithfully I reveal the whole secret to you. The grouping of letters is called a syllable; the union of syllables is called an utterance. You lead me back at last to the elements of the grammarians, which, though small to the sight, are nevertheless of the greatest power. According to Plato, the business of letters is of no small weight; he himself proves their riches—or rather, their delights—with the greatest industry, sharp study, and sagacious wit in the book titled *Cratylus*. <note>A dialogue by Plato exploring whether names have a natural connection to the things they name.</note>

<margin>Vowels?</margin>
But let us return: truly, just as in this most excellent five-letter name <note>The "Pentagrammaton" (IHSVH/Jesus), which Reuchlin constructs by adding the letter 'S' to the center of the four-letter name of God (IHVH).</note> some parts are vowels and one part is a consonant, so also there are two syllables in the whole word. The first arises from pure vowels, the second from vowels combined with a consonant. I ask you to recall the Pentateuch <note>The first five books of the Bible, attributed to Moses.</note>, and remember that the best and greatest God wished to be signified by no likeness, no effigy, no image, but by voice alone. Therefore, Moses preaches to the people in this manner: "The Lord spoke to you," he says, "from the midst of the fire; you heard the voice of his words, and you saw no form at all." And then he adds: "That you might know that the Lord himself is God and there is no other besides him... from heaven he made you hear his voice so that he might teach you, and on earth he showed you his great fire." <note>Deuteronomy 4:12, 35-36.</note> These are the words of Moses. Continue listening. Voice is spirit. For they define voice as "struck air"; thus was Nicodemus instructed in the Gospel of John: "The spirit breathes where it wills, and you hear its voice, but you do not know where it comes from or where it goes." <note>John 3:8.</note> God himself is also a spirit; thus was the Samaritan woman taught. <note>John 4:24.</note> Therefore, vowel letters are the most fitting signs of God, since they are named from "voice" <note>original: "vocales" from "vox" (voice).</note>. Now let this go, for divinity is designated by the vowels themselves.

<margin>Consonant?</margin>
Now let us pass to the consonant, which we consider more corporeal because, by itself and without a "vocal spirit," it has no sound in the mouth but only "sounds with" <note>original: "consonat," a pun on the Latin word for consonant.</note> others. That consonant is not a letter chosen by chance, nor snatched up imprudently, nor admitted through ignorance or lack of skill, nor contrary to expectation or hope. Rather, it was chosen from all characters by the providence of the highest God—which surveys all things and dispenses all things according to the eternal measure of his reason—from all the characters available; it is the letter which, among the Latins, is called "S." Moreover, if you remember, Baruchias taught us in yesterday's discourse that the word "Es" is considered divine, which is interpreted as "fire." <note>original: "Es," referring to the Hebrew word אש (Esh).</note>

<margin>Fire <note>original: אש (Esh)</note></margin>
This is that greatest fire of God which he showed to us on earth along with the fatherly voice, saying: "This is my beloved son." And he said: "I have come to send fire upon the earth, and what do I wish but that it be kindled?" <note>Luke 12:49.</note> So that you may understand more clearly and more brightly (for I will not deal with you through riddles and obscure windings of words, but openly and plainly), place these things in your deepest senses: the matter, in my judgment, will be no small thing. "Es," which signifies "fire" in Hebrew, is this body—not divine, not ethereal, not a phantom, not merely appearing to exist, as the Manichaeans <note>An ancient religious group that believed Christ did not have a real physical body, but only the appearance of one.</note>...

<summary>Reuchlin explains the linguistic composition of the name of Jesus, arguing that adding the consonant 'S' (representing fire and the physical body) to the vowels of God's unpronounceable name makes the divine "effable" or pronounceable.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Capnion’s (Reuchlin’s) argument from Book Three of On the Wonder-Working Word. Having established that the name of Jesus is a "five-letter" name (Pentagrammaton) created by adding a consonant to the four-letter name of God (Tetragrammaton), he now identifies that consonant as the Hebrew letter 'Shin' and explains its mystical and linguistic significance.</meta>

### ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD
<meta>catchword: has</meta>

...the Manichaeans, Eutyches, Dioscorus, and Timothy "the Cat" <note>Timothy Aelurus was a 5th-century Bishop of Alexandria. These figures were considered heretics for their varying views on the nature of Christ, often denying his physical, earthly reality.</note> raved in their dreams; rather, we are discussing that earthly fire which Moses names "fire on the earth," since we are examining it according to the Pythagorean Cabala <note>Reuchlin famously synthesized the Jewish Kabbalah with the mathematical mysticism of Pythagoras.</note>. O, what a pleasant thing to hear! Truly, we shall find that it consists of the same numerical measure as this name of ours, *ihsuh* <note>Reuchlin’s specific spelling of the name of Jesus (I-H-S-V-H), which inserts the letter Shin (S) into the middle of the Divine Name (I-H-V-H).</note>, of august majesty, with equal harmony and uniform proportion. 

Furthermore, you will perhaps say that this letter "S," standing in the middle to form the second syllable, ought not to be named in the manner of the Gentiles <note>The Latin and Greek speakers.</note>, but should be pronounced according to the custom of the Holy Tongue (for so they call the idiom of the Hebrew speech), since we are conducting our exercise upon the Holy Scriptures. O, how I would not hesitate to lead you more secretly into the inner chambers of the mysteries, if I were to answer with the speech of Paul of Tarsus when he says, *Let it be known therefore to you, that this salvation of God is sent to the Gentiles, and they will hear it.* <note>original: "Notum ergo fit uobis quoniam ethnicis & gentibus missum est hoc salutare dei, & ipsi audient." — Acts 28:28.</note> 

But I shall remain within the Holy Tongue, and thus I will explain the most sacred Hebrew name more easily and also more clearly, according to the manner and property of that same language. For Baruchias <note>The Jewish scholar in Reuchlin's dialogue.</note> has maintained in his disputation—so that he may boast of himself—that this speech is more suited to sacred things. Come now, if you wish to explore this very consonant with precision, you must first take care not to forget the vowels that enter into the composition of this name. For there are only four, I H V H, from which the unutterable Tetragrammaton <note>The sacred four-letter name of God (Yahweh) which was not pronounced in Jewish tradition.</note> consists, and which most appropriately demonstrate the divine nature, as was shown quite extensively and sometimes most memorably yesterday by this companion of ours. 

Since these letters cannot be spoken by the human voice or faculty, it was indeed fitting for the God who was to be incarnated to assume, besides those four, another character along with the flesh. By this, the unpronounceable sound and the then-invisible note of the ineffable God could now sometimes grate between human teeth and thus be called out "effably" <note>Pronounceably.</note>; so that what was previously un-callable might now be felt as callable. 

The Hebrews call this consonant *Shin* <note>The 21st letter of the Hebrew alphabet, which Reuchlin associates with fire and the Incarnation.</note>, which the priest Jerome interprets as "teeth," writing thus to Paula of the City <note>St. Paula, a close associate of Jerome in Rome.</note>: "The seventh connection, which is the last, so that there may be a mystical understanding in that sevenfold number itself: *Coph, Resh, Shin, Tav* <note>Hebrew letters: Qoph (K), Resh (R), Shin (S), and Tav (T).</note>; that is, the 'calling of the head' and 'signs of the teeth.'" Through the teeth, articulated speech is brought forth, and through these signs one arrives at the head of all things, who is Christ, through whom one also comes to the Kingdom of Heaven. 

I ask you, what is more sacred than this mystery? What is more pleasant than the delight of this mystery? What foods, what honeys are sweeter than to know the wisdom of God, to enter into His hidden places, to look upon the sense of the Creator, and to learn more fully with spiritual wisdom the words of the Lord your God, which are mocked by the wise of this world? These are not my words, but Jerome’s—a man of such great authority—lest I seem to some to want to devise a fiction. 

Since the teeth are the most powerful instruments of speaking, of which the letter *Shin* is said to be the most famous, is it any wonder if all the grammarians, more noble than the rest (to say nothing of the theologians themselves), agree with me that this element, and no other, was most conveniently summoned for the pronunciation of the ineffable name? I certainly do not know by what agreement the correct institution of that venerable, or rather adorable, name has been [established] by no one, not even by one or another of the learned men and masters of divine philosophy, in a probable way...

<summary>Reuchlin argues that the Hebrew letter 'Shin' (meaning 'teeth') was added to the four-letter name of God to make it pronounceable, symbolizing the Incarnation of Christ. He cites St. Jerome to prove that this letter allows the divine name to be articulated by human speech.</summary>

### ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

...would dare to stand. Nor is there any other name that remains more holy and more sacred, whose letters are God, whose syllables are spirit, and whose expression is both God and man. Why do you stand stunned in amazement? Why do you gaze at me with eyes fixed? Why do you hold your expressions so intently? If you wonder at this, let me add even this: that which previously for so much time—indeed, for so many centuries—was unutterable, has now been made utterable by the joining of one single letter—not a vowel, but a consonant. And if you still marvel, I will add this: when the Word descended into the flesh, then the letters passed into a voice. See how openly and how faithfully I reveal the whole secret to you. The conceiving of letters is called a syllable; the union of syllables is called a word. You lead me at last back to the basic elements of the grammarians, which, though small to the sight, are yet greatest in power. The business of letters is of no small weight if we believe Plato; he himself proves their riches—indeed, their delights—with the greatest industry, sharp study, and sagacious wit in the book titled *Cratylus* <note>Plato’s dialogue on the philosophy of language and whether names are natural or conventional.</note>. But let me return: truly, in this so excellent five-letter name, part are vowels and part is a consonant. Thus also the whole expression consists of two syllables, the first of which arises from pure vowels, and the second from vowels combined with a consonant. I ask you to remember the Pentateuch <note>The first five books of the Bible.</note>, and recall that the Best and Greatest God willed to be signified by no likeness, no effigy, no image, but by voice alone. For they define voice as "struck air"; thus Nicodemus is instructed in the Gospel of John: "The Spirit breathes where it wills, and you hear its voice, but you do not know from where it comes or where it goes" <note>John 3:8</note>. God himself is also spirit; so the Samaritan woman was taught <note>John 4:24</note>.

<margin>Vowels?</margin>
Therefore, vowels are the most fitting signs of God, since they take their name from the voice <note>In Reuchlin's linguistic mysticism, vowels represent the divine spirit, while consonants represent the physical body.</note>. Now leave this aside, for divinity is designated by the vowels themselves. Now let us pass to the

<margin>Consonant?</margin>
consonant, which we consider "more corporeal" <note>solid or physical</note> because by itself and without the vocal spirit it has no sound of the mouth, but only "sounds with" [con-sonat] another. That consonant letter was not chosen by chance, nor snatched up recklessly; it was not admitted out of ignorance or lack of skill, nor contrary to expectation or hope. Rather, it was selected from all characters by the providence of the highest God, who watches over all things and dispenses all things according to the eternal balance of His reason. Among the Latins, this letter is named "Es" <note>The letter S</note>. Moreover, if you remember, Baruchias <note>One of the characters in this dialogue.</note> taught us in yesterday's discourse that the word "Es" is considered divine, which is interpreted as "fire."

### ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

Instead, it is a terrestrial fire, which Moses names "fire on the earth," since we are discussing it according to the Pythagorean Kabbalah <note>Reuchlin often blended Pythagorean number theory with Jewish Kabbalistic mysticism.</note>. O, what a pleasant thing to hear! Truly we shall find that it consists of the same numerical measure as this name of our Jesus's august majesty, with equal harmony and balanced proportion. Furthermore, you will perhaps say that this letter "S" which stands in the middle and forms the second syllable is not rightly named according to the custom of the nations, but ought to be pronounced according to the custom of the Holy Tongue (for so they call the idiom of the Hebrew speech), since we are engaging in a contest concerning the Holy Scriptures. Oh, how I would not hesitate to introduce you more secretly into the inner sanctuaries of the mysteries, if I were responding with the speech of Paul of Tarsus, when he says:

> Be it known therefore unto you, that this salvation of God is sent unto the Gentiles, and they will hear it. <note>original: "Notum ergo sit uobis quoniam ethnicis & gentibus missum est hoc salutare dei, & ipst audient." Reference: Acts 28:28.</note>

But I will remain in the Holy Idiom, and thus I will explain the most sacred Hebrew name more easily and more clearly through the manner and property of that same language. For Baruchias maintained in his disputation—as he himself boasts—that this speech is more suited to sacred things. Come now, if you wish to explore that very consonant letter with precision, you must first take care not to forget the vowels that enter into the composition of this name. There are only four, IHVH, by which the ineffable Tetragrammaton <note>The "Four-Letter Name" of God: Yod, He, Vav, He.</note> consists, and which most appropriately point out the divine nature, as was shown quite extensively and sometimes most memorably by our companion yesterday. Since these cannot be spoken by human voice or faculty, it was indeed fitting for the God-to-be-incarnated to take on another character along with the flesh, so that the unpronounceable sound of the then-invisible God—that unutterable mark—could now at last hiss between human teeth and thus be spoken aloud, so that what was previously un-invocable might now be perceived as vocable. The Hebrews call this consonant *Sin* <note>The letter Shin/Sin (ש), which Reuchlin inserts into the middle of IHVH to create IHSVH.</note>, which Jerome the Priest interprets as "teeth," writing thus to Paula of the City:

> The seventh connection, which is the last, in which a mystical understanding is also made in that very number seven: *Coph, Res, Sin, Thaf*, that is, the calling of the head, the signs of the teeth. <note>original: "Septima connexio quæ est extrema... uocatio capitis dentium signa." St. Jerome’s explanation of the Hebrew alphabet.</note>

Through the teeth, the articulated voice is brought forth, and in these signs, one arrives at the Head of all things, which is Christ, through whom one also comes to the Kingdom of Heaven. I ask you, what is more holy than this mystery? What is more pleasant than the delight of this mystery? What foods, what honeys are sweeter than to know the wisdom of God, to enter into His hidden places, to look upon the mind of the Creator, and to learn more fully through spiritual wisdom the words of the Lord your God, which are mocked by the "wise" of this world? These are not my words, but the words of Jerome, a man of such great authority that I might not seem to some to want to invent a fiction. Therefore, since teeth are the most powerful instruments of speaking—from which the letter *Sin* is said to be the most famous—is it any wonder if all the grammarians more noble than the rest agree with me (to say nothing of the theologians themselves) that this element, and no other, was most conveniently summoned for the pronunciation of the Ineffable Name? I know not by what pact the correct institution of that venerable—or rather, adorable—name has been probably explained by no one, not even by one or two of the learned men and masters of divine philosophy...

<meta>This page continues the discourse of Capnion (Reuchlin’s persona) as he transitions from discussing the phonetic nature of the holy name to the mathematical and historical logic behind the timing of the Incarnation. He is bridging the gap between the "simple" spirit of the Tetragrammaton and the "material" multiplicity of the world.</meta>

### ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

...things have been observed up to this point, and by using their discoveries for this discourse of ours, we might be relieved of no small burden of meditation. Yet, there are certain suitable aids and supports for this work which are found hidden in their volumes; one should rely on these no less for the discovery of the cause than for the diligent confirmation of what has been discovered. An outstanding example of this exists, which we previously fished out of an epistle by Jerome. I perceive that your minds have conceived no small delight in discussing this name, *ihsuh* <note>original: "iħsuh." This is Reuchlin's specific Kabbalistic spelling of the name of Jesus (YHShVH), created by inserting the Hebrew letter *Shin* (S) into the middle of the unpronounceable Tetragrammaton (YH-VH).</note>; I will add something of joy so that you may be further delighted and listen to me with a more cheerful countenance. By mathematical reasoning, it has been discovered that the "body" must be sought in the composition of multiplicity, while the "spirit" is found in the simplicity of units. Thus, the closer any given thing approaches unity, the more spiritual it is and the more cleansed from the appendages of matter.

We possess that most simple divinity of the Word of God, which belongs to all spirits and is designated by unity itself. However, the sign for this in the Hebrew disciplines is said to contain those four vocal characters of the *Tetragrammaton* <term>Tetragrammaton: The four-letter Hebrew name of God (Y-H-V-H), traditionally considered too holy to pronounce.</term> name. It remains for us to seek the "body" of the Incarnate One and its character. Let us therefore look toward multiplicity and the time of the Incarnation, so that by the proportion of the sign to the thing signified, we may emerge more perceptive. First, at what age of the world did He who came into the world for its salvation assume a body? You will rightly answer me, with the Apostle in agreement: "In the last or final days" <note>original: "In ultimis siue nouissimis diebus." A reference to Hebrews 1:2.</note>. For we are those upon whom the ends of the ages have come.

Truly, regarding the ages of the world, different people have handed down various traditions. Ovid and the crowd of poets affirm that there are four ages of the world <note>The Gold, Silver, Bronze, and Iron ages.</note>; Hesiod says five, like the statue in Daniel <note>Referencing the vision in Daniel 2 of a statue made of five materials, symbolizing successive kingdoms.</note>; others say six, according to the *Hexaemeron* <term>Hexaemeron: The "six days" of creation described in Genesis.</term> by which God created all things. The rest say seven, which is also my opinion, but those who call the future world the "seventh age" do not divide them according to my judgment. For then God will create a new heaven and a new earth, and make all things new, as the sacred words suggest. Therefore, that will not be an age of *this* world, but a new world—not merely a Sabbath, which the Hebrews consider the seventh, but a "Sabbath of Sabbaths" and a duration of eternity, not of time.

Now, however, Christ—both the Word of God and God—came into the flesh in the seventh age of this unstable world. I call an "age" a certain state of the world between two notable and common changes, and its, so to speak, foreign commotions. Certainly, I judge the first to have been when the heavens and the earth were finished, along with all their adornment. And "God saw all that he had made, and they were very good, and there was evening and morning, the sixth day." <note>original: "Viditq̃ deus cuncta quę fecerat, & erant ualde bona, & factum eſt ueſpere & mane dies ſextus." Reference: Genesis 1:31.</note> Then followed a great change of the world after the seventh day, when God spoke to Adam: "Because you have listened to the voice of your wife, and have eaten of the tree of which I commanded you that you should not eat, cursed is the earth in your work." <note>original: "Quia audiſti uocem uxoris tuę... maledicta terra in opere tuo." Reference: Genesis 3:17.</note> From the creation of the world, therefore, until the transgression of Adam, the first age of the world is established by my meager judgment. From that until the flood of Noah is the second age. The third will be said to have been from Noah to Abraham and his circumcision, when the people faithful to God were divided from the idolaters by the river Euphrates. This is why Abraham was surnamed "Eber" <note>Eber is the biblical ancestor of the "Hebrews." Reuchlin is using a popular etymology linking the name to the Hebrew root *‘avar*, meaning "to cross over."</note>—not from the language which he had preserved intact from his father Heber, son of Shelah, but from the crossing of the Euphrates, as if he were "from across the river." From Abraham to Moses...

<summary>Reuchlin argues that the name of Jesus represents the "body" or material manifestation of the spiritual Tetragrammaton, occurring in the seventh and final age of the world. He begins outlining a chronology of these seven world ages, starting from Creation to the time of Moses.</summary>
<keywords>Tetragrammaton, Jesus, world ages, Incarnation, Reuchlin, Eber, Hebrew etymology, Kabbalah, numerology, chronology</keywords>

He is bridging the gap between the "simple" spirit of the Tetragrammaton and the "material" multiplicity of the world.</meta>

# BOOK THREE

...we shall count the fourth age by the written law and the sacred and manifold ceremonies. The fifth until the kingdom of the Hebrews, when, after the destruction of Troy, men began to reckon time by lustra <term>lustra: A five-year period used in Roman chronology for census and purification.</term>. The sixth until the founding of the city now called Rome, when individual matters were governed by the uncertain hand of a king, with no law spoken or enacted, except for those minor sanctions which Romulus thereafter brought to the people, which he called *Curiatae*. There exist volumes by Sextus Papirius <note>original: "Sexti Papyrii." Refers to Gaius Papirius, a legendary early Roman jurist said to have collected the laws of the kings (*Ius Papirianum*).</note> on these matters; read them more carefully if you can. That age began to number the years by Olympiads, just as the preceding one did by lustra, until the age of Christ, when the whole world was registered for the annual poll taxes and tributes of the Romans, in what we call the "indiction" <term>indiction: A fifteen-year cycle used in the Roman and Byzantine empires for tax assessments and dating.</term> down to our own times, containing three Olympiads and three lustra, which is now the seventh age. If you judge these things to be considered by me truly, fairly, and not lazily, I shall rejoice that no small fruit of my studies is present. But if, as I fear, it turns out much otherwise, I will at least show you some effort on my part, and I will seem at least to have wished to please. Nevertheless, I will play upon my own genius, and just as if the matter were so, I will place Christ at the end of days—that is, I will establish that he assumed a body around the seventh age of this world.

But the body, as we said, must be sought in multiplicity. Therefore, if after Unity—which signifies God, not a number—you were to fashion seven houses of numbers from all the numerical figures among the Hebrews, and enclose within each house a company of three arithmetic letters in order, it will clearly appear in the seventh house, and in the middle place of its connection, the mark *Sin* itself <note>original: "Sin." The Hebrew letter *Shin* (ש), which Reuchlin interprets as representing the "fire" of the Holy Spirit or the Word made flesh.</note>, designating the mediator of God and man, the God and man who made both into one. 

Moreover, the connection of the seventh house is specifically this: *Res*, *Sin*, *Thaf* <note>original: "Res, Sin, Thaf." These are the Hebrew letters *Resh*, *Shin*, and *Tav*, which spell *Reshit*—meaning "beginning" or "head."</note>, which in Latin is interpreted as "of the Head." The designation of *Sin* is as if the alphabet were predicting and foretelling by prophecy that the letter *Sin* itself would be the most fit character for figuring the name *ihsuh* <note>original: "ihsuh." Reuchlin’s Kabbalistic spelling of the name of Jesus (YHShVH).</note>, whom God the Father gave as the head over the whole church, which is his body, as we read written to the Ephesians. This is what Moses seems to me to have once clearly predicted in Deuteronomy, in the twenty-eighth chapter of that book, when he prophesies to the coming nation thus: "If, however, you listen to the voice of *I.H.V.H.* your God" <note>original: "I.H.V.H." The Tetragrammaton, the four-letter unpronounceable name of God.</note>—behold, four vowels! Then, after an abundance of blessing, it follows: "All the peoples of the earth shall see that the name of the Tetragrammaton has been called upon you." This "heap of speech" consisting of these three things—namely, the *Name*, the *Tetragrammaton*, and *Called*—when collected together, is understood by the frequent use of the letter *Sin* according to the Kabbalah <term>Kabbalah: A school of Jewish mysticism that Reuchlin adapted for Christian theology, focusing on the hidden meanings of the Hebrew alphabet.</term> of the Hebrews. Thus, if the last things correspond to the first, this prophecy contains nothing else but this: if you hear the voice of the Tetragrammaton—that is, when the Tetragrammaton becomes audible, which is to say, effable <term>effable: Capable of being spoken or pronounced.</term>—then the name of the Tetragrammaton called through *Sin* shall be upon you. It is just as if he said: "If it is necessary for the ineffable name of the Tetragrammaton to become effable, it will necessarily be called through the consonant which is named *Sin*, so that it may become *ihsuh*, who will be over you, your head and your lord." Blessed be the God and Father of our Lord *ihsuh* Christ, who from above instills in us the knowledge of the true name of his only-begotten Son and our Savior, or according to the Greeks, our Healer <note>original: "sanatoris." A play on the Greek *Iesous*, which Renaissance scholars sometimes associated with *iasis* (healing/remedy).</note>. This...

### ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

Up to this point, those things have been observed which, by using the discoveries of others, might relieve us of no small burden of meditation for this discourse of ours. Yet, there are certain suitable aids and supports for this work which are found hidden in their volumes; one ought to lean diligently upon these not only for the discovery of the cause, but for the confirmation of what has been discovered. An excellent example of this is what we recently fished out from a letter of Jerome <note>original: "Hieronymi epiſtola." Likely referring to Jerome’s discussions on the Hebrew names of God.</note>. I perceive that your minds have conceived no small delight in discussing this name **IHSVH** <note>original: "ihsuh." This is the "Pentagrammaton," a central concept for Reuchlin where the Hebrew letter 'Shin' (S) is inserted into the middle of the four-letter name of God (YHWH) to form the name of Jesus (YHSVH).</note>; I shall add some joy so that you may be more greatly delighted and listen to me with a more cheerful countenance.

By mathematical reasoning, it has been found that the "body" must be sought in a composition of multiplicity, while the "spirit" is sought in the simplicity of units; thus, the closer any thing approaches unity, the more spiritual it is and the more purged from the attachments of matter. We have that most simple divinity of all spirits in the Word of God, which is designated by Unity itself; yet its sign, according to the Hebrew teachings, is learned to contain those four vocal characters of the **Tetragrammaton** <term>Tetragrammaton: The "four-letter" unpronounceable name of God in Hebrew (YHWH).</term>. It remains for us to seek the body of the Incarnate One and his character. Therefore, let us look toward multiplicity and the time of the Incarnation, so that from the proportion of the sign to the thing signified, we may emerge more clear-sighted.

First, regarding Him who came into the world for the salvation of the world: in what age of the world did He assume a body? You will rightly answer me, in agreement with the Apostle: "In the final or last days." For we are those upon whom the ends of the ages have come. Concerning the ages of the world, however, various men have handed down various traditions. **Ovid** and the crowd of poets affirm there are four ages of the world <note>Ovid describes the Golden, Silver, Bronze, and Iron ages.</note>; **Hesiod** says five, like the statue in **Daniel**; others say six, according to the *Hexaemeron* <term>Hexaemeron: The "six days" of creation described in Genesis.</term> by which God created all things. The rest say seven, as is also my opinion; but those who call the future world the "seventh age" do not divide them according to my sentiment. For then God will create a new heaven and a new earth, and make all things new, as the sacred words suggest. Therefore, that will not be an age of *this* world, but a new world—not merely the Sabbath which the Hebrews consider the seventh, but the "Sabbath of Sabbaths" and the duration of eternity, not of time.

Now, however, Christ—both the Word of God and God—came into the flesh in the seventh age of this unstable world. I call an "age" a certain state of the world between two notable and common variations, and its, as it were, foreign commotions. Truly, I judge the first to have been when the heavens and the earth were perfected, and all the adornment of them. And God saw all the things he had made, and they were very good, and evening and morning became the sixth day. Then a huge change of the world followed after the seventh day, when God spoke to Adam:

> Because you listened to the voice of your wife, and ate
> from the tree of which I had commanded you that you should not eat, cursed is the earth in your work.

Therefore, from the creation of the world until the transgression of Adam, the first age of the world is established, according to my meager judgment. From that until the flood of **Noah** is the second age. The third shall be said to have been from Noah to **Abraham** and his circumcision, when the people faithful to God were divided from the idolaters by the river Euphrates. Thence Abraham was surnamed **Eber**, not from the language which he had preserved whole for his own (being the son of **Shelah**), but from the "crossing" of the Euphrates, as if "from across the river" <note>original: "transfluuialis." Reuchlin is playing on the etymology of "Hebrew" (Ivri) coming from the root "to cross over" (avar).</note>. From Abraham to Moses...

<summary>Capnion explains the mathematical relationship between the simple Spirit (Tetragrammaton) and the material Body (the Incarnation). He begins a chronological division of world history into seven ages, defining the first three: Creation to the Fall, the Fall to the Flood, and the Flood to Abraham.</summary>

# BOOK THREE

We shall count the **fourth age** from the written law and the sacred and manifold ceremonies. The **fifth** up to the kingdom of the Hebrews, when, after the destruction of Troy, men began to calculate times by *lustra* <note>original: "lustris." A Roman period of five years.</note>. The **sixth** up to the founding of the city now called Rome, when individual matters were governed by the uncertain hand of a king, with no law dictated and no law passed, except for those minor sanctions which Romulus thereafter brought to the people, which he called *Curiatae* <note>The "Leges Curiatae" were early Roman laws passed by the Comitia Curiata, the oldest assembly in Rome.</note>. Regarding these, the volumes of Sextus Papirius <note>A legendary figure in Roman law said to have collected the laws of the kings.</note> exist; read them more carefully if you are able. That age began to count years by **Olympiads** <note>original: "olympiadibus." Four-year Greek chronological units.</note>, just as the preceding age did by *lustra*, up to the age of Christ, when the whole world was enrolled for the annual taxes and tributes of the Romans. This we call the **Indiction** <term>Indiction: A fifteen-year cycle used in the Roman Empire to date documents and calculate taxes.</term> down to our own times, containing three Olympiads and three *lustra*, which is now the **seventh age**.

If you judge those things to have been considered by me truthfully, equitably, and not sluggishly, I shall rejoice that no small fruit of my studies is present for me. But if, as I fear, it turns out much otherwise, I will at least have shown you some effort on my part, and I will seem at least to have wished to please. Nevertheless, I will play with my own talent, and just as if the matter were so, I shall place Christ at the end of days—that is, that he assumed a body around the **seventh age** of this world.

The "body," however, as we have said, must be sought in multiplicity. Therefore, if after **Unity** (which signifies God, not a number), you fashion seven "houses" of numbers from all the numerical figures among the Hebrews, and enclose within each house a company of three arithmetic letters in order, it will clearly appear in the seventh house, and in the middle place of its connection, that the sign **Shin** <note>original: "Sin." The Hebrew letter ש (Shin/Sin). Reuchlin groups Hebrew letters in triads: (1,2,3), (4,5,6)... the 7th group being Resh (200), Shin (300), and Tav (400).</note> designates the Mediator of God and man: the God and man who made both one.

Moreover, the connection of the seventh house is specifically this: **Resh, Shin, Tav**, which is interpreted in Latin as "of the Head" <note>original: "Res, Sin, Thaf... Capitis." The root R-Sh-T in Hebrew refers to a beginning or head.</note>. **Shin** is the designation, as if the alphabet were foretelling and presaging by prophecy that this letter **Shin** would be the most fitting character for forming the name **IHSVH** <note>original: "ihsuh." Reuchlin’s Pentagrammaton: the ineffable YHWH made pronounceable by inserting the letter Shin (S), resulting in the name of Jesus.</note>, whom God the Father gave as the head over the whole church, which is his body, as we read written to the Ephesians.

This is what Moses seems to me to have once clearly foretold in **Deuteronomy**, in the twenty-eighth chapter <note>original: "capite duo de trigesimo." He is likely citing Deuteronomy 28:10.</note>, when he prophesies to the coming nation thus: "But if you hear the voice of **I.H.V.H.** your God"—behold, four vowels. Then after the abundance of blessing, it follows: "All the peoples of the earth shall see that the name **Tetragrammaton** <term>Tetragrammaton: The four-letter name of God (YHWH).</term> has been called upon you."

Indeed, this "heap of speech" from these three—namely, "Name," "Tetragrammaton," and "Called"—collected together, is entirely understood through the letter **Shin** by frequent usage according to the **Kabbalah** of the Hebrews. Thus, if the last things correspond to the first, this prophecy contains nothing else than: "if you hear the voice of the Tetragrammaton"—that is, when the Tetragrammaton becomes audible, which is to say, utterable—"then the name Tetragrammaton called through **Shin** shall be upon you." It is not otherwise than if he were saying: "If the ineffable name Tetragrammaton must become speakable, it will necessarily be called through the consonant named **Shin**, so that it becomes **IHSVH**, who will be over you, your head and your lord."

Blessed be God and the Father of our Lord **IHSVH** Christ, who from above instills in us the knowledge of the true name of his only-begotten Son and our Savior—or according to the Greeks, our Healer. This

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

For Greek authors derive the name from healing and curing <note>original: "medendo & sanando." Referring to the Greek word *iaomai* (to heal), which sounds like *Iesous*.</note>. Hebrew grammarians, however, derive it from saving, so that they think **ihsuh** <term>ihsuh: A Renaissance Latinization of the Hebrew name "Yeheshuah," interpreted as the Tetragrammaton (YHWH) with the letter "Shin" (S) inserted in the middle to represent the Word made flesh.</term> is the same as "savior." Yet "savior" is a common name, while **ihsuh** is most specific, such that it applies to no other than the incarnate Son of God. The variety of the letters from which the word is composed makes it so; such a word has never been heard like any other since the beginning of the world. In the memory of our fathers, there were many saviors called "Jesus," but with different letters and different syllables. For no one had ever dared to use these specific letters together for their own name, because the whole of divinity was contained within them. 

But whoever was considered to be endowed with some holiness greatly desired to obtain one of these sacred characters for their name, such as Abram receiving the letter *h*. For God said to him, "No longer shall your name be called Abram, but you shall be named Abraham." And **Hosea** obtained the *I*. For when Moses decided to send men to explore the land of Canaan, he placed Hosea son of Nun, of the tribe of Ephraim, in charge of them. So that he might have a more wholesome strength, Moses changed his name and added the letter *I*, the first letter of the ineffable name, and called Hosea son of Nun "Joshua" [Ihofue], which our people also translated as "Jesus." 

We also read of another from Beth-shemesh, whose memory is recorded in the Book of Samuel. There is a third by a similar name in the Book of Ezra, the son of Jehozadak, who does not keep the *h* but is named **Jeshua** [iesue]. We also find in the prayer of the prophet **Habakkuk**, in the third-to-last verse, when he says: "But I will rejoice in the Lord and exult in God my Jesus." This is again read composed of smaller syllables as *Iese*, that is, "salvation." It is as if the prophet were saying, "I will exult in God my salvation." This should by no means be considered the proper name of God, but rather an epithet or a descriptive construction. 

There is also a fourth, the high priest standing before the angel, from whom the angel took away his iniquity as Zechariah watched. He, like the son of Nun, is written with similar letters as **Joshua** [Ihosue]. It is thought that Zechariah, in that place, saw a shadow of the coming true light. We read of a fifth, Jesus son of Sirach, who lived in the time of Ptolemy, King of Egypt, surnamed Euergetes, and who is believed to have published the book known as *Ecclesiasticus*. 

However many there may be who are named "Jesus" in our tongue, none of them possesses those exact letters which this incarnate Word of God—our God, through whom the Creator made even the ages—possesses. His excellent name, full of admirable majesty—a renowned, happy, august name, a most weighty name of the greatest dignity and most powerful authority in heaven, of the highest magistracy on earth, pleasant to angels, revered by men, and terrifying to demons—is composed of **five letters**, that is, from vowels and one consonant, by the marvelous craftsmanship of the Holy Spirit. 

Just as he whose name it is is acknowledged to consist of divine and human essence—for as the rational soul and flesh are one man, so God and man are one Christ—even so the name **ihsuh** is pronounced with the vowels of the **Tetragrammaton** <term>Tetragrammaton: The four-letter Hebrew name of God, YHWH, often considered too holy to speak aloud.</term> and one consonant, **Shin** <note>original: "Sin." Reuchlin identifies the Hebrew letter "Shin" as the element that makes the unpronounceable name of God (YHWH) pronounceable as Jesus (YHShWH).</note>. This announces him who is *Iah* (that is, God) and *sauah* (that is, veiled). To him, Isaiah speaks: "Truly you are a hidden God, the God of Israel, the Savior." This is difficult to believe, most excellent men, if this weak speech of mine were not strengthened by the sacred monuments of the prophets. Therefore, I pray you, hear Isaiah speaking again...

<meta>This page continues the linguistic and mystical analysis of the Holy Name, moving from the naming of Hosea on the previous page to the specific symbolic nature of the letter Shin (which Reuchlin writes as "Sin") and how it identifies the Messiah as both divine and human, distinct from historical figures named Joshua.</meta>

### BOOK THREE
k ii

<margin>6</margin>
> For Zion’s sake I will not keep silent, and for Jerusalem’s sake I will not rest, until her righteousness goes forth as brightness, and her salvation is lit like a lamp, and you shall be called by a new name which the mouth of the Lord has named. <note>original: Isaiah 62:1-2</note>

Now, if this common surname had belonged to many among our ancestors, Christ would certainly not have possessed the **new name** which "the mouth of the Lord has named." For first, the name **Joshua**, which the Latins translate as **Jesus**, was not bestowed by God himself but by Moses. How then could the mouth of the Lord add a new name to Christ that human invention had previously devised? But lest the words of the prophet escape us, let us see whether a lamp, in its outward form and shape, is consistent with the letter **Sin** <note>The Hebrew letter *Shin* (ש), which Reuchlin interprets here as a three-branched lamp-stand</note>. With its physical outlines drawn around it, the lamp is equal and similar to that letter; in it, like the element **Iod** <note>The Hebrew letter *Yod* (י), the smallest letter, representing a spark or the presence of God</note> which designates divinity to the Hebrews, the wick shines. Its oil joins the lamp and the wick to the fire in such a way that, without all these things joined together, the brightness of the lamp cannot drive out the darkness. For this reason, Solomon sang:
> Your name is oil poured out. <note>original: "Oleum effusum nomen tuum," from Song of Songs 1:3</note>

Among the Hebrews, the word for **oil** begins with the letter **Sin** <note>Hebrew: *shemen* (שמן)</note>; therefore, the **Sin** is the mark of oil—that is, it stands as a fitting seal of the poured-out and expressible name. This name so saturates the person of Christ that whatever is said of one nature in him is also held to be true of the other. **Jesus** was born a man from a virgin; **Ihsuh** <term>Ihsuh: The "Wonder-Working Word," formed by placing the letter Shin (representing the Word) into the middle of the four-letter name of God (YHWH)</term> was also born as God from an incorrupt woman. Thus, she is rightly revered not only as the **Christotocos** <note>original Greek: "Christ-bearer," meaning mother of the Messiah's human person</note>, that is, the parent of Christ, but also as the **theotocos** <note>original Greek: "God-bearer," or Mother of God</note>. In this matter, **Nestorius** <note>A 5th-century bishop declared a heretic for suggesting that Mary was only the mother of Jesus's human nature, not his divine nature</note> acted imprudently and foolishly, seeking to measure divine action by logical fallacies. It is a great thing, worthy of wonder, but nonetheless true: in one subsistence, God was both born and God has died. And why say more? The Son of God is as much the Son of Man as the Son of Man is the Son of God. "Who do they say the Son of Man is?" The apostles replied, "Some say Elijah, others Jeremiah, or one of the prophets." Then the Son of Man questioned them—he who was clothed in flesh, he who stood there, he who wept, he who was to be betrayed, he who was to be scourged, he who was to be crowned with thorns, he who was to carry the cross, he who was to die: "But who do you say that I am?" **Peter**, the summit, the rock, the pillar of faith, and the mouth of the apostles, answered:
> You are the Christ, the Son of the living God. <note>original: Matthew 16:16</note>

But why speak so much of the apostles? Profane and unbelieving men might think they were merely flattering their teacher. Let us listen to Truth itself, which says:
> No one has ascended into heaven except the one who descended from heaven, the Son of Man who is in heaven. <note>original: John 3:13</note>

The Son of Man descended from heaven, and the Son of God ascended to the Father. Read Paul's letter to the **Ephesians**:
> He who descended is the very one who also ascended above all the heavens. <note>original: Ephesians 4:10</note>

Hear Christ speaking of himself:
> What if you were to see the Son of Man ascending to where he was before? <note>original: John 6:62</note>

Moreover, he was in heaven before, not in the form of a servant, but in the form of God. Now you clearly see what the oil of divinity, poured out bodily, has accomplished: how it anointed the whole Christ. It was not like other "christs" <note>Anointed ones</note>—**Saul**, **David**, or **Cyrus**—whom the grace of the Spirit sanctified by working through a horn of oil from the outside. Rather, it was as the present, indwelling deity, not merely assisting but remaining within, substantially saturating the whole of humanity with an essential anointing. Concerning this, the prophet says:
> God, your God, has anointed you above your companions. <note>original: Psalm 45:7</note>

By this, it came to pass that the "poured-out oil" provided him with a name more peculiar and excellent than those of his partners and fellows.

<summary>Reuchlin argues that the name of Jesus is unique and divine, using the Hebrew letter Shin as a mystical symbol of a lamp and oil to explain how Christ's human and divine natures are inseparable. He contrasts Christ's essential anointing with the external anointing of Old Testament figures like David or Saul.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the speaker’s explanation of how the addition of the Hebrew letter Shin (which Reuchlin writes as 'Sin') transforms the four-letter name of God into a five-letter name. This linguistic shift represents the mystery of the Incarnation—how the divine Word became human.</meta>

### ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

...which exists as the name called the **Tetragrammaton** <term>Tetragrammaton: the four-letter name of God in Hebrew, YHWH</term>, [transformed] by the letter **Sin** <note>Reuchlin uses 'Sin' to refer to the Hebrew letter *Shin* (ש)</note>, that is, **ihsuh** <note>This is Reuchlin's spelling for the five-letter name (Pentagrammaton) of Jesus: Y-H-Sh-V-H</note>. For it must be written with these five letters—this most holy and adorable name, **ihsuh**, which our ancestors, abbreviating it by the first three characters alone, depicted as **ihs**, as is correctly shown today in the most ancient manuscripts. This was much like the custom of the noble Romans, who would designate their first names with one, two, or three letters. Thus, imitating our fathers, we have similarly become accustomed to mutilating such a noble word with these three letters, **ihs**, so much so that the 's' is placed in the middle rather than at the end. 

In this matter, many—and indeed distinguished—grammarians of our age, being ignorant of the "barbarous" <note>In this context, 'barbarous' refers to non-Classical languages like Hebrew</note> Hebrew language, thought that the Latin aspiration 'h' was actually the seventh letter of the Greek alphabet, called **eta** <note>original: ἦτα</note>. They thought this because that Greek letter is often turned into our 'e'. For this reason, they determined in their rules of spelling that this name, which is worthy of worship, should be written without an aspiration [an 'h'], hardly noticing that Priscian of Caesarea <note>A famous 6th-century Latin grammarian</note> established his laws for Latin speakers, not for Hebrews. 

For unless that same twin aspiration [the letter 'h'] follows both after the 'i' and after the 'u', how will it imitate its original **Tetragrammaton**, or how will it be truly affirmed that it is written with five figures? For if a final letter 's' is found in either Greek or Latin speech, it should be judged not as part of the essential substance of this name, but as part of its grammatical ending, which does not remain fixed but is frequently changed into an 'n' in Greek or an 'm' in Latin. There are, therefore, only five marks by which the most sacred of all names, the **Pentagrammaton** <term>Pentagrammaton: a five-letter holy name</term>, is formed. Concerning this, we find this Sibylline verse <note>Referring to the *Sibylline Oracles*, a collection of ancient prophecies often cited by Christians as predicting Christ</note> consisting of three lines among the prophecies themselves:

> For then indeed the Son of the great God shall come to men, bearing flesh, becoming like mortals on earth. Carrying four vowels, and the voiceless one within him. <note>original Greek: Δὴ τότε γὰρ μεγάλοιο θεοῦ παῖς ἀνθρώποισιν. Ἥξει σαρκοφόρος θνητοῖς ὁμοιούμενος ἐν γῇ. Τέσσαρα φωνήεντα φέρον τὸ δ' ἄφωνον ἐν αὐτῷ.</note>

This was translated from Greek into Latin by us as follows:

> The very offspring of God, the great parent, about to come, shall be seen in mortal likeness in ailing flesh. Therefore he shall bear four vowels, and one sounds together [one consonant]. <note>original Latin: "Ipsa dei soboles magni uentura parentis, Mortali similis in carne uidebitur ægra. Quattuor ergo feret uocales, consonat una."</note>

Now, the patience you have humanely shown in listening to me, out of your goodness of character, has caused me to make my discourse longer in its delivery. For this reason, I earnestly ask that you grant pardon for my lack of restraint and for this liberty I have taken—not so much in formal speaking as in mere talking. For I seem now to have sufficiently, and perhaps even excessively, fulfilled my promises to you regarding the **Wonder-Working Word** in that **Pentagrammaton**, the name **ihsuh**. This is the most proper name of the Ruler of all, who, departing from us, testified these things about himself, saying: "All power is given to me in heaven and on earth." <note>original: Matthew 28:18</note> We should know this, according to the Epistle to the Ephesians: 

> what is the exceeding greatness of the power of God toward us who believe, according to the working of his mighty power which he worked in Christ, raising him from the dead and setting him at his right hand in the heavenly places, far above all principality, and power, and might, and dominion, and every name that is named, not only in this world but also in that which is to come, and has put all things under his feet. <note>original: Ephesians 1:19-22</note>

And Paul says to the Philippians: "He exalted him and gave him a name which is above every name, so that at the name of **ihsuh** every knee should bow, of things in heaven, and things in earth, and things under the earth." <note>original: Philippians 2:9-10</note> He has been made so much better than the angels—as it stands in the letters sent to the Hebrews—as he has inherited a more excellent name than they. To conclude this burden of proof gathered from all these documents...

<summary>Reuchlin argues that the name of Jesus is a five-letter "Pentagrammaton" (IHSHVH) created by inserting the Hebrew letter Shin into the holy four-letter name of God. He uses Greek prophecy and New Testament epistles to justify this specific spelling as the "Wonder-Working Word."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the speaker's argument that the name of Jesus (spelled by Reuchlin as the five-letter 'ihsuh') is the supreme source of power.
